# Changelog

## Unreleased
- 🛰️ **`/decat` no longer meets a question with Discord's own *"Bot is thinking…"* — the wait is a card, with a joke or a short line out of the bot's own `/quote`, and no joke at all for a question about somebody's own life**: *"While Its Thinking Show Like DeCat Is Thinking Though GoldFish Brain Or Joke Like This Or Quote From /Quote or smt improvement or fun"*. The wait was the one part of this feature with nothing to look at, and a Deep answer off somebody's laptop is not a short wait.
  - **The card is a real first response rather than a defer.** The callback used to answer the interaction with `defer(ephemeral=…)` and let Discord's placeholder say *"Bot is thinking…"* in Discord's own voice, over an empty message; it now sends a card — the lane's colour, the joke line, a footer naming the work in progress — and the answer **replaces it in place**, so a question is still one message from the asker's side. The refusals are untouched and still arrive *before* the card (`offline rn` and the Mac's switch are not edits to a card that promised the machine was working), which is what that part of the cog's docstring now says instead of "before anything is deferred".
  - **A joke is a gift you can only give to somebody who is not upset, and that rule is a function of the question.** `copy.jokes_allowed` is false for the *personal* lane and for anything that reads like somebody in danger, and those questions get `copy.THINKING_KIND` (*"give it a moment — this one gets thought about properly"*) instead of any of the eight joke lines. The reason is the whole point of the rule: this card is the **first** thing a person sees, not the last, so a question that has just said *"i can't cope"* must not be met with a goldfish. The quote obeys the same gate, because a quote corpus carries lines like *"Give up on your dreams and die for us."* that are funny in the right company and cruel in the wrong one.
  - **The quote is borrowed from `cogs/fun.py` rather than copied, and a line too long to glance at is dropped rather than cut short.** A second copy of a corpus somebody maintains with episode numbers and moods attached is a second thing to keep in step — the failure this repository has a class of laws about. So the *rule* lives in `decat/copy.py` (`select_quote`, which takes the rows and returns `("", "")` for junk, for an empty corpus, and for a 400-character line), and `cogs/decat.short_quote` is only the borrower, importing the corpus **inside the function** with an `except` around it: a corpus that moved or broke must leave the wait with the joke lines rather than nothing at all. 108 of the 144 lines in `QUOTES` fit in `SHORT_QUOTE_MAX = 88` characters, so there is plenty to pick from; a cut quotation would have been a misquotation, which is why the long ones are skipped instead of trimmed.
  - **The line is a hash of the job id, not `random`, and that is what makes it testable as well as varied.** `copy.pick_index` is a 31-hash, so one question always gets the same line (the law asserts the card is equal to itself for one seed) while two questions get different ones (the law asserts more than one line across twenty-four seeds) — and no test here can be flaky. The footer carries the depth, in two sentences rather than one, because a `Fast` question that sat there for a minute would otherwise look like a broken `Fast`: `reading the live web on the operator's own Mac — then re-reading its answer` against `one quick pass … — no re-check`.
  - **Eleven new laws, and one of them was watched to fail rather than asserted in prose.** `tests/test_decat.py` went 42 → **53**: one question one line across seeds, no joke and no quote on a personal or crisis question (with the plain lines checked one by one, not just the kind sentence), the two footers differ, a quote at the limit is shown and one character over it is not, junk rows reach nothing, a seed that is not a string still picks something, the corpus actually supplies a line (and attributes it), and the cog **sends the card instead of deferring, before it waits on the machine**. Making `jokes_allowed` return `True` unconditionally fails three of them at once (`AssertionError: True is not false`), which is the mutation that matters here.
  - **The whole suite on the commit that shipped — `316b4af`, from a `git worktree` rather than the shared checkout — is 3,512 tests, 2 failed, 2 errored, 3 skipped.** The four are the same machine-only four as last pass and none is Decat's: three radio laws needing `davey` (not installed) and one mirror law needing `huggingface_hub` (not installed). The 11-test difference from that run's 3,501 is exactly this pass's laws. `/help`'s Decat entry gained the sentence naming `/quote`, and the panel's law that every command it names exists still passes because `/quote` is a real command in `cogs/fun.py`.
  - **Live on the Space and read back:** the code landed as commit **`6840f06202ea3a2a0a8c3dc5a9926212b0d18ef5`** (this entry rides on top of it), runtime **`RUNNING`** on that sha, `/health` answering `{"status": "healthy", "service": "dacat-gateway"}`, `Loaded cogs.decat` in the boot ring, `/api/decat/next` still **401** without a credential, and the mirror check reading `to send: 0` — the five changed files and nothing else went up. **What has never been seen is the card itself**: the first live qualification of it is the first question somebody asks, and it is the same four owed runs on the Mac (a web question, a picture, the switch off, the lid shut) that will show it.
  - **One sentence of this file's own Decat entry is now out of date, and it is corrected rather than quietly left.** It says the cog *"defers **after** it has something to defer (offline and switched-off are answered before the `thinking` message, not as an edit to it)"* — true of the commit that bullet describes, and no longer true of the code, which has no defer left in it at all. The gate order it names is the part that still holds.
- 🛰️ **`/decat` lets the asker choose how hard it works, gains a test that drives both halves over the real routes with no Mac and no model, and answers *"is there a double command?"* with a law**: *"Add a fake Mac to the test suite: an in-process stand-in agent that polls the real routes and answers, proving the whole wire without Ollama. And Also ig there is double cmd. And ig let ppl decide they want deep ans or fast ans. And How will I turn on or off?"* Four asks, four answers — and one of the four was a defect this pass had created and not noticed.
  - **`depth: Deep|Fast`, and the heuristic it replaced is gone.** The depth travels on the wire (`Job.depth`, a new *optional* field, so `VERSION` stays 1 — an older laptop ignoring it reads the default, which is exactly the intended behaviour) and the agent reads it for three things: which model answers, whether the second pass runs, and how much it reads. **`Deep` is the default** and is what the feature already did; `Fast` is one completion on the small model, one search, two pages. The rule that used to decide this on its own is deleted (`SHORT_QUESTION = 160`), because it was the wrong place for the decision: only the person asking knows whether they are mid-conversation or checking a fact they are about to act on, and *"is it safe to drive with a broken wrist"* is nine words and worth the good model. `Fast` on a long question and `Fast` on a picture both mean the small model now, because that is what was chosen rather than what was inferred.
  - **`Fast` never borrows the word *checked*, and that needed a third state rather than a toggle.** A fast answer is read from live sources that nobody went back over — grounded but unverified — which the card would have rendered as *"checked against the sources it cites"*, this feature's own standard violated by its own footer. So `copy.QUICK_LABEL` (*"drafted from the sources it cites — the quick answer, not re-read"*) is now the middle of three, and the two states around it are unchanged, which is what makes it a decision instead of a rewording: an answer with no sources still says *"no live sources reached"*, and the depth and the model are both on the card. **The asymmetry in the parsers is deliberate and pinned**: an unreadable *mode* fails **closed** to `Private` (saving is what cannot be taken back) while an unreadable *depth* fails **open** to `Deep` (the guess costs seconds and buys the better answer).
  - **The switch question has three answers and the card that shows the symptom names all three.** "How will I turn on or off?" is not one switch: the **Mac's** (`decat off` on the laptop, or the one button at `http://127.0.0.1:8787`), the **server's** (the Decat switch on its dashboard Systems page, which removes the command from that server's picker rather than refusing it), and the **deployment's** (`DECAT_GUILDS`, the hard allow-list that no dashboard click can widen — currently the two named servers). `/bot decat` now carries a field saying exactly that, beside the machine's live state, so the answer travels with the symptom instead of living in a document nobody opens; `decat/README.md` has the same three, and the Mac's switch is the one the asker hears about, because *"the Mac's switched off rn"* is a different sentence from *"offline rn"* on purpose. **And the `Fast` lane can no longer be a lie**: the installer pulled only the big model while writing `DECAT_FAST_MODEL=qwen3-vl:4b` into `agent.env`, so the first `Fast` question on a fresh install would have been an error about a model nobody pulled — it pulls the 3.3 GB small model now, and `decat doctor` gained a line for it, present exactly when a second model is configured.
  - **The stand-in Mac: both halves over the real routes, with only the model, the search and the picture faked.** `TestTheWholeWireWithoutAMac` drives the shipped Flask app through `test_client()` (in-process, no socket, no port) with the **real** `decat.mac.agent.Transport` pointed at it by injecting only its two HTTP calls — so the question crosses as the bytes the route actually produced, and parses back into an *equal* `Job`. The worry it exists to answer is not that either half is untested, it is that each might be tested against a **copy** of the other: a fake transport on one side, a fake relay on the other, and no test anywhere that the two agree, which is the rot `decat/protocol.py` was written to prevent and could not itself detect. Nine laws, and the two that matter were **watched to fail rather than asserted in prose**: deleting `"depth": self.depth` from `Job.to_wire` kills three of them (including the round trip, with `Job(depth='deep') != Job(depth='fast')`), and making `_decat_ok` a no-op kills the stranger's-poll law with `200 != 401`. It runs in a fifth of a second, because the held-open poll is bounded to one second in the test and every poll in it has a question waiting.
  - **"Is there a double command?" — the honest answer is two-part, and the half that is a defect now has a law.** A name registered **twice in one scope** is the defect: Discord keys a command by its name, so the second registration replaces the first and the handler behind it becomes unreachable in silence — including inside a group. That is `test_no_two_commands_share_a_name` in `tests/test_commands.py`, over the real loaded tree and every group's verbs. The other half is not visible from the tree at all and is the one the question was actually about: `/dbstatus` sat in three servers' pickers (and in Discord's global lane) with no handler behind it, because it had folded into `/bot` and **the sync lane only ever adds or updates** — its one delete rule is restricted to the global lane *and* to names the registry still declares, so a **renamed** command is exactly what it can never let go of. Those four stale registrations were deleted by hand last pass and re-read as gone; the durable fix is a retire-list the per-server lane may also delete from, still owed and still named rather than attempted, because that lane writes to every server the bot is in. **The deliberate pair is a different thing**: `/decat` is every member's way to ask, and `/bot decat` is the operator's read-out of the machine behind it — two audiences, two permission rows, one of which no member ever sees.
  - **What was actually run here.** The whole suite ran on the **commit that shipped** — from a `git worktree` at `97f357a`, not from the checkout another thread is still editing — which is the only tree whose numbers mean anything: **3,501 tests, 2 failed, 2 errored, 3 skipped**, seventeen more tests than the previous pass and **the same four failures, none of them this change's**: three radio laws that need `davey` and one mirror-tool law that needs `huggingface_hub`, neither installed on this machine. Both mutations above were run and reverted before that count. CI remains the one check this pass could not run at all, because the account it would run on is suspended.
  - **Reported rather than quietly fixed: one sentence of this feature's own changelog was wrong.** The bullet below describes `/api/decat/next` as answering `204` when nothing is waiting; the route answers `200` with `{"job": null}`, which is what the wire test now asserts and what the laptop actually got on its first live poll. A status code claimed from memory and printed as a read-back is the same failure this file exists to catch, one level up.
- 🛰️ **`/decat` asks the operator's own laptop a question and the laptop answers it with the live web — because the only model in this feature runs on a machine nobody can send a training set to**: *"Add a slash command `/decat` that relays a question from the bot's backend to my MacBook Air M5 (16 GB / 512 GB), where Ollama runs, answers it using the web with citations, and posts the answer back to Discord. If the Mac is offline, say 'sry, offline rn — can't really msg bbg 😿'. `Private` saves nothing anywhere. A switch on the Mac turns it off. Advice and therapist-style answers are fine with a small 'I'm not a professional' warning."* It is built as asked, and the two honest constraints are stated rather than papered over: **the answer is not 100% accurate and no copy here claims it is**, and **the Mac has no inbound port**, so the relay is the laptop polling the Space rather than the Space reaching into a home network.
  - **The transport is a poll, and the whole of it is three routes on the Space.** `GET /api/decat/next` is what the laptop calls (it hands over at most one queued question and answers `{"job": null}` when there is nothing — one status a poller reads, rather than a second one it has to tell apart from an outage), `POST /api/decat/answer` is the answer coming back, and `GET /api/decat/status` is the operator's read-out — machine state, queue depth, counts, last model and lane. The credential is `X-Decat-Token` (a JSON `token` also works) compared with `secrets.compare_digest`, and it falls back to `ADMIN_PASSKEY` when `DECAT_TOKEN` is unset, so a deployment that never sets the new variable is not a deployment that never answers. Deliberately **not** passkey-throttled, because a laptop polling every fifteen seconds is a poller, not a brute-force. All three are named in `keep_alive.GATED_PREFIXES` rather than left to the fail-closed default, because "safe by accident" is the failure that classification test exists to catch.
  - **The model is `qwen3-vl:8b` and its location is the privacy story, not a footnote.** It is about 6 GB, takes text and images, and runs on the operator's own machine — **no model vendor is called, so nothing is trained on anybody's question by a third party**, which is the answer to "a model that won't train on user data" that actually holds: there is no third party to agree with. A short picture-less question (<160 chars) takes the `qwen3-vl:4b` lane, and `decat fast_model` is blanked by default so a deployment that only pulled the big model still works. `decat/config.py` carries the one-line override for each of `DECAT_HOME`, model, fast model, `OLLAMA_HOST`, `DECAT_TOKEN` and `DECAT_GUILDS`.
  - **"Answers using the web with citations", and the maximum-accuracy promise is kept by structure rather than by asking the model nicely.** The question is turned into a few searches (`decat/mac/tools/web.py`, DDG by default, Brave and SearxNG optional), pages are fetched through a **per-question host allow-list** and flattened to readable text, and only then is a draft written with **numbered sources** the card prints verbatim; a **second pass re-reads the draft against the sources and strikes a sentence it cannot support** (with a floor, so a verifier that returns nothing cannot empty the answer). An answer with **no live sources** is labelled as such on the card instead of being dressed up as a search result. What the model is *told* is pinned in `decat/mac/prompts.py` — retrieved text is **data, not instructions**, and that sentence is a law in the tests rather than a comment.
  - **The two notices that must never be lost are attached by a function, not by the model remembering.** `decat.copy.lane_of` decides the lane from the question (a factual question, advice, or something personal), and `warning_for` attaches the small *"I'm an AI, not a professional"* line to anything legal, medical, money, safety or personal — with a separate crisis card naming a helpline for the questions where being told to *see a professional* is not enough. The model is also told to write both; the copy module is what makes them appear whether or not it does, and the tests walk the word lists that route them.
  - **`Private` is a fact about storage, not a label on a card.** `decat/mac/store.py` refuses a non-public job outright (an exception, not a convention), and the relay on the Space keeps a question only for the life of its own request — `relay.settle(job.id)` **deletes the slot, the answer and the words** before the card is edited, so a queued question cannot be read out of the Space afterwards. The Space's process keeps nothing in either mode; `Public` appends one JSONL line (counts and timings, `0600`, one file per month) to `~/.decat` on the laptop, which is why the policy can say *only the operator's machine, only for a public question*. `tests/test_decat.py` has a law named for it (`TestTheRelayKeepsNothing`) that greps `stats()`, the queue and the in-flight map for the question's own words after a settle.
  - **The scope is exactly two servers, and the switch on the Mac is a third gate that says a different thing.** `DECAT_GUILDS` names the servers (`1550227368889684058`, `1542278896148422676`); anywhere else answers *"decat doesn't run in this server."* The module switch is per-server and **off by default** (`enabled_by_default=False`, the shape Alone Time ships in), and the laptop's own switch is a file-backed `decat on|off` with a loopback page at `http://127.0.0.1:8787` — a Mac that is awake, in-scope and switched off is **`"the Mac's switched off rn"`, which is not the same sentence as the offline one**, because they are different problems with different fixes. The offline line is the one that was asked for, word for word.
  - **`/dbstatus` folded into `/bot dbstatus` to pay for the slot, and the cap law records why.** Discord allows a hundred top-level commands and the tree was at exactly a hundred, so the database report moved into the `/bot` group — alongside a new `/bot decat` read-out of the relay — and the report itself moved to a new `utils/dbstats.py` so both the command and the tests can read it without Discord. `helpers` folded nothing: `command_count()` stays at **100**, `system_count()` moves 18 → **19**, `docs/discord-directory.md` says nineteen, and the cap law's docstring now names Decat as the reason `/dbstatus` moved. `tests/test_dbstatus.py` was rewritten around the move (`TestTheMoveToTheBotGroup`) rather than deleted, because the *report* is still the thing being pinned; only its address changed.
  - **The gateway is thin and the laptop is a folder.** `cogs/decat.py` is the only Discord-facing file: it draws the card from `decat/card.py` (Discord-free, so the words are testable), defers **after** it has something to defer (offline and switched-off are answered before the "thinking" message, not as an edit to it), and waits with `asyncio.to_thread(relay.wait, ...)` — blocking the loop on a laptop that went to sleep is how a bot stops answering anything else. The rest of the feature is `decat/` — `protocol.py` (the wire, fail-closed to private), `config.py`, `copy.py`, `card.py`, `relay.py`, and `decat/mac/` (agent, ollama client, prompts, web and image tools, history, the switch page, a `doctor`, a launchd `install.sh`, and an operator `README.md`). The Mac half installs with `bash decat/mac/install.sh` and is checked with `decat doctor` — Ollama, the model, the search host, the credential and the Space, in that order.
  - **What was actually run here, and what only the laptop can answer.** The whole suite ran natively on `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3.14` (3.14.8, `discord.py` 2.7.1 in a throwaway target): **3,486 tests, 7 failed, 2 errored, 3 skipped**. None of the nine is this feature's, and four of the fixes above are what that run found — an unused `import re` left in `cogs/server_control.py` by the `/dbstatus` move, a `TERMS.md`/`PRIVACY.md` line that had wrapped *"AI agents"* across a newline and broken the disclosure law, the three new routes needing their side of the public/gated split written down, and **`/decat` needing the cog-level module gate every other gated cog has** (the law is that every command a server loses is one the bot would refuse, and a command hidden with a live callback is that law broken). The rest are named rather than waved at: `automod.py`'s new `rule.edit()` (another thread's uncommitted work), two dashboard/design laws from the same uncommitted set, `scripts/mirror_space.py` needing `huggingface_hub`, and the two radio laws needing `davey` — neither library being installed on this machine, and both of those files untouched, rather than any test weakened to accommodate them. **CI would be the authority for the merge and cannot run at all: GitHub is gone for this account** — the remote answers *"Repository not found"*, and the API names the reason the push does not (`{"message": "Sorry. Your account was suspended"}`, 403 on both `/repos/1tsRajOwO/Meow` and `/user`) — so the deploy went to the Space through the Hub's own commit API, the door the previous passes already used, and one proof cannot be had here at all: the end-to-end run needs the machine that has Ollama on it, so what is owed is `decat doctor` green followed by four live `/decat` runs — a web question, a picture, one with the Mac's switch off, and one with the lid shut. **And the run that decides it is the one on the commit that shipped rather than on the checkout another thread is still editing**, from a `git worktree` at `9f87e21`: **3,484 tests, 2 failed, 2 errored, 3 skipped — and all four are this machine rather than this change.** `huggingface_hub` is not installed, so `scripts/mirror_space.py`'s own `--check` refusal cannot be exercised here (`ModuleNotFoundError`), and `davey` is not installed, so the radio's two laws fail with its own card saying `davey` is missing by name. The other thread's automod/dashboard/design failures are simply absent from that tree, which is the honest statement of what was verified: **nothing in the shipped commit fails for a reason Decat caused**, and the only check this pass could not have run at all is CI's, because the account it would run on is suspended.
  - **And it is live on the Space, read back rather than assumed.** The Space carries it as commit **`851a72915a2f145d77874745227b5e6d80f0e2ea`** — runtime **`RUNNING`** on that sha, `/health` answering `{"status": "healthy", "service": "dacat-gateway"}`, and the boot log's `Loaded cogs.decat` among the twenty-three. The scope the request names is a deployment fact and is now set: `DECAT_GUILDS` holds the two servers, read back from `/api/decat/status` as `"servers": [1542278896148422676, 1550227368889684058]`, and `POST /api/set-module` answered `{"success": true, "module": "decat", "enabled": true}` for both. The three routes behave as written without a credential — **401** on `/api/decat/next` and `/api/decat/status` — and `/api/decat/status` with the passkey answers counts and timings and `"last_poll_seconds": null`, which is the honest "no laptop has ever polled yet". **Discord's own read-back is the interface**: `/decat` is present in both named servers and **absent from the third** (Data Base, which the allow-list excludes), with `question` required, `mode` offering exactly *Public* and *Private*, and `image` an attachment — and `/bot dbstatus` and `/bot decat` sitting beside it.
  - **The rename walked into something the command lane has no answer for, and it is reported here because it was live rather than argued about.** Removing `/dbstatus` from the tree frees the slot locally and does **nothing** on Discord: `commands_sync.diff` only ever adds and updates, and the one deletion rule in this tree (`removable`/`prunable_globals`) is deliberately restricted to the global lane *and* requires the name to still be declared — so a **renamed** command leaves a dead registration in the picker of every server that had published it, plus a copy in the shared lane. That was observed, not reasoned: all three of the bot's servers still held a guild-scoped `dbstatus` and the global lane held a seventh name, none of which routed to anything any more, so `/dbstatus` in the picker would have answered *nothing at all*. The three guild copies and the global one were deleted (`204` each) and re-read as gone, which is the whole of the live symptom and the first time this tree has ever removed a command from the registry — the durable fix is a **retire-list the per-server lane may also delete from**, and it is named in the handover rather than attempted here, because the lane that would carry it writes to every server the bot is in.
- 🗣️ **A whole-server lockdown stops the members, not the people running the server — administrators and the owner keep talking**: *"wat if its whole server lockdown and admin can't msg fahh allow admin and owner to msg"*. The previous pass made a locked channel strict for everyone, administrators and the owner included, because a permission *cannot* stop them and the sweep is the only thing that answers them — which is right for one room and wrong for a whole server: a raid lockdown nobody can coordinate through is a lockdown the staff lose. **So a lock now records who it holds, not only how to undo itself.** `/lockdown-server` is the one lock that leaves administrators and the owner talking; `/lockdown` on a single channel is unchanged and still holds for everyone, which is the decision the screenshot came from.
  - **The scope lives in the row, because the enforcement must never guess which command wrote a lock.** `lockdown_state` gains `scope` (via the same idempotent `db.ensure_columns` the loans, support and Alone Time tables use), written by the same `save_lockdown` that records the undo — one row, both answers, so "is this locked" and "who is it held for" can never disagree about a channel. `db.lockdown_locks` is the enforcement's own reading (`(channel_id, scope)`) and `db.lockdown_rows` stays the *undo*'s (`previous`, `locked_at`), because the two are asked at different moments by different callers; a row written before the column existed answers `channel` — **the strict answer**, since a lock that was already there is not one that should loosen itself when the upgrade runs.
  - **The sweep asks both questions from one cached read.** `_LOCKED_CACHE` now holds `{channel_id: scope}`, and `locked_channel_ids` (the panel's reading), `is_locked` (the wall's question) and the new `lock_scope` all answer from it — still one query per server per ten seconds, still forgotten by every lock and unlock path. `staff_exempt(scope)` is the pure rule the whole thing turns on: **only `server` exempts rank**, and a blank or unknown scope answers strictly. A thread answers with its parent channel's scope, exactly as it does for `is_locked`.
  - **What the exemption actually stops is all three halves of the sweep.** An administrator's or the owner's message in a whole-server-locked channel is not deleted, **no DM is sent** (a message that stayed is not one to warn about), and **nothing they send counts toward the burst ladder** — so their messages can never be the next person's timeout either. Rank is read from the member's own permissions (`Administrator`) and the guild's `owner_id` rather than a role's name, because a server may call a role anything and what matters is the power Discord grants it. In a per-channel lock every one of those laws is unchanged, and the previous pass's laws pin it: an administrator *is* removed, warned and timed out there.
  - **The cards say which lock you are about to get, rather than the reader finding out by typing.** `/lockdown-server`'s card now reads *"...but **administrators and the server's owner can still talk**, because a whole server nobody can coordinate through is a lockdown the staff cannot run. `/lockdown` on one channel is the stricter lock: that one holds for everyone, administrators included."*, and `/lockdown`'s own card is untouched. **A lock adopted from before the ledger says which kind it was**, read back from the row rather than guessed: an old server-wide lockdown left the same overwrite on *every* text channel and nothing else does, so `adoption_scope` adopts a guild where every manageable channel holds the shape as **one server-wide lockdown** (staff exempt, and the boot log says `adopted 3 as one server-wide lockdown (#a, #b)`), and anything less as the per-channel lock it looks like. The adoption card in the log now says which of the two it is before somebody types into a channel and finds out.
  - **Eleven new laws in `tests/test_server_control.py`** (102 in the file, 92 of them async): the pure rule (`staff_exempt` true for `server` alone, false for `channel`, blank and `None`), each command recording its own scope and a server-wide lockdown leaving an existing per-channel lock alone, an administrator's message staying **and no DM**, the owner staying with **no timeout after a full burst**, anybody else still removed **and warned**, the administrator's messages never entering the ladder (`note_locked_message` answers nothing after ten of them), **one channel's lock still holding the staff**, a thread answering with its parent's scope, **a ledger written before the column holding everyone** (the table is rebuilt in its old shape, the boot's `db.setup()` adds the column, and an administrator is still removed), and the adoption inference both ways (every manageable channel → one server-wide lockdown; one of several → that channel only). The fakes grew the one thing Discord reports and the sweep now reads: `_FakeMember(administrator=True)`. **Three mutations were watched to fail**: the exemption never applying (five laws), the exemption applying to every lock (four, including the per-channel strictness the last pass shipped), and the adoption never inferring a server-wide lock (the inference law).
  - **What was actually run here.** The whole suite ran natively on `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3.14` (3.14.8, `discord.py` 2.7.1 in a throwaway target): **3,386 passed / 5 failed / 29 skipped**, the five being the same pre-existing failures in another thread's uncommitted automod/dashboard/design work, reproduced without these files.
  - **The deploy carried the pass, and the pass carried a defect the deploy found: the live database never got the new column, so the whole feature read as *nothing is locked* on the machine that matters.** The Space's log ring answered `[CONTROL] could not read the lockdown ledger: no such column: scope` — five lines, in the window a person watches — which is `locked_channels` failing its one read and, by its own deliberate design, returning `{}`. That is the enforcement seeing an unlocked server: no removals, no warnings, no exemption, every server. The reading was the deliverable here, not the guess: the live backup was **downloaded from Supabase storage** (`bot-backups/bot.db`, uploaded 15 minutes earlier by the running loop) and `PRAGMA table_info(lockdown_state)` answered five columns — `guild_id`, `channel_id`, `previous`, `locked_at`, `locked_by` — and **no `scope`**. The prior code could not have produced that error (its read is `lockdown_rows`, which never mentions the column) and the deployed files are byte-identical to HEAD, so the new code was genuinely running against an old-shape file.
  - **The cause is that a boot-time `db.setup()` is not a promise about the file underneath it, which this repository already learned once and wrote down.** `utils.cloud_sync` restores a backup by `os.replace`-ing `bot.db` under the running process, so the file can be older than the code reading it, and the column was added by `db.setup()` alone. The defect reproduced locally against the real downloaded file, in the same breath: running the shipped `db.setup()` on that copy **did** add the column and read the lone row (`guild 1542278896148422676`, channel `1555562743510073394`) as `channel` — so the code was right and the guarantee was in the wrong place.
  - **The fix is the same shape as the meetings tables'**: the ledger's table and its post-ship column are stated once (`db.LOCKDOWN_SCHEMA`, `db.LOCKDOWN_COLUMNS`) and made by **two callers** instead of one — `db.setup()` as before, and `ensure_lockdown_schema`, which `save_lockdown`, `lockdown_locks`, `lockdown_rows` and `forget_lockdown` each run on their own connection before they touch the table. `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` is a no-op on an existing table, which is why the column goes through the same idempotent `ensure_columns`; a reader may call it every time, and the single call in `setup()` is now the shared definition rather than a second copy of it.
  - **A twelfth law, and the mutation that proves it is the bug**: `test_a_restored_ledger_heals_without_a_boot` rebuilds the ledger in its old shape and calls **no `db.setup()`** — then asks for the lock's scope and runs the real listener over an administrator's message. Removing the one `ensure_lockdown_schema` line from `lockdown_locks` makes it fail with **the exact live symptom** (`'' != 'channel'`, and `[CONTROL] could not read the lockdown ledger: no such column: scope` on stdout), which is the confirmation that the law is pinned to the defect and not to a description of it. Against the real downloaded backup the reader added the column **in place** and returned the existing lock as `channel`, with no boot involved. `tests/test_server_control.py` is now **103**; the suite is **3,387 passed / 5 failed / 29 skipped**, the five unchanged pre-existing failures in another thread's files.
  - **And the fix is live on the Space, read back rather than assumed.** The Space carries it as commit **`cf0c8c21a6f2037b11047211b4cc1bf45e3c00f0`** — runtime **`RUNNING`** on that sha, `/health` answering `{"status": "healthy", "service": "dacat-gateway"}`, and all three files **byte-identical to HEAD by sha256**. Its boot log carries the line the last deploy was missing — `[DB] Added column(s) scope to lockdown_state.` — and the log ring now holds **zero** `no such column` / `could not read the lockdown ledger` lines where the previous deploy held three. The live backup was then downloaded again and `PRAGMA table_info(lockdown_state)` answers **six** columns with `scope TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'channel'` last, and the beta server's one row (`guild 1542278896148422676`, channel `1555562743510073394`) reads as **`channel`** — the strict lock it actually is — rather than being lost. **Still owed, unchanged**: the in-Discord proof, which needs a human typing into a locked channel.
- 🔓 **A channel locked before the ledger existed arms itself at boot, so an old lock starts holding without an `/unlock` and `/lockdown` dance**: *"Make channels locked before the ledger existed arm themselves, so an old lock starts enforcing without an unlock-and-relock dance."* Every `/lockdown` before the last pass wrote a permission and nothing else, which left channels that are **locked and unrecorded** — a deny the enforcement walks straight past, because the row is what it reads. The beta server's original lock is one of those, and until now the only way to make the bot notice it was to open the channel and lock it again.
  - **Discord's audit log is the only evidence an old lock has, and asking it is the whole safety of the pass.** A channel is a **candidate** when it holds exactly the overwrite the old command wrote — Send Messages denied and *nothing allowed* (`looks_like_our_lock`), because that command replaced the overwrite, so any allow bit means somebody has changed it since — and is text, is manageable, and has no row (`adoption_candidates`). The log then decides. The three overwrite actions (`overwrite_create`/`update`/`delete`) are **separate reads**, so the newest change to a channel is not simply the first line that comes back: every entry about a candidate is kept and the one with the highest snowflake id (`_entry_id`) is the one that decides, and adoption happens only when that newest entry's `user_id` is this bot's. A channel an administrator denied by hand — or one where the bot's *older* edit sits underneath an administrator's newer hand-deny — is staff talking among themselves, and a sweep that started deleting their messages and timing them out would be this feature mistaking somebody else's decision for its own. A log that cannot be read is not evidence: it adopts nothing and prints why.
  - **The row records no previous overwrite, because there is none to restore.** Every pre-ledger `/lockdown` replaced whatever the channel had and never wrote it down, so adoption is *only* the row: no permission is touched, and `/unlock` opens the channel by **removing the deny** rather than inverting it — the action the old `/unlock` took, without the stray allow it used to hand out. A channel already in the ledger is skipped, so adopting never overwrites the undo from before a real lock; a channel the bot cannot manage is left out, because a row for a lock it cannot lift would be a trap — messages removed with no way to open the room again.
  - **One pass, at boot, and one card in the server's log.** `ServerControl.adopt` is a `tasks.loop(count=1)` started in the cog's `__init__` — a loop rather than an `on_ready` listener, because `on_ready` fires again on every reconnect and this must not keep asking the audit log the same question — and cancelled in `cog_unload`. Each server is surrounded by its own `try`, so one guild cannot stop the next; where anything was adopted, the log gets one card naming the channels, saying they were locked before this bot kept records, and that `/unlock` in a channel opens it again. The channels themselves stay silent, which is the point.
  - **Fifteen new laws in `tests/test_server_control.py`** — the shape (a denied send with nothing allowed; an allow bit is somebody else's change; a plain deny is not a lock), the decision (the bot's own create is adopted; an administrator's entry is not; the newest of *any* kind of change decides rather than the first action read; an entry about another role does not speak for `@everyone`; a log that never mentions the channel, or cannot be read, adopts nothing), the boundaries (already in the ledger, an allow bit, a voice channel, a channel it cannot manage), the row (written with nothing to restore; no permission touched), the payoff (**an adopted lock answers `is_locked`, and the real `/unlock` removes the deny and clears the row**), and the cog end to end (**the boot pass adopts, the log card names the channel, and the listener then deletes a message posted into it and warns its sender**). The fakes grew a `Guild.audit_logs` (no log means no evidence rather than an empty page), an `_AuditEntry` carrying its snowflake, and an `_AuditLog` that can answer a different page per action type. **Four mutations were watched to fail** rather than asserted in prose: the ownership check inverted (five laws), the ledger check ignored (one), the id comparison replaced with first-match-wins (the cross-action law), and the reading's candidate count made to show the adopted count instead (the law that a server is answered whether or not anything was claimed).
  - **One line per server in the host log, because a pass that says nothing when it finds nothing cannot be told from a pass that never ran.** The first deploy of this pass proved why that matters: there was no way to read back from the Space whether the pass had run at all. `adopt_legacy_locks` now answers every guild — *“locks older than the ledger in guild …: N candidate(s), adopted M (#name, …)”* — and the cog stopped printing the same line twice. That reading is what the read-back below quotes.
  - **What was actually run here.** The whole suite ran natively again on `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3.14` (3.14.8, `discord.py` 2.7.1 installed into a throwaway target): **3,375 passed / 5 failed / 29 skipped** in 62s, and the five failures are the same pre-existing set named in the bullet below — another thread's uncommitted automod/dashboard/design work — reproduced on the same tree without these files. `tests/test_dbstatus.py` needed its stand-in bots to grow the `guilds` and `wait_until_ready` a real bot has: the cog now starts a task in `__init__`, and a stand-in without them made that task fail in the background — an error a test did not cause is an error a test run has to explain.
  - **And it answers the deploy, which is how this pass was read back.** The Space carries the pass as commit `7f8104421dbb58adf75b1e4338ac4ab861298856` and the reading as `549446ef71f5e61cf112f108190f858c8e4dd228`: runtime **`RUNNING`** on the second, each commit's files **byte-identical to HEAD by sha256** (6, then 3), `/health` answering `{"status": "healthy", "service": "dacat-gateway"}`, and a boot log whose only traceback is an asyncio loop being finalised at process start (`ValueError: Invalid file descriptor: -1` inside `base_events.__del__`) — **above** the bot's own first line, not from this pass. The three servers answered in the boot log, and the beta guild's line is *“`[CONTROL] locks older than the ledger in guild 1542278896148422676: 0 candidate(s), adopted 0.`”* with the same `0 candidate(s), adopted 0` for `1527842997301940354` and `1550227368889684058`. **Zero candidates in the beta is the correct answer, and it is verifiable from Discord rather than taken on trust.** `#💫・valorant-premier` (`1555562743510073394`) in *Hmm* (`1542278896148422676`) — the channel the screenshot is about — holds an `@everyone` overwrite of `allow 0, deny 34359740480`, which is **exactly** what `_set_deny` writes; that value cannot be on the channel without the ledger row, because the row is written *before* the permission and a failed write aborts the change. Discord's audit log for that channel agrees: the newest `@everyone` change is the bot's, at **17:45 UTC today**, after the previous pass was live. So the `/unlock` + `/lockdown` dance this request exists to remove **had already been done** when the request was written, and the honest reading is *nothing needed adopting*, not a claimed live adoption. Every boot from here re-asks the question; the first lock that predates the ledger in any server — a channel in another guild, a `/lockdown-server` channel nobody re-locked — is claimed at boot with its row, a card in the server's log, and the sweep behind it.
- 🔨 **A locked channel is locked for administrators too, because a permission is not a wall — and walking into it is warned and then timed out, whoever you are**: *"Make it Force Deleting the all the msg Buz Im able to send the msg even delete admin msg"* — sent with the screenshot of it happening: a channel that had just answered `/lockdown` with the *"Channel Locked Down — Members can no longer send messages"* card, and the administrator who ran it typing "Hi" underneath it — followed by *"make A Strict warning like not soft to anyone sending msg delete the msg and dm that person with warning and put in timeout if they spam even admin or god"*. **The copy was the defect, not the implementation.** Discord's `Administrator` permission and a guild owner bypass channel overwrites *by design*, so `set_permissions(send_messages=False)` writes a door that reads as locked and is not one — no permission bit this bot can set changes that. What answers it is a listener that removes what they send, tells them why in their own DMs, and times out a burst past the wall: that is what a locked channel is now.
  - **The registry is the undo's own ledger rather than a second one.** `utils/server_control` already wrote every channel a lockdown touched into `lockdown_state` with the overwrite from *before* the change, because a bulk permission edit needs the server's own undo. That same row now answers *is this channel locked* for the enforcement, so the two can never disagree about which channels are held, and the row leaves exactly when the undo runs. `locked_channel_ids` reads it behind a **ten-second per-guild cache** — the question is asked on *every message* and a query per message is not a price a listener may pay — invalidated by every lock and unlock path, so a `/unlock` stops the removals in the same breath. A read that fails answers **nothing is locked**: a database that could not be read is not a reason to start deleting what people wrote.
  - **Everything a person wrote is removed; the bot's own cards are not.** A member's, a moderator's, the one who placed the lock, the owner's — `is_removable` says yes to all of them, because none of them is something a permission can deny. The bot's messages stay, because the card that announces the lockdown is one of them and a lockdown whose notice deleted itself would look like the failure it exists to prevent; Discord's own notices (a pin, a join) are left alone too, since they are not somebody talking and are most of what Discord refuses to delete anyway. A **thread** is held by the channel it hangs under (`parent_id`), because the lockdown denies *creating* a public thread and a thread that already existed is still a place to type. Each removal goes to the **host log** with the channel and the author id, and a `Forbidden` there names **Manage Messages** rather than the exception — not to the moderation log, because a wall that copies a flood into the log is not a wall, and the card is what tells the room why.
  - **Every removal is warned by DM, and the warning is strict about what happens next.** The sender gets one card in their DMs: what was removed, that the channel is locked so *nothing posted there is kept — administrators and the server's owner included*, and — when they are below the burst threshold — the sentence that says what a burst earns. A DM rather than a line in the room, because a bot sentence under a deleted message is a conversation and a locked channel is not having one. **The burst is the timeout**: `SPAM_LIMIT` messages (`5`) inside `SPAM_WINDOW_SECONDS` (`10`) is a flood, and the ladder is **ten minutes, an hour, a day** — the second burst within `SPAM_ESCALATION_SECONDS` (`1 hour`) of the first climbs a rung, and an hour of quiet puts them back at the bottom. The rules are pure with an injectable clock (`note_locked_message`, `spam_timeout`), the map is in memory and pruned past `SPAM_MAX_KEYS`, and every lock and unlock **forgets the ladder** — the counters belong to one incident, not to a person's permanent record.
  - **The timeout has no exception for rank, and this is deliberate rather than accidental.** It does *not* go through `moderation_actions.apply`, whose hierarchy guard exists so a moderator cannot act on an administrator: the user's ask was "even admin or god", the actor is not a moderator but the lock's own rule, and the rank of the person still typing is exactly what it is there to ignore. Everything *durable* about it still goes through the doors — the case and the log card are `moderation_actions.record(...)`, a refusal is `failure_note_card` in the same log — so a rank-free timeout is a recorded one. **What Discord itself refuses is said rather than swallowed**: the **Moderate Members** permission, a role above the bot's, and the server's owner (whose timeout no bot may perform) each come back as a sentence on the member's card and a line naming the fix in the log. Lockout is also told to the room: the `/lockdown` card and the `/lockdown-server` card now read *"...for everyone, administrators included — and the sender is warned by DM. A burst of messages earns a timeout: ten minutes, then an hour, then a day."*, and `_lock_warnings` names **Manage Messages** and **Moderate Members** on the card when the bot is missing either one.
  - **The per-channel verbs are the same door now, which closed the guardrail gap this tree had been carrying.** `/lockdown` and `/unlock` used to write a permission directly and record nothing, which is why unlocking a channel that way could only *allow* `@everyone` to talk — a permission the server may never have granted — and why `tests/test_guardrails.py` listed `/lockdown`'s permission writes as a pre-existing gap it was not allowed to bless. Both verbs go through `utils/server_control` now, one row at a time, and `/lockdown-server`/`/unlock-server` use the server-wide plan and ledger instead of looping `text_channels` themselves. A channel already in the ledger is left exactly as it is by a second lock (its row holds the overwrite from before the *first* one), and a channel locked before there was a ledger is still unlocked — by *removing* the deny rather than inverting it. The cog's pinned direct-write inventory is now the `/slowmode` value plus the two writes this pass adds — `message.delete()` and `member.timeout()` — each with its reason written beside it, and the permission edits that used to sit in the cog are in the module where they are recorded.
  - **A card that promises removal while the permission is missing is the same defect one layer down.** Locking needs **Manage Channels** and removing needs **Manage Messages**, and a bot can hold one without the other, so `/lockdown`'s card says so in words when it cannot (`_removal_warning`). The lockdown card itself now reads *"Every message sent here is deleted until it is unlocked — for everyone, administrators included"*, and `/unlock` says the permissions are the ones that were here before.
  - **Thirty-eight new laws in `tests/test_server_control.py`** — the per-channel ledger (one row, a second lock not rewriting the first, one channel released without releasing the others, a channel locked before there was a ledger, an unreadable ledger answering *nothing is locked*, a thread answering with its parent) and the enforcement (an administrator's message removed like any other, the bot's own card left where the channel can read it, Discord's notices left alone, `/unlock` stopping it in the same moment, moderation off policing nothing, a channel that is not locked, a direct message, a burst of messages being one query, the cache forgetting on both paths), plus the ladder (below the limit is not a burst, the message that reaches it *is*, the escalation holds at its last rung, an hour of quiet resets it, messages outside the window never count, one person's burst is not another's and not another channel's, and forgetting one server leaves the others alone) and the strict half of the enforcement (every removal warns its sender by DM, the fifth message is a timeout with `datetime.timedelta(seconds=600)` handed to Discord, the timeout is recorded as a *timeout* case, a refused timeout is told to the member and written to the log, the **owner** is warned while Discord is never asked to time them out, a closed inbox does not stop the removal, and a warning goes to the member rather than into the locked room) — and **one law that drives the whole path**: the real `/lockdown` callback, the real ledger, the real listener, then a message from the administrator who ran it, gone and warned; then the real `/unlock` callback and the next one left alone. **Two mutations were watched to fail** rather than asserted in prose: `is_locked` neutered to `False` killed three laws, and `is_removable` made to answer yes to everything killed the bot-card and pin-notice laws.
  - **What was actually run here, and what is owed.** This machine turned out to have a working **Python 3.14.8** (`/opt/homebrew/bin/python3.14`) behind the 3.9.6 that every earlier pass in this file had to work around — the checkout's `.venv` points at a 3.14 framework that is gone, but the interpreter is installed — so the **whole suite ran natively for the first time in this conversation**: `3,360 passed / 5 failed / 29 skipped` in 67s, with the five reproduced on the same tree **without these four files** (they are the uncommitted automod/dashboard/design work another thread has in this checkout) and the failure *set* diffed rather than the count compared. `discord.py` installed at **2.7.1**; CI (3.11, its own pins) stays the authority for the merge. **And it is live on the machine that matters**: `git push origin main` was attempted first and **GitHub is gone for this account** — the remote answers *"Repository not found"* — so the commit went to the Space through the Hub's own commit API (the door `scripts/mirror_space.py` already uses, which is the only one open to a tree with binaries in its history), as commit `2fad3133d1b89f3242d02a32974fa97f4c725777` on `1tsRajOwO/meow`. It was read back rather than assumed: the Space's runtime is **`RUNNING` on that sha**, `/health` answers `{"status": "healthy", "service": "dacat-gateway"}`, the boot log's last lines are the 23 `Loaded cogs.*` — `cogs.server_control` among them — and **all eight files this commit carries are byte-identical to HEAD on the Space** (sha256, per path, downloaded back). **Still owed, and now two things**: the live proof in the beta server — a locked channel, a person typing, `[CONTROL] removed a message…` in `/api/live-logs`, and the warning in their DMs — and the deploy tool itself: `scripts/mirror_space.py` refuses a `HEAD` that is not on `origin/main`, which is a precondition this account can no longer satisfy, so the next pass needs a way to mirror with the dead remote already gone.
- 🕯️ **Alone Time, second pass — the cards stop shouting, the reason stops existing, and a notice stops landing in the room it is about**: *"Redesign the current card to be highly minimalistic and modern... Remove reliance on default text emojis... Implement an absolute privacy lock on the specific text inputs... Stop sending automated notices directly into the Voice Channel text chats... Implement a strict 5-minute cooldown timer for join requests."* Five of the six asks are this pass. The sixth — **cache the name before the bot changes it and give it back** — shipped in the previous one as the ledger plus `restore_name`, and is named here rather than re-claimed; what this pass adds to it is the reading that made the promise visible on the cards.
  - **No card this feature draws carries a text emoji any more, and the one symbol that stays is on the channel's own name.** Every card is built from `utils/style.py`'s type scale and nothing else — one heading, small labels, body text, one accent — and the `🔒` Discord's sidebar needs to read a room as locked stays on the *channel*, while `alonetime.plain_name` keeps it out of the cards, because a card drawn by this feature already says the room is locked. A new law scans the holder's card, the public notice, the turned-away member's card and `/alone status` for emoji ranges (`\U0001F300-\U0001FAFF`, `\u2600-\u27BF`, `\u2B00-\u2BFF`, `\uFE0F`), so the next one somebody types into a sentence fails instead of shipping.
  - **The reason is not private because a card hides it — it is private because it is never written down.** `db.save_alone_time` **no longer accepts `note` at all**, which is the shape that makes the promise hold rather than a convention somebody could forget, and the reason now lives in `alonetime.REASONS` in the running process alone: read back onto the holder's own ephemeral card and onto `/alone status` when the holder runs it, and nowhere else — not the public notice, not `/alone list`, not the moderation case (which says *"gave a reason this feature never writes down"* rather than quoting it), not the dashboard's panel (which publishes ids and counts, and never published names). The legacy `alone_time.note` column is **blanked on every boot**, so a lock taken before this change stops holding somebody's words the next time the bot starts, and the operator document says to blank it by hand for a request answered before that boot.
  - **A request to join is delivered, not published.** `/alone ask` — a new verb on the existing root, so the tree stays inside Discord's hundred — or the *Ask to join* button on the notice a turned-away member finds. The holder is **DMed** a card with *Allow in* / *Keep out*; only their own press changes the whitelist, and the press is checked against the request's own `holder_id`, because a forwarded screenshot is not a permission. **Allowed** adds the member to the guest list and raises the room's cap; **Keep out** changes nothing and the asker is **not told**, since a refusal in public would be this feature publishing somebody else's answer. A DM Discord refuses falls back to the notice channel **and says why it is there** (*"Your DMs are closed to me"*), because a request recorded as delivered when nobody can read it is the failure this whole path exists to avoid. Requests are memory, not rows: thirty minutes at most, dropped when the room is released, and gone on restart.
  - **One request per member per five minutes, and the clock starts only when a request is actually delivered.** `alonetime.REQUEST_COOLDOWN_SECONDS = 300` over a bounded in-memory map, read through `request_left`/`note_request` — pure arithmetic with an injectable clock, like every other rule in that module — and a member inside their wait is told `4m 12s`, in seconds rather than the rounded minutes `/alone status` uses for how long a room has been held, because telling somebody *4m* with 4m 59s left is how a wait feels longer than it is. A holder whose DMs refused the bot has not been asked, so that asker is not charged the wait.
  - **Nothing automated goes into the locked room any more.** One resolver, `_notice_channel`, is where every notice is addressed: the server's own setting first (a new, **eleventh declared setting**, `notice_channel`, for a server that wants to name the channel itself), then the house channel `utils/bot_channels` binds for automated output, then `#general` and its spellings, and only then the room — the last resort rather than the first, because a server with none of the above still needs the notice where the people it concerns can read it. The five refusals in front of a request are each a sentence too: the room may be open again, it may be quiet hours (which nobody can lift for one person), the asker may be the holder, they may already be on the list, they may be inside their wait, or their earlier request may still be unanswered.
  - **`tests/test_alonetime.py` grows by 31 law methods in four groups** — **the private reason** (the saved row holds no note, the boot purge blanks a legacy one, `save_alone_time` refuses a `note` keyword outright, the status reading has no *Why* row, the reason is shown to the holder and to nobody else), **the notice routing** (the setting, the bound house channel, `#general`, the room as the last resort, and that a notice never lands in the locked room), **the join request** (delivery to the DM, the fallback that names itself, one request per five minutes, the four refusals, allow raising the cap, keep-out telling nobody, only the holder answering, expiry, and a release dropping every request for the room) and **the typographic cards** — **154 in total**, with the file's stand-ins grown to match the real shapes (`Member.send`/`dms`, `Channel.sent_ids`, `Guild.get_member` over a member list, `Interaction.message`).
  - **What was actually run here, and what is owed to CI.** This machine still has only `/usr/bin/python3` **3.9.6** (the checkout's `.venv` points at a Python 3.14 framework that is not installed, `aiosqlite` is absent, and the cog needs 3.10+), so the suite cannot run. It was run the only way it can be: a harness importing the shipped `utils/alonetime.py`, `utils/db.py`, `utils/features.py`, `utils/bot_channels.py`, `utils/modlog.py` and `utils/style.py` under a `sqlite3`-backed `aiosqlite` shim and stubs for what needs a gateway — **501 checks, green**. It covers every pure rule at fixed clocks (the cooldown arithmetic, the reason store's trimming and per-server scoping, the request map and expiry, `plain_name`, the status reading with no *Why* row), the database half against the shipped DDL (a fresh row stores no note, a legacy note is blanked by the boot purge, the ledger keeps the previous name, guests write, the turn-away counter moves, a release forgets the row), and two static laws over the half that cannot be imported: **every `alonetime.*`, `db.*`, `features.*`, `style.*`, `bot_channels.*` and `modlog.*` attribute the cog and the test file read exists**, and **all 303 module-qualified call sites across those two files bind against the real signatures** — with the binder itself watched to fail, since a check that can only pass is not a check. **Still owed, and CI is the authority for it**: the cog half of `tests/test_alonetime.py` (it needs `discord.ui`, `app_commands` and rendered components, and a stub would test the stub), the rest of the suite (`test_features`, `test_permissions`, `test_command_scope`, `test_legal`, `test_style`, `test_naming`), and the live Discord check in the beta server. Two records move with the code: `PRIVACY.md`'s Alone Time bullet now states the reason and the requests in the words the implementation keeps, `docs/legal/data-requests.md` gains the one-line `UPDATE` that answers a deletion request for a reason typed before that boot, and the three legal documents move to **Version 7** together because their shared version/date rule is what makes any one of them worth trusting. The policy's Discord card was the thing to watch while growing it: rendered through `cogs/legal.py`'s real pagination it is **cut either way**, both before and after this paragraph, the *rest of it* field names the published page, and the paragraph costs one section of on-card body — which is the renderer's designed answer to a document growing, not a silent loss. That render, and the two laws pinned over it, are part of the harness run above.
  - **And CI's first run on this commit was red for a reason the harness was structurally blind to, and the deployed bot wore the symptom for four hours without anything calling it broken.** `@alone_ask.autocomplete("room")` sat on a parameter typed `discord.VoiceChannel`, and Discord only allows autocomplete on string, integer and number options — a check `app_commands` makes *at class-body time*, so `cogs/alonetime.py` raised `TypeError` at import and the whole cog never loaded: 70 errors and 12 failures across the suite (every `test_every_cog_loads`, every tree-membership law, every alonetime test), and on the Space a bot whose tree held **99 roots instead of 100**. The deployment hid it well: `/api/status` answered `connected: true`, the registry validated, the dashboard's scope panel reported *"Discord holds 8 commands, matching what this server's switches say"* — because the panel compares Discord against the local tree, and the local tree was missing the command too. What found it was reading the panel's own `modules.alonetime.commands: []` next to its `enabled: true`. The fix keeps the picker (the option is `str` now, the id resolved back to a channel in the command, with the constraint written where the next person will read it), and the same local run — `discord.py` in a throwaway target directory and a loader that injects the future import this machine's 3.9 needs — then played the whole class body and **154 alonetime laws at 0 failures** (the three that cannot run here are `cogs/help.py`'s `str | None` evaluated by `discord.py`, a line CI has always passed on 3.11, plus the tests that mock-patch the environment around an *async* method, which 3.9's `mock.patch.dict` silently skips and 3.11's does not). That run found two more the import failure had been sitting on: the turn-out refusal card said *could not be moved* where the first pass's law asks for **could not move**, and the typographic test wanted the turned-away card in the landing room's chat — which is the very voice-channel chat this pass promises notices will never enter, so it is now a law that the chat stays **empty**. Owed, and CI's again: the full suite, and the Space's own log for `Loaded cogs.alonetime` after the re-deploy.
- 🧹 **The suite is green on main for the first time since Actions came back, and the run that made it green was the third defect the red had been hiding: a skip budget no failing job had ever reached.** The six failures the Alone Time beta's run reported were named in this file as not the beta's; this pass fixes all six, and *then* the job failed again on the gate underneath them — `41 test(s) skipped (budget 10)` — which had never executed because the failures exited first.
  - **Five of the six asked the test job for things it does not have, and the sixth could only pass on the day it was written.** `tests/test_ship.py` read a built `_site` it never built: the argv law asserted that the uploaded directory exists and two probe laws compared the served bytes against that build's, so on a clean checkout — and in the job — they failed for a reason that had nothing to do with what they claim. What they are about is the path and the comparison, so the tree they need is written into a temporary root and `ship.ROOT` is pointed at it; the builders themselves are pinned by the law that reads `site.yml`, and no test has any business running one. `scripts/mirror_space.py` answered a tokenless run **before** it looked at the tree, so the two refusals — a dirty tree, an unpushed HEAD — could not be exercised without a secret; both are local git facts, asked before anything is sent, and a machine without a token still learns what is wrong with its tree. And `meetings.upcoming()` read the wall clock, so the law about scoping a listing to one server could only pass on the day its fixed `NOW` named — the reader takes the moment it is asked at now, the way `create()`, `due_times()`, `claim_notice()`, `mark_notice()` and `set_rsvp()` beside it already do. All three were run here through a harness that executes the shipped test code (this machine's only interpreter is 3.9, and the suite needs 3.11): the two mirror refusals, the three ship laws and the meetings law are green, the `--check` law with a stand-in for the `huggingface_hub` this interpreter cannot parse.
  - **The gate underneath them was 41 skips against a budget of ten, and the arithmetic was exact**: 26 from `tests/test_composer.py::DashboardTemplateCase`, 13 from `tests/test_scheduler.py::ScheduleTemplateCase`, one from the module `tests/test_shell.py` retires by fixture, and one genuine platform skip (starlette is installed where the Space runs, not in the test job). Both classes were retired by the dashboard rebuild, each with a reason that said the same thing — the scripted composer and the scripted scheduler are **forms** now, and the intents they protected are pinned in `tests/test_dashboard_pages.py`, which does hold them (36 tests, `TheComposerIsAForm` and the schedule-write laws among them). That is the shape the workflow's own comment names: *retired tests were replaced rather than parked*. These were parked, and parking them cost the suite its accounting — two classes that ran nothing were 39 of the 41 skips, so no change that ran anything could have brought the count under the budget. They are deleted, and the pointer in `tests/test_dashboard_pages.py` records the deletion instead of describing classes that no longer exist. The retired module stays: its reason travels in its own docstring, and one skip is what the budget's headroom is for.
  - **The hang in the first run of this pass is written down because it was not this pass', and a red check hides exactly this kind of thing.** The first attempt at `09aaf14` reported no failures at all: it produced a **120-second watchdog dump** inside the voice-reward loop (the last line before the dump is the second member's `Scaled payout`, and the three threads are an idle aiosqlite worker, an idle executor worker and the loop waiting in `select()` with no timers), a flake on a path this pass never touches; the same commit's rerun ran the whole suite in **111 seconds** and passed. What the passing run revealed was the skip gate, because the failure had always fired first.
  - **Read back**: `6aeb843` — `Ran 3296 tests in 109.9s`, `OK (skipped=2)`, `3294 test(s) ran, 2 skipped, 3296 collected in total`, every step of the job `success`. The floor was not moved (2,200) and the count that runs did not change: the same **3,294** tests that ran before ran after, with the two parked classes' 39 laws deleted rather than the arithmetic adjusted to fit them.
  - **One thing could not be pushed from this machine, and is written here instead**: the workflow's own comment explains the skip budget and its history, and the update saying what the 41 were is **local to this checkout** — `.github/workflows/` requires a token with `workflow` scope and this account's token has none. The facts it would have carried are above, which is where a reader looking for them looks first anyway.
- 🌙 **Alone Time — one voice channel locked to the member holding it, and the promise "not even an administrator" made honestly rather than optically**: *"Add A New Feature Alone Time That Will Lock The VC From Accessing Not Even Admin Or Owner Can Access The Vc And It Will be Locked And More Features."* This pass turns it into the thing it is being run as — **a beta in one server**, with the per-guild switch as the only lever and a session the holder owns — and fixes the defect that first made it look broken. The request as written cannot be done with a permission, and saying so is the first design decision here: **Discord's `Administrator` permission and the guild owner bypass channel permission overwrites by design**, so a `Connect: false` overwrite is a locked-looking door an administrator walks through. The lock is therefore **three layers, strongest first, and the card says which are in force**. **The capacity is what holds**: `user_limit` is enforced at Discord's voice gateway for everybody and no permission bit raises it, so a room capped to the people allowed inside turns the next arrival away with Discord's own *channel is full* — administrator, owner or member. **The deny is what reads as locked**, so everybody without Administrator is refused before they arrive. **The sweep is the backstop** — an `on_voice_state_update` listener that puts back anybody who still ends up inside — and it is where the honesty lives: it needs **Move Members**, and where Discord refuses the move the refusal is *recorded, reported to the holder, and named on the status card* rather than swallowed. **A guest list raises the cap** to fit the guests, which leaves the sweep doing the deciding; that trade-off is stated on the card, because the cap and the sweep are not the same promise.
  - **One registry row, ten declared settings.** `utils/features.py` gains `Feature(id="alonetime", …)` with **ten declared settings** — who may lock a room (everyone / a named role / administrators), the room's capacity and quiet-hours length, whether a lock survives the holder leaving, whether a turned-away member is told, whether the log gets a case, where a turned-away member goes (back where they came from, disconnected, or the AFK channel), a cooldown between locks, and a `max_hours` ceiling. It ships **off** now, like the market, starboard, Voice Company and the legacy pair — the beta's whole shape, and it needed no new machinery: `enabled_by_default=False` keeps `/alone` out of every other server's picker entirely (`utils.command_scope` removes a disabled command rather than refusing it), and `modules.set_enabled`/the dashboard's own toggle is the one flag that turns it on for the server running the beta. No guild id appears anywhere in the code, and turning the default back on later is deleting two lines.
  - **One command slot, seven verbs, flat on purpose**: `/alone start|stop|status|guests|list|release|server`. Flat rather than nested because `utils/permissions.walk` is one level deep and a nested subgroup would silently escape the audience audit; one root because Discord allows 100 top-level commands and this bot is at **100** with it. Four of the seven are **every member's** and carry no `has_permissions` at all — the holder check that stops a stranger releasing somebody else's room is a body check inside the callback, the same shape the market's role gate and `/customquote delete` use — while `list`/`release` are `/lockdown`'s audience and `server` is `/lockdown-server`'s.
  - **`utils/alonetime.py` is the rules and the engine and `cogs/alonetime.py` is the gateway**, the split `utils/company.py` and `utils/server_control.py` already use, so every rule worth arguing about (`verdict`, `capacity_for`, `release_reason`, the ledger's `encode_overwrite`/`decode_overwrite`/`restore_name`) is testable without Discord and the engine is drivable with a stand-in channel. The **ledger is written before the first permission change** — the room's previous `@everyone` overwrite as `allow:deny` (or *there was none*), its previous `user_limit` and its previous name — because a lock that applied and failed to record itself has no way back; that is `utils/server_control.lockdown`'s rule, one room at a time. If the writes then fail, the row is dropped again, so nothing is left claiming a room is locked when it is not.
  - **The rename is not the lock, and a rate limit must not cost one.** Discord limits a channel rename to about **two per ten minutes**, and the first version put the rename inside the same `try` as the permission writes — so a 429 on the name un-wrote the ledger row and answered *"nothing happened"* for a room that **was** locked, leaving nobody able to release it. Both the lock and the release now do the name **last, on its own, best-effort**: a refused rename keeps the lock and the row and is reported as `Report.name_warning` ("the room is locked, but Discord would not take its new name just now"), and a refused *restore* still forgets the row and hands the room back. The room's name is decoration; the cap and the overwrite are the lock, and the two are no longer allowed to fail each other.
  - **A custom name needs to know what it set.** `/alone start name:"Study"` renames the room to `🔒 Study`, and the holder can rename it any time from their card — so the ledger gained `locked_as` (via the same idempotent `db.ensure_columns` the loans and support tables use), which is *what the lock actually set* rather than something computed from the old name. `restore_name` restores only while the room still carries that, so a room somebody renamed afterwards is left alone and a custom name is not left wearing the lock's mark for ever.
  - **A lock owes the room as it already is.** The listener answers *arrivals*, and an arrival is not the only way somebody is inside: a lock that starts under two people would otherwise answer "this is yours" with a second person still sitting there. `alonetime.strays_in` is the pure rule (holder, guests, guest roles; not this bot, not another bot, not somebody Discord's own permissions let through) and one pass right after a successful lock moves the rest out with the same notice and case the sweep writes. Quiet hours skips that pass on purpose: a server-wide window is not one person's room.
  - **A reconcile loop, because nothing unlocks by itself.** Discord keeps the overwrite and the cap across a restart, so the loop is what ends a lock whose holder left, whose room is gone, whose ceiling passed, whose quiet-hours window is over — and **every lock at once when a server switches the system off**, since a system that is off must not be holding somebody's room. It never releases on an *unknown* reading: a voice state that could not be read is not evidence that the holder left.
  - **The extras the ask's *"and more features"* bought**: a whitelist whose cap moves with it (`/alone guests`, and a **picker and a Rename button on the holder's own card** — the persistent `AloneView`, registered once with fixed `custom_id`s so a card from before the last redeploy still answers, resolving the room from whoever pressed it; the holder and nobody else, so a moderator's lever is `/alone release` and not somebody else's guest list), quiet hours across every voice channel (`/alone server`, the voice twin of `/lockdown-server`, ending on its own clock), a card in the room a turned-away member was sent **to** (not the room they can no longer read), a case in the server's moderation log for every lock, release and turn-away, and **a dashboard panel on the Server page** listing every locked room with a Release button that goes through the same `release_channel` the command runs — via `_on_bot_loop`, because a release performed off the gateway's cache would empty the ledger and leave Discord holding the lock. Voice Company learned one line in return: it never joins a room Alone Time has locked, and it leaves one that gets locked while the bot is sitting in it.
  - **What it deliberately does not have, so nobody goes looking**: a per-room expiry timer. A lock ends because its holder released it, because the holder left, because the server set a ceiling, because an administrator released it, or because the system was switched off — never because a clock nobody asked for ran out.
  - **The limits are stated on the card, the help category and the directory listing rather than left to be discovered**: the Administrator bypass and which layer answers it; the two permissions the bot needs and the sentence when one is missing; the refusals Discord can still make (the owner's own move is the one most likely to be refused); the fact that a lock survives the bot being offline while the sweep does not; and **the one thing no bot can do** — whether a member's voice *activity* appears on their Discord profile is **their own account setting** (Activity Sharing), not a channel permission, and there is no app API for it. `/alone status`, the room's card and the help category say exactly that instead of implying a stealth this feature cannot deliver, and nothing hides the channel from its own members to fake it.
