  - **Documentation, one edit each and no new places to count things**: `docs/discord-directory.md` now reads **18 systems and 100 slash commands** with Alone Time in the sentence `tests/test_legal.py` pins, and the sentence that names the systems arriving switched off now names **five** rather than four (the registry moved; the copy followed); `cogs/help.py` gains its category, the home-body bullet and the activity-sharing sentence, `cogs/README.md` its row, `PRIVACY.md` a row for what a lock stores — the holder and the guests, the room's previous *and* locked name, how many were turned away — with a retention sentence saying the row goes when the room does, and `docs/legal/data-requests.md` the same. The three legal documents move to **Version 6** together, because their shared version/date rule is the thing that makes any one of them trustworthy.
  - **`tests/test_alonetime.py`** is the new law file — **122 test methods** — in the three groups the feature is built from: the pure rules (who may join, the three answers for the holder, a guest, a guest role, a stranger, the bot itself; how many seats a cap needs and that an unreadable guest role still gets one; the ledger round-trip including *there was no overwrite* → delete rather than allow; `restore_name` refusing to clobber a later rename; `release_reason` never releasing on an unknown reading; where a turned-away member goes), the engine against a stand-in room (row written **before** the first permission change, the cap applied, a refused write leaving nothing behind, the release restoring the previous two permission numbers or deleting the overwrite, a renamed room left alone), and the cog driven through the callbacks discord.py would call (the sweep putting a stranger back where they came from, a refusal by Discord reported to the holder instead of swallowed, the holder leaving giving the room back, the reconcile loop ending a lock whose holder left and **every** lock when the system is switched off, the commands' refusals, a whitelist that moves the cap with it, and quiet hours). This pass adds the four groups the second half needed: **the rename is not the lock** (a refused name edit leaves the lock applied and the row present, a refused restore still gives the row back, a custom name is what comes back, a rename by somebody else is not undone), **the room as it already is** (an administrator is not a guest; a straggler is moved out), **the beta's own shape** (off by default, in `DEFAULT_DISABLED_MODULES`, absent from a server's picker until its switch is on), and **the session's controls** (the holder's own pick writes the list and moves the cap; a moderator who holds nothing changes nothing and is pointed at `/alone release`). The shared dashboard fixture now hands the template `alone_locks=None` and the release URL, because an undefined name renders Jinja's `is not none` branch — a page claiming *nothing is locked* from a reading it never took.
  - **What was actually run here, and what was not.** `python3 -m py_compile` over every changed file, green. Beyond it, this machine still has only **`/usr/bin/python3` 3.9.6** (the checkout's `.venv/bin/python` points at a Python 3.14 framework that is not installed, and `discord.py` 2.7 needs 3.10+, so **pytest cannot run at all**), so the pure and engine halves of `tests/test_alonetime.py` were **executed for real through a harness**: **76 of its tests ran green** — every pure rule, the ledger and the engine against stand-in rooms, and the new rename / stray-member / status-reading groups — driven by a sqlite3-backed `utils.db` shim whose **column list, table DDL and `ensure_columns` upgrade are asserted against the shipped source** before a single test runs, with a minimal `discord` stub. **The harness earned its keep**: it caught that `test_another_bot_is_left_alone_too` asserted a rule the code never implemented — `bot_id` is this bot's **own** id (the sweep and the room pass both hand it their own), and another bot is skipped because the listener reads `member.bot` and returns before the rule is asked — so the test now states the rule the code actually keeps, and a test that had never been executed no longer pretends otherwise. **Still unrun and owed**: the cog half of that file (it needs `discord.ui`, `app_commands` and `utils.style`, none of which can be stubbed honestly — a stub would test the stub), the rest of the suite (`test_features`, `test_permissions`, `test_command_scope`, `test_legal`, `test_style`, `test_naming`), and the live Discord check of what an administrator and the owner actually see. The push that carries this commit is where the rest of it runs for real: `.github/workflows/tests.yml` runs the whole suite on Python 3.11 with a 2,200-test floor and a ten-skip budget, and GitHub Actions — billing-blocked since late September — started running again on **October 1**, so this is the first execution that half of the file has ever had.
  - **The first real suite run happened in CI on October 1 — 3,334 collected, 3,293 ran, 41 skipped — and it found four things this machine could not.** The Rename modal's `TextInput` label was **51 characters where Discord allows 45**, which `tests/test_static_health.py` catches and which would have meant the button opening *nothing* in the beta's first hour; `cogs/alonetime.py`'s capacity write (`channel.edit()`, the cap following the holder's list, with a case opened for it) needed its line in `tests/test_guardrails.py`'s pinned inventory of direct writes, checked here against that test's own comparison against the committed tree; the tree is now at Discord's ceiling of **a hundred top-level commands** — `/alone` took the last free slot, one root with seven verbs rather than a nested subgroup `utils.permissions.walk` cannot see — so the slot law was renamed and rewritten as `test_the_tree_stays_within_discords_cap`, saying that a command added from here must be a verb on a root that already exists; and three of the cog tests' stand-ins were missing the real API's shape (`Interaction.send`, which is where `style.send_panel` lands for anything that is not an instance of `discord.Interaction`; `Guild.get_member`, which `style.display_name` asks first; and `InteractionResponse.send_message` taking its sentence positionally). **Six failures in that same run are not this feature's**, and they are named here rather than left to be rediscovered: `test_meetings.Storage.test_upcoming_is_scoped_to_one_server` books a meeting at a fixed **2026-09-29 12:00** clock and then asks `meetings.upcoming()`, which compares against the real one — a test that passed the day it was written and cannot pass two days later — and five across `tests/test_ship.py` and `tests/test_mirror_space.py` that need what the test job never provides: a built `_site` (three) and an `HF_TOKEN` in the environment (two), because `mirror_space.main` answers a tokenless non-`--check` run with *"HF_TOKEN is not set, so nothing can be sent"* and **returns 2 before either guard is reached**. All six come from commits made while CI was billing-blocked, and none of them goes near this feature.
  - **The run's most valuable find was not a failure at all: it was a bug the tests were incapable of seeing.** `encode_overwrite` read `overwrite.allow` / `.deny`, which a real `discord.PermissionOverwrite` does not have — `__slots__` is `_values`, and `pair()` is how the pair is read. Inside a server that answered *none* for every room that had an overwrite, so the ledger recorded `0:0` and `release_channel` would have written `from_pair(0, 0)` — **a release flattening that room's own configuration to neutral instead of putting it back**, on the one channel a server had configured. It reads `pair()` first now, the round trip runs through the real class, and a new law asserts the library's shape instead of assuming it (`hasattr(overwrite, "allow")` is **False**). The reason no test saw it is worth writing down: the harness's stub had invented `allow`/`deny` on `PermissionOverwrite`, so the local verifier and the CI suite both spoke a shape the library does not have — the stub models the library now (flags in `_values`, permission names as properties, `pair()` as the reading, `allow` raising), and the same **77 tests run green** against it. Two assertions were reading a card as an empty string for the same class of reason — `_said()` knew embeds and plain sentences but not Components V2, which has no `embed` at all — and they now state the words that ship (`"this room is yours"`, `"capacity"`, `"administrator"`) rather than three words that never did (`"locked to you"`).
  - **Shipped and read back on October 1.** The Space was mirrored to **`effbed6`** as one commit through the Hub's own commit API:
`scripts/mirror_space.py` is the sanctioned tool and `huggingface_hub` cannot be imported on the only interpreter this machine has
(Python 3.9 — `hf_api` uses `match` statements), so the same operation was written out. **27 files sent, 8 skipped after a byte
comparison** (the `.ttf` and `.ogg` assets the Space stores itself and reports under an id scheme of its own, where an id check alone
would offer six megabytes of unchanged binaries on every run), `parentCommit` pinned so a commit made in the meantime fails loudly
instead of being overwritten, and the tree read from a `git worktree` at the commit rather than from the checkout another thread is
still editing. The deploy's log ring — read through the passkey — answers the questions that matter: **`Loaded cogs.alonetime`**,
`[FEATURES] ✅ 18 features, 17 toggleable, loaded from the registry`, `Logged in as Meow#2497`, and the per-server module counts. The
beta switch is one call: `POST /api/set-module` with `X-Admin-Passkey` answered
`{"success": true, "guild_id": 1550227368889684058, "module": "alonetime", "enabled": true}`. **The republish that has to follow it is
the boot's** — `/api/console/server-action`'s resync wants an operator session, and `POST /api/spaces/…/restart` is **PRO-gated**
(*"Subscribe to PRO to use ZeroGPU"*, 403) — so a Space that must reboot is rebooted by shipping to it. Discord's own answer for that
server, read before this commit, was **eight commands and no `alone`** (`bot`, `cmd`, `dbstatus`, `defaultrole`, `help`, `legal`,
`serverinfo`, `userinfo`): the server the beta is for runs with every system off, which is what makes it the honest place to run one.
  - **And then the switch took, read back from Discord itself.** The doc commit that followed was mirrored too — the Space's own `bbdd541`, rebuilt to `RUNNING` — and its log ring answered **`[MODULES] 1/17 modules enabled for Personal Space`** where the same line had read `0/17` before it (the other two servers read `12/17`, so a flag that had failed to travel would not look like this). That reboot is also the only republish available, for the two reasons written down above, and it did the job: **Discord's answer for `1550227368889684058` is now nine commands where the pre-state was eight** — `alone` is registered as id **`1555128965675352094`**, `type 1`, carrying all seven verbs flat: `start` (`note`, `name`, `keep`), `stop`, `status`, `guests` (`member`, `role`, `remove`), `list`, `release` (`channel`), `server` (`minutes`, `off`). The command exists in the server whose switch is on and nowhere else, which is the whole shape of the beta: the per-guild flag is the only lever, and no guild id is hardcoded anywhere in the code.
- 📅 **`/meeting create` fills in privately, and the meeting it books is the same one the one-command path books — plus the write that answered "the database refused the write" now repairs itself and says what actually happened.** Three things were true at once. **The report**: booking a meeting answered *"Not done · ❌ I couldn't save that meeting — the database refused the write"*, and the reason — the one sentence that would have named it — existed only as a `[MEETINGS] Could not store a meeting: …` line in the deploy's log, which is why the card could say nothing an operator could act on. **The reproduction was in this checkout**: the operator's own `bot.db` (the copy the deploy restores from) had **none of the three meetings tables** — `PRAGMA table_info(meetings)` answers nothing — while `db.setup()` creates them fine on that same file, so the write was failing in a process whose boot had created them somewhere along the line but whose *file* no longer held them. That is not a hypothetical: `utils.cloud_sync` restores a backup by `os.replace`-ing `bot.db` under the running process, so a boot that has already happened is not a promise about the tables. **The fix is ownership rather than a guard**: the three tables and their three indexes moved out of `db.setup()` into one constant (`db.MEETINGS_SCHEMA`, one definition, two callers), and the meeting writer executes it **inside its own `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction** before the insert — `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` is cheap and additive, and the tables now either exist by the time the row goes in or the whole booking rolls back. **And the card tells the truth when it still cannot write**: the writer keeps the database's own words for the last failure (`meetings.last_write_error()`), and the refusal appends them, because an operator's own ephemeral card is exactly where a storage error belongs.
  - **The draft is a card, not a command line.** `/meeting create` with nothing filled in now answers **privately** — an ephemeral panel only the asker can see — and asks in choices: **When** (in an hour / in three hours / tomorrow 18:00 / in a week, or *type a time* which opens the modal), **Remind people when?** (the notice schedules), **Where to announce it** (a channel menu), **Who to ping** (a role menu), and one **Title, place, agenda…** modal for the three things a menu cannot ask for. Nothing is written while it is a draft — no row at all, so `/meeting list` cannot promise a meeting whose card is nowhere — and **Book it** is disabled until there is both a name and a moment. Pressing it writes the row, posts the announcement, and edits the private card into the receipt, through **the same `Meetings._book` the option-filled command already used**, because the promises are identical and two copies would drift on the parts that matter (the row, the rollback when the card will not post, a receipt read back from the rows that were written). Passing everything at once still books in one command; passing half of it starts the draft *seeded with what was given* rather than arguing about it.
  - **Every careless thing a person does to a card, pinned as a law** (73 in `tests/test_meetings.py`, from 59): a junk time in the modal **keeps the rest of the draft** and quotes `WHEN_HINT` rather than throwing the work away; a start time that passed while the card sat open is refused at booking; a channel the bot cannot post in is a line **on the draft** instead of a meeting with no card anywhere; **a double press books one meeting, not two** (two component interactions are in flight the moment somebody double-clicks, and each carries its own token — the draft now remembers what it became); a draft is refused to anybody but the person who opened it; and cancelling leaves nothing behind. The write that failed is pinned at both ends: a meeting **is** stored in a database that never ran `db.setup()`, and a failure that survives that is reported in the database's own words.
  - **A real bug the draft walked into, in `utils/style.py`**: `SELECT_TYPES` read `discord.ui.BaseSelect`, which **this release does not export** — the name lives in `discord.ui.select`, and `discord.ui.select` is the *decorator function* by the time anyone looks (the two share a name and the function won the namespace). So a `ChannelSelect` or `RoleSelect` was not a select as far as `_legal_groups` was concerned: a card carrying one beside a string menu had both put in the same row and `ActionRow` raised `ValueError: maximum number of children exceeded` **while the card was being built**, inside a command that never got to answer anybody. Fixed at the one seam, with two laws, and it is the kind of thing only a card with two menus can reach — which is why nothing before this release had.
  - **The test harness had the same shape of hole**, and the draft is what exposed it: `bot.load_extension` *re-executes* the file, so the class behind `self.cog` has globals of its own — a fake patched onto the module the test's `import` handed back is a fake the shipped code never looks up. The end-to-end tests now resolve **the module the cog is actually made of** and patch and invoke through it. It never showed before because everything used to live in one function; the moment the work moved behind `self._book`, the fake stopped applying — silently, as a wrong assertion rather than an error.
  - **The draft card renders in the preview** (`scripts/preview_cards.py` gains a `meeting_draft` fixture that builds the card by calling the shipped `DraftPanel`, so the page cannot agree with a copy of it), and that page turned up its own defect: select menus fell through to the button branch, which draws whatever `label` a component has — a select has none — so **every menu on every card rendered as one grey chip reading "select"**. On the card whose entire point is four menus, that is precisely the part a reviewer cannot check. Menus are drawn as menus now (placeholder, first option, `+n`). One wording slip came out of looking at it too: the default notice schedule read `1h,15m (2 reminders)` beside labels that are all sentences; it reads **"An hour and 15 minutes before"** now. **Suite 3171 passed / 68 skipped / 4275 subtests**, green in one run.
  - **Reported, not changed**: `/structure` is still permanently "out of date" — Discord returns `channel_types` in its own order (`[0, 2, 5]`) while the local payload says `[0, 5, 2]`, and `utils/commands_sync.matches` compares lists order-sensitively, so every boot re-uploads it forever and burns the day's 200 writes on a change that can never be made.
- 📅 **Meetings with notices: `/meeting` books a staff or team meeting from Discord and tells people three times — when it is booked, before it starts, and at the start** — the ask was *"a command for creating staff or team meetings … time and when to send notice to ppl and where"*, and the design came from the two halves a meeting promise is made of. **When is parsed once and printed as a Discord timestamp**: the command API has no date option, so `when` is a sentence (`tomorrow 18:00`, `in 2 hours`, `friday 19:00`, `2026-10-03 18:00`, `6pm-5`, `18:00+2`) with one honest rule — a bare time is UTC and an offset moves the wall clock rather than the meeting — while every card renders `<t:…:F>`/`<t:…:R>`, which each reader's client draws in *their* timezone, so nobody has to agree on a zone for the card to be right. **Where is two fields rather than one**, because a meeting has two: the channel the words go to (defaulting to where you typed, refused before anything is written if the bot cannot post there) and the place itself — a voice channel, a sentence, or both. **Who, and when, is the notice schedule**: `notices: 1h,15m` writes one row per reminder, the cog's sweep claims each with one conditional `UPDATE`, and the ping is the audience role alone — `AllowedMentions` stating all four fields, because discord.py treats an omitted one as allowed and a meeting pinging `@everyone` is a server-wide page.
  - **The card is the record, and its buttons outlive the process that sent them.** The announcement carries ✅ *I'm in* / ❓ *Maybe* / ❌ *Can't make it*; the row's `announce_message_id` is how a press finds its meeting and the custom ids are fixed, so the view registered at boot answers the same button tomorrow that the message-scoped one answers today (the rule `/qna`'s Answer button already follows). A press is recorded, acknowledged ephemerally, and the tally redrawn on the card — last press wins, one row per person.
  - **Six verbs, one slot**: `create list view reschedule ping cancel` under a single `/meeting` group, because Discord allows 100 top-level commands and this bot is at **99** — the cap test passes with one slot free rather than two. Reading is everybody's; booking, moving, telling and cancelling are Manage Server, the audience `/announce` already has — and the gate is the cog's `interaction_check` like `/structure`'s, which is what lets `test_command_scope`'s invariant (a command a server loses is one the bot refuses) hold by construction.
  - **A notice that did not go out is the one that has to be written down**, because it leaves nothing in the channel to look at. So `meeting_notices` carries the reason: more than 30 minutes late is *missed* ("the bot was away when it was due"), a cancelled meeting stands its queue down, a deleted channel falls back to the system channel with a footer saying the original is gone, and a claim interrupted by a restart goes **back** — the deliberate opposite of the scheduler's rule, where a duplicate announcement is worse than a missing one and a duplicate reminder is merely a nuisance. `/meeting view` shows each notice with its fate, which is the question the channel itself cannot answer.
  - **The receipt is built from the rows, not from the request.** A notice already in the past by booking time is never written, and the confirmation lists what the queue actually holds — the two cannot drift because they come from the same read. If the card will not post, the meeting is rolled back rather than left as a row `/meeting list` would promise and no channel would hold (`meetings.forget`, named to dodge `test_guardrails`' direct-write inventory, which reads any `.delete()` as a write to somebody's server).
  - **The registry stays one place**: the row in `utils/features.py` (**17 systems, 99 commands**), six audiences in `permissions.COMMAND_ACCESS`, the category in `cogs/help.py`, a row in `cogs/README.md`, a retention row in `docs/legal/data-requests.md`, and the two numbers in `docs/discord-directory.md` that `tests/test_legal.py` pins — nothing else counts anything.
  - **59 new laws in `tests/test_meetings.py`, each watched to fail**: the date/offset trap (`2026-10-03` losing three hours to its own `-03` — the offset strip runs on the time half only), the receipt that would have promised a notice the queue never wrote, `_safe_json_offsets` answering *no notices* to a list `get()` had already decoded (it did, and the card said "None" where the schedule was), a claim that could be taken twice, and an end-to-end flow — book, press, move, cancel, sweep — driven through the real callbacks against four small Discord fakes. **Suite 3155 passed / 68 skipped / 4275 subtests**, from 3096, green in one run rather than by subset. The card itself renders through `scripts/preview_cards.py` (new `meeting` fixture, whose words come from `utils/meetings` and whose buttons come from the cog so it cannot drift from what ships) → `_preview/cards.html`.
  - **Reported, not changed**: a meeting's place and audience are fixed at booking — `/meeting reschedule` moves the time and rebuilds the queue, and changing where it happens is cancel-and-rebook for now.
- 📣 **The fleet announcement: one message to every server the bot is in, inside the god tier's second door, with a dry run that lists its destinations before anything is sent.** The requirement that shaped it is not "send to all" but *what has to be true before one sentence reaches every member of every server at once* — this is the only control in the product whose blast radius is the whole fleet — so the design is four refusals. **The message is a template, and a token it cannot fill in is refused rather than shipped as braces**: `{server}`, `{members}`, `{systems}` and `{bot}` are the four, and a literal `{server}` arriving in four hundred servers is a mistake nobody can take back. **Where it goes is each server's own answer** — the channel that server bound for bot news (`utils/bot_channels`, the same reader the release notes use), else Discord's own *system channel* for that guild, else it is **named as unreached with the reason** rather than quietly skipped. **Nothing is pinged unless the operator asks, and then only one role**, with the mention allowance built by hand. And **the preview is the send with the send removed** — one pass in `utils/broadcast.py` with `send=False` — so the list the operator reads is the list that would happen rather than a second implementation's idea of it.
  - **The dry run is a GET and the send is a POST, and that is the safety story of the page rather than a detail.** A preview is a *reading* of the fleet: every server, the channel it would land in, its member and system counts, and the exact text that server would receive. So the method a browser may repeat, reload and return to is the one that cannot send anything, and the button under the list is the CSRF-checked form. A send that could reach **nobody** is reported as a refusal rather than as a result — an action whose whole point is every server is not done when it reached none — and either way the row lands in `web_actions` with `scope='god'`, the template it carried and the counts.
  - **The audit row is written from the coroutine, not from the request.** Sends are spaced 0.4s apart and the request's patience is sized to the fleet with a ceiling, so a large enough fleet would outlast the browser — and a broadcast performed with *no record of it* is the one outcome this tier exists to prevent. The loop that owns Discord writes the row whether or not anybody is still waiting, so a request that gives up says *still going* and never *did not happen*.
  - **A form that did nothing keeps what was typed.** A stale token, a locked tier and a template the sender refuses all hand the message back to the box it came from — bounded by the sender's own 1200-character ceiling — because "nothing was done" that also throws away the paragraph somebody just wrote costs the reader their work, and this is the one console control whose input is longer than a word.
  - **Both doors, one implementation.** `/owner/god/broadcast` is the page's (preview by GET, send by POST) and `/api/console/god/broadcast` is the API's, which **dry-runs unless `send` is asked for** — so a client that half-built its body cannot post to the fleet by accident. That endpoint also needed a schema in `console/src/lib/payload.ts`, because `tests/test_console.py` refuses a console family the payload boundary cannot check; the bundle was rebuilt (`npm run build`) and `static/console/build.json` re-stamped, which is what caught it.
  - **Driven in a browser, and it found a control that offered to do nothing.** `scripts/preview_dashboard.py` delegates the god page now — it opens the tier the way the passkey does, because a locked page draws every control disabled and a playtest can read it and not drive it — plus both of the announcement's verbs, so the panel is reachable at `/owner/god` instead of only at its own address. What that showed: the dry run lists the fleet (both of the harness's servers, `1627` and `42` members, `11 of 16` systems each) with *no channel is bound for bot news, and it has no system channel* in the one column that matters, and with **nothing reachable the send button read *Send it to 0 servers*** — a primary button offering to broadcast to nobody, which the server refuses anyway. It is disabled and says why now, because a control that cannot do anything is a control somebody presses to find out. The suite would not have caught that: every law about the refusal reads the *server's* answer.
  - **26 new laws** — 16 in `tests/test_broadcast.py` (the template check, the four destination cases, the preview-is-the-send, the mention allowance) and 10 in `tests/test_god.py::TheAnnouncementIsADryRunFirst` (the dry run lists every server and posts nothing, the send lands where the list said, a locked tier refuses both verbs, a send that reached nobody is recorded as refused, a stale token keeps the message, the page offers the module's own four tokens). **Three were watched to fail by mutation**: `AllowedMentions(roles=[x])` leaves `everyone=True` in discord.py — two laws caught it, which is why the allowance states all four fields rather than the one it means; an audit row written as `ok=True` regardless of the result; and a stale token that dropped the message.
  - **Suite 3096 passed / 68 skipped / 4246 subtests**, from 3069 at the start of the pass, with the whole suite green in one run rather than by subset.
- 📦 **The image the machine runs was missing two files the process reads, and both failures were silent by design — the history page answered *0 entries* about a changelog the image did not carry, and the clickwrap gate was open.** `.dockerignore` listed `CHANGELOG.md` and `docs/`, and both are opened *at runtime*: `keep_alive.HUB_CHANGELOG` reads the changelog out of the directory `keep_alive.py` lives in — the hub's "what changed" page and the console's Changes page, which is the same reader — and `utils/consent.py` hashes `docs/legal/sub-processors.md` into the revision the gate compares a session against. **Every one of those readers falls back rather than raising** (a missing changelog is an empty history, a missing document is a revision that cannot be computed, a missing font is Pillow's bitmap one), which is right on its own and is together exactly how an image that has lost a page looks like a deployment where nothing happened. Measured on the live Space rather than argued: `/hub/changes` answered *"0 entries, newest first"*.
  - **And the second consequence was worse than the empty page.** `consent.version()` returns an empty string when any document's mtime cannot be read, and `consent.needs()` answers **no** for every session when the revision is empty — so the agreement every sign-in is sent through, the page whose entire reason for existing is that it stands at the door, was not standing there on the only machine that serves it. The gate reads as absent behaviour on a page nobody can reach without a session, which is why nothing else noticed. **What was measured after the fix is the first half and the shape of the image**: `/hub/changes` went from *0 entries* to **405**, with the entries' own emojis drawn where the same placeholder dot used to be — which is the container proving it can read a file at its own root that it could not read before, and the second file is dropped by the same list in the same image. The *revision* itself is not askable from outside: `/agree` is behind a sign-in by design, so the clickwrap's half of this is the code path (`version()` → `needs()`) rather than a reading, and that is written down here rather than implied by the sweep below.
  - **Shipped and read back**: `bfb0e60` on all four Pages hosts and mirrored to the Space, whose `/hub/changes` then answered 405 entries — measured on the live address, not on a local build.
  - **This is the second time this file has excluded something the process reads**, and the first one is written into the file itself (`assets/` is deliberately not ignored any more, because the loader falls back to Pillow's bitmap font and reports nothing). So the list is checked against the code that opens these files now, in both directions: `tests/test_image.py` derives what must be carried from `keep_alive.HUB_CHANGELOG`, `consent.DOCUMENTS`, `consent.BUNDLE_EXTRAS` and `card.FONT_DIR`, so a reader added later moves the law without anybody remembering it — and asserts the file still drops something, because an image that carries the tests, the workflows and the archived cogs is an image that ships a copy of the repository. **Suite 3069 passed / 68 skipped / 4244 subtests**, and the law was watched to fail by putting `docs` and `CHANGELOG.md` back: both paths named, and nothing else.
- 🧭 **The console's clickwrap was wearing the public site's header, and the machine that runs the bot was kept current by a command that did not exist** — two gaps found by following the *deploy* path rather than by looking at a page, and both of them are the shape this file keeps recording: one reader answering a question that has three answers, and a procedure that only works while somebody remembers it.
  - **`/agree` is the one page of the console's host with no page of its own to override the nav.** The clickwrap is served on **all three** addresses from one template — the public site, the member dashboard and the operator console — and it extends the shared frame, so its header is decided by which address the request arrived on. `_frame_nav` answers the member app's list and nothing on the console's host, and the public site's fallback is exactly right there — so the operator's own agreement offered **Home, Systems, Get started and Radio**, every one of which `dacatowner.pages.dev` answers with a 301, on the page that stands between the operator and the fleet. There are two readers now (`_console_frame_nav`), answering with the console's own addresses and *only* on the console's address, and the page draws the same `console_nav` macro `/owner` and its door draw — a second copy of five lines is how this repository's other duplicated lists came apart. **The address itself was the part that had to be got right**: the first version compared against `hub.live_url`, which is the *machine* one hop behind the console and never what a browser asked for — a reader that would have answered nobody. It is `hub.console_url` (`OWNER_CONSOLE_URL`, with the published address as the fallback), the same shape as the two address readers beside it, and a law asserts the two are not the same host so that mistake cannot be made quietly.
  - **And the Space — the host with no commit stamp — had no repeatable way to be updated.** `.github/workflows/sync_hf.yml` force-pushes `main` to it and has never run on this account, so the machine every other host proxies to was mirrored by whoever happened to remember a git push, which is how it sat several commits behind both Pages hosts while every check said otherwise. `scripts/mirror_space.py` sends the tracked tree as **one commit** and refuses the two things that would make it a lie: a modified tracked file (the Space would run code no commit contains) and a `HEAD` nobody else has (a hash no one else can resolve). `--check` is the same comparison with nothing sent; `--prune` is what deletes, and it is opt-in because the database the Space runs on is not a tracked file.
  - **The diff it reports is measured, and one part of it cannot be — stated rather than guessed.** The Space reports a blob id per file, and for the files it stores as text that id *is* the git blob id, so `git ls-files -s` gives an exact comparison for nothing. For some binary files it reports an id in a scheme of its own: **ten unchanged assets sit in that list** (`.ttf`, `.ogg`) whose bytes are byte-for-byte identical to this tree, which the first version of the script offered to re-send on every run — a mirror whose report is work it did not do. Those are read back and compared byte for byte, which is the only honest answer available, and the law that holds it was watched to fail by deleting the byte comparison.
  - **And `ship.py` now reads `.env`.** The three builders run as subprocesses of it and inherit its environment, and nothing on that path had ever read the file this project documents as the way to configure a machine — so a value living there reached the bot and none of the hosts it proxies. That is not hypothetical: `DISCORD_CLIENT_ID` is in `.env` here, and a build from a plain shell shipped a site whose invite button was absent with nothing in its output to say why. `load_dotenv` never overrides what is already exported, so a value set on the command line still wins.
  - **And a row's emoji was being read off a headline that had never matched.** The hub's "what changed" rows draw an icon per entry, and the icon was looked for on the *matched bold lead* — while `_LEAD` requires a line to begin with `**` and every entry in this file begins with an emoji instead, so the field was an empty string for all of them: every row on every page has been drawn with the same placeholder dot, with the real emoji sitting inside the bolded sentence beside it. The emoji is read from the entry's own text now, so the row and the headline stop saying it twice.
  - **Twelve new laws**, watched to fail where the property is one a mutation can express: nine in `tests/test_mirror_space.py` covering the four ways a mirror can be quietly wrong — sending a file no commit holds, sending the local database or a build directory, re-sending a binary the Space stores itself, and deleting something the repository never tracked — two in `tests/test_owner_site.py` for the console's reader answering on exactly one address (and not the machine's), and one in `tests/test_hub.py` for the emoji round trip. **Suite 3065 passed / 68 skipped / 4231 subtests.**
- 🤖 **Auto Moderation was not a system, it was a line inside another system's description — and the question that started this pass was a user asking why there was no switch for it**: *"Why is there no option to check or turn off auto moderation…"*. The honest answer was that there was nothing to turn off, because `Moderation` had swallowed `automod` as one of its gate commands and the flood rule it ran was three constants nobody could see. It is its own feature now — **16 systems, 98 slash commands** — with **seven declared settings**: block invites, block links with a per-server allowlist, an anti-spam rule whose message count and window are both the server's, and how long a flood should mute for (including *never*, which logs the flood and mutes nobody). Moderation keeps its own commands and loses the shields sentence that promised a filter it did not own.
  - **The mechanism is the part that matters more than the feature.** Every knob is declared once in the registry (`utils/features.Setting` — id, label, help, kind, default, bounds, choices), and the dashboard, the API, the cog and the settings table all read that one declaration. `_posted_setting_value` validates a submitted value **against the declaration rather than trusting the form**: an unlisted key is refused, an integer is checked against its own bounds, a choice against its own list — and a stored value that has been corrupted falls back to the *default*, never to a larger allowance, because the only direction of corruption that matters on a flood rule is the one that mutes more people for longer. Two laws of the registry itself now exist: a feature cannot declare the same setting id twice, and a setting key cannot collide with another feature's.
  - **The link filter compares hosts, not strings.** `evil-github.com` and a bare `github.com` are different answers, so a host is allowed when it *is* the entry or **ends with `.` + the entry**, and a `*.` prefix or a leading dot in the allowlist is stripped rather than obeyed. The three separators a person actually types (comma, space, semicolon) all work, and the blocked-message branch points at the appeal, the same as the invite filter beside it.
  - **Structure's destructive ceilings are read from the registry now rather than from a dict beside it** (`utils/confirmations` derives `CEILINGS`/`MAX_CEILING` from the four `confirmation_ceiling.<action>` settings), so *how many channels may be deleted before somebody is asked* is a value the same page that lists the system can change, and a corrupt one falls back to the default the registry declares.
- 🎭 **Reaction roles were archived, broken, and are now a live system with a dashboard card**: `deleted_features/cogs/reaction_roles.py` gated on `"moderation"`, pointed at a hostname that has not existed for two rebuilds, and had no guild or channel columns at all — a role table that could not tell one server's rows from another's. The rebuilt cog adds a role from a **message link** (a bare id would have to be guessed at, and a link carries the guild and the channel with it, both of which are now columns), refuses a cross-server link, refuses a role at or above the bot's own top role with a sentence in words, and logs Discord's own refusals rather than swallowing them. `permissions.role_grant_problem` guards the escalation on the **web door** too, because a form is a door.
- 🔐 **And the forwarded headers were being believed by anyone who could send them** — measured on the live Space, not argued: a request carrying `X-Forwarded-Host: evil.example` came back with a `redirect_uri` built on `evil.example`, and `X-Forwarded-For` was read straight into the rate-limit bucket, so a forged chain could both aim the OAuth redirect and share a bucket with itself. `X-Forwarded-Host/Proto` are now believed **only** when the request carries our worker's own `X-Meow-Proxy` value, compared constant-time against `OWNER_PROXY_SECRET`, and `X-Forwarded-For` is **not read at all** — a request without the signed stamp is answered from `request.remote_addr`. All three builders (`build_site`, `build_hub`, `build_owner`) now strip the hop headers, set their own, and stamp the visitor (`CF-Connecting-IP`) and the proxy secret, and a build with no secret configured says so in a NOTE rather than shipping a stamp nobody can verify.
  - **The live-probe reproduction is a law now**, pinned in `tests/test_hardening.py` under the name of the property it holds (*a forwarded chain is not evidence and is not read*): the unsigned forged host is refused, the signed one is obeyed, the client IP falls back without a stamp, and the builder's own stamp survives the round trip.
- ✅ **Suite 3053 passed / 68 skipped / 4218 subtests**, from 3026 at the start of the pass, every red cleared and none introduced; the builder set (`test_hub`, `test_pages_site`, `test_owner_site`, `test_ship`, `test_deploy`, `test_ai_guard`) green at 239 passed. The listing copy the directory submission is graded on (`docs/discord-directory.md`) carries the two new numbers, and `tests/test_legal.py` reads them out of the registry rather than trusting the file.
- 🧪 **A playtest of the built site found two defects, and both were on the front door.** Driven as a first-time visitor would, against a local build with a canned `/api/figures`: the hero, the eleven dashboard views, all six questions, the theme picker and the phone menu; the command reference's search, its folded tails and a deep link into a group; the station table's two filters, *Show all*, the copy control and its empty state; and every reading page plus the 404.
  - **A phone folded the hero's four cards three-and-one.** Measured at 390px rather than looked at: `repeat(auto-fit, minmax(76px, 1fr))` inside a 346px panel made three columns of 81px, so *117 commands* wrapped onto a row of its own and `1,284` overflowed its cell by 7px — which took the page's own `.wrap` over the viewport with it. Four cards are a **stated** row now (four on a desktop, two on a phone, with a value that may shrink for the widest figure the card holds), which is what the measurement asked for and what the eye asked for: a stray fourth card reads as a mistake. Re-measured at 390 after the fix: 2×2, no element wider than its box, `document.scrollWidth` back to 390.
  - **And the front door answered its own question twice, differently.** Step 01 read *"The invite asks for Administrator"*, and the first question below it answered *"No. The invite asks for what the systems you keep need"* — one URL, two permission claims, on one screen, for a visitor deciding whether to install. The link the button offers really does carry `permissions=8` (`discord_auth.INVITE_PERMISSIONS`), so the *answer* was the wrong one: it described the minimal invite, which is the second link beside it. The answer now says what the step says, and a law holds the two together.
  - **Two laws, both watched to fail** — the phone shape (reverting `.figures` to `auto-fit` fails it) and the permission answer (restoring the old sentence fails it). The paths that worked were left alone: the tablist swaps one panel at a time with the roving `tabindex` intact, the six questions open in place, the command search reports *7 of 117 match “market”* and opens the folded tail a hit is hiding in (*1 of 117* for `richlist`, group and tail open, row in the viewport), a deep link lands with its group open and its summary clear of the header, the station filters answer *2 of 308 in Finnish* and *1 of 308 in Finnish · Finland*, *Show all* paints 308 and flips to *Show the first 24*, the copy control says *Copied* and reverts, the light palette's button ink measures **5.26:1** (the fix from an earlier pass, still holding), and every page's only fragment is the skip link, which resolves.
  - **Reported, not changed**: `/systems`' activity band still closes with *"A dash is a reading nobody has taken yet"*, which was written when the only reason a dash survived was that nobody had read the tracker — a dash can now also mean the proxy did not answer. The sentence is not false, and the page's own status line covers the case, so it is a copy decision rather than a defect, and it is recorded here rather than quietly reworded.
- 🧊 **Five notes from reading the published site, and four of them were the same defect: the page doing work to look complete rather than showing the thing it claims** — *"Nothing is connected"* on `/systems`, *"AI SLOP SHIT"* on its command-weight chart, *"TOoo long"* on its command reference, *"this whole page full of text"* on `/radio`, and *"Looks Ass"* on the front door's hero. Each was answered by deleting something or by pointing the page at its own data, and the deletions are as small as the adds.
  - **"Nothing is connected" was two dashes printed over a working endpoint.** The band headed *Counted, not claimed* drew `—` where servers and members reached belong, and the same deployment's `/api/figures` — proxied back to the machine by `build_site --proxy` — was already answering **200** with `43` and `1,284`: nothing asked. The two cells the page cannot fill now carry the key they are waiting on (`data-figure="control:servers"`), `/systems` ships the URL, and `motion.js` writes only the cells the server marked as waiting, leaving a dash a dash when the answer does not carry that figure and when the read fails. **And *waiting* is now the page's own answer rather than the template's guess**: `keep_alive._tile()` writes `waiting` beside each value, so a card that holds a number carries no hook at all — the first draft put one on every cell and let the reader decide, which is a second author for a value the host had already counted.
  - **"AI SLOP SHIT" was a chart whose whole content was printed three lines under it.** Fifteen bars of command counts, sized against the largest system, headed *Which system is the big one* — over the list that prints each system's count in a column of its own. `_system_bars()` and the section went, and with them the last `data-bars` markup in the repository.
  - **"TOoo long" was 117 rows each carrying a *Copy* button and its own search blob.** The reference is an index now: the fifteen system groups, collapsed, with a *Copy* per command still inside them — the same names, one click deeper, and the section is **1,219px** where it was the page's tallest band. The folded tail behind a group now tells the search the truth as well: a match inside `+N more` opens both the tail and the group, because a search that found something the reader cannot see found nothing.
  - **"Looks Ass" was a panel that said its own product was fake.** The hero's preview drew the registry's two counts — printed three inches away in the hero's own meta line — and closed with *Sample data — nothing here is connected*, which was honest about the first draft and became an admission the moment the panel could count the deployment. It now draws **the same four cards `/systems` opens with, from the same reader** (`_home_stats()` is gone; the front door and the reference cannot disagree about a figure neither of them authors), the two the registry answers for are counted up by `motion.js` and the two only the gateway knows are dashed until `/api/figures` fills them, and the panel's last line says which half is which. The proportions moved with it: the headline is down a step (**59.5px → 48.9px** at 1440) and **the two columns are centred against each other**, because top-aligned they left a 91px hole under the buttons — the two numbers the eye reads as unfinished. Measured after: the hero is **553px** of a 900px viewport where it was 617, the panel's four cards are `43 · 1,284 · 15 · 117`, and the copy and the panel are centred on the same line to within a pixel.
  - **And an inner page was shouting louder than the front door that introduced it.** `pages.css` drew every page head at `--text-display`: measured on `/systems` at 1440, **76.5px over two lines above an 18px lede, with 88px of padding above it** — a **472px** band for a title and a sentence, on the pages that exist to be read. The page head is the hero's own scale now (**48.9px**, 44px of padding) and measures **353px**; the header, the first band's heading and the figures below it are all in one screen now, which is what let *Counted, not claimed* be read rather than scrolled past.
  - **"Full of text" was seven tellings of the same thing on one page.** `/radio`'s hero lede was four dense lines, its first band carried a four-line paragraph about Discord's command budget beside its heading, its second a two-line note about vocabulary, and its three failure modes were paragraphs. Each is one line now, the counts lead, and the one command that opens the radio gate (`/bot legacy state:On`) stayed *in* the line that names the gate — the law in `tests/test_pages_site.py` holds that pair together, which is what caught the trim that dropped it.
  - **Two laws were re-anchored and one got stronger on the way.** `test_no_figure_is_invented_while_the_bot_is_asleep` used to assert that *servers* and *members reached* were **absent** from the front door, because the panel drew the registry's two cards and nothing else; it now asserts they are **present and dashed**, on both pages that draw them, which is the claim a visitor of a sleeping host reads and would catch a `0` where the old wording could not. The three tile pins that matched the literal string `tile__value">` were widened to `[^>]*` — the attribute is part of the tag, and a pin on a tag's exact spelling is how a hook lands without a law noticing.
  - **Suite 130 failed / 2,611 passed / 68 skipped — the same numbers as the run before this pass, and the check is the set**: `comm -13` against the pre-pass failing ids is **empty**, with both figure laws green after being rewritten rather than relaxed. Verified by reading the built pages back in a browser with a canned `/api/figures`: the front door's panel and `/systems`' first band both fill (`43` / `1,284`), the command reference folds to an index, and `/radio`'s hero is two lines.
  - **Not deployed**: everything here is uncommitted on `main` at `179d0cc`, and `meow-bot.pages.dev` republishes on push — so the published copy still serves the version these notes were written about.
- ✂️ **The front door's fifteen-system band was a third of the front door, and the answer was to delete it rather than trim it**: *"Remove this tooo long"*, pointed at `<section id="systems">`, and measuring it first is what decided the shape of the fix — on the deployed page the band was **1,549 of 5,000 pixels** at 1440×900 and **2,096 of 6,510** at 482px wide, the longest section on the page by a factor of two, and every row in it was the same fifteen systems `/systems` lists, read from the same registry. So the band is gone, its two anchors were rewritten to `/systems` (the hero's *See what is in it* and the preview panel's *see all*), and the built front door went **64,814 → 34,863 bytes** with `id="systems"` and `data-explorer` at zero occurrences. **The anchors were the part that had to be got right, and the reason is in the build**: a fragment is never sent to the server, and `build_site.rewrite_url` leaves `#systems` alone while it rewrites `/systems` to `systems.html` — so leaving either link as a fragment would have shipped a link that scrolls nowhere on the published copy, silently, from the run that also reported success.
  - **What left with it, one author at a time.** `landing.js` loses the explorer (its filter, its counters and its row-drawing) and keeps the preview panel's tablist, so the front door no longer hides or counts a list at all — one renderer fewer that can disagree with the registry about how many systems exist; `landing.css` loses the `.explorer`/`.chip`/`.system` rules and the two `@media` blocks that only restyled them; `keep_alive._landing_categories()` and the `categories` context key go with the chips they fed; and `explorer_json` goes with the payload named after a thing that no longer exists. **Three more keys were dead before this pass and are now gone rather than inherited**: `commands`, `commands_json` and `ticker_commands` — with the ticker loop that built one of them and `_landing_commands()` that built another — had no reader left anywhere in the repository; their only consumer is the archived `Deleted/pre-rebuild-2026-09-24/templates/home.html`, which is how a page can shed a console and a marquee and leave their payloads in the view.
  - **The band was carrying a fact, and it moved rather than left.** `tests/test_pages_site.py` asserts the legacy system's own registry sentence — *switched off until an administrator asks for them* — on "the page that lists all fifteen", and until this pass that page was the front door, because the band rendered each registry description inside a closed drawer whose markup ships regardless. `/systems` is now the page that lists all fifteen, so it prints it: a new `features.ships_off()` reader (the third answer beside `always_on_names()`, built from `dashboard_modules()` and `DEFAULT_DISABLED_MODULES` so the sentence is the feature's own `description` and a registry edit moves the page with it) feeds one sentence under the list, and the law is anchored there. **And the law got stronger on the way**: reading "the page" would have passed on a list that had quietly lost a row — three other bands on `/systems` name systems too — so it now reads the list's own `<ul class="list">`, and truncating the template's loop to `features[:3]` fails it.
  - **One new law, because this change's failure mode is silence**: every bare `href="#…"` on the served front door must have an element carrying that id — a fragment is never sent to the server, so a link to a section that has left the page scrolls nowhere, logs nothing and throws nothing. Restoring the hero's `href="#systems"` fails it with `['systems']` named.
  - **And a pin that had been passing for the wrong reason went red, which is the useful kind of red.** `PreviewHarness::test_the_entry_point_the_usage_line_names_is_served` asserted the literal string *Meow* in the served landing page — and it kept passing after the rebrand only because the band printed the economy's registry description, which still calls the house account **Meow Bank**, so the old word was on the page while the brand was not the thing being found. It reads `keep_alive._bot_display_name()` now. Confirmed against a clean `git worktree` of `HEAD` rather than argued: the test passes there and fails in this tree before the fix, and the only source of the word on the page was the band.
  - **Suite 130 failed / 2,611 passed / 68 skipped**, and the check is the set: `comm -13` against the pre-change failing ids is **empty**, with the four `tests/test_consent.py` pins that were red in that baseline now green because they belong to `179d0cc`, which the baseline predates. Mutations watched to fail: the hero's anchor put back as a fragment, a `data-explorer` reintroduced, the `/systems` list truncated, and `ships_off()` emptied.
  - **Reported, not changed**: *Meow Bank*, the *Meow Fee*, `meow.css`, `MeowAnim` and `MeowTheme` are in-product and internal names the rebrand left where they were. They are consistent across `utils/features.py`, `templates/dashboard.html` and `templates/market.html`, so this pass read them as deliberate rather than as leftovers — but they are the last place a visitor can meet the old word, and renaming them is a product decision rather than a defect.
- 🚀 **The developer hub was the one host in this repository with no publish path at all, which is precisely how it sat an entire rebrand behind**: `Site` builds `_site` and `_owner` and has no job for `_hub`, so `meowdev.pages.dev` changed only when somebody remembered to build and upload it by hand — and nothing anywhere asked that host what it was serving, so a violet palette and a 404 announcing the retired name lived on a public address until a playtest read the page. `scripts/ship.py` now builds, uploads and verifies it beside the other two, and **the release check names all four addresses** — three Cloudflare Pages projects and the machine.
  - **The fourth address cannot be given the same check, so it is given the one it can answer.** The Hugging Face Space is *pushed* to rather than uploaded (`.github/workflows/sync_hf.yml` force-pushes `main`) and no deployment of it carries a commit stamp, so *which commit are you serving* is not askable there — the check says so in words rather than borrowing the Pages hosts' verdict. What it does answer is `/health`, and that answer names the product, so a machine still replying `"service":"meow-bot"` **fails the release**. That is not hypothetical: it is exactly what the live Space replied the first time it was asked, while every page had already moved. **And the name is derived, not typed** — `brand.GATEWAY_SERVICE` is built from `brand.NAME`, `app.py`'s `/health` returns it, and the check compares against the same value — so a rename moves the answer and the check together instead of leaving a literal in the one field a whole rebrand missed; a law reads the served line rather than the file, because the first version of it passed on the comment explaining the expression.
  - **A per-host probe, because a host only serves its own build.** The byte check fetched `static/meow.css` from whatever it was checking, and the hub's build ships its own assets and not the site's sheet — so asking the hub for that file would have reported *the build has no static/meow.css* on every run: a red check for a reason that is not a deployment, on the host that had just been added to the check. Measured on a real build, not argued: `_hub/static/` holds `hub.css`, the marks, the cursors and the search index, and no `meow.css`. Each host now names its own probe, and the hub's is `static/hub.css` — the file its accent token is declared in, so the bytes hashed are the bytes a rebrand moves. The report's column now aligns to the longest address as well, since the machine's name is twice the length of the longest project name.
  - `docs/deployment.md` gains the hub's section and a table of all four addresses — what deploys each one, and what *is it current* means there — with a law that fails when an address is checked at release and not documented, which is the drift that put the hub behind in the first place.
  - **And the byte claim was being made from a directory the run had not built.** The first `--verify-only` after the hub shipped reported `meow-bot` as *serving other bytes than this build's* — a red check about a deployment that was correct. `meow-bot` is published by Cloudflare from its own build, so `_site/` is not the artifact that was uploaded: the local one had been built at 01:17, the sheet changed at 01:46, and **a fresh build of the same commit matched the live host byte for byte** (`251780b583db` on both) while the stale directory did not. A verification run now reports *bytes not compared* and names the command that makes the claim available, instead of answering from the wrong side; three laws, one mutation killed.
  - **Shipped and read back.** `meowdev` production is `7d0cd30` with `static/hub.css` this build's bytes; the live hub's 404 reads *Not here · Keystone Hub* and its title *Overview · Keystone Hub*; the only `8b7bff` left anywhere on that host is the comment recording which violet it replaced. The other addresses as of that run: `meow-bot` current, `meow-owner` a commit behind (no Git integration), and the machine still answering `"service":"meow-bot"` — a push to the Space, not an upload.
  - **Fourteen laws, each watched to fail**: five mutations were run against the new code and all five were killed — the hub's probe swapped for the site's sheet, the hub dropped from the shipped hosts, the gateway check no longer comparing the name, the machine dropped out of the reported addresses, and the Space's answer typed instead of derived. **One caveat for whoever runs mutations here next**: reverting a *same-size* mutation with `cp` within the same second leaves the compiled `.pyc` valid, so the next test run reads the mutated bytecode from cache — which is what made `built=wait` → `built=True` appear to survive its own revert, and why the revert now clears `scripts/__pycache__` too.
- 🌐 The four hosts were then read as a visitor reads them, and the rebrand had reached three: the **developer hub was still the old colour**, the **Space card still announced the old name**, and the hub's own 404 — the one page of it written by hand — still said *Meow Hub*. None of it was in the parts anybody had checked, which is the shape this file keeps recording: a rebrand is not done when the pages a template renders agree, it is done when every surface a person can reach does.
  - **The hub wore a violet.** `hub/static/hub.css` is deliberately self-contained — it is a separate Pages project with no access to `static/meow.css`, and `tests/test_hub.py` asserts it declares its own palette — and that arrangement is right, and it is also how a *second* declaration of one brand sat in a file nothing compared: **`--accent: #8b7bff`**, an indigo→purple card on huggingface.co, and **the page's ambient glow spelling the same violet as three `rgba()` channels**. The tokens move to the brand's teal (dark `#2dd4bf` with `#8bf0e4` as the second gradient stop; light `#0f766e`, the shade the site itself uses for accent *text* on a light ground, because this hub paints text in `--accent` where the site keeps two names for the two contrast floors), the glow moves with them, and `tests/test_brand.py` now holds the two files together — the hub's dark accent against `utils/brand.ACCENT_HEX`/`ACCENT_TEXT_HEX`, its light accent against the site's light `--accent-text`, and a *declarations-only* check that the retired violet is gone, which is what caught the glow: a comment is allowed to name the value it replaced, a declaration is not.
  - **The Space card is YAML, and nothing had ever read it.** `README.md`'s front matter is what huggingface.co renders as the tile for the host that actually runs the bot, and it said `title: Meow`, `emoji: 🐾` and an **indigo→purple** gradient — the pre-rebrand name, the cat, and the accent the hub had just stopped using. It is `Keystone` on 💠 with the nearest pair of words the card's closed colour vocabulary allows, and a law asserts the title is the module's name rather than a literal.
  - **And the page behind that card was three unlabelled widgets.** `app.py` — the one file in this repository no test had read — drew `# 🐾 Meow Discord Bot - 24/7 Cloud Gateway`, a `Ping Test` box, a `Query ZeroGPU` button and an `Output` box, and answered *"Meow Engine Active"*. It now draws the brand, says what the address **is** (the machine: the gateway held open, the database, the API the other hosts proxy to) and links the three doors a visitor actually wants — the site and per-server dashboard, the operator's console, the developer hub — with the GPU box labelled as the **load check it is**, because ZeroGPU refuses to start a space whose `@spaces.GPU` function is not bound to an event, which is the whole reason it exists. The theme is the product's palette built through `gr.themes`, wrapped in a `try`: this is the only file here whose failure mode is not a wrong colour but **a machine that does not boot**, so a renamed Gradio token gives a plain page and a line in the log rather than an offline bot. The startup contract is untouched and now written down — port forced before the import, `_SERVER_STARTED` set so `bot.py` adds no second set of threads, Flask mounted as the catch-all so the API and the bot are one process — and `/health`'s public `service` field stops reporting a codename no page uses.
  - **The hub's changelog page was publishing literal asterisks**, and that one was already red: `utils/hub.plain()` unwraps markdown pairs, and two entries in this file carry an **unpaired** `**` whose partner an earlier pass had eaten, so *"Suite unchanged at 148 failed / 2,429 passed / 41 skipped**"* was on a public page of a host that renders no markup at all. A doubled asterisk is never prose, so leftovers are dropped — the pre-existing failure in `tests/test_hub.py` is green, and the next entry with a stray marker cannot leak it either.
  - **Suite 135 failed / 2,577 passed / 41 skipped → 134 failed / 2,584 passed / 41 skipped**, and the check that matters is again the set rather than the count: **no new failing id, one fixed**, with every remaining red a pin on a surface the earlier rebuild archived. **One thing this could not fix, and it is the largest brand string in the product**: every backend-rendered page — `/login`, `/dashboard`, `/market`, `/owner` — still reads *Meow*, because `keep_alive._bot_display_name()` deliberately prefers the **live Discord application's name** and the application is still called that. `utils/brand.py` says so in as many words; renaming it in Discord's developer portal is what moves all four pages at once, and no code change substitutes for it.
- 🔎 The rebrand and the bandwidth pass were then used the way a stranger would use them, and four of the five defects the pass found were on a surface a person reads rather than in the code that draws it. Nothing here is a feature: it is the difference between *the page renders* and *the page says something true*, which is the only thing a playtest can find.
  - **Radio's own page listed none of its commands, under a heading that promised three.** `/radio` is the one address on the site that describes the system — it is in the header of every page — and its section *One group, three faces* shipped with an **empty list**, because the route asked the registry for a feature called `"radio"`, a name no feature has answered to since radio and the anonymous question box became one switch called `legacy`. `features.get` answers `None` rather than raising, `list(feature.commands) if feature else []` answers `[]`, and an unset name in a template is empty rather than an error, so the page drew a heading, a lede about Discord's hundred-command budget, and nothing else. It reads the `legacy` feature's **`/radio` half** now, through `features.feature_entries` so the name is what a person types and the sentence after the em dash stays the page's own.
  - **And the same page never said the system arrives switched off.** The rebrand put radio behind a default-off flag, and radio's page — the only place a visitor can read what it does — described the commands, the table and the failure modes and closed with *"one system among 15, and switchable like the rest"*, which a reader takes to mean *available, and you may turn it off*. It is the other way round: a visitor installed the bot, typed `/radio`, and was refused by the gate for a fact no page had mentioned, with the enabling command — `/bot legacy state:On` — nowhere on the site either. The closing sentence now says the system **arrives switched off with the question box**, and the section that lists the commands carries the switch and the fact that `/radio` says which one is off rather than failing quietly.
  - **Every module card on `/dashboard/systems` offered `//starboard set`.** The registry writes commands the way a person types them (`"/market buy"`, leading slash included — the same convention `command_count()` strips with `lstrip("/")`), and the card's own line prefixed a second slash: **fifteen cards and 117 rows, every command on the page unrunnable and un-copy-pasteable**, on the page an administrator reads to decide what a system does. Nothing failed, because `//starboard set` is valid text; the law that now holds it reads the **served** page, takes only the whitespace-separated tokens that begin with a slash (a note may legitimately contain one — "win/loss"), and requires each to start with exactly one slash *and* name a root the registry declares, so a card cannot invent commands in the other direction.
  - **The light palette painted its primary button and brand mark at 3.74:1.** White on `#0d9488` — the dark and high-contrast palettes measured 10.6:1 and 14.2:1 — and the pair is one no law looked at: the design laws measure the accent **as text** (`--accent-text` on a surface) and the accent as a **graphic** (3:1 for fills and rings), while `--text-invert` is the ink painted *on* the fill, which is what the brand mark's K and the button's label are drawn in. The light palette paints the house ink on the same fill now — **5.26:1**, measured in the browser after the transition settled, in all three palettes — and `tests/test_theme.py` measures the ratio in every palette because the light one was the only palette where a colour was merely hard to read, which no browser reports as anything at all.
  - **And the preview harness was drawing the site frame without ever being handed it**: `/dashboard` and `/market/<symbol>` were rendered with the page's own context alone, so the wordmark came out a **lone "."** and the `<title>` ended on its own separator — the identical defect the route itself was repaired for, alive in the tool somebody opens *to decide whether the page is right*. Both renders merge `keep_alive._site_context()` (and `_invite_context()` where the route does) now, the two kwargs they duplicated are gone, and a law reads the harness's source so the frame has one owner there too.
  - **Verified rather than asserted, on the real wire.** Gzip, from a locally served app: `/systems` 60,724 → **6,531** bytes, `/api/market/quotes` 13,527 → **2,558**, `/login` → **1,986**, `/api/figures` → **351**; `/static/*` is deliberately left alone (the published copy is served those by the CDN's own edge). The market's polling was counted rather than read — `window.fetch` instrumented in the page shows `/api/market/quotes` at **t = 0, 15, 30 s**, i.e. the 15-second floor the price refresher sets, with the candle read once at 0 — and the pause is `pollWhileVisible`'s, whose timer stops while `document.hidden`. **Suite 135 failed / 2,577 passed / 41 skipped**, and the verification that matters is the set rather than the count: every failing test id is one that was already failing at the pre-rebrand HEAD (**139 → 123 ids, `comm -13` empty**), so the rebrand, the bandwidth pass and this one fixed sixteen reds and introduced none. Fourteen laws were added across the five files the defects lived in, and each was watched to fail — the doubled slash fails fifteen subtests, the retired `"radio"` lookup fails two, the light palette's white ink fails on `3.74:1` — before the fix went in.
- 🗜️ The same bytes were being paid for three times over, and this pass found all three: the backup that goes out after every change, the per-server pictures that go out twice, and the pages and JSON every browser fetches. It started from the one number that had to be measured rather than assumed, the bot's own database, and followed it through the three places it is spent.
  - The backup was the biggest item on the bill and nothing about it was sized on purpose. bot.db is 2,543,616 bytes here, it is uploaded to Supabase after every change and snapshotted hourly, so on a five-minute timer it is roughly 730 MB a day of egress before anybody opens a page — which is the shape of a bandwidth cap running out. Three changes, and they compound: the snapshot is now compacted with VACUUM INTO rather than copied page for page by the backup API, the payload is gzipped at the one door every upload goes through, and the objects keep their names. Measured through the real readers: 2,543,616 to 1,150,976 (compacted) to 730,680 bytes (gzipped), a 3.48x reduction that applies to every change and every snapshot, with gzip alone worth 3.04x when there is nothing to reclaim.
  - Compaction is not decoration, it is what makes a deletion real. The pages a deleted row leaves behind travel in every copy the backup API takes, so a table that prunes itself keeps costing its old size forever; measured here, deleting 3,200 rows changed the uploaded payload by 0.6 percent without it and by 12.6 percent with it. VACUUM INTO is byte-for-byte repeatable, which the change detection depends on and which was verified rather than assumed, and a host whose SQLite is too old falls back to the page-for-page copy rather than losing its backups.
  - No migration, because the reader decides by looking: a stored object that begins with the gzip signature is inflated and one that does not is handed back exactly as it was, so the bot.db the bucket holds today still restores and the import is transparent to every caller. If gzip would make a payload larger — it does for incompressible bytes — the payload is sent as it is.
  - The pictures were the part of the file gzip cannot help with, because a JPEG is already compressed, and they were bounded at 4 MB per field and stored twice: once to Discord inside the member edit and once in the database that becomes every future backup. utils/identity.tame_image now shrinks a picture once, at both doors, and refuses three things: bytes it cannot decode, an animation, and a re-encode that came out larger. Measured: a 4032x3024 phone photograph 237,256 to 4,750 bytes (49.9x), and a noisy 2000x2000 upload right at the old ceiling 4,690,680 to 79,258 (59.2x). Transparency survives as PNG, everything else becomes JPEG at 85, an avatar is kept at 512px and a banner at 1024px, and the tests that upload the magic-byte-only fixtures still get their bytes back untouched, which is the property that matters.
  - The database was also carrying 3,200 queued messages for guild 777001 — the suite's own fixtures, which reached production the way tests/__init__.py describes: a test inserted through the default path, the bot on that same checkout uploaded the result, and the deploy restores from it. They are invisible in Discord and they travel in every backup. They are pruned once, behind a marker, by the shape of a Discord snowflake rather than by that one fixture number, so a row whose guild could not be a guild goes and every real server's queue is untouched — and the helper commits its own delete, because the first version relied on the caller's transaction and reported a count of rows it had not removed.
  - The pages and the JSON are text, and text gzips five to eight times over. utils/compression.py gzips every answer this process writes that is worth it — over 500 bytes, a compressible type, a body that has one, nothing already encoded — and it is registered before every other after_request hook because Flask runs those in reverse, so it appends to the Vary the security pass wrote rather than replacing it. Measured on the real routes: the landing page 63,766 to 8,111 bytes (7.9x), /systems 60,405 to 6,327 (9.5x), /login 5,918 to 1,979, the market board 13,527 to 2,564 (5.3x), /api/figures 982 to 266. This matters on the published site specifically: its worker forwards the browser's own Accept-Encoding, so the origin answers compressed and the edge has nothing left to squeeze.
  - The last item was not a size at all but a frequency. One market tab polled a 13.5 KB board every eight seconds and the candles every minute, which is about 152 MB a day from a single window nobody has to be looking at, and the bot refreshes its prices at most once every fifteen seconds — so half of those answers were identical to the one before them. The board now polls at that floor and the polling stops while the page is hidden, resuming the moment it is shown, and the Hub's own status read does the same. One open tab costs about 16 MB a day instead of 152, and a backgrounded one costs nothing at all.
  - 42 new tests across four files (compression 20, the backup wire 7, the pictures 11, the fixture rows 4), all of them written to fail first: the layer's floor and its four refusals, the length that must describe what is actually sent, the compressed body that must inflate to the answer it replaced, an object written by the older release, a corrupt packed object, the compaction compared against the copy on the same file, and a real server's queue surviving the prune. Suite 138 failed / 2,568 passed / 41 skipped, with the set of failing test ids byte-identical to the one measured before this pass — zero new reds, and no red fixed either, since every remaining one is a pin on an archived surface or a stale registry entry named below.
- **🧱 The management tier has a first half that ships *behind* its guardrails — `/structure` builds and clears away channels and roles, and nothing in it can fire a mass change off one option**: the order was the point. A bot that can delete a server's channels is a different product from one that publishes a leaderboard, and the difference is three properties that are cheap now and impossible to retrofit once a server has lost a channel to a command nobody confirmed. **Size is checked first** (`utils/confirmations.py`): every action that destroys something has a declared ceiling, below it the count *is* the guard — deleting the one channel somebody named is what they asked for, and a prompt for it trains people to click through prompts — and above it nothing happens until the person who typed the command presses a button. The press is checked against the invoker's own id (a confirmation a bystander can give is decoration), a timeout is not an answer, and the prompt names the count, the ceiling and the fact that Discord keeps no copy to restore from. The ceiling is a per-server, per-action stored setting read by one function; a corrupt or out-of-range value falls back to the default rather than becoming a larger limit, which is the only direction of corruption that matters here. **Every write opens a case** (`utils/structure.py`, with `utils/moderation_actions`): the six actions go through one door that performs the Discord call, opens the row in `mod_cases` and posts the server's own moderation log entry through `utils.modlog`, so — in the brief's words — no shipped command modifies server state without an audit entry. Refusals are facts too: `@everyone`, a managed role, a role at or above the bot's own top role, a name Discord will not take. Each is answered in words *and* written to the log, because a log that only holds successes reads like a bot that never refused anything. The verbs live in `moderation_actions.ACTIONS` beside the member actions rather than in a second table, since a case is one list and `Case #12` has to mean one thing. **And the door is the only writer**: `tests/test_guardrails.py` parses `cogs/structure.py` with `ast` and fails if any Discord write in it has a receiver other than the door — the command layer has no way to delete a channel except by asking the door.
