The last law in that file is the one that ages: it walks every `cogs/*.py`, lists the files that still write to a Discord object directly, and requires that set to equal a **pinned inventory with a written reason per entry** — so a *new* direct write cannot appear without somebody writing down why. Two entries are named as gaps rather than blessed: the ticket channel lifecycle in `cogs/tickets.py` and the lockdown permission edits in `cogs/server_control.py` predate this door, and closing them is its own pass. **23 laws, six mutations watched to fail**: the ceiling rule neutered, a bystander's press counted, a destructive action losing its ceiling, the door skipping the case, a corrupt ceiling accepted as a bigger limit, and a direct write added to the cog. The seventh did **not** kill anything and that is recorded rather than hidden — deleting the migration marker's *presence* guard changes no outcome today, because the carrier is `INSERT OR IGNORE`, so what is pinned is the property a server can observe (a switch turned off after the change stays off across a boot) rather than the mechanism.
- **✅ Verified against a measured baseline rather than against the record of one — and the verification found four failures the work itself had caused**: the plan quoted `148 failed / 2,429 passed / 41 skipped` from the repo's own last changelog entry, so before anything moved this round re-measured it (it reproduced exactly) and then reconstructed HEAD read-only (`git archive HEAD` into a temp directory, run with the same interpreter) so the *set of failing test ids* could be diffed instead of the count. That is what caught the four new failures a count alone would have hidden: `/servers` now says *“12 of 13 systems on, off: Legacy — Radio & Anonymous Q&A”* on a server that never touched a switch (re-pointed to assert the named system rather than the absence of the line), the help card pointing at a `/config legacy` that did not exist (the help panel's own law, which reads the panel and the real command tree together, caught a command name I invented), the mark family's floor of two drawings against a brand that now ships one wordmark and one icon, and the radio's refusal no longer saying “turned off” (the wording the other fifteen module gates use). Each was fixed rather than re-pinned to pass, and the diff is now empty in the new direction: **148 failed / 2,429 passed → 137 failed / 2,510 passed**, 41 skipped throughout, with every remaining red a pin on a surface the earlier rebuild archived. **What this does not do, stated rather than implied**: the brand sheet's approval page and the bounded voice pass (Phase 1's last two steps), the onboarding/scheduling/analytics halves of the management tier, the dashboard's capability hub and consent panel, and the legal pass over what the new features store — the tier's first half is channels and roles, and the guardrails it built are what those next commands are meant to be added behind. Two pre-existing gaps are pinned in the inventory above rather than quietly inherited: the ticket channel lifecycle and the lockdown permission writes still touch server state without opening a case.
- **🗄️ Radio and Anonymous Q&A are kept, whole, behind one default-off switch — because the alternative the brief rules out is that a server using them wakes up broken**: the two systems that shipped before this bot became a management product are one feature (`legacy`, `enabled_by_default=False`) gating both root commands, which is a registry edit rather than new machinery: `enabled_by_default` already existed, `utils/command_scope` already removes a disabled command from *that server's* picker rather than merely refusing it, and both commands kept their own names — so nothing enters `commands_sync.RETIRED_COMMANDS` and no server's muscle memory is broken. Nothing was deleted: both cogs are still loaded (as core cogs, because `Feature.cog` holds one path and the module is two files), both groups still publish `play`/`stop`/`doctor` and `ask`/`inbox`/`reply`, and the radio's own files are untouched. **The flag is reachable where the decision is made**: `/bot legacy state:On|Off` — inside the `/bot` group rather than a new `/config`, because Discord allows a hundred top-level commands and this tree keeps a slot free on purpose (a law asserts it) — with the same `administrator` audience as `/bot plan`, the same `modules.set_enabled` writer the dashboard uses, a republish so the picker follows the switch, and a coalesced cloud backup, because a switch that a restart could silently revert is the defect the dashboard's own toggle already fixed once. **Both refusal messages name the flag** (`/bot legacy state:On`) rather than only saying “turned off”, which is the difference between a dead end and an instruction. **The state is carried, not asked for, and on evidence** (`utils/db._carry_legacy_module`, once, behind a marker): a server that deliberately turned either system on has a stored row and keeps it; a server with no row at all was on by default, so it is carried when something was *left behind* — a held seat, the station it last played, a saved language or advertising preference, or a question that went through the box; and a server with none of those never used either, so the new default (off) is the honest answer rather than a guess about a default nobody chose. A server that switched both off on purpose stays off, the old rows are left exactly where they are so a rollback finds its own state, and a second boot does not re-read the evidence. **31 laws** in `tests/test_legacy_module.py`, including the one that caught the real hole: the deliberate-off guard reads the *old* names, and a subquery matching any row of any module at all would look identical in every other test here — an empty `guild_modules` makes it a no-op — while silently skipping every server that had ever touched another switch, which is all of them. It also carries the two numbers the registry owns onto the listing copy the directory submission is graded on (`docs/discord-directory.md`), which is what a registry change is supposed to do.
- **📻 Every station's *Copy* button was invisible on a desktop pointer and was still a click target — 308 of them, one per row**: found by driving `/radio` in a browser rather than reading it, and it is the two-authors shape this file keeps recording. The reveal rule named one owner — `.command:hover .command__copy` — and the station table's rows are `<li class="radio-row" data-station>`, so on a fine pointer wider than 860px **not one of the 308 buttons was ever revealed**: `opacity: 0` resting *and* hovering, measured on the served sheet. Nothing raised, because a button with no visible affordance is still a button: an **invisible 61×30px control sat in the middle of every row**, in the same column position on all 308 of them, and a click meant for the row copied a station URL silently. The phone was fine and that is why it survived — two `@media` rules force `opacity: 1` on a coarse pointer, so the defect existed only where a mouse is. `static/pages.css` gains the rule the second owner needed, above the pointer rules: `.radio-row:hover .command__copy, .radio-row:focus-within .command__copy { opacity: 1; }` — `:focus-within` because the keyboard reaches the same control. Verified on the served sheet (`pages.css?v=6ab5208862c4`, lines 618–619): hovering row 2 at 1280×860 computes **`1`** while the row rests at **`0`**, and the button's own feedback was measured rather than assumed — a `MutationObserver` on one *Copy* logs `Copy → Copied → Copy` at **80695 → 80695.2 → 82297.2 ms**, i.e. the 1600ms revert `wireCopy()` promises. **`/systems`' 110 copies were checked with the same hover and do reveal** (its rows are `.command`), which is what pins this to the second surface and not to the rule: the hovered row's button computed `1` with every sibling at `0`. `tests/test_pages_site.py`'s `TheCatalogueIsARowWide` gains the law, and it is written to survive the next rename: it reads the row class **out of the template** and the reveal **out of the sheet** (split on `"\n.command__copy {"`, because a bare `.command__copy {` lands on the row's own grid rule first), asserting the resting `opacity: 0` *and* a `:{row}:hover` rule that sets it back to `1`.
- **🧭 Eleven dashboard pages were served with no site frame at all — the wordmark was a lone `.`, the footer had no title, and the retention line said nothing is kept while every other page says 365 days**: found by opening `/dashboard` in the browser, which is the only way this class of defect is ever found, because **an unset name in a template is empty rather than an error**. `templates/dashboard.html` extends `site_base.html`, and the frame's own names (`bot_name`, `bot_initial`, `retention_days`, `system_count`, `entry_count`, `kofi_url`, `selling`, `invite_url`…) come from `_site_context()` and `_invite_context()` — which every other served group merges (the market route had been repaired the same way one round earlier) and which `_dashboard_page()` alone never did. So the whole group was drawn from names nobody sent: `<title>` ended in a blank bot name, the brand rendered just `"."`, `.foot__title` was empty, the counts sentence was missing entirely, the support link was gone — and the retention line fell back to *"Nothing is kept longer than it is needed"* on the one surface where a server's administrator is being told what the bot keeps. The render now leads with `**{**_site_context(), **_invite_context(guild_id=guild_id)}` and the two kwargs it duplicated (`selling=`, a second `_invite_context`) are gone from the tail, since those names are `_site_context()`'s. **Verified on the served route in a real signed-in browser, not on the fixture**: brand **Meow Bot.**, footer title **Meow Bot**, *"15 systems and 110 commands"*, *"Messages are kept 365 days"*, the support link present, and **all eleven addresses answer 200** with the brand and their own titles. That distinction is the reason the defect survived a green suite — the dashboard tests render through `tests.test_composer.render_dashboard`, which passes `bot_name` itself, so the frame was complete in the fixture and blank in the page. Two laws in `tests/test_site.py` read the page and the source respectively: `EverySignedInPageSharesThatSameFrame` walks every address in `keep_alive.DASHBOARD_PAGES` and asserts the **served** body carries the brand, the footer title, the counts sentence, the retention sentence and the support link when it is configured, and `TheFrameIsOneFunctionEverywhere` reads `keep_alive.py` and requires `_site_context(` inside the render call of each served group, so the one group that skips it cannot come back quietly. **The frame's own sentence then made a second, older confusion visible on that page**: the state row's `chip('Systems', '14 of 14')` counts the *switchable* subset while the frame counts the **15 declared** systems — the two numbers about one word that `utils/features.system_count()`'s docstring already warns about — so the chip now says **`Switchable 14 of 14 on`**, the Hub's own vocabulary for the same split ("switchable per server"). Driven further while signed in rather than argued: every dashboard page's own controls are forms, and the module switch POSTed from the browser to a server this session does not administer is answered **"You do not administer that server."** with the switch still reading *On* — no optimistic flip, no write. **Suite unchanged at 148 failed / 2,429 passed / 41 skipped**, every red pre-existing (the archived console app and the retired selling gate), with no file outside the two repaired surfaces moving in either direction.
- **🧭 The member dashboard is eleven addresses again, and they are served rather than forwarded — `/dashboard` and every sibling answered 301 to `/servers`, so the per-feature management this bot exists for was nowhere to be found**: the template behind the group was swept into the archive while its routes, its readers and forty of its own tests still needed it, and the whole group was answered by forwarding — which reads like a working link and is a page that does not exist. It draws its own pages now: `templates/dashboard.html` over the one `DASHBOARD_PAGES` table (eleven addresses, `DASHBOARD_GROUPS` for the rail and the copy for each page beside the table it describes), with **every reading taken from the function the matching `/api/*` endpoint already answers with** (`_management_context`, `_progression_context`, `_schedule_queue`, `_channel_choices`, `_identity_state`, `command_scope.summarise`, `command_publish.describe`, `_overview_live`), so a page and a script cannot answer differently about the same server. **Every control is a form** that POSTs to the endpoint that always owned that write, and the module switch's write core was extracted so the page's switches and `/api/toggle` share one implementation rather than two; the template ships **no inline script and no `style` attribute**, which `tests/test_dashboard_pages.py` asserts, because the host's CSP carries no `unsafe-inline` and a page whose readings need a script has no readings on the day the script fails. **The two ledgers are readings, not controls.** Measured against a real server's rows: the economy page prints its own wallets, banks, circulation, Meow Bank and ten-row leaderboard (**120,971,844 · 18,271,023,616 · 18,391,995,460 · 1,156 · 10 accounts**), and the levels page prints the rates **stored for that server** (**15 XP · 4–10 🪙 · 3 XP · 2 🪙**, the row in `level_settings` and not the deployment's defaults) with its own ledger (30,089,472 XP, highest level 179, 1,072 messages, 463 voice minutes) — and neither page offers to write a balance, because that is the operator's console alone. **Four defects came out of driving it rather than reading it.** The first attempt reached a **stale process** — the dev server on 5059 predated the code by an hour and a half, so an authenticated `/dashboard/economy` still landed on `/servers` while the unauthenticated `/login` redirect made the code look new; on a restarted process all eleven answered **200 on their own addresses**, each with its own copy, its own forms and nothing in the console. A notice promised *"pick one below and every page will fill in"* to a sign-in that was offered no picker at all, so the sentence is now drawn only where the picker is and names the ⚡ Open Dashboard door otherwise. The publish line's card was printing its reader's own field names (`age seconds`, `at`) beside the sentence that already said all of it in words, because `facts()` walks a mapping's scalars — the Commands page states the reading, its tone and its two counts now, server-rendered, from the one author in `utils/command_publish.describe`. And the page had **no motion while every other page of this site reveals as it scrolls**: putting the reveal on the whole band was the wrong answer and was caught in the browser at `opacity: 0` with `is-pending` still set after a second, because a 1,575px band under an 860px viewport never reaches the observer's 12% on load — it is on the cards' grids now, which is where the shared-system law always said it belonged, and the same measurement confirms the reveal lands on a wheel scroll. **Two suites that described the document this replaced were the last thing still red beside it**: `tests/test_command_publish.py`'s nine scripted-panel tests are retired with their reason written where they stood (the panel's durable half — one author for the sentence, and the scope the route resolves, including the sentinel `0` that must not borrow Discord's global lane — is pinned on the page *and* on the route, which is where that bug could actually be seen), and `tests/test_site.py`'s page list gains the dashboard as one of the pages that reaches the shared stylesheet through the layout, since asking it to link `meow.css` itself would be asking for a second copy of the shell's own head. **`tests/test_dashboard_pages.py`: 34 tests / 15 subtests** over the eleven pages as they ship; **suite 159 failed / 2,415 passed → 148 failed / 2,426 passed**, with no file outside the rebuilt surface moving in either direction.
- **👑 The operator console is served again — `/owner` answered 500 to the one account that can open it, because its shell was archived with the app it loaded, and it is now eleven pages drawn from the readers the API already answers with**: the address handed out one HTML file for the bundled browser app in `console/`, that app went into the archive with the rest of the pre-rebuild surfaces, and the shell went after it — so the door worked, the passkey worked, and every address behind them raised `TemplateNotFound` for the only person who can reach them. It draws its own pages now: `templates/owner.html` over one `_owner_context(page, guild_id)`, with **every reading taken from the function the matching `/api/console/*` endpoint already calls** (`_fleet_rows`, `_console_totals`, `_console_systems_for`, `_overview_live`, `_progression_context`, `command_publish.describe`, `sessions.reading`, `qna.owner_inbox_sync`, `uptime_status`, `site_guard`, `config_audit`, `hub.read_changelog`), so a page and a script cannot answer differently about the same server. The rail is `OWNER_PAGES` and `OWNER_GROUPS` as they are already declared, the copy for each page sits beside the table it describes, and the whole thing ships **no inline script and no `style` attribute**, which `tests/test_owner_console.py` asserts page by page because the host's policy has no `unsafe-inline` and a template that gained one would look perfectly fine in development. **Three defects came out of building and driving it, none of which a unit test would have raised**: a page whose switches could not be read said *off* rather than saying so (the states are now reported per row, `known` beside them); the rail drew the console's own front page **last**, because the builder appended the pages no group placed instead of leading with them — and the grouping law had *already* recorded that exactly one page is deliberately ungrouped and that it is the home page; and the audit trail's missing table was reported as *"Nothing yet"*, which is a failed read drawn as an empty history, so `_owner_recent` now comes back with the rows **and** whether they could be read at all.

The page's own controls are **forms that POST and redirect**, not buttons waiting on a script: the most consequential controls in this project have to work while a page's JavaScript does not, because that is exactly when something is wrong. Driven end to end in a browser rather than argued: the passkey door, the clickwrap it hands a first sign-in to, the console behind it, and the tier opening with the page's own form — chips flipping to *open · 14m left*, all four actions enabling, *Resume* marked as already the case, and the wrong passkey answered with *"That is not the owner passkey"* while the tier stayed shut. **`tests/test_owner_console.py`: 18 tests, 42 subtests**; **suite 289 failed / 2,308 passed → 279 failed / 2,329 passed**, and the endpoint suite that pins the API's shape is green on the refactor.
- **🌑 The market page was painted over by its own background — a fixed, opaque layer at `z-index: 0` sat above seventy-two instruments, the order ticket and every label, while the chart and the header survived**: found by driving the repaired page instead of trusting the 200, and the measurement is what named it. `meow.css`'s `.aurora` is `position: fixed; inset: 0; z-index: 0` and its own background ends in an **opaque** `linear-gradient(var(--bg), var(--bg-deep))`, so it paints over every in-flow *static* element in the document — which is a page's whole content unless the page says otherwise. The frame's wrapper has said otherwise since the layer was written (`.shell`, `position: relative; z-index: 1`), and **that is exactly why this went unseen**: every page extending the frame was fine, and the market page — the one page that brings a body of its own — was not. The two symptoms have one cause: a canvas inside a positioned panel and a `position: sticky` header are painted in the positioned layer and survived; the rail's rows computed **white on `rgb(16,16,18)` at `opacity: 1` with nothing above them in the hit-tested stack** and were invisible, and the rest of the page was black. One pair of declarations in `static/market.css` fixes it — the rail, the chart, the ticket and every figure on it paint now — and `TheBackgroundIsBelowThePage` holds it from both ends: the layer must still be the fixed, opaque base layer (the premise, asserted so the law cannot quietly go stale), and every template that draws the layer must lift the wrapper it draws its page inside, with the wrapper read out of the *template* and the answer out of the sheets that template links rather than from a list inside the test.
- **🏷️ The market page's brand was blank — and had been since the page was written — because an unset name in a template is empty rather than an error**: noticed while reading the repaired page in a browser, where the header showed the wordmark's trailing dot and nothing before it. `market_context()` describes the *instrument*; `bot_name` and `bot_initial` come from `_site_context()`, which every other page merges and **this route never did** — so the brand had been rendering from names nobody sent, and the markup tests could not see it because the markup is correct. The route now renders `**{**_site_context(), **market_context(instrument)}`, and the two mappings sharing no key is asserted rather than assumed, so a merge cannot silently swap an instrument for a site name. Measured on the served page: mark **M**, wordmark **Meow Bot.**, live price beside them, 122 market tests green.

The rule this round used is the one to keep: a swept file that live code still names was not archived, it was **broken**.
- **📉 `/market/<symbol>` answered 500 and drew a broken-image glyph where its brand belongs, because the page included a template the redesign had already archived**: the market page was rebuilt in the current language and then swept by mistake (it is referenced by live tests, by `scripts/build_owner.py` and by the `/market/<symbol>` route), and it came back with two references to the generation before it — `{% include "theme_picker.html" %}` and `<img src="/static/logo.svg">`. The link it calls *Dashboard* now says *Your servers* and points at `/servers`, because that is the page it opens.
- **🚪 The console's eleven addresses answered 500 to anybody who had one bookmarked — and one of them was the sign-in door's own default**: the pages were archived with the pre-rebuild site while twelve places in `keep_alive.py` still named them, from `_door_target`'s default and `oauth_next`'s landing to the help text's *"The console"* and the public error page's suggestion, so a member who signed in arrived at `TemplateNotFound` and a 500 with a way onward. The fix is stated **once, at the address, rather than twelve times at the destinations**: the group now answers **301 to `/servers`** — the live member surface, which is what every action in the group belongs to — and **302 to `/login`** first for a visitor with no session, so nobody is forwarded to a page that would only forward them back. - **🖱️ The cursor layer was fighting eight components that each set their own `cursor: pointer` — so half the page drew the system hand while the links beside it drew the paw**: *"fix the cursor"*, and the defect is in the one thing a visitor's hand reads before they read a word. The layer at the bottom of `static/meow.css` names `button`, `label`, `select`, `summary`, `[role="tab"]`, `[role="switch"]` and `[data-copy]`; eight rules further up the sheet named *classes* — `.btn`, `.top__menu`, `.switch`, `.chip`, `.system__toggle`, `.showcase__item`, `.mock-switch`, `.faq__question` — and **a class outranks an element**, so each of those controls quietly drew the plain hand. Measured on the served page rather than reasoned about from the sheet: `a[href]` computed the paw, and the *Add to Discord* button — the one control the front door is built around — computed `pointer`, as did the theme button, every chip, every tab, the mobile menu and the FAQ. All eight declarations are deleted (every element they named is covered by the layer), and the layer's comment now says what it is: the only author of a cursor in these sheets. **A second hole came out of planting the state instead of assuming it**: a *disabled* text field computed the **I-beam** while a disabled button computed `not-allowed`, because `input[type="text"]` (0,1,1) outranks a bare `:disabled` (0,1,0) — the rule names its elements now, which is the one state a visitor has to be able to recognise from the pointer alone. Three laws hold it: nothing above the layer declares a cursor, no page sheet declares one at all, and the `not-allowed` rule names its elements.
- **🗂️ `/radio`'s table was two thirds of the page and could not be asked a single question — so it is a table you can ask now, and 7,476px became 1,858**: found by measuring the page instead of reading it, while the round was supposed to be about the front door: at 390px **section 02 was 7,476px and 690 of the page's 1,097 words**, one list of forty-eight rows, no search, no filter, and the only advice on it was to go and type into Discord. Every row is still in the document — nothing is fetched and no script builds the list, because a second renderer is the failure this project has shipped twice — but the page opens with **two dozen** of them and the search and the two menus in front of the rest reach all **308**. The menus are the catalogue's own lists (`index().languages`, `index().countries`), so the page cannot offer a language the bot's picker does not count; the rows carry their own haystack (`_station_rows()` in `keep_alive` — name, genre, country, language and the station's tags, lowercased), so the search has one author and the template has none; the count line answers in the visitor's own words (*`2 of 308 in Finnish`*, *`1 of 308 in Finnish · Finland`*, *`0 of 308 matching “zzzz”`*); a search is an instruction to look at everything, so asking lifts the cap and clearing puts it back; and the `ad-free` tag the catalogue records on 28 stations is on their rows, because that is the one claim on this page a visitor chooses a station by. **The cap has exactly one author**: the template marks the rows past `STATION_PREVIEW` with `data-beyond` and writes the sentence from the same number, the sheet hides what the template marked, and the script counts the marker for its own label — the first draft hardcoded `24` in the JavaScript next to the template's own number, which is the two-authors defect this repository keeps meeting, one line apart.
- **🙈 `hidden` was not hidden, on any element whose sheet sets `display` — a filter set `hidden` on 306 station rows and not one of them left the screen, while the count beside the list said “2 of 308”**: this is the largest defect the playtest found and it was in the *platform*, not the page. The UA sheet says `[hidden] { display: none }` and **any** author rule that sets `display` outranks it, so this project had grown a patch list — `.command[hidden]`, `.cmdx__bar[hidden]`, `.cmdx__status[hidden]`, `.cmdx__empty[hidden]`, `.cmdx__group[hidden]`, `.system[hidden]` — with a comment explaining the rule above it. The list was complete right up until the next element to ship `hidden` was written: measured live, a filtered-out station row still computed **`display: grid` at 359px tall**, and the two controls that exist only for a browser *with* JavaScript (the tools bar, the button that opens the rest) were on screen with nothing to do. The fix is the patch list's general form, once, in the shared sheet — `[hidden] { display: none !important; }` — and `HiddenMeansHidden` holds it from now on: exactly one `[hidden]` rule, in `static/meow.css`, and no sheet patching it back one selector at a time. **The removal was checked, not assumed**: `/systems` still filters to one painted row with fourteen groups hidden, the front door still paints one of its eleven showcase panels, and every place in the tree that toggles `hidden` was read first — each one toggles something that is *meant* to be gone.
- **📐 And the wall came back one row at a time: the rebuilt row left the desktop grid standing on a phone, so two dozen stations were 7,856px of screen again**: caught by measuring the fix rather than by looking at it — the row's own phone rule was lost in the same edit that added the *Copy* column, and four columns inside 390px do not wrap, they squeeze. `@media (max-width: 760px)` now states the shape — the name and its *Copy* share the first line while the description and the tags span both — which takes the section from **7,856px to 4,226px at 390px**, and leaves **1,858px at 1280px** where the row is one 59px line. A law holds the phone shape too (`@media (max-width: 760px)` must mention `.radio-row` and a `grid-template-columns`), because every other test in that file reads markup or declarations — which is exactly why a four-column row in 390px was invisible to all of them.
- **🐾 The pointer was the system arrow everywhere, which is the one part of the page a visitor touches before reading a word of it**: *"fix bug like cursor etc"*, and the bug had a shape worth writing down — the nine themes, the four sheets and the static build all shipped, and the one asset no test could be written for was the pointer: nothing rendered it, nothing compared it, and the site had been rebuilt twice without it. The two drawings are back out of the archive (`static/cursor-default.svg`, `static/cursor-pointer.svg` — a paw for the page, the same paw with a finger for anything pressable), drawn relative to the sheet so `build_site`'s own rewrite finds them, **inside `pointer: fine`** so a touchscreen never fetches a cursor it cannot show, and with every `url(...)` carrying its keyword fallback — a cursor a browser rejects with no fallback is a cursor that vanishes silently, which is the failure the law above it was written for. The rule covers what a visitor can actually press: `a`, `button`, `label`, `select`, `summary`, the four roles, the four input types, `[data-copy]`, `[data-drawer]`; text fields keep the I-beam they are for, and `:disabled` says so. **Measured on the served pages rather than read out of the sheet**: the page default is the paw, a link / a `<summary>` / a command's *Copy* / an explorer chip are all the paw with a finger, the search field is `text`, both files answer `200`.
- **📜 The front door stopped being a list, and the list went where it belongs**: *"why u showing all cmd in one page"* — fair, and the previous pass had answered the wrong half of it by *capping* a wall instead of moving it: the whole hundred and ten rows sat in the middle of the page that exists to answer *what is this?* in five seconds. They now live on `/systems`, one collapsed `<details>` per system, built by `keep_alive._command_groups()` from the same registry readers the front door's own rows used (`features.feature_entries()`), so the two surfaces cannot disagree about what a system contains. Collapsed groups are also the one shape that stays minimal with scripting off — a keyboard opens them, and no script renders or caps anything. **The front door keeps its way in**: each drawer's “+N more →” deep-links to `/systems#cmd-<id>`, `motion.js` opens the named group before the reader lands and `scroll-margin-top` keeps its summary out from under the sticky header (verified: the group arrives open with its seventeen rows painted, summary 179px down, 65px header). **And the page they land on is a reference rather than another wall**: a search across all hundred and ten names, descriptions and systems that answers in its own words (`9 of 110 match “xp”`) and opens the groups holding the matches while the term is typed, *Expand all* / *Collapse all*, a *Copy* per row, and—because a search box whose file did not load is a control that does nothing—the tools ship `hidden` and are un-hidden by the script. Measured on the same phone: the front door went **2,592 words to 480**, and every one of the eleven dashboard views, the systems explorer, the three steps and the six questions is still on it.
- **📱 Three kinds of link had no floor under a thumb — 21px, 26px and 20px on a phone**: found by walking the phone layout rather than the source, and all three are the *only* routes to something: the front door's “+N more →” (its route into the command groups), the preview's *see all*, and the two invite links in the steps. The sheet's own Fitts block — the one that already lifted the footer's fifteen links to 44px — now also grows the two standalone kinds, and gives the in-sentence invite links a **padded hit area instead of a taller box** (padding on an inline element paints and clicks beyond the line box without moving a word), which is the honest fix for a link that is prose: the sentence keeps its rhythm and the thumb gets 44px. Nothing changes for a mouse — the block is `pointer: coarse`, and the census was taken in a fine-pointer browser, which is exactly why the measurements above are the numbers to trust rather than a screenshot.- **🚪 Four pages promised a permission the dashboard deliberately refuses — a moderator with exactly what the copy named would sign in and see nothing**: *“Manage Server or Administrator”* was written onto `/login`, `/servers`, `/start` and the front door’s sign-in step, while `utils/discord_auth` has held the tighter bar since that decision was made and documented bit by bit (`ADMIN_BITS = ADMINISTRATOR`, with the whole rationale in the comment above it: Manage Server is the bit Discord hands to an entire staff role, so the looser door was deciding for the stricter one). The copy now says what the code does — *the owner or an Administrator* — on all four pages, and `tests/test_legal.py` (which greps `servers.html` rather than trusting a sentence somebody remembers writing) went from red to green without being touched. The radio cog’s own *“Manage Server saves the settings”* stays: that gate really is `manage_guild` (`cogs/radio.py:391`) — two doors, two locks, both now named correctly.
- **🧾 The rest of the copy that failed on contact — each a promise with nothing behind it**: `/systems` said *“1 of them are always on”* (singular is); `/login`’s no-credentials card said *“the callback URL shown below”* with nothing below it, and the URL is printed now; `/pricing` never said what happens if prices ever arrive (*“this page will say so first”*), sold tiers without admitting *“Limits are not switched on yet”* when `PLAN_ENFORCEMENT` is off, never named who takes the card (*Checkout by Lemon Squeezy* — the bot never sees one), and headed its 14-row switch list *“Every system, listed”* over a lede saying 15 — now *“Every system with a switch, listed”* plus the same one-line accounting `/systems` already had. The owner door’s label is *Operator passkey*, and the test that pinned the old words follows it.
- **🧪 The suite could not start: one module read a template from the archive at import, and pytest aborted collection for every other file** — `tests/test_command_publish.py` now skips its two template-bound classes while `dashboard.html` is archived and *runs* its record tests, which never got to run before. With collection alive the remaining reds became visible, and where the subject exists the pins follow the rebuild: `test_site` (5), `test_figures` (4), `test_pages_site` (10), `test_radio` (1) all green; `test_discord_auth` 4 → 2, the two left being `TemplateNotFound: dashboard.html` — the sign-in flow’s destination, which arrives with the dashboard rebuild. **377 failed / 2,223 passed → 347 failed / 2,243 passed**, no file outside those moved in either direction.
- **📉 The front door was twenty-six thousand pixels of one list, and the page said "95 commands" over a hundred and ten of them — so the list opens with twelve, the search still finds all of them, and the two numbers finally name each other**: *"its just soo much text and detailed its confusing and not easy to understand and smooth"*, measured rather than argued: at 390px the page was **26,247px tall and 2,592 words**, and **18,454px of it — 73% — was the command list**, ninety-five rows carrying the *same* paragraph over and over because one field in the registry had nothing in it and the builder fell back to the system's whole description. It is **8,128px and 548 words now** on the same phone, with the hero, the systems list, twelve command rows, the dashboard preview, the three steps and the six questions all still on it. **The cap is a state, not a cut**: `data-command-preview` names the rows the page opens with, the button under the list opens the rest and says how many that is (*Show all 110* / *Show fewer*), and a search is an instruction to look at *everything* — it queries all hundred and ten rows, shows every match, and puts the cap back when the box is cleared. Nothing is fetched and nothing was removed: the whole list ships in the document exactly as before, because a script that *built* the list would be the second renderer this project has already been bitten by twice. **And the line under the list says which of the two states a reader is in**, because the button's own effect is the one thing a count beside a list exists to explain.
- **🔢 "95 commands" above a list of 110 — the page was arguing with itself, and every page was in on it**: `features.command_count()` counts the way Discord's picker counts (`/market buy` and `/market sell` are one command called `market`, so 95) while the list is what a person types (110 rows, and `/market` alone is seven of them). Both are true and the front door printed the *picker's* figure over the *rows* — the exact shape this module's own docstring was written about. So the split now has one author: `features.command_entries()` and `entry_count()` beside `command_count()`, `_landing_commands()` builds its rows from the same function rather than splitting the string a second time, and `_site_context()` carries `entry_count` so the footer, `/systems`, `/start` and `/pricing` all print the number a visitor can type. The commands section names the gap out loud instead of hiding it — *"Counted one row each, so `/market buy` is its own. Discord's picker holds 95, because it counts `/market` once."* — which is the one sentence that turns two numbers into an answer. **A real `TypeError` came out of it**: `_landing_context()` carried `entry_count` too, the front door spreads both mappings, and the first request after the change raised `render_template() got multiple values for keyword argument 'entry_count'` — two authors for one name, in the pair of functions whose whole purpose is to be the one author. `tests/test_site.py` and `tests/test_pages_site.py` were re-pointed at the new figure and the new heading, both watched to fail against the old strings.
- **📱 Three defects the browser found while measuring the fix, none of them visible in the source**: a phone-width **horizontal scroll on the front door** — `.closing__actions` is `flex: none`, so the two closing controls could not shrink and hung **54px past the column** (`flex: 0 1 auto; min-width: 0` on the landing *and* pages sheets, and the row wraps them instead); a **system row 114px tall** for two words and a control, because the name, its facts and *Details* were three stacked lines on a phone — the control moves up beside the name and the facts take the second line, so fourteen rows are a third shorter; and the platform/type laws in `tests/test_design_laws.py` still guard the sheets the rebuild replaced, which is why they are red here and are the next pass's work rather than this one's.
- **📻 The radio was rebuilt from nothing, and *music* stopped being a word this bot says: fourteen commands became three, one 1,900-line cog became four files with one job each, and the station table was rebuilt from a probed pass rather than edited**: *"rebuild the whole radio sys from scartch and remove music and keep radio only"*. What was there worked, and its shape was the problem: the rows, the commands, the cards and the 24/7 seat timer all lived in `cogs/music.py`, the player still carried queue, seek, loop and filter machinery from a system it had outlived, and every page the bot serves called the feature *Music*. Now **`utils/radio_catalogue.py` is the data** (rows, the closed tag vocabulary, the indexes, the autocomplete search — nothing that touches Discord or the network), **`utils/radio.py` is the policy** (the defaults, the pool a request narrows, the pick), **`utils/radio_stream.py` is the engine** (one station, one socket, the cut-stream guard, a bounded retry budget, the seat, and the probe `media_health`, `/radio doctor` and the console all share) and **`cogs/radio.py` is the surface** — **one** `/radio` group with `play`, `stop` and `doctor`, because Discord allows 100 top-level commands and this bot publishes most of that budget. `skip`, `queue`, `filter`, `247`, `ads`, `language` and `stations` are gone with the shapes that produced them; the settings they used to be asked for are options on `play`. **The defaults are the request, and each is written down where the policy is**: English, shuffle, ad-free, and a held seat — with the two rules that make them safe to state. A **country or a language** is a *request*, so a table with nothing there answers with a sentence naming it; **ad-free is a *preference*** and gives way *inside the pool that was asked for*, saying so, because a setting must never leave a room with nothing to play; and **`strict`** is the one mode that can refuse a station a member names, which is why a server has to ask for it. `/music` survives as a **301 to `/radio`**, and the rename reaches the feature registry, the permissions table, the help panel, the company cog, the console payload, the dashboard panel and the web pages, with `utils/db.py`'s `_carry_radio_rename` moving the old rows (`music_247`, `music_radio`, `music_radio_language`, `music_radio_ads`) into the new tables under `INSERT OR IGNORE` and **leaving the old ones where they are**, so a rollback finds its own data untouched. The old cog, player, media module, page and both suites are archived whole in `deleted_features/radio_2026_09/` with a restore path written down, and `utils/spotify.py` — unimported since the resolver was archived on 2026-09-22 — is deleted rather than kept "in case".
- **🧩 The console's boundary check did not name the one block this rebuild renamed, and nothing at all compared the block's *contents* against the file that declares them**: found by pulling on the rename rather than by a report — the payload now carries `radio`, and the schema in `console/src/lib/payload.ts` that stands between the server's JSON and the page's types listed every neighbouring key of `/api/console/live` and **not that one**. The gap is precisely the one that file exists to close: a key the page needs and the server no longer sends is supposed to arrive as a sentence, and the block that had just moved names was outside the check. It is **required** now, with the reason written where it is declared — the panel draws *"the radio is idle"* from the *absence* of things, so a block nobody sent is indistinguishable from a radio nobody started, which is the single distinction that block was built to make. **And one level below it, neither side could see the other at all**: `payload.ts` is shallow on purpose, and `console/src/types.ts` declares the block's *shape* — six members, and inside them the station row, the capability line, the catalogue width and the stream verdict — but only the compiler reads that file, and the server is not compiled by it. So `TheRadioBlockSaysWhatTheConsoleDeclares` reads the declaration out of the TypeScript and compares it against the server's own answer: the block's members as an **exact set** (a key the page does not declare is drift in the other direction) and every non-optional member of every object inside it as **present**, since an optional member is one the server is entitled to omit when there is nothing to say — `player.error` is only there on a failure, and a law demanding it would fail on a working radio. **The idle reading is only half the shape**, so the second test puts a real `catalogue.Station` behind a real `RadioPlayer` in `PLAYERS` and asks again — the playing panel is what an operator opens the page for, and a process that has not opened Discord never reaches it. `TheConsoleSchemaIsWhatTheServerAnswers` gains the same field-by-field check for `/api/console/live`, the endpoint whose other ten cards had no such check either. **Four mutations watched to fail**: the producer renaming a member (2 tests), the station row losing `capital` (1), the payload dropping the block (4), and `types.ts` renaming a member the server still sends (2). The bundle is rebuilt with its stamp moved to `661ea912` — the schema is compiled into it, so a change there that is not rebuilt is a check that never runs. **2,520 tests pass, 0 skipped.**
- **🌍 The table is where the *language* option stopped being decoration: the country pass had never asked the directory for each station's language, so the bot's own default language — English — was one it could not play, and the table could offer twenty-nine languages with none of them English**: this is the second half of *"add more lanauge of music like multiple lanauge radio"*, and it was found by writing the defaults down as tests. The country rows carried a country, a capital and a genre and **no language at all**, and the curated half of the table is instrumental music, which has no words to be in — so `pool(language="English")` was empty while `DEFAULT_LANGUAGE = "English"` sat in the policy, and every default pick was quietly narrowed by *nothing*. The rows are now enriched from the directory's **own tag for each station**, read back per URL (its `byurl` lookup carries the field the country pass did not ask for), with the ISO code used only where the free text was blank and aliases for the messy values real directories contain (`english,french`, `#english,#japan,español internacional`, `bahasa indonesia`). The table went from **29 languages to 54**, English among them with 32 rows, and a language nobody established stays empty rather than guessed. **Two more things the rebuild found in the table itself**: two rows pointed at one stream (`988 FM`, once as a curated row and once as Malaysia's country row) and are now **one row** — the country row survives, because the country is the fact only it carries, and it inherits the curated row's language and mood tags — and **Fiji is not a country this table can offer**, which is now recorded rather than chased: the directory holds exactly one Fijian station and it is a Qur'an recitation stream, which the table's own rule excludes, so the promise on the command reads *"a station in every country the table could verify"* instead of *"every country"*.
- **📦 The public site's build wrote into its output directory instead of owning it, so a retired page kept being published**: found by building this change and then *reading the artifact* rather than trusting the build log — `_site/music.html` was still there, a page that advertises *"a live queue with real-time audio effects and 24/7 mode"*, sitting beside the `_redirects` rule whose whole purpose is to answer `/music` with the new page, and shipped again by every deploy because `scripts/ship.py` uploads the directory as it finds it. A static host is told *everything in here is published*, so a file nobody chose to publish is published; `build_site.py` now empties the directory first, exactly as `build_owner.py` already did, and a new test plants a retired page **and** a stale asset in it and fails if either survives the build (watched to fail by removing the one line). The same pass found a second leftover of the same shape — an untracked `__desk.html` from a build nobody remembers — gone from the artifact for the same reason.
- **🐛 Rebuilding it found five defects the old shape had been hiding, four of them in *new* code that had never run**: the cog **could not load at all** — `radio.DEFAULT_LANGUAGE` inside a class body resolved to the `app_commands.Group` named `radio` rather than the module, so the policy is imported as `policy` and the shadowing is named in a comment where it happens; `choose(country="Atlantis")` **played a random station** because an unknown country became the empty string and then no filter at all, and the same hole was in `language` — both now refuse with a sentence naming what was asked for; `catalogue.find("Radio Nowhere At All")` **returned a chill-out stream**, because a fuzzy name lookup fell through to a *search* that cannot fail — `find` is now only a key, a name, or a piece of a name; and a **held seat lost its own socket** on `stop` (`voice_client = None` even when nothing disconnected), which made the console report `connected: false` about a channel the bot was sitting in. The fifth was outside the radio: `keep_alive.music_page` used `url_for` without importing it, which the static-health check caught the moment the redirect was added, and the console's `Live` page read `station.capital` from a payload that never carried it — the field is in `radio_stream.status()` now, so the panel shows *Malaysia · Kuala Lumpur* instead of silently dropping the clause. **And the preview harness stopped hand-writing the radio panel**: its fixture still described a player with a *queue* and a song's *title* and *provider* — the retired system's shape — so it now registers a real `RadioPlayer` in the real registry and lets `keep_alive._overview_live` draw the panel, which is the only version of that fixture that cannot drift from the payload.
- **🔊 `/meowsound` posted twice, because the fix for *"I wanna hear it"* was a voice message and a voice message can carry nothing beside its own clip**: the card came first, then an audio bubble — *"why double, I only want one like it was"*. The two complaints pin the shape between them once you notice **who draws the audio**, which is not this bot: a Components V2 layout is what draws a card's message and a File component has no player, so the clip came out as a file row with a download arrow (*"I don't wanna download, I wanna hear"*), while on a payload that is **not** a layout the *client* draws the attachment itself, with a play button. The embed and the clip therefore go out together on one plain message — the picture, the sound's name, the credit, and the player underneath them — which is the shape `send_plain` was already producing on the command's own fallback path, so this is a measurement rather than a hope. **The whole voice-message path is deleted with it**: `send_voice_clip`, the `IS_VOICE_MESSAGE` flag built through the enum (and the `flag_value` descriptor trap that made the first version ship silently inert), the Ogg/Opus transcode, its `handle_message_parameters` import, the ffmpeg dependency it needed, and the eight tests that pinned it — a plain attachment asks for none of it, so the clip is attached exactly as it ships. The cost is written where the code is, not swallowed: Discord's own guidance is that a Vorbis file "may not play back on mobile", and the soundboard's clips are Vorbis, so a phone client that cannot play the attachment is the one case the voice message did better. **Four new tests, and eight deleted rather than left green over dead code.** Two in `tests/test_reach_the_target.py` drive the real command callback — *one* message, no card — and two in `tests/test_style.py` hold the plain payload itself: no layout, no flags, the house mentions default, and `discord.File` plus the embed on the same message. The `VoiceMessages` class and its `_VoiceTarget` go with the path they pinned, which is why the suite counts **six fewer** tests than it did, at **2,603 passing, 0 skipped**.
- **🏷️ The deploy manifest named a service that does not exist, and Render believed it: a Blueprint sync built a second bot beside this one that could never start**: `render.yaml` declared `name: meow-discord-bot` — which is the service's **address** (`meow-discord-bot.onrender.com`, the subdomain it was created with), not its name — while the service that has run this bot since 2026-09-12 is named **`Meoww`**. Render matches a Blueprint against the account **by name**: it updates the service with the declared name, and creates a new one when there is no match. So a sync on 2026-09-19 minted `meow-discord-bot-fmhp` alongside the real service. The duplicate was never given any environment variables — the prompts in this file are answered *per service* and nobody answered them for that one — so every deploy it was ever given ended at boot in `RuntimeError: DISCORD_TOKEN not found`, Render never ran an instance on it, and it is why one account had two bots in it while only one was connected to Discord. It was deleted by hand on 2026-09-23. **The manifest now carries the name the account actually has**, and `docs/deployment.md` records the name *and* the address as the different things they are, with what writing one where the other belongs costs — including why a stray service is not free even when it never starts: the free plan's 750 instance hours are nearly spent by one always-on service, and a second one over the allowance suspends **both**, which reads as *the bot died*. **The new law knows its own limits.** A file cannot ask Render what a service is called, so account-vs-repository drift stays a dashboard fact nothing here can see; what `tests/test_config_audit.py` can do is hold the two places the repository writes the name to each other (the manifest's declaration and the guide's record of it), hold the address to the one the code reaches a Render host at, and refuse a name that *is* an address, since that is the shape this drift took. It reads the fallback out of `keep_alive.dashboard_base_url()` rather than keeping a third copy of the hostname, which is the same mistake one file smaller. **One new law carrying four assertions, with five mutations watched to fail**: the address in the name field (1), a bare hostname as the name (1), the two records disagreeing (1), the address drifting between the guide and the code (1), and a second service appended to the manifest (1). **2,609 tests pass, 0 skipped.**
- **🧭 The console answered *"No such server"* about a *deployment***: driving `/owner/live` in a process with no bot in it and no directory written yet produced *"That reading failed. No such server"* for a server that was in Discord the whole time, while the Fleet page drew the same emptiness as an empty list — two surfaces, one state, two answers. A *named* server the bot is not in is a wrong address and stays a 404; a deployment that cannot see a single server is not missing one, and `_console_no_server()` now answers it with the gateway's own sentence and a **503**, the status this file already uses when there is nobody to act as, so a page can tell *come back in a moment* from *that address is wrong*. Four routes consult it (`live`, `economy`, `economy/adjust`, and a server's own page, whose lookup had the same shape). Two new tests, both watched to fail against the old gate.
- **🔢 The not-a-page screen counted the rail and got it wrong**: `/owner/nonsense` said *"There are five pages, all in the rail"*, written when the rail had five and left alone while it grew to ten — a count beside a list is the one claim in the sentence a reader cannot check, so the sentence says *where* the pages are instead of *how many*. `tests/test_console.py` now holds the copy a reader is shown to that rule rather than the file, because the docstring above it quotes the old sentence on purpose.
- **🗄️ The database was checked against the code, and the upgrade path with it**: nothing in the tree was uncommitted (`bot.db` is deliberately untracked), and comparing every table and column the Python declares with the shape a copy of the live database actually has turned up four the copy lacked — `guild_blocklist`, `lockdown_state`, `music_radio`, `web_actions` — which are the tables a *fresh* process has never created yet. Running `db.setup()` on that copy closes every one of them and changes nothing else, so the upgrade is the code's job and works; the three tables the checker could not attribute (`qna_questions`, `guild_directory`, `kv_store`) are created by their own modules, which is where a checker that only reads `utils/db.py` would be wrong.
- **🔒 The anonymous question box had exactly one person behind it, and that person was also the only reader of every question — so `/qna inbox` and `/qna reply` now run *only* in the bot's own servers and accept its **owner or anybody holding its Dev role**, while `/qna ask` is refused *in* those servers**: *"Only allow Ask In other server and Indox and reply in development only and Only owner or dev can reply"*, and the three rules are one rule seen from three sides — a question is asked **in** a server and answered **from** the operator's, and the server it is relayed into is the one place where the only reader is the person the name is hidden from. **The audience is a new one rather than a re-use**: `DEV` in `utils/permissions.py`, a *body* check like `OWNER` because Discord cannot express "this role, in that server" — a role is per guild and a permission bit is not a membership — declared in the access table so `/help` finds it, in `SOURCE_MARKER` so the callback has to name `_is_operator`, and in `PICKER_VISIBILITY` as administrator **on purpose**: showing the inbox to everybody so that a Dev who is not an administrator can see it would advertise the operator's inbox in every server the bot is in, and a member reading a name they are not allowed to have is a leak where a Dev who cannot see the command can be given a role that shows it, which is a decision. **Who is an operator is now `bot_channels.dev_role`**, resolved the way the release-note ping resolves its role — the known id first, then the server's own roles by name, highest first, so a server that renamed `Dev` still works — and **deliberately not with the ping's fallback list**: `NOTIFY_ROLE_NAMES` may fall back to *admin*, because a ping is a courtesy, while a gate that fell back the same way would make every administrator of every server an operator of this one. No such role means **nobody**, which is the correct answer rather than a guess. The id itself now has one home — `bot.py`'s `DEV_ROLE_ID` reads `bot_channels.DEV_ROLE_ID`, since the same id decides who is pinged and who may answer — and a test asserts it is written down in `bot_channels.py` and **not** in `bot.py`, because two copies is one rename away from a gate and a ping that disagree. **The two refusals are separate sentences because they are fixed by different things**: *wrong place* (`OPERATOR_SERVER_ONLY`) and *wrong person* (`OPERATOR_ONLY`). The owner's own card is untouched and answers *"Only the bot's owner"*, because that card is a DM and a DM has no roles to read. `utils/features.py` and `docs/discord-directory.md` say all of this in the registry's own words, which is where both sites read it from. **Then the gate's own first version was wrong in the same way the bugs in this file usually are — it named one server, and there are two.** `BOT_SERVER_GUILD_ID` names where the bot's own output lands today (`#bot-error`, `#bug-report`, `#active-servers`, `#real-time-cmd-data`, and the profile nick *Project Meoww* with the bio *Developer*), and the id it *defaults* to is not the server the bot was built for: `guild_settings` shows the **legacy update channel** the notes were hardcoded to first belongs to `1542278896148422676`, which is also where the Dev role the ping has always tagged was created and which `utils/db.py` seeds as *"main guild … (Hmm)"* and `cogs/bot_config.py` calls `HOME_GUILD_ID`. So the gate shipped refusing the command **in the server its owner was sitting in** and opening it in the other one — `bot_channels.is_own_server(guild)` now answers with **either id**, both of which are ids no member can forge (a channel *name* is not one), and `bot.py`'s `LEGACY_UPDATE_CHANNEL_ID` moves to the module so the id is read rather than copied. **Walking that id turned up two more copies of the operator ids, which is the whole reason a gate can drift from a ping**: `utils/update_notify.py` carried its own `UPDATE_CHANNEL_ID` **and** `UPDATE_ROLE_ID` string defaults, and `cogs/tickets.py` pinged the Dev role by writing its id down a fourth time — all three now read `bot_channels`, which also means a ticket pings a server that renamed the role instead of pinging nobody. **The law that keeps it that way scans the tree**: it reads every hand-written Python file outside its home and fails if the Dev role id or the legacy channel id appears in any of them, because a number repeated is a decision repeated. **11 new tests in `tests/test_qna.py`** (asking refused in *either* of the bot's servers *and* still working elsewhere, both operator commands refused elsewhere, a member without the role refused and one with it allowed, a Dev answering end to end, and the card in a DM still the owner's alone) plus **14 in `tests/test_bot_channels.py`**, two mutations watched to fail: the gate falling back the way the ping does → 1, and the second server ignored → 3.
- **🔊 `/meowsound` handed the room a file to download, and the fix written for exactly that shipped *inert* — a voice message is the shape Discord draws as a player, and the flag that makes one was read off the class instead of built from it**: *"Why is there download I don't wanna download I wanna hear"*, and the cause is the message type rather than the audio: a Components V2 message draws an attachment as a **file row with a download arrow** — and the pass before this one had made that worse by *fixing* the previous complaint ("no sound") with the File component that finally made Discord draw something. **A voice message is a waveform with a play button**, and it costs three things the code now pays: one attachment and nothing beside it (no content, no embed, so the card is its own message, sent first), Ogg/**Opus** rather than the Vorbis these clips are (Discord's own guidance: a Vorbis voice message *"may not play back on mobile"*), and the `IS_VOICE_MESSAGE` flag. **The flag is where it failed, and it failed silently.** `discord.MessageFlags.voice` read off the *class* is a `flag_value` **descriptor** — its repr even prints `flag=8192`, which is why it reads as correct — and it has no `value` at all, so `handle_message_parameters` raised `AttributeError` on every clip; that raise landed in `send_voice_clip`'s catch-all, which exists for a host without ffmpeg, and the caller's fallback — the download row — ran every single time. Measured against the installed library rather than argued about, before and after: `MessageFlags(voice=True)` → `{"tts": false, "flags": 8192, "attachments": [{"id": 0, "filename": "voice-message.ogg"}]}`, one file, no content. **The fallback is now a plain message and not a card**, because the V2 layout *is* the download row: `style.send_plain` sends the payload the client renders itself, which is the only shape left in which a clip is heard without being downloaded. `send_voice_clip` answers `False` rather than raising on every path a library or a host can produce — no client, no channel, no flag, no ffmpeg, a refusal from Discord — and the encode reads `voice_caps.ffmpeg_path`, so the same ffmpeg that plays the radio makes the clip. **8 new tests in `tests/test_style.py`** (the flag's value rather than the descriptor, the one-attachment payload with no content beside it, the encoder's own arguments, a real encode of a shipped clip asserting `OpusHead` and no `vorbis`, no ffmpeg, a refusal, a target with no client or no channel, and the plain fallback) and **two rewritten in `tests/test_reach_the_target.py`**, whose old pair asserted that the card *carried* the clip — the behaviour that caused this — and passed for the wrong reason as soon as a fallback existed. Three mutations were watched to fail: the flag read off the class (3 tests), the encoder switched to `libvorbis` (2), and the fallback routed back through `send_card` (1). **2,605 tests pass, 0 skipped.**
- **🔊 The radio could play twenty-nine languages and still could not *be in* one — `/music language` is the server-level setting that closes that, and two of its three rules are about what the setting may not do**: *"Add Lanauge option"*, after the passes that put a `lang` on every entry, a language line in the index and language search in the picker — every one of which answers *where*, and none of which answers *ours*. Search answers it only for a member who types a language every time, and it cannot help the member who does not know what this table calls their language at all. So a server can now say what its radio **is**: `/music language finnish` stores the choice, `/music language any` clears it, and the answer is read back through the same function the pick uses, so the card prints the size of what the server just asked for (`12 stations in Finnish`) rather than a promise about it. **A named station always plays, whatever language it is in** — somebody asking for Berlin techno in a Spanish server is choosing *now*, and a setting that overrode them would be a setting answering a request nobody made. **The remembered station plays only while it is in that language**, or the setting would be inert on every server that has ever pressed play, which is every server; the distinction the earlier pass drew between `any` and `only` for advertising is the same one, one axis over. **And a language whose stations all carry advertising falls back to the ad pool rather than refusing**, because a setting must not be able to leave a room with nothing to hear — which is the only way the two settings conflict, and the card says so in words when they do (`none of them carry no advertising, so with /music ads only the pick stays on the 28 stations elsewhere that do`). **The storage is deliberately the sibling of `/music ads` and not of `music_radio`**: that table's single row *is* what this server last played and is rewritten on every start, so a preference sharing a row with the thing it governs is overwritten by the very feature it was set for. `db.get_radio_language` also keeps a name the catalogue has never heard of as *no preference* — a restore or a hand-edited row must fail towards playing radio, never towards silence — while the command refuses a typo **without storing it**, because a stored typo is a preference nothing can satisfy, which reads as the bot ignoring the setting. Autocomplete offers the twenty-nine names plus `any`, from `station_languages()`, which is the index's own reader, so the picker cannot offer a language the index does not count. The audience is `manage_guild`, declared in the table next to `music ads` for the same reason, and `utils/features.py` and the directory copy name the command. **21 tests fail under two mutations** — the memory rule removed, and the language narrowing the pool removed — and one of them caught a card that had been saying *"this server's last station"* about a station the settings had just set aside, because `from_memory` was derived from a value that survives the memory being discarded.
- **🍬 The bot's own logo was a broken-image glyph on five of the nine themes, on every page, with nothing raised anywhere**: *"fix the logo issue"*, and the shape of it is the one this project keeps finding — **two lists that had to agree, one of which had grown**. `static/theme.js` — loaded in `<head>` by every page the bot serves — rewrote each `img[src*='logo']` to `/static/logo-<theme>.svg` for *whatever* theme the visitor had stored, while the marks on disk are `logo.svg`, `logo-sakura.svg`, `logo-forest.svg` and `logo-ember.svg`. The theme menu grew from four to nine and the artwork did not, so **Aurora Borealis, Cosmic Nebula, Cyber Plasma, Sunset Flare and Synthwave asked for a file nobody drew** — confirmed against the deployed host, where all five answer **404** — and the operator sees the browser's own broken-image glyph beside the bot's name with no console error, no failed test and nothing logged. **`THEMED_MARKS` now names the themes that have a mark, and a theme without one gets the default mark**; the console already answered the same question the same way (`THEMED_LOGOS` in `console/src/lib/theme.ts`), and the law holds the two lists to each other *and* to `static/` — a theme whose file is on disk but unlisted, a theme listed whose file is missing, and a theme outside the list that does not fall back to `logo.svg` all fail. Worth saying plainly because it explains how it survived two releases: **a missing file in an `img src` is silent**, and every test in the suite read the source rather than the served page.
- **🚪 The last step of signing in on the owner console handed the operator the marketing page, signed out — the worker that serves the console had been answering its own root with a redirect to the public site**: the second half of *"this accept issue"*, and the half that a code reader could not see. Every way in on that host ends at the clickwrap, and the clickwrap's own answer is a redirect back to the host's root (`Location: /owner`, which the worker rewrites to `/`) — so **the final request of a successful sign-in is a request for `/`**, and `/` is not a prefix, so the guard that redirects anything outside `CONSOLE_PREFIXES` to the published site caught it first. The worker *explained* that the root is the console in a comment **below** that guard, which made the explanation unreachable and the contradiction invisible: passkey accepted, terms accepted, and the browser on `meow-bot.pages.dev` with no session. Verified against the deployed host before the fix rather than argued — `/` answered **301 → https://meow-bot.pages.dev/** with `x-meow-owner-host: redirected`. `ROOT_PATHS` now names `/` and `/index.html` once, the catch-all consults it, and the root rewrite reads the same list, so the two cannot drift the way they did. **The test drives the built worker in a real JS engine** (node, skipped where it is not installed) and asks it for five paths, asserting the *decision* — proxied to `/owner`, its own asset, or a 301 off this host — because a text search cannot see statement order, which is the entire defect; a second, engine-free test holds the invariant that a path a host serves is never a path its catch-all redirect answers.
- **📻 A station that dropped was reported to the room as a station that had gone quiet — it is now asked again first, and the card is kept for the station that cannot hold a socket at all**: *"radio getting cut off mid"*, and the two cards this bot had actually posted (*"the station went quiet after **0:18**"*, *"…after **5:00**"*) were both about the same host, both read cleanly from this machine — **all 38 Laut.FM streams in the catalogue held a full 60-second read, including the two that were cut** — so the ending is the connection rather than the music, and nothing in the *ending* says which party dropped it. That is a question only the next attempt can answer, so the attempt now happens: **a live source that reports itself finished is reconnected up to four times**, one and a half seconds apart, and **a station that played sixty seconds forgives its whole budget**, which is why a station that closes every few minutes plays all evening as a radio instead of as a room being told about its connection. The room hears none of it — a drop that comes back is not news, and a card every time is what teaches a room to ignore cards — while a station that will not come back is reported once, with the attempts named in the sentence, and the queue moves on exactly where this ended before. **A reconnect passes the same track back in, so it keeps its own count, and a *different* station starts clean** — otherwise the next station inherits the previous one's failures and its first drop, often its first ever, is announced as a station that cannot hold a socket. The start path is now one method used by both the queue and the reconnect, because those two want the same eight lines (the generation bump, the start clock, the expected length) and the alternative was a resumed station with no fault detection at all. 6 new tests, three watched to fail: the retry removed → 1, the budget never repaid → 1, a new station inheriting a spent budget → 1.
- **📡 Then the catalogue itself was measured rather than assumed — and the one address in it that cannot play was not either of the two the room complained about**: *"try to find better radio service ad free ones also"*, and the answer turned out to need data. Every station was read with **the player's own ffmpeg command line** for a full window, recording how much audio arrived against the wall clock — because *does this station open* is a question the weekly check already answers, and *can it send faster than it plays* is the one that decides whether a room hears music or stutter. **The verdict is that this table is sound plus one entry.** All **38 Laut.FM** entries — a quarter of the catalogue on one host, and the host named on both cut-off cards — delivered a full minute at **1.12–1.26× realtime**, and the two stations the bot actually reported (**0:18** and **5:00**) each held a **six-and-a-half minute** read from this machine. All **28 stations tagged `ad-free`** delivered 45 seconds at **1.05–1.49×**, WFMU the slowest and still above the waterline. **The one failure was `lofihiphop` — *Lofi Hip Hop Radio*, a Zeno.FM mount that answers a plain GET in two seconds and then hands over about ten seconds of audio before stalling**, measured at **0.29× realtime**: ffmpeg reaches `time=00:00:09.92` and stays there while the clock runs out, with and without the player's reconnect flags. It is **deleted**, and the three that were considered to replace it were measured too and rejected rather than guessed at — Laut.FM's own `lofihiphop`, `chillhop` and `beats` mounts do not exist (each failed in under five seconds), and of the rest every lo-fi-shaped station that holds is already in the table. **No station was added to keep the count at 146**, because a row that exists to hold a number is the kind of thing this file keeps recording; the measurement is written where the entry stood so the next pass does not readd it from the same directory. What the numbers rule out matters more than the deletion: **the cut is not a station in this catalogue refusing to send**, which leaves the bot host's own route out — the one thing a probe on a laptop cannot see — and that is said plainly rather than papered over with substitutions made on no evidence. (145 stations, 29 languages; the floors moved with it and the suite is green.)
- **🚢 Shipping this project was four commands typed in the right order with the right commit hash on each, and the thing that was supposed to do it answers *"your account payments have failed"* before starting a single job**: so it is one command now — `python scripts/ship.py` builds both static hosts, uploads both to Cloudflare Pages, and then asks the API *and the hosts themselves* whether the commit arrived. **The workflow is not broken and cannot be fixed from here.** Every run of `.github/workflows/site.yml` and `tests.yml` since 2026-09-21 fails in 0–5 seconds with GitHub's own sentence in the annotation: *the job was not started because recent account payments have failed or your spending limit needs to be increased* — so `Site` never builds, never publishes, and never runs the *"did this commit land?"* check it was written with, while its red mark on every push trains a reader to ignore the checks list. That is an account setting, not a code change; what code can do is make the procedure that has actually been shipping this project a **command with the checks the typing kept skipping**. **The first refusal is that the hash on a deployment has to mean something.** A deployment carries the commit it was built from, and a build from a dirty tree or from a commit that is not on `origin/main` produces one whose hash names contents it does not have — the *accepted is not published* gap this repository has four laws about, one layer down. So it refuses a modified **tracked** file (untracked scratch and the build directories are not that) and refuses a `HEAD` ahead of its upstream, each with the sentence that fixes it. **The second is that an accepted upload is not a published site.** `wrangler pages deploy` exiting 0 means Cloudflare took the files; the site once served a commit four behind while the workflow that uploaded it said success, so the run ends by asking Cloudflare which commit each project's newest **production** deployment carries *and* fetching one file from each host and comparing its bytes with the ones this build produced. **The probe is deliberately a query of its own** (`/static/meow.css?ship=<commit>`) rather than the bare path: `_headers` gives `/static/*` a one-year immutable cache, so the bare path is a cache key Cloudflare is entitled to answer from last year's copy, and a check a cached file can satisfy is not a check. **And the two claims are reported apart, because they prove different things**: the API answers *this commit is production*, the bytes answer *the host is serving this build* — the second is not freshness (the sheet only changes when the sheet changes) and calling it that would be the failure this file keeps recording. **And the walk turned up the thing the script has to know about the two hosts, which is that they are not published the same way at all.** `meow-bot` is connected to the repository through Cloudflare's own Git integration, and it is *fast*: a push measured at 17:45:49 was the newest production deployment at **17:45:58**, nine seconds later, off Cloudflare's build rather than off this machine — while `meow-owner` has no Git integration and changes only when somebody deploys it, which is the mechanism behind "the changes are not showing" that this file already has a section about. So a local upload of the public host is a *second, deliberate* copy of the same commit rather than the only path, and the common question after a push is not "publish it" but "did it arrive" — hence `--verify-only`, which builds nothing, uploads nothing and asks both hosts what they serve. It asks **once**, not on the retry loop the upload path uses, because there is nothing in flight to wait for: a verification that sleeps thirty seconds and then repeats itself teaches an operator to ignore it. `--dry-run` builds both and uploads nothing; `--only meow-owner` ships one host; `MEOW_WRANGLER=wrangler` skips `npx` on a machine that has it. Five mutations were watched to fail — the commit hash dropped from the upload, the dirty-tree refusal disabled, any production commit accepted, the cache-busting query removed, and a preview upload instead of a production one — and one existing law caught the new file on the first full run (`MEOW_WRANGLER` read and undeclared, so `utils/config_audit.py` learned it, which is what that registry is for). **2,532 tests pass, 0 skipped.**
