A channel's bitrate is where Discord's own client should encode and it is **64 kbps on an ordinary server**, while every audio-only Opus song is ~130 kbps, so the test failed for every song and re-encoded all of them down to 64. Sending the provider's own packets costs nothing and cannot sound worse than re-encoding them, so the clause is gone. **The encoder was tuned for a phone call, because the library it comes from is a voice library.** `discord.py` writes `-fec true -packet_loss 15` into every `FFmpegOpusAudio` command line and never sets `-application`, and the in-band redundancy it is asking for is carried by libopus's **speech** path. Measured against a musical signal at 96 kbps: `-application audio` **−43.83 dB**, adding discord.py's FEC **−39.53 dB**, and `-application voip` **−36.97 dB** — about **7 dB** of avoidable loss, of which turning FEC off removed the whole 4.3 dB share even with `-packet_loss 15` still present. `utils/player.py` now appends a `MUSIC_ENCODER` (`-application audio -frame_duration 20 -vbr on -compression_level 10 -fec false -packet_loss 0`) *after* discord.py's flags, where ffmpeg's last-occurrence-wins rule makes them the ones that take effect. **And the encoder was told to aim at the channel rather than at the source.** `encode_bitrate()` now takes the source's own rate as the target, never under what the channel asks for, never past 256 kbps — a 160 kbps recording is no longer halved before libopus has done anything else, because encoding above the source cannot restore what is gone and encoding below it throws away what is not. End to end on the exact command line, against a 160 kbps source on a 64 kbps channel: the old path error was **−42.95 dB**, the new one **−51.83 dB**. **Two further defects came out of the same reading.** A second `-af` flag does not chain with the first, it *replaces* it — so a filter chosen with `/music filter` vanished in silence whenever the volume was not at 100%, and the filtergraph is now built as one comma-joined chain; and the engine line on the now-playing card names the bitrate it settled on (`pass-through (131 kbps, untouched)` / `ffmpeg encode [Normal (studio clean)] 160 kbps`), because "why does this sound bad" should be answerable from Discord and not from a container log. **Separately, a right-sounding song that was the wrong recording:** with YouTube refusing this host the second provider becomes the first one asked, and SoundCloud's answer for the studio single was *`we can't be friends (wait for your love) [Acoustic Version]`* — so an **arrangement** now counts as a different recording in the scorer's disqualifying markers, alongside `cover` and `remix`, word-bounded and skipped when the query asks for that flavour, so *"Ariana Grande acoustic"* still finds one. Worth being plain about what this does and does not buy: only YouTube serves Opus, so the pass-through half helps exactly when YouTube can be reached, and on a host YouTube refuses the whole gain is the second and third fixes — not discarding a source's bitrate, and not padding it with speech-path redundancy.
- **📣 The events that cannot be posted to a channel now arrive anyway — a server added, a join wave, and a flood against the site each go out over a webhook that does not need the gateway, silently, and the fields that leave are the ones the policy publishes**: *"connect web to <webhook> so when someone comes or add bot it sends a silent msg in that channel so we are ready for anytype of ddos or anything else. Basic Details like OS Ip Etc… everything should be under tos and privacy policy and legal docs in legal words"*. The three events worth waking somebody for are exactly the three a channel inside a server **cannot** receive: a server that has *just* added the bot is in none of that server's channels, a down gateway is the reason the web process is answering at all, and a request flood arrives at the web surface, which has no gateway to begin with. So there is a second channel — `ALERT_WEBHOOK_URL` — and it is deliberately the plainest transport here: one HTTP POST, on a daemon thread, from a module with no dependency but `urllib`. **Four properties, each because the alternative is worse.** It is **silent** (Discord's suppress-notifications flag), so the channel can be watched without paging anybody who is asleep. It is **bounded** — one message per kind per 30 seconds, 120 per process — because a flood is precisely where an unbounded notifier is worst: the thing watching the flood must not be the amplifier. It **never raises and never blocks**, so a slow receiver cannot hold a page open. And it is **off until configured and says so**: an unset URL is a supported state, and the Status page reports it as a *missing setting* rather than as a quiet success, which is the version of a disabled feature that costs somebody a night. **The disclosure is the feature's specification, not a paragraph beside it.** What travels is a closed list — the event kind; the requesting address and the client's own user-agent string, which is what names an operating system; the path and the coarse country; the counts that make an event legible; and a server's name and id — published in **`PRIVACY.md` §5** and **`TERMS.md` §12** in the documents' own register (lawful basis: legitimate interest in the integrity and availability of the service; limited to what identifying and repelling the request requires; not profiled, not sold, not shared for advertising, not used for training), and **asserted as an exact key set by `tests/test_alert_webhook.py`** so a new field is a failing test rather than a silent policy change. The join alert names the inviter and **how old that account is** — a hundred invites from accounts minutes old is what a raid looks like, and it is the only signal that separates an invitation from one. Proven live against the operator's own channel before it was committed to.
- **🚪 Music no longer dies when a provider refuses the host — because the search now has a third door that asks for no credential at all, and the card that used to say *"a provider refused"* now names the platform that did**: *"Most Important Still not working to a perma fix"*, over a `/play` of a Spotify link ending in *"Music: the provider refused this host"*. Two providers is a choice between two doors that are both shut on a datacenter address — YouTube answers one with a bot check, SoundCloud with a 403 — so an operator who has configured neither cookies nor a PO token had nowhere left to go, and the previous attempt (a client ladder) could only help a host YouTube still tolerates. **The answer is a provider that never needed tolerating**: [Audius](https://audius.co)'s open API hands over a **direct audio URL** for a catalogue track and asks for no account, no cookie and no player client — no bot check to pass, no signature to decipher — so a refused host now plays the song instead of explaining itself, verified end to end with YouTube *and* SoundCloud forced to refuse. It is third rather than first because its catalogue is narrower for a mainstream request, and being third means it is reached exactly when the other two fail. **The diagnosis was wrong before the fix**, and separately from it: the chain raised *"the last failure"*, while `source_order` deliberately moves a refusing provider to the back — so a SoundCloud refusal was reported as YouTube refusing a platform that had barely been asked, and *that false sentence is what both the member's card and the operator's alert were built from*. A refusal now outranks a provider that simply found nothing, and it is raised **named**. **The client ladder had gone stale in silence**: of the thirteen clients yt-dlp 2026.08 knows, its second rung did not exist — `tv_embedded` had been removed upstream, so the rung had become a copy of the first, spending a full extraction on every already-failing search to learn what the first attempt had just said, and nothing anywhere reported it. The ladder is now filtered against the extractor's own client table, so the next rename costs nothing instead of costing speed on exactly the hosts the ladder exists for. `/music doctor` and the extractor panel gained **the doors that are left and the order they will be knocked on** — the reading that stops "the provider refused this host" from being the whole story — and the operator's remedy now says which credential is short and which platform needs it.
- **🫂 The card that arrives uninvited stopped naming a command its reader cannot run, and the Overview stopped being four numbers and a great deal of space**: *"why u showing cmd to use in multiple cmd to normal user? they can't even use"* and *"Add More Features Its Kinda Empty"*, over the companion's own greeting and the dashboard's Overview. **The card was the real defect**: `/company` is declared with the **Manage Server** audience, so Discord hides it from the picker of nearly everybody the card lands in front of — a footer carrying `/company mode:Off` was therefore a card that read as broken to the only person it addressed. It now says *who can act* and names no syntax at all, and the rule is asserted as a rule rather than a phrasing: **no slash token, no option name, anywhere in the card**. The copy warmed with it, in the bot's own voice — *"sat down next to you. Take your time, I'm not going anywhere"*, and the stream field stopped explaining `self_stream` and the missing Go Live API *to the person who was just noticed*, which was true and useless in equal measure. **The Overview answered four questions about the deployment and none about the server**, which is why it read as empty; it now carries **who is in a room and who is in one on their own** (read off the gateway, reporting what it could read rather than painting a zero over a cold process), **what the player is doing** with the provider order behind it, and the four destinations people actually come here for. The Server page gained **what the bot's own role lets it do here** — read from `guild.me`, costing no request — because the failure that panel exists to pre-empt is a button that reports success and changes nothing: the bot was invited without *Manage Channels*, or its role sits below the one it is changing.
- **🎧 A song that the provider refuses is now played from somewhere else instead of dying — and the card that used to say *"that stream was cut off"* over a provider that never handed over a stream now says what actually happened**: *"i want smt that doesn't die"*, over a screenshot of the exact failure: a track found, a disambiguation menu answered, and then `DownloadError` — *"the provider could not be read"* — with the queue moving on. **The cause is a network, not a bug.** yt-dlp negotiates a stream through one of several *player clients*, and a datacenter IP is refused by the default one: the same commit that plays every song on a laptop resolves nothing on the host, which is why this survived every test and every local run. So the extractor now climbs a **ladder of client sets** — yt-dlp's own choice first (the only rung that negotiates audio-only Opus), then the clients that keep answering when the default is refused — and it keeps climbing until one returns *one URL a player can open*, which is the distinction that had been missing: a rung can answer with 47 formats and still have nothing playable, because the selected format is a video/audio pair that would need muxing. The rung that worked is remembered for the process, so a host that has settled does not re-learn it per song; a *network* failure stops the climb immediately, because four more extractions cannot give a host a route it does not have; and an operator's `YTDL_PO_TOKEN` is the first rung rather than the only one, which it used to be — a token the provider had stopped honouring disabled every fallback, so the one deployment configured to cope was the one that died first. **And before a track is given up on, the same song is looked for somewhere else.** SoundCloud is the second provider because this resolver already understands it end to end, and because it is markedly harder to refuse a datacenter IP on; the recording is identified by its **own known length** rather than by another search's confidence margin, so a four-way tie between same-named uploads is an answer instead of silence. The card *says* it substituted, because an unexplained swap is the wrong-song bug wearing a different hat, and a rescue that cannot itself be streamed is no rescue — so it is not offered. **Four defects came out with it.** The fault card had one headline for every kind of failure and printed *"That stream was cut off"* over a `DownloadError` — nothing had been cut off, and the sentence sent whoever read it to look at the wrong thing, so each kind now has its own headline, colour and advice, and the substitute is drawn as information rather than as a warning; SoundCloud identity was the **numeric API id**, which as a path resolves to a *user*, so `soundcloud.com/1856469195` 404s (the rescue reached that on its first real run) and now the page address is the identity; `watch_url` rebuilt the same broken URL from that id; and the weekly media check gained a **third bucket** for things that are neither dead nor unchecked — an extractor answering *through a fallback* is working today and one provider change from working not at all, and filing it as dead would send somebody to fix a link that resolves. `Probe` reports which client answered and whether it had to climb, `/music doctor` prints it, the console shows `climbing` as its own status, and the weekly pass reads it — but stays silent about it, because a card every week about something that works is how a channel gets muted. 2320 tests pass, 20 new.
- **🛡️ A server's staff can moderate from the website and the operator can act on a server as a whole, and both surfaces are built on one rulebook rather than two — because the alternative was Discord's own audit log crediting the bot for what a person asked for**: *"add options on dashboard — 1. manage members like kicking, ban, remove etc… 2. manage whole server like locking down and etc… and for owner dashboard, manage server — can kick myself, or ban that server, and other things"*. **Two new dashboard pages, from the nav down.** `/dashboard/members` is a roster — searchable, with the ban list beside it, because the member you want to unban is by definition not in the roster — and one action form: warn, time out, remove a timeout, kick, ban, unban, clear warnings, each with a reason that reaches the member and is the record. `/dashboard/server` is the whole server at once: a **lockdown** that denies Send Messages, thread creation and reactions to `@everyone` in every channel the bot can manage, a **lift** that puts back *exactly* what each channel had before, and **slowmode** applied to every channel with Discord's own ceiling clamped rather than refused. **The rulebook is one module, not two copies of it.** `utils/moderation_actions.py` is the guard `/kick` has always used — you may not act on an administrator, the owner, or somebody at your own level, and an actor whose own member could not be read is *refused* rather than waved through — and the cog is now a thin caller of it, so a kick from a phone and a kick from a webpage take the same code path to the same audit row. `utils/server_control.py` holds the plan/apply split for the bulk changes: the plan is pure and needs nothing but the guild object (which is why the page can still say what a lockdown *would* do when the ledger could not be read), and the apply writes the ledger **before** it touches a channel, because a bulk permission change that failed to record itself would have no undo. **The website's record exists because Discord's does not name the person.** A web action credits the *bot* in Discord's audit log — the bot is what performed it — so `web_actions` is the only place the signed-in administrator's identity is attached to what happened, and the page prints the last twelve. **The console gained the four things only the operator can do**: republish a server's command list, leave it, leave *and block* it, and take it off the blocklist — with the blocklist enforced on the way in rather than only on the way out, because a list that a re-invite can walk around is a note to self rather than a rule. **Three defects came out of building it, and all three were found by the suite rather than by looking.** The panels vanished their own controls when a read failed, so the ids the page's script reaches for did not exist — the copy is now *inert and says why*, controls present, disabled, with the reason in the field beside them; **the dashboard's rail became nine pages and the design law that said "seven" was a cap rather than a rule**, so it is chunked into groups the way the operator's rail already was, with the same arithmetic asserted (no group over five, five groups at most, every page placed in the order it was declared); and the preview harness had been rendering an empty `<nav>` on this page since the rail was split, because it never passed the page list, so the one tool that exists to look at the rail was the one place it could not be seen. **Considered and not done:** a DJ-style permission model for who may moderate from the website. Today it is the session's own principal (the person must actually hold the Discord permissions for what they ask), which is the same rule the slash commands apply and needs no new settings table — a "moderators may use the web panel" toggle would be a second way to express one permission.
- **🎵 Music is back, and it is a rebuild rather than a restoration — because the archived cog's three complaints were three *mechanisms*, not three bugs: it searched again for a song it had already found, it mistook an aborting stream for a finished one, and it never asked whether the host could play at all**: *"We will start the work on music bot again — create a plan how to do that so it will work perfectly."* **"It plays a different song"** was identity: the archived cog kept the search *query* on each track and, when a stream URL expired, ran that query again — and a search is not a lookup, so the refresh could return a different upload than the card the member was reading, mid-song, where nobody could see it happen. Every track now carries a **stable identity** (`provider` + that provider's own id, `utils/media_source.py`), re-resolution asks for *that recording*, and the search happens exactly once, at request time. **"The music is short"** was the same class of bug one layer down: a stream that dies reaches `discord.py` as a finished track, so the queue used to move on in silence — and the archived `FFMPEG_BEFORE_OPTIONS` (`-err_detect ignore_err`) is precisely what hid it. The player now builds **one** ffmpeg command line in one place (no such flag), counts through a **generation counter** so a stale `after()` callback cannot advance a queue that has already moved, and compares how much actually played against what the provider declared — a track that ends at 30% of a declared four minutes is reported as **cut off**, with the engine's own last words, to the member *and* to the operator, because "the song was really short" and "this host was cut off" are different facts and only one of them is somebody's playlist. **"Music is broken here"** is `utils/voice_caps.py`, which probes **before** anything joins a channel: an ffmpeg binary (system, then `imageio-ffmpeg`'s static build), an Opus library *if there is one*, and `davey` — which this `discord.py` refuses to open a voice connection without, so a host missing it looked exactly like a server where nobody had asked for a song. Every missing reason is printed separately, because each one is a different fix, and the boot line, `/music doctor`, `/api/voice-debug` and the owner console's new **Music panel** all read that same probe. **The command surface is the other half of the plan, and it is arithmetic**: Discord allows **100 top-level chat-input commands per scope** and this bot publishes 88 roots, so the archived cog's twelve roots could not come back as they were. `/play` keeps its own slot — the one name people type from memory — and the other sixteen live under **one** `/music` group, which is why the registry now reads **14 systems, 95 commands**. **What is deliberately not there is as considered as what is**: `/music radio` streams an HTTP station directly and never touches the extractor, so it keeps working on a host the platforms have blocked (the one failure that cannot be designed away, and the reason `YTDL_COOKIES_B64` / `YTDL_PO_TOKEN` exist as documented answers rather than an escape hatch); `/music 247` remembers its seat in the `music_247` table and reclaims it on boot; the queue lives in the process and a restart empties it; nothing is recorded and nothing is stored per member. **And the one thing that could rot silently forever got a probe**: `yt-dlp` stays installed, importable and reporting a version while answering every search with *"Sign in to confirm you're not a bot"*, and every link in the tree can be healthy on that day — so `utils/media_health.py` grew a **third kind of target** that asks the extractor for a known recording weekly (`media_source.self_check`, Blender's own upload, `YTDL_PROBE_TARGET` to override), and reads the failure for what it is: a provider refusing this host is a **finding**, a host with no route out is **`unchecked`** at the same rule as any other "could not ask", and the version travels inside the reason because *one release behind* is the common case and invisible without it. `media_health.extractor_state()` is the cheap read of that verdict for the console and `/health` (no network call, and an honest *not asked since this process started*), while `/music doctor` and `/api/voice-debug` ask **now** — a version string is not evidence. Music also reaches every web surface: the public `templates/music.html` (command list read out of the registry, so the page cannot advertise a command the bot does not publish), the hub's registry-driven catalogue, and the console panel that draws capability, player and extractor in one card. **2,245 tests pass, 0 skipped** — the two long-standing skips are gone, because the music suites came back rewritten against the new modules (`tests/test_music.py`, `tests/test_music_matching.py`, 8 new cases in `tests/test_media_health.py` and a new `tests/test_voice_caps.py`); `tsc` clean, the console bundle rebuilt and re-stamped, and all three static builds pass. Also in this pass: `.dockerignore` no longer hides `assets/` (the fonts `utils/card.py` draws the now-playing card with — an image missing them renders in Pillow's bitmap font and says nothing), and the Dockerfile installs `ffmpeg` + `libopus0` as an *upgrade* rather than a precondition, since the native runtime can already decode and encode.
- **🗜️ Three complaints from one afternoon of using the console were one broken switch, one reading nobody took, and one command hidden by a rule the card did not mention**: *"company cmd isn't there", a red toast reading *"the server answered something this build does not understand (`guild_id` should be string, is number)"*, and *"there are ppl online but not showing on dashboard"*. **The toast was the switch and the switch was the toast**: `/api/toggle` and `/api/toggle-all` answered with `guild_id` as a JSON *number* while the console's schema — the boundary every payload crosses before a page sees it — says string. A snowflake that crosses as a number comes back as a different number, so the type is a law, not a style choice; and because the check throws *before* the page can use the answer, the failure was not cosmetic: the click landed in the caller's `catch`, the knob sprang back, and the follow-up that reads whether Discord was actually told to republish never ran — which is how a module could be on in the database, on in this console, and absent from Discord's picker with nothing on screen to say which. Both routes answer text now, the response carries **`republish`** (whether the bot was up to hear the request at all), and a switch that could not queue one says *saved, but the command list was not republished — try again in a moment* instead of a green toast about a command nobody can type yet. **"People online but not showing" was the one thing the Live page promised never to do**: `_live_presence` counted `Member.status`, on the theory that the attribute is absent without the Presence intent. It is not — discord.py always has it, and Discord answers **offline for every member** — so the card printed *1 online, 35 offline* for a server full of people, the one online being the bot, whose presence its own process sets. Five figures, none of them a reading. The guard is now the **intent** (`presences` *and* `members`, since presences only arrive for the members a client tracks), `read.presence` goes false, and the page draws the dash and the switch that turns it on. And the intent is now askable: `ENABLE_PRESENCE_INTENT` goes through the same portal check every other privileged intent does (`portal_intents` reads Discord's own application flags, `drop_ungranted_intents` drops what the portal has not granted — asking for one it has not makes Discord refuse the *whole* connection), declared in `config_audit` and `.env.example`, off by default, with the Live page naming it in the gap notice. **"/company isn't there" was the honest half of a card that overpromised**: the greeting's footer told whoever was alone to run `/company mode:Off`, but `/company` is declared with the Manage Server audience — so Discord *hides it from the picker* of a member without that permission, and the card was pointing at a command its reader could not see. The footer says *an administrator can stop this* now, the console's publish line says out loud that Discord's own client caches the command list, and a module switch that adds commands reports whether the republish was heard. **2,158 tests pass, 2 skipped** — the new ones are the kind that fail on the mutation: `TheConsoleSchemaIsWhatTheServerAnswers` reads each schema out of `payload.ts` and compares it field by field with what the server actually answers (watched to fail by putting the raw int back), `_live_presence` is asserted absent with the intent off, present with both intents on, and absent with presence-but-no-members, and the footer is pinned to the audience it is addressed to.
- **🤖 The bot's own moderation decisions had no record and no appeal — and while checking the tree against the seven reference documents, the smallest thing it found was 10px type**: a pass over *"those stuff … according to its job"*, where "those" is the Frontend/UIUX, Backend/API, Security, Accessibility, Privacy, Legal and Bot-Platform references. **The real find was the bot's automatic moderation**, because the bot-platform rules ask a question the code could not answer: *what happens after a decision nobody made?* `/automod` deletes an invite and times out a flood **by itself**, and the only trace was a notice that deleted itself after ten/fifteen seconds — so a moderator asked "did the bot mute somebody?" had nothing to read, and the member it hit had no way to say it was wrong. Every automatic action now writes a card to the server's own moderation log through **one shared door** (`utils/modlog.py`: resolves `log_channel_id`, validates it, and reports the two refusals — nothing configured, a channel that no longer exists — as facts rather than exceptions), naming the rule that fired, the member, the channel and whether Discord actually accepted the action *(`timeout` can be refused and the filter then reads as if it worked — the card says **not muted** and how to fix it)*. The **`/appeal`** command is the other half: any member can file one review per five minutes, it lands as a real `Appeal` case in the log with their own words and their last three cases attached (and says out loud that nothing reverses automatically, and that a moderator answering it uses `/unmute` or `/unban`, which are logged like every other action), falling back to a DM to the server owner when no log channel is set — and the notices now end with the one sentence that names the door: *"If that was wrong, run `/appeal` — a human will read it."* **The type was the other real find, and it was everywhere**: the dashboard sheet carried **43 declarations between 10px and 11.5px** — the hint under the identity fields, the label on every metric, the server name in the rail — and the public sheet and two templates carried 18 more (`0.625rem`, `0.6875rem`, three `11px`), all under the 12px floor both reference sets state flatly. Every one is 12px now, the `.doc` measure came in from 76ch to 72ch and the console's Gaps list from 92ch to **72ch** (the laws' ceiling is 75 characters a line), three fields that only had a `placeholder` — the admin passkey, the per-server nickname and the per-server bio — have real labels, and the console hands `reducedMotion="user"` to its animation library, which is the only way a JS spring can hear a setting that a media query cannot reach. **The laws are now tests rather than a paragraph**, which is the point of the exercise: `tests/test_design_laws.py` grew a **floor under every font size** in all three sheets and every template (resolving `rem` against the root the sheets actually set, and saying in the docstring why `em` and `%` are deliberately not guessed at), a **measure check** (a line longer than 75 characters fails, and a sheet that declares no measure at all fails), a **name on every field** in both the templates and the JSX, and a **reduced-motion** check for the sheet *and* for the library hand-off. `tests/test_automod.py` is new and 15 of its tests are about the record rather than the filters: an invite deletion and a flood timeout each leave a card, a refused timeout is recorded as refused, an action with nowhere to log still acts and raises nothing, an appeal becomes a case, reaches the owner when there is no channel, tells the member when there is nowhere to send it, keeps its cooldown, and two source-reading guards fail if a future filter is added without an audit call or a notice that omits the appeal. **2,129 tests pass, 2 skipped** (30 in the design-laws file, 15 new here); `tsc` clean.
- **🪤 *The Architect's Legal Framework: Version 3.0* was handed over as a PDF, and it asked for something a footer link cannot be: three layers of anti-AI defence, a real clickwrap, and a Terms document that says who owns what — so the bot now refuses named crawlers on every host, answers a trap no person can reach, and asks a sign-in to press one of two buttons that are exactly the same size**: *"fix those stuff and compile and stuff according to its job."* The framework's own sentence is the reason none of this is a `robots.txt` edit — *"robots.txt is a request, and the collectors with the most to gain are the ones least likely to honour it"* — so the posture is three layers, and each exists for a way the one before it is ignored. **The directive**: `utils/ai_guard.py` builds `robots.txt` (each AI crawler — GPTBot, ClaudeBot, CCBot, Bytespider, PerplexityBot, Google-Extended, Applebot-Extended and 23 more — named with `Disallow: /` **before** the wildcard, because a rule written after `User-agent: *` is read by nobody) and `ai.txt` (the training prohibition in the convention crawlers look for, at `/ai.txt` and `/.well-known/ai.txt`), and **all four hosts now write those same two functions' output** — the app serves them, and `build_site`, `build_hub` and `build_owner` call them, where two of those hosts had published `User-agent: * / Allow: /` and nothing else, which is exactly how a policy ends up true on one host and absent on the next with nothing to show for it. **The reservation in headers**: `Content-Usage: train-ai=n`, `tdm-reservation: 1`, `tdm-policy` and `X-Robots-Tag: noai, noimageai` (plus `noindex, nofollow` on everything behind a sign-in) on every response from every surface, because a header reaches an API answer, an image and an error page as readily as it reaches HTML — and a static host has no Flask, so the `_headers` files carry them too. **The refusal**: a declared AI crawler is answered **403** with the addresses of the two documents it is choosing to ignore, on every path except the directives themselves (a crawler that cannot fetch the rule it is breaking has an argument, and refusing the policy would be an odd way to enforce it). **The trap**: two paths no page links a person to, disallowed in the directive, absent from every sitemap, `tabindex="-1"` and `aria-hidden` in every public footer, answered with a decoy that says what it is rather than a poisoned payload that invites an argument about who deceived whom — because the trap's value is that it cannot be argued with. A fetch is the only signal in this codebase that identifies a collector **positively** rather than guessing at one from behaviour, so it is recorded with the agent, the address, the country and a count (folded, because a collector that asks four hundred times should be one line with a count and not four hundred lines when somebody finally has to read it) in `net_guard_events` for 180 days, appears as counts on `/health` in its own `anti_ai` block, and reaches the operator as one card that names the path, the agent, the address and the two things that actually stop it: **a network block rather than a user-agent rule** (one costs the collector the proxies it rented, the other costs it one line), and the ledger row that a complaint is built on. The clause behind all of it is Terms §14, the policy is `AI_POLICY.md` (served at `/ai`, machine-readable at `/ai.txt`), and the operator's playbook — including the Cloudflare WAF expression to paste — is `docs/anti-ai.md`. **And the Terms became a clickwrap**, which is the half the framework is emphatic about: terms a person was never made to acknowledge are the terms most likely to be found unenforceable, and *a crawler cannot assent*, so a prohibition it never saw was never a term. Every door (the passkey form, the one-time link, Discord's callback, the owner console's form) now wraps its destination in `/agree`, which lists the three documents, shows the **revision** — the SHA-256 prefix of the files themselves, so a sentence added in September is not covered by an agreement made in June, and the dashboard asks again at the next sign-in — and offers two answers of exactly the same size: same class, same width, no pre-selection, no countdown, no confirmshaming, and a decline that signs the session out and records nothing. Accepting writes one row (revision, scope, address, browser, time) that the console's Access page can read; **a ledger that cannot be written still lets the person in and says so in words**, because the failure mode worth designing against is a clickwrap that quietly stops recording and keeps looking like it works. The policies themselves are never behind it — a policy you must accept before you may read is not a policy — and `PUBLIC_PREFIXES` / `GATED_PREFIXES` classify every route in the app, with a test that walks Flask's own route table and fails on a new route that was not classified. **The documents were rewritten to the framework's own clause list**, since the point of the exercise is legal rather than technical: the Terms gained the anti-AI clause, clickwrap and revision recording, output ownership with the similarity-of-content caveat, user warranties, third-party disclaimers, an explicit *no professional reliance* section with human oversight, **indemnity** with its procedural mechanics, **binding arbitration with a jury and class-action waiver and a 30-day opt-out**, notices and takedowns, a **grievance officer** with 7-day acknowledgement and 30-day resolution, export controls and sanctions, and a general section; the Privacy Policy gained the lawful-basis table, the rights section (GDPR, UK GDPR, CCPA/CPRA and the DPDP Act, with the grievance route), automated decisions and the human-review path (Terms §12), a cookies section that says out loud why there is no consent banner — one strictly-necessary cookie and nothing to consent to — security stated plainly without claiming encryption at rest, and transfers. **One real bug fell out of writing them**: the Terms now exceed Discord's 6,000-character embed ceiling, and `cogs/legal.py` added the *"the rest of it"* pointer **after** filling the card — so the card came to 6,053 characters, which Discord rejects outright, i.e. the failure was not a truncated policy but *no policy at all* in the one command a member runs to read it. The pointer is measured and reserved now, in both the character and the field budget, and the test asserts the ceiling holds *including* the field that says where the rest of it is. **2,128 tests pass, 2 skipped** — 72 new in `tests/test_ai_guard.py` and `tests/test_consent.py`, and the ones that matter are the negative ones: a browser, a monitor, `curl` and a reader with no `User-Agent` at all are still served, `GPTBotSimulator` is not OpenAI, the document that governs a crawler is still readable by it, every route is on one side of the public/gated split, the two answers on `/agree` are byte-identical in markup, declining records nothing, and an unwritable ledger leaves the person signed in with the page admitting what is missing. `tsc` clean; nothing in the console bundle changed.
- **🔐 A security pass over the changed surface found six things — two were real, two were only right because somebody typed a constant that way, and two were the scanner being unable to see a guard**: Snyk's SAST run over the working copy, with every finding traced rather than muted. **Real**: `find_free_port` probed a port by binding **every** interface (`0.0.0.0`) to answer a question about one, and it binds loopback now; `scripts/preview_cards.py` fed a **command-line argument straight into `exec`** — the one place in this project where a command line reaches code — so a revision is now validated by shape before git is asked for it (`--output=…`, quotes, whitespace and backticks are all refused) and the test asserts both halves, the checked name *and* the argument list that makes a shell metacharacter inert. **Guarded rather than fixed**: two URLs already pointed at the right host, and one of them carries a **bearer access token** while the other posts a **licence key somebody paid for** — so `discord_auth.checked_endpoint` and `plans.checked_endpoint` now refuse anything that is not HTTPS to `discord.com` / `api.lemonsqueezy.com`, called *before* the request is built and asserted to be on the path every outbound call takes, because "the endpoint is a constant" is a comment and a host check is a rule. The tests include the confusions that matter: `discord.com.evil.test`, `discordapp.com`, a userinfo host, a plaintext downgrade, and the loopback address. **Not real, and written down**: two DOM-XSS reports where the value reaching `<img src>` is a same-origin relative path produced by `iconSrc`, which refuses `//host/x`; React escapes it and assigns the attribute as a property, so the report is the scanner not *seeing* the guard — which is why `tests/test_outbound.py` now pins the guard, the funnel every request goes through, and that no page builds an icon URL by hand. 13 findings closed, 5 carried with their reasoning, and the one that matters most is the last: the scan is a starting point, not a verdict.
- **🎨 The owner console was rebuilt to a written set of design laws — and, more usefully, the laws are now *tests* rather than a paragraph somebody has to remember**: *"rebuild the whole /owner from the bottom: new UI, new UX, new rules"*, against the 60-30-10 palette rule, a single type scale, WCAG contrast, whitespace, consistent components, Fitts's and Hick's laws, keyboard navigation and immediate feedback. The type scale is the visible half: body text was **13px in components and 15px on the page** and is now 16px everywhere a sentence is read, with four smaller tiers for type that is *not* prose (an eyebrow, a table cell, a note under a figure) — the sheet had 74 hand-written font sizes in twelve values, including a 12.5px that existed on one element, and every one of them now resolves to a `--type-*` token. Contrast is measured rather than eyeballed: text is ≥ 4.5:1 against the surface it is drawn on and graphics ≥ 3:1, in **all four console themes** and in the public sheet's own palette, and the one token that cannot carry text (`--accent` on the public sheet, 4.01:1 by design) now has a test that fails if a rule ever paints type with it — the fix is always `--accent-text`, which is 8.94:1. Controls have a floor as a *token* (`--target-min: 44px`, `--tap-min` on the public sheet, Fitts's law): buttons, fields, rail links, palette rows and the theme swatches all read it, and the swatches went from a 22-pixel circle to a 44×44 target with the dot drawn inside it. Focus is one global `:focus-visible` ring, and an `outline: none` is only allowed where the same rule draws something else — which turned the two places the sheet had quietly removed one (the palette's input, the public forms) into a visible state instead of an absence. And **nine pages is past what a person scans**, so the rail is grouped — Fleet, Work, Owner, with the Overview above the headings — from `OWNER_GROUPS` on the server rather than a list in the React app, so a page nobody placed is drawn ungrouped rather than vanishing. `tests/test_design_laws.py` holds all of it: contrast per theme, the type floor, a console rule that invents a font size, one `h1` per page, `alt` on every image, the focus rules, the target floors, the nav arithmetic, and a scan that fails if a component writes a colour literal of its own — which is how the sheet's twelve stray `rgba(...)` values and one `#fff` became seven named neutral overlays.
- **🫂 Somebody alone in a voice channel is the loneliest surface this bot has, so it now sits with them — and it says out loud, on the card and on the dashboard, the one thing it cannot do: watch their stream**: *"when someone is alone in vc the bot will join that vc and say like hi u are not alone Im with u … and if they are streaming can he watch the stream?"* The honest answer to the second half is **no**, and Discord's own API is why — a bot has no access to Go Live, there is no endpoint for it and the gateway never carries the video — so what the card says is what is true ("you're live, and I can't watch a stream"), and what the owner's console shows is the *fact* (`self_stream` on somebody's voice state) rather than a picture that would be invented. The first half is a feature with four rules, each one a reason a person would otherwise be right to be annoyed, and all four are in `utils/company.py` (pure, testable without a gateway) with the connection in `cogs/company.py`: **alone means alone** (a channel whose only occupant is one human, and a second *bot* in there counts as company of a kind, because two silent bots in one room is a comedy nobody asked for); **it listens to nothing and says nothing out loud** (deafened *and* muted, which is a state everybody in the channel can see rather than a claim in a document); **it arrives slowly and leaves quickly** (a 12-second grace period, because somebody alone for a moment is somebody whose friend is reconnecting, and it disconnects the instant its reason to be there is gone — a second human, an empty room, or the module switched off); and **it never follows somebody who walked out on it** (leaving the channel it is in is an answer, remembered for five minutes). A move rather than an exit is treated as a move — the same situation one room over — and it holds **one** voice connection because a Discord client can only be in one channel at a time, so a second join would silently kick the first. The greeting carries the `:pastelpink:` emoji as a resolved `<a:name:id>` tag **plus** the picture as the card's thumbnail, in that order of preference, because a custom emoji tag only renders in a client that shares a server with it and the thumbnail renders everywhere; the whole thing is `/company` — server-scoped, administrator-only, `mode:Auto|Off` and `greeting:on|off` — declared in `features.py` (thirteenth system, ninety-second command) and `permissions.py` like every other switch, with a cog-level gate so the command the registry hides is the command the bot refuses.
- **🔤 The cards got a real typographic pass — a type scale, a spacing scale, one accent instead of ninety, and no rule that separates nothing — and the before/after is a page rather than an opinion**: *"a real typographic pass — hierarchy, spacing, one accent, no orphan separators"*, and each of those four turned out to be a specific defect rather than a matter of taste. **Hierarchy**: an embed's field *name* was rendered `**bold**`, so it was exactly as loud as the value it labelled — a card of eight fields was eight bold labels and eight plain values with nothing saying which was the subject. A field is now a `label()` (Discord's `-#` small text) over body text, the title is the only level-two heading on a card (a body that opens with its own `##`, which is what an embed's title plus a `##` description arrives as, is demoted to a group), `section()` is a group, and `heading()` survives as an alias. **Spacing**: there was no scale at all — separators were placed by hand at each call site, which is how one card gets three gaps and the next none. Four named levels now (`GAP`, `BREAK`, `RULE`, `RULE_LARGE`) placed by a `_place()` that holds three invariants: never a spacer as the first or last child, never two spacers side by side (the stronger one wins), never a rule where it separates nothing. **No orphan separators** was two real bugs, both visible in the preview: a card whose only content is its closing line opened with the hairline that closes it (`separator(visible=True)` as the first child), and a merged embed's later fields landed *under* the footer rule because `embeds_to_layout` reached into `container.add_item` — the rule ended up mid-card, separating the close from the content that followed it. There is now one way to add content to a built card, `Panel.append()`, which inserts above the closing block, and `clean_text()` removes a hand-drawn divider (`━━━━━━━━━━━`), a run of blank lines or a trailing newline before the card is assembled, so the card's own rule is never the second one in a row. **One accent**: the palette held nine colours of which five were decorative and *unused anywhere*, while the cogs picked their own hex instead — 251 literals across `cogs/`, not one of which consulted the table — so the bar down the left edge was a different colour from one command to the next. It is now the product accent plus four states, `accent_for()` is the only door (a value `ACCENT_ALIASES` knows becomes the state it means; anything else becomes the accent), the help panel's twelve category hues and both hex literals in `cogs/tickets.py` go through it, and a test walks the tree and fails if a new call site writes `accent=0x...`. **And the review is a rendering, not a claim**: `scripts/preview_cards.py` builds twelve fixtures through the real module — a roleplay card, a leaderboard, a receipt with every hygiene sin in it, two embeds merged into one card, an embed conversion, the soundboard's file — reads each one back out of `to_components()` (the JSON Discord receives) and draws the working tree against any revision of `utils/style.py` loaded out of git, side by side, with the component/rule/gap/label/accent counts under each card; an orphan rule is called out in red on the before column and gone on the after. Nothing is copied into the script, which is the rule the rest of this repository lives by — a second copy of the builder would agree with its author instead of with the code. The guide's own colours were the last of it: twelve category accents became at most five meaningful ones, and the local preview of the whole product is now one command. **1,958 tests pass, 2 skipped**, 33 new in `tests/test_style.py` — the four spacing invariants, one level-two heading per card, the accent table both ways round, a stray colour that must land on the accent, a state that must survive however it was spelled, the text-hygiene rules, and appended content landing above the footer — with the rendering in `scripts/preview_cards.py` (`python scripts/preview_cards.py --before-rev HEAD~1`), writing a git-ignored `_preview/cards.html`.
- **🔍 Nothing in the product can notice a dead picture, so the bot now asks itself every week and reports what stopped existing — and the first real pass found a finding about the check itself**: *"Add a self-check to the bot that probes every hardcoded and resolved media URL weekly and reports the dead ones instead of letting them rot silently."* The failure it exists for has no symptom anywhere: every roleplay card pointed at a hand-typed `media.tenor.com` URL, all of them answered **404**, and what a member saw was an empty box Discord draws without complaint — nothing raised, nothing logged — and the same rot was still in the tree this week (`/marry`'s proposal and the wedding card, both 404). `utils/media_health.py` probes **two kinds of thing, because the bot sends two**: media written into the source, which is *found* by scanning the files that may hold one rather than listed by hand (tests, docs and retired cogs are skipped, because a deliberately broken URL in a test is not a fault to report), and media that is *made* — one target per roleplay action and per pet, read off `utils/gifs`'s own tables, so an action added there is covered without anybody remembering this module exists. The clock lives in the **database**, not in the process, and that is the whole reason "weekly" is weekly: Render restarts this bot often enough that an in-memory timer would mean a sweep of every URL on every deploy, which is how a check becomes load and then gets switched off. Three rules, each learned from the bug: **a `200` is not evidence** (a body whose content type is not media is reported as *not media* — an HTML error page served as `200` is the common case), **a clean week is silence** (a weekly *everything is fine* is how a channel gets muted, and this card's only value is being read when it arrives), and **a pass never raises** — a target that could not be asked lands in `unchecked`, kept apart from `dead`, because *"this link is broken"* and *"I could not reach anybody"* are different sentences and only the first one sends somebody to edit a URL. Findings go to the operator channel through `operator_log.media_alert`, which names **which** target, **where** it is written (file and line, or `action:`/`pet:` for a resolver that came back empty, which is the other fix), and **what the probe actually got**; counts and never names are on `/health` as its own `media` block, beside the site guard's and the uptime one's, because that endpoint is unauthenticated by policy; and `scripts/check_media.py` runs the same pass by hand — `--quiet`, `--json`, `--kind` — exiting non-zero on a dead link, which is what a CI step would call. **The first pass against the real tree found 23 targets and one dead one, and the dead one was the check's own false positive**: `CDN_HOST = "https://cdn.discordapp.com/"` in `utils/discord_auth.py` is the prefix every avatar URL is built from, and a bare host answers 403 — a URL with no path names a *service*, so the scanner now refuses one instead of reporting a constant as a broken picture. With that fixed, every one of the 23 — all twelve actions, including the two social cards whose hand-typed links were 404 an hour earlier, both pets, and the images in `cogs/fun.py` — answered with real media. **1,925 tests pass, 2 skipped** — 49 new, 43 in `tests/test_media_health.py`, five driven through Flask (`/health`'s block, and that it carries no URL), the loop's four branches (a fresh pass is not repeated, a due one is recorded and said, a clean one says nothing, a failing card cannot end the loop), and the delivery card itself in `tests/test_operator_log.py`.
- **🐾 Four complaints about the same card — *no gif*, *no sound*, *it isn't tagging anyone*, *why does it say Playing* — were four separate layers of one message, and the two that looked like Discord being flaky were the library telling the truth about a payload nobody could see**: the roleplay cards pointed at hand-typed `media.tenor.com` links and **every one of them answered 404**, which Discord draws as an empty box and the bot cannot detect; they now resolve a picture at the moment they are used, from two keyless providers, and a URL is only used after a request confirms it answers with an `image/*` body — nothing confirmed means no picture, never a broken one (`utils/gifs.py`, 17 tests against a fake session). `/meowsound` uploaded its clip and Discord **never drew it**, because a Components V2 message only shows an attachment that a component *references* (Discord's own component reference says so in one line: *"Attachments won't show by default - they must be exposed through components"*), so the card now carries a File component for every upload — and the same fix makes any future file-bearing card work. `/action`, `/rizz` and `/roast` named people inside a `TextDisplay` and sent `AllowedMentions.none()` with them, so the card reached nobody: the cards now pass an opt-in ping naming exactly the target, and `utils.style.pings` sets `everyone=False, roles=False` explicitly because discord.py treats an *omitted* field as allowed — the first version of it would have left `@everyone` pingable from a card. **"Why is it showing Playing thing"** turned out to be grammar, not a bug: the verb in front of the line is Discord's, chosen by the activity *type*, so a line written as a headline renders as **"Playing Meowing at the wall 🐈 | /help"** — every shipped line now reads as a continuation ("over your server", "with the laser pointer"), a `custom` type is offered for a line with no verb at all, and both rules are pinned by tests instead of by prose.
- **🧰 The people who were never tagged got their apology, by hand and only once — a script that reads one channel's history, wakes nobody up twice, and cannot post anything without `--apply`**: *"a person used poke and that person didn't get tagged … the bot will send tag that person with that msg and note"*, for the small hours of the 21st in one server. `scripts/repair_untagged_actions.py` finds the bot's own cards in a stated local window, reads the mentions **out of the component tree** — a received V2 message hands discord.py component *objects*, not JSON, and its parsed `mentions` list is empty, which is exactly how a card naming two people reports nobody — and replies with a short note plus the quoted line, recording each message in `guild_settings` so a second run is a no-op. It was run in dry-run first, then applied to nine cards in the Hmm server's `#general`; the local marker row lives in the gitignored `bot.db`.
- **🐱 "Some might not like cat and those stuff" — the bot's voice is now a per-server choice (`/flavor`: cat, dog or neutral), and it says out loud what it cannot change**: every fun card was written for a cat and the only way to escape it was to switch the whole Fun module off. One setting per server now picks the picture and the wording — cat keeps exactly what every server already had, neutral draws no animal at all — while the bundled audio stays what it is, because the files in `assets/sounds` *are* cat recordings and pretending otherwise would be the worse lie; the soundboard's footer says which pack it is playing and names the switch. Server-scoped on purpose (*"a member who does not want cats cannot overrule everyone else"*), declared in `features.py` and `permissions.py` like every other command, and covered by 18 tests for the setting and the commands that read it.
- **🧱 Five pieces of the same shape are now one piece each — the environment has one reader, the limiters one window, the scopes one object, the sessions a ledger — and the owner host stopped being a second copy of the whole application**: a pass of *"what would change the shape of the next change"* rather than *"what is broken today"*, and every item started from a count rather than a hunch. **`os.environ` was read in ~52 places across `keep_alive`, `bot.py`, `cogs/` and `utils/`, none of which knew the whole set** — while `utils/config_audit.py` declared all 55 settings, with what each one turns on and what its absence costs, and held them together only by a test. That meant adding one variable (`OWNER_PROXY_SECRET`, last pass) was five edits in five files, and a module could read `(os.environ.get(X) or "").strip()` with its own idea of what blank means. `config_audit.get()` and `config_audit.flag()` are now the only door to the environment — same stripping, same set of spellings for "on" (`1`/`true`/`yes`/`on`, which four modules had spelled four ways) — and the rule is **enforced rather than documented**: `tests/test_config_audit.py` walks the source and fails if `keep_alive`, `bot.py`, `cogs/` or `utils/` reaches for `os.environ` at all, with `scripts/` exempt on purpose (a different machine, and the parity test still proves every name it reads is declared). **Three copies of the same sliding window became one, and the copies had not agreed.** `utils/ratelimit.py` is the arithmetic — a deque of attempt timestamps per key, time-based edges rather than a counter to reset — shared by the sign-in form, the passkey API and the message composer; what the copies had drifted into is the reason it exists: **the composer's copy counted a request as a failure the moment it was *allowed***, so "you may send" and "you have failed" were the same event, and nobody could have found it by reading the other two. The class also carries the *reading*, because a window is security state a person can otherwise not see: `snapshot()` answers **who is currently blocked and for how long**, how many keys the window is tracking, and how often it has refused somebody — so a mistyped passkey and a stranger probing the sign-in page stop looking identical from the console. Per-process is stated rather than papered over, with the process's own uptime printed beside the counts. **A Flask session is a signed cookie, and a signed cookie cannot be taken back** — so "sign me out everywhere" only existed as *change `FLASK_SECRET_KEY` and restart the host*, which signs out the browser you are doing it from. `utils/sessions.py` is the missing half: a `sid` in the cookie, a row per signed-in session (scope, server, Discord account, address, when it started, when it was last used), and revocation that lands on the next request through a five-second cache rather than at the browser's next reload. Three rules are deliberate and pinned: **a session with no `sid` keeps working and is adopted on its first request** (deploying this must not sign out every open console, and a build that cannot write to the database must still be usable), **a `sid` the ledger does not know is refused** (a database replaced by an older backup is the one case that must never answer "still valid"), and a write failure is *said* rather than raised. **`auth_scope` was a bare string compared at eleven call sites and written in five, with the meaning of each scope living in whichever comment needed it** — so a scope that gained a second condition (`user`, when it stopped being allowed to edit identity in servers it does not administer) had to change everywhere it was read. `utils/principal.py` is that table in one place: `global` is the passkey and the whole fleet, `user` is the servers the account administers *recomputed from the bot's live guilds on every request rather than trusted from the cookie*, `guild` is one server for as long as the bot is in it — and every caller asks the object (`may_act_in`, `is_operator`) instead of comparing literals. **The console had no boundary with its own API, so a shape disagreement arrived as a broken card**: `console/src/lib/payload.ts` now holds one schema per endpoint — the fields a page actually reads, with their types — and a response that disagrees names the **endpoint**, the field and what was expected, in a banner, rather than rendering an em dash where a server count should be. The `Reading`/`—` rule the Live page was written around (a reading that was *not taken* is never a zero) is now a primitive both pages use instead of a convention one page followed. **And the owner host stopped being a reverse proxy of the whole application.** `meow-owner.pages.dev` now answers a short prefix list — the console, its own API, `/api/image`, `/login/discord`, `/logout`, and `/static/` from its own CDN — and **301s everything else to the public site with the path and query string intact**. That is a fix for a class rather than an untidiness, and both members of the class were found by using the host: a page the backend renders refers to `/static/...`, which exists here only if the build copied it, so a missing stylesheet 404s **on this host alone and silently** (that was the unstyled dashboard); and every door the backend has was a door here too, which is how the console grew a second entrance whose guard nobody was reading (`/login` answered 200 on this domain, measured). One asset contract now, and the console's own sign-in is the only one proxied. The strongest layer is the one that is not in this repository and is therefore written down in `docs/deployment.md` instead: **Cloudflare Access on `/owner*` and `/api/console/*`**, which asks for an identity at the edge before Render sees the request — scoped to those prefixes, because hiding the root would leave a signed-out browser unable to be told what it is missing. **1,787 tests pass, 2 skipped** — 50 new, and the ones that matter are the guards themselves: the environment walk (watched to fail by putting one `os.environ` read back), the limiter's snapshot with a blocked key in it, adoption of a legacy session and refusal of a forged `sid`, the principal table's three scopes, a payload that disagrees about one field, and the worker's prefix list read back out of the generated JavaScript. `tsc` clean; the bundle is rebuilt and its stamp matches `console/src` (31 sources, `28eb747f154e`).
- **🔑 "There is no pass when entering the owner website" was true, and the reason was that the Discord button *was* the door** — plus two more holes the same pass turned up, one of which let any passer-by close the owner's sign-in for fifteen minutes. **The console's only credential is `ADMIN_PASSKEY`, and the callback handed "operator" to a session on a completely different basis**: `if user_id == OWNER_USER_ID: auth_scope = "global"`. That is an *identification* — who this is — being spent as an *authorization*, so anyone holding the owner's Discord session (or sitting at a machine where it is signed in) reached the whole fleet — every server's channels, members, economy and ops tail — without ever typing the credential that is supposed to guard it, and the sign-in page's "Owner Discord Sign In" button read as a convenience rather than as a second door with no lock. **The two are separable now and the console is built on that**: `owner_candidate` records *who* signed in, `auth_scope` still requires the passkey, and `/owner` sends a candidate to the sign-in page instead of to `/servers` — because sending them to the picker hid the one page that can raise the scope, so the button the owner was told to use led somewhere the owner could not get back from. The page says so in a sentence ("That is the owner's Discord account, and the console still wants the passkey"), which is the difference between a form and a refusal. A deployment that genuinely wants the shortcut back gets it in one variable — `OWNER_DISCORD_ELEVATION=1` — and `render.yaml` commits `0`, because a prompt is where a switch gets answered differently on two hosts and one of them then opens the console without the pass. **The sign-in limit was counting Cloudflare, not people.** `_client_ip()` reads the *rightmost* forwarded hop, which is correct on Render and wrong on `meow-owner.pages.dev`, where the request arrives from a Cloudflare edge: every visitor on that domain shared one bucket, so **five wrong passkeys from anybody locked the sign-in page for everybody for fifteen minutes** — a stranger could do it by hand, repeatedly — while the worker forwarded the caller's own `X-Forwarded-For` headers along with it, so a client could also describe a chain it was not part of. The worker now **strips every hop header it is asked to forward** (`X-Forwarded-For`, `X-Forwarded-Proto/Host`, `X-Real-IP`, `Forwarded`, `CF-Connecting-IP`, and its own two) and stamps the visitor in `X-Meow-Client-IP` **signed with `OWNER_PROXY_SECRET`**; the app believes that name only when the signature matches, compared in constant time — a header anyone can send is worth exactly nothing as a rate-limit key, which is the whole reason it is signed. A build without the secret says so out loud, because that failure is silent: limits still work, they just count everybody as one. **And a security pass with the Snyk connector found three real things in fifty-eight reported** — the rest are traced in the notes, not muted. Two endpoints echoed the exception's own text into a 502 body (`"Discord did not accept it: {e}"` — a channel id, a status, sometimes a path) when the answer only needs to say what happened, so `_send_error` logs the whole thing and answers in one sentence. The row-count helper built `SELECT COUNT(*) FROM "…"` from whatever name was in `sqlite_master`; the rule is now the shorter one — **only names that are nothing but letters, digits and underscores are counted at all**, so the query is never built from a name that could carry syntax, and a name outside that set is left out rather than reported as unreadable. And both console pages put the API's `icon` straight into `<img src>`, which is an attribute the browser *acts on*; `iconSrc` accepts a path on this origin and refuses `//host/x`, which is somebody else's host wearing a leading slash. **1,737 tests pass, 2 skipped** — 22 new, and the ones that matter are driven end to end: the owner's own id is signed in, lands on the passkey page, and only gets the console after the passkey; the committed `0` is pinned so the shortcut cannot come back by accident; and the stamp, the signature and the fallback are each asserted with the header forged, absent, wrong, un-configured and non-address-shaped. **What this does not do**: it does not deploy itself. Setting `OWNER_PROXY_SECRET` to one value in **both** places (the Pages build that runs `scripts/build_owner.py`, and the service's environment) is what separates the sign-in buckets, and until it is set the owner host keeps the shared one. `ADMIN_PASSKEY` must also be set on the service — it is, verified against the live host, which is why this pass could confirm the door was real rather than guess.
- **🛰️ The owner's console can now see what a server is *doing* — a **Live** page with every channel, who is sitting in each voice room, and where the talking is — and the three defects beside it were all found by looking at the surfaces rather than at the code.** `/api/console/live` is one server's *present tense*, which nothing else here could answer: `/api/console/overview` reads the registry, the fleet reads member counts, the economy reads a table. The roster comes from the gateway's own `voice_states`, which is the one reading that is complete with **every privileged intent off** — a voice state carries a user id and a channel id, so *"somebody is in Lobby"* is known even when their name is not, and the page prints the id it has rather than a nickname it invented. Message and voice totals come from the tracker's own two tables over the tracker's own windows, so "7 days" here means the same seven days `/activity week` means. **The page is built around one rule: a reading that was not taken is never a zero.** A cold process knows no channels, the Presence intent is routinely off, and a build machine has no `bot.db` — so the payload carries a `read` block, the page prints an em dash where a card has nothing to draw, and a band at the top says which reading was missing and why. Presence is the clearest case: without the intent nobody can say who is online, and a page that answered "0 online, 3 offline" would be inventing a measurement about somebody's server. **The dashboard on the owner host was rendering unstyled — a heading over one black rectangle — and the cause was the *proxy*.** `meow-owner.pages.dev` answers `/static/` from Cloudflare's CDN and reverse-proxies everything else, so a page the backend renders arrives here referring to files that only exist if `scripts/build_owner.py` copied them: `dashboard.css`, `motion.js` and `gateway.js` were never on the list, so the per-server dashboard 404'd on its own stylesheet **on that host alone** while answering 200 on Render — and a missing stylesheet is not an error in any console a person reads. The list is fixed and the *rule* is now a test: `tests/test_owner_site.py` reads every `/static/...` out of this host's templates and fails if the builder does not copy it, because a hand-written list of asset names is the same second copy that let it drift. **And the cursor was two pointers at one coordinate.** The paw was drawn *at* the pointer, where the browser's own arrow already is and where what a click hits is decided — so it landed half over the arrow and read, correctly, as a rendering artifact. The arrow owns the point again; what follows it is one hairline reticle that trails on a spring, stretches along its own direction of travel by how fast it is moving, tightens rather than balloons over anything clickable, and stops its rAF loop as soon as it has caught up. `cursor: none` still appears nowhere, so text selection, resize handles and the I-beam keep working. **The public site's phone menu is a sheet now rather than a column of links**: rows with a hairline between them, an arrow that only appears under pointer or focus, the server marking the page you are on through `aria-current`, and `overflow-y: auto` on the nav — which is a fix, not a flourish, because six rows of display type do not fit a landscape phone and the version before centred them with no way to scroll. The four console pages that existed keep their layouts and gain a shared type-and-space scale, an uppercase masthead over a hairline rule, and a bracket-cornered strip for figures that are readings rather than containers. Verified by driving both surfaces in a browser, where the preview harness had to be taught about channels and voice states for the same reason it was once taught about the economy: against an unseeded gateway the new page renders "0 channels · 0 in voice", which is honest and teaches nothing about the page. **1,715 tests pass, 2 skipped** — 16 of them new, 8 in `tests/test_console.py` and 8 in `tests/test_owner_site.py`.
- **🎨 Theme picker now lives on every surface** — the dashboard, the login page, the servers page and the market all offer the four-theme control that the landing page and the console already had. The market chart, which is a canvas that cannot read CSS, now reads its palette from the same custom properties the rest of the page uses and repaints live when the theme changes, so choosing Sakura recolours the chart, the candles, the grid and the volume bars together rather than leaving the largest element on the screen in Discord's grey-and-blurple. The dashboard topbar stopped wrapping into two rows at 1440px — the secondary destinations (Servers, Pricing, Add to a server) moved into a dropdown menu, leaving one tidy row with search, the server switcher, Market, theme and sign out. The servers page dropped the redundant "Open now" pill that sat beside the primary button for the same action.
- **💰 The console can now see and move coins — an Economy page with one server's circulation, its richest members, and the only control on the whole surface that writes money — and building it turned up a fourth thing the preview harness could not show**: `/api/console/economy` reads the same `economy` table `/balance` reads (per server, because a balance is keyed by `(user_id, guild_id)` — the same person holds different coins in each one), and it reports **three** figures rather than one, because they are three different claims: what members hold in wallets, what they hold in banks, and what **Meow Bank** has collected. That last one is not decoration — `utils/meowfee.py` *banks* the fee instead of burning it, so coins that have left every member's balance are still in the server, and a page that summed wallets under a heading about circulation would print a total that does not add up. The fee row follows the server's own switch (absent means on, read without an event loop through a new `db.read_setting_sync` that mirrors `get_setting`'s decoding exactly), and the 25-member list is ordered by net worth. **The write path is the interesting half**, and it is deliberately narrow: `POST /api/console/economy/adjust` takes one action and one amount — add/remove to a wallet or a bank, or set exact balances, which is the owner-only `/eco-admin set` in Discord — behind the same CSRF token and the same operator scope as every other console route, with **no approval flow to skip** because a server administrator cannot reach this surface at all. A debit is **conditional** (`wallet >= ?`, rowcount decides) rather than clamped: `MAX(0, wallet + amount)` confirmed removals that never happened, which is the defect `/eco-admin remove` and `settle_bet` already exist to fix, so the console refuses an over-withdrawal with the number that refused it — *"that wallet holds 250 🪙 — there is not 10,000 to remove"* — and never reports a balance it did not write. Every change lands in the Ops tail as one line naming the actor, the action and the resulting balances, so an out-of-band balance change is accountable after the fact. **And the harness itself was answering an empty console**: every `/api/console/*` route is `keep_alive`'s real view and every one of them lists servers through `BOT_INSTANCE`, which `scripts/preview_dashboard.py` never had — so the Fleet page listed nothing, the Systems matrix had no columns, and the two new pages had no server to be about, which is exactly what a preview must not do (it looks like the page is broken rather than unseeded). It stands a stub gateway up while serving and seeds a small economy for its own stand-in servers; verified by running it, where the same routes now answer two servers, a 22-cell matrix, 90 declared and 90 published commands, and a four-member economy with the fee reading. 1,697 tests pass, 2 skipped; 13 new ones drive the writes against the table itself — a credit lands where the bot reads it, an over-withdrawal leaves the balance alone, `set` leaves the field it was not given, member ids cross as text, and a refused write moves nothing.
- **🧮 Every link in the operator's console opened the wrong server, the systems matrix — the one screen whose whole job is a switch per server — drew **"?" in all 33 of its cells**, and the per-server page answered **"No such server"**: three symptoms, one defect, and it is that a Discord id cannot survive a JavaScript number. `String(Number("1542278896148422676"))` is **`"1542278896148422700"`** — which is exactly what the address bar showed and exactly what the server was asked for. A snowflake is 18-19 digits, a double is exact to about 16, and the console sent ids as JSON *numbers*: the fleet list handed the app a rounded value, the app built `/owner/servers/…42700` out of it, and the API answered honestly that no such server exists. The matrix failed the other way around — its keys are the *server's own* exact strings, because Python has no such problem — so every lookup with a rounded key missed and every cell fell through to the placeholder that was written for "this server's state could not be read". **The fix is to stop arithmetic from touching an identifier**: `_console_id()` serialises every id in every `/api/console/*` payload as text, `ServerRow.id` is a `string` in the app rather than a `number` so the compiler refuses the next `Number()`, and the router passes the address straight through to the API. Server-side rows keep their integers deliberately — `modules.CACHE` and every SQL statement are keyed by the same `int` the gateway hands out, so a string there would be a second cache entry for one server. **And the surface that was missing is here**: a **Commands** page, backed by a sixth operator endpoint (`/api/console/commands`), listing every root command the registry declares beside what Discord was actually *told* — one row per command, the system that gates it, and a per-server column that says **off here, by name**, because "the bot does not have it" and "this server switched it off" are the same thing from inside Discord. Two cards carry the two ways a command list can disagree with itself: declared-and-not-published is a command that cannot be typed, and published-and-not-registered is one no switch can ever take away; a sleeping gateway reports `tree_read: false` rather than claiming every command is missing. Verified against the live measurement that started it — the reported id, its rounded rendering, and the matrix keys, all asserted in `tests/test_console.py`, which now checks that every id crosses as text, that the matrix is keyed by the very strings the app was handed, and that each command's per-server availability equals `command_scope`'s own answer rather than a second opinion about it. **1,684 tests pass, 2 skipped.** New in this pass: the Commands page and `/owner/commands`, and 25 sources in the console bundle instead of 24.
- **🐾 The cursor is a paw on the point with a ring trailing it, the console's config row names the settings it is about instead of saying "attention" and stopping, and driving the live page turned up two more defects the file never showed**: two complaints about the operator's own console, and both had a worse cause than the symptom. **The old cursor was a 36px amber donut holding the platform's own paw glyph, and it *inflated* to 1.9x over everything clickable** — so it grew exactly when it was covering the label it pointed at, and macOS draws that glyph as a pair of paws, which reads as a rendering artifact; it is now a drawn SVG paw with a lagging ring that tightens rather than balloons, off on touch and for `prefers-reduced-motion`, and it does not exist until the pointer moves. **Two defects came from driving the page and reading its console**: a pointer outside the window — an automation harness parks the mouse at x=-9933, and a pointer can walk off a multi-monitor edge — sent the companion flying across the viewport from nine thousand pixels away, and `event.target` is only *sometimes* an Element, so the document's missing `.closest` threw a TypeError out of the handler on every such move. **"Attention" was a dead end**: `config_audit` counts and deliberately does not name, because `/health` is public and "this deployment has no sign-in secret" is not a sentence to hand a stranger. Both console payloads now carry the names behind the operator's sign-in — presence only, never a value — each rendering as one collapsed line whose body is the *cost* rather than the description, because five names are scannable and five sentences of consequence are a wall.
