The five capability settings it names on production are the real answer: `DISCORD_CLIENT_ID` is declared in `render.yaml` and absent from the running service, which is the setting this module's own docstring uses as its first example of invisible configuration — it is set now, so the login page can offer Discord again.
- **🧭 Two addresses that are not pages were answering with pages — `/owner/nonsense` silently drew the **Overview**, and `meowdev.pages.dev` was still serving a build from before the rebuild, which is why the update "did not show"**: different causes, one shape of fault, and the second one is the answer to a question the first would have raised in the console. **The console's router ended with `return { kind: "overview" }` for anything unmatched**, so a mistyped URL or a retired bookmark rendered a screen that *looked* correct at an address that was wrong; the rail's `aria-current` (correctly) highlighted nothing, which meant the only evidence a reader had was a missing highlight — and the console disagreed with itself into the bargain, because an unknown *server* already answered "No such server" while an unknown *page* answered with a page. Found by typing one. Now only `/owner` itself is the Overview and everything else unmatched is a **"Not a page here"** screen that names the address it was given and offers the way back, inside the same shell. **And the hostname in the bookmarks was a second Cloudflare project**, which the API made plain rather than a guess: `meowdev.pages.dev` has **no Git source, no build command and no output directory**, its only deployment was `trigger: ad_hoc` from `0e6e2b4f`, while `meow-bot.pages.dev` is wired to `1tsRajOwO/Meow` and had built `961ac0ae` on the push — two hosts, one of which *cannot* be updated by a push and had not been touched since an afternoon publish by hand. So that address now **forwards**: 301, with the path preserved (`/systems` and `/privacy` land on the real pages, deep links keep their path), the hub's own pages mapped to something real rather than a 404 (`/commands` → `/systems`, `/deployment` → `/start`, the rest → the front door), and the hub itself still buildable with `scripts/build_hub.py` if it is ever wanted back. One address, one site, and it cannot go stale again. Verified both: the console's fallback and its fix driven in a browser (the bogus path, an unknown server id, a reload), and the redirect by `curl` against the live hostname for six paths including a retired one. 1673 tests pass, 2 skipped.
- **🔎 The exposure guard's own card promised the leak would clear on its own, and a day and four deployments later six repository paths were still being served from Cloudflare's cache — past their own `max-age`**: the check itself worked, its *reassurance* was wrong, which is the worse failure of the two because it tells a reader to wait. Asking the live host as a visitor does, `README.md`, `bot.py`, `requirements.txt`, `render.yaml`, `.env.example`, `cogs/economy.py` and `utils/cloud_sync.py` all answered **`200` with the real file** while the cache-busted probe for the same paths answered `404` — a stored copy from the deploy that published the checkout, served with `cf-cache-status: HIT`, `age: 65296`, on responses carrying **this project's own** `max-age=300, must-revalidate`. So a copy is outliving its stated lifetime by a factor of two hundred, and every remedy the card offered had already been tried: four deployments followed the bad one and the copies stayed, a client `Cache-Control: no-cache` was handed the same stored copy, and `Purge Everything` does not reach a `*.pages.dev` host. **What the card says now is the measurement and the truth**: the deployment is not the problem, a redeploy will not clear it, closing it takes a purge that only Cloudflare can perform on a host this project does not own, and the state is **open** rather than pending. **And the reading is only from the address that asked** — the same module, run on the bot's own host, answered `cached: 0` / `clean` for the six paths that a request from another region was still being handed source from, which is why a green `/health` is not a green website and the card now says so. Three copies of the false claim were corrected (`utils/site_guard.py`'s docstring and `describe`, and the Discord card's remedy in `utils/operator_log.py`), and the test that had been pinning *"it expires on its own"* now pins the purge instead — it was asserting the bug. 1672 tests pass, 2 skipped. The leak itself is **not fixed**: it needs a purge or a move off that hostname, and it is the one finding in this file that a commit cannot close.
- **🔒 Three real sinks closed — an unescaped SQL identifier, an image `src` trusted from the API, and a hand-rolled escape chain in the preview harness that served `a&amp;lt;b` — and the eight findings left by the scan are each traced rather than silenced**: the scan was run as a gate before committing, and separating the true findings from the noisy ones was most of the work. **(a)** `cogs/server_control.py` counted every table with `SELECT COUNT(*) FROM "{name}"`, interpolating the name straight out of `sqlite_master`. A table name is an *identifier* and cannot be a bound parameter — `?` stands for a value, never for a name — so it is quoted by hand, and a name containing a quote closed the quoting early: SQLite read the rest as junk, raised a syntax error, and the count came back **`-1`**, which in that function means *unreadable*. A perfectly readable table was reported as one nobody could read. Doubling the inner quote is the whole fix, and the new test plants a table actually named `a"b` and drives the real function, so it fails on the old code with exactly that `-1`. **(b)** Both console pages put `server.icon` straight into `<img src>`. Every value is our own `/api/image?...` by construction, so nothing on screen changes — but `src` is an attribute the browser **acts on**, `javascript:` in it is a script, and `img-src 'self'` does not save a page from the URL its own API handed it. The rule lives in `api.ts`, once, applied to both icon-carrying payloads **as they arrive** rather than in each page that happens to draw one, because a page that forgot would be the entire defect; `//host/x` is somebody else's host despite the leading slash, so only a genuinely-ours path or an inline image is let through, and the test asserts both endpoints route through it. **(c)** The preview harness restated production's market 404 and rolled its own escaping, escaping `&` *after* `<` and `>`, so `a<b` was served as `a&amp;lt;b` — the entity instead of the character, on the only input that route ever reports, which means it was wrong on every use it had. Production's own route already called `_html.escape`; the harness does now. **The eight findings that remain are traced, not muted.** Two SSRF reports (High and Medium) follow a tainted value into `urllib` through `utils/discord_auth._read_json` and `utils/plans._post` — where the address is a module constant (`TOKEN_ENDPOINT`, and `LICENSE_API/{path}` with a literal path at all three call sites) and the tainted string is a **form field**, the redirect URI and the licence key, never the URL. `find_free_port` binds `0.0.0.0` in a one-statement probe of whether a port is free, and there is no service behind it. The two *server information exposure* reports are exception text returned to the **operator** from `_require_auth_api()`-gated endpoints, which is the point of those sentences: *"Discord did not accept it: …"* is what tells an administrator what to fix. One report is now guarded rather than fixed — the console's two DOM-XSS flags still appear, because the predicate that refuses a foreign URL sits upstream in `api.ts` and the scanner does not follow it across the file boundary. Two `localStorage` variables called `KEY` were also renamed: they hold storage key *names*, and a false alarm left in a scan is one more line to read past before the real one. 1672 tests pass, 2 skipped, and three guards were watched to fail by mutation — the identifier left unescaped, the fleet icon trusted again, and the harness escape removed.
- **📄 Both policies now say how the Bot was built in the words a listing form actually asks for — "AI coding assistants **and autonomous AI agents**" — and the sub-processors page names the AI providers that receive nothing**: the disclosure had one half that could go quietly false and one half that was simply out of date. The out-of-date half is the source claim: the Bot was written and is maintained with AI agents doing work, not only assistants completing lines, and a document that says less than the truth about its own provenance is the kind of sentence a reviewer notices. The half that could go quietly false is unchanged and still the more important one — **no part of the running Bot is AI**, no model is called, and nothing anybody sends reaches one — and it stays falsifiable rather than aspirational: `tests/test_legal.py` greps the tree for a provider in an import, a URL or a client constructor and fails, telling the author to update the disclosure in the same commit. What is new is that the *other* sentence is held too. `docs/legal/sub-processors.md` now says plainly what a reader of the AI section would otherwise have to infer — **there are no AI runtime sub-processors**, naming OpenAI, Anthropic, Google Vertex and AWS Bedrock as providers that receive no messages, no prompts, no guild metadata and no logs, while the process itself executes deterministic Python — and the guard list in `docs/legal/README.md` gained the clause in the same place it lists every other way the pair of documents can drift. Both policies were tightened in the same pass and both still fit inside Discord's 6,000-character embed ceiling, which is enforced rather than remembered: the ceiling is what the `/privacy` and `/terms` cards are rendered into, and a document that outgrows it loses its last section without saying so. **`docs/discord-directory.md` was rewritten from a statement about the repository into the walkthrough a submitter follows** — the eligibility checklist, the five portal steps in order, the copy to paste including a transparency and AI-disclosure section, and Discord's AI and Developer policy requirements — because the document's reader is somebody with the portal open, and "where this repo stands" is not a thing that page can answer for them.
- **🩹 `static/console/index.html` was reachable in production — Vite's dev entry, copied into the bundle by the build, served to anyone at a URL that loaded the app without its boot payload and painted an empty page**: found by probing the live deployment rather than by reading the config, and it is the same class of defect as a page that renders but says nothing true. `console/index.html` is the entry `vite dev` uses, and **`vite build` treats an HTML entry as a document to publish** — so a file whose own comment says *"this exists only so `vite dev` has an entry point, in production Flask renders the shell"* was in `static/`, which is served. Opening it loaded `app.js` with no `console-boot` block beside it, and that block is the only thing carrying the CSRF token and the signed-in operator — the session cookie is `HttpOnly`, so the script can never fetch either. The symptom was an empty page and one console error. **The build starts from `src/main.tsx` now**, which keeps the file's dev job and removes its public one; `vite` still serves the root `index.html` in development, because that is what dev does. **And the failure mode is answered rather than thrown**: the boot payload is read in `main.tsx` before the first render, so a stray load of the bundle — a bookmark of the asset, a cached URL, anything that gets here without the shell — renders a sentence naming `/owner` and a link to it, verified in a browser with the payload deliberately absent (correct text, correct link, no console errors). Two guards, one of which caught itself: the bundle must contain only `app.css`, `app.js` and `build.json`, and the config must declare `input: "src/main.tsx"` — asserted as the input it names rather than as the absence of the string `index.html`, because the comment explaining the fix contains that string, so the first version of the rule failed on its own documentation. **1,667 tests pass, 2 skipped.** Verified in production as well: Render is serving this commit (the deployed `build.json` hash is byte-identical to the committed one), every `/api/console/*` route answers **401 signed out**, and all six `/owner` deep links answer 302 to `/login` rather than the 404s and 500s the old build would have given.
- **🖥️ The operator's management site is private, real, and served by the bot itself — a bundled app with five pages, working switches and motion, replacing a public read-only page of prose that could never have been a management tool**: *"its a site for me to manage the bot and no one else... too much text no animation and nothing is working"*, and the diagnosis turned out to be architectural rather than cosmetic. The site that was deployed to `meowdev.pages.dev` was a **static Cloudflare host with no auth anywhere in it** — `grep` for `login|session|auth` across its script and eight templates finds the words only as *prose* — and its worker was read-only **by design**: `GET`/`HEAD` only, one allow-listed path (`/api/status`), cookies dropped. That is the correct policy for a public CDN host and it is also exactly why nothing there could ever work. **Your sign-in lives on the bot's own origin**, and `pages.dev` is on the public suffix list, so a cookie cannot be shared across it: a static host has no session, so every control it drew was a promise it could not keep, and every number it printed was a mirror of the private console one click away. So the management surface moved to where the session actually is. **`/owner` is now a bundled app** (`console/`, React + Vite + TypeScript + `motion`, built into `static/console/` and served by Flask) with five real pages — **Overview, Servers and a per-server drill-down, the Systems matrix, Ops, Changes** — against five new operator-only JSON endpoints (`/api/console/overview|fleet|server/<id>|systems|ops|changes`) that reuse the helpers the server-rendered console already had, so nothing about *what* the console knows changed. **The switches are the point of the surface and they are the dashboard's own endpoints** (`/api/toggle`, `/api/toggle-all`, with the CSRF token), optimistic so the knob moves under your hand, springing back with the server's own sentence if it refuses. **Every route is gated by a new `_require_operator_api`**, and it is deliberately not `_require_auth_api`: that answers *is somebody signed in*, which is the right question for one server and the wrong one for the whole fleet, so a one-time link — a genuine sign-in that belongs to one server — is refused **by scope**, exactly as `/owner` has always drawn the line on its first line. **Three defects were found by driving it rather than by reading it.** The health rows read `verdict`/`state` out of `site_guard`, `config_audit` and `uptime_status`, and **none of the three modules answers with either word** — all three rows printed an em dash on a card that otherwise looked fine; they read `status` now, with the explanatory `detail` kept as the tooltip, and two tests hold it: one asserts each block carries the key, the other reads the `.tsx` files, because Python cannot see which key a TypeScript file reads. The palette's **first** item ran `window.location.reload()`, so opening ⌘K and pressing Return threw the whole app away and reloaded the document — destinations come first now and the reload action is gone, since every page polls and the two that want a manual control own it. And `@app.route("/owner/<path:sub>")` **passed `sub=` to a view that did not accept it**, so `/owner` answered 200 while every deep link answered 500 — the app's own tests caught it, and it is the kind of bug that only shows up on the addresses nobody types. **The bundle is committed, which this repository otherwise refuses to do with generated output**, and the rule is kept in substance rather than in letter: the host that serves the console is a Python runtime on a free tier with no guaranteed Node, so a deploy that had to build the app could fail to build it — and `static/console/build.json` records a hash of `console/src` plus its config, recomputed by `tests/test_console.py`, so an edited-and-unrebuilt console fails the suite instead of shipping a copy that is quietly one commit behind. **`templates/owner.html` and `tests/test_owner_console.py` are deleted** rather than left beside the new thing, and **six test files that pinned server-rendered markup were re-pointed at where the data now lives** — the uptime rows to the payload and the two source files that fetch it, the gateway badge to the shell's boot payload, the theme and shell sweeps to `console.html`, the selling walker to a direct assertion that this console links to no shop page in either state. Verified through the real surface: the shipped bundle driven in a browser against payloads captured from the actual view functions — the fleet lists both servers with their system counts, opening one shows 11 switches at *11 of 11 on*, flipping one posts `{guild: 777001, module: "leveling", on: false}`, the row flips, the heading follows to **10 of 11 on** and the toast names the server; the matrix renders 11 systems × 2 servers as 22 real cells with no unknowns; Ops shows the tail, Changes quotes 40 entries, the palette opens with focus in its input and Return **navigates** instead of reloading, and the theme picker moves `data-theme`, `localStorage` and the address-bar colour together with no console errors. **1,666 tests pass, 2 skipped**, 31 of them new in `tests/test_console.py`; two guards watched to fail by mutation (a one-character hex change and the scope check loosened to admit a one-time link). **Still open and worth saying plainly:** the public hub is *still deployed* at `meowdev.pages.dev` and still shows the read-only mirror this entry is about — retiring it to a sign-in door is a change to the hub's own eight pages and ~650 lines of its tests, which is its own pass rather than a corner of this one.
- **🐌 The console said "Unknown server" because the gateway cache it read is empty for the first minute after every restart, and the badge said "Live" through a reconnect because it was reading a flag that never clears — and the lag turned out not to be the server at all**: three reports, one cause each, and the measurement changed the answer to the third. **A server's name was being read from Discord's gateway cache and nowhere else.** `_known_guilds()` returned `BOT_INSTANCE.guilds` and nothing more, and that cache is **empty in a fresh process** until the handshake finishes — so for tens of seconds after every deploy or wake-up *every* console page was headed `Unknown server`, and an operator signing in with Discord was offered **no servers to pick from at all**, because `_visible_guilds()` derives from the same list. On a free-tier host that stops the process when it is idle, that window is the window a visitor is most likely to arrive in, which is why "sometimes wrong" read as "always wrong". `utils/guild_directory.py` writes the names down — at boot, on a join, and on any page render that found one — and `_known_guilds()` falls back to it, live rows first so a rename still shows immediately. **A leave event must forget the row**, because the picker is built from that list: `forget` runs on the removal and `reconcile` runs on every boot, which between them cover both ways a server goes away — while the bot is connected, and while the process was down. **`reconcile` refuses an empty guild list** rather than trusting it, and that guard is the whole reason it is a function: `bot.guilds` is empty during the exact window this module exists to cover, so a reconcile that believed it would delete every remembered server at the moment they are the only copy, turning the fix into a worse version of the bug. **Two defects in my own module were found by its tests rather than by reading it.** `reconcile` first passed `guild.member_count or 0`, and `Guild.member_count` is `None` without the members intent — which this bot keeps off unless asked — so the boot reconcile would have replaced every server's real count with a zero nobody measured, silently, for every server at once; `None` now means *Discord did not say* and keeps what is stored, while `0` remains a legitimate count for an empty server. And `remember` compared against a cache it had not loaded, so an unloaded cache looked *exactly* like "no row" and every call looked like a change — the console rewrote the row on every page load — so it reads first and then compares. **The badge was a fact about the past wearing the word for the present.** Both consoles compared `bot_ready`, which is `_has_started`: a latch, set on the first successful login and never cleared, so `{% if bot_ready %}` painted **"Live"** while the gateway was reconnecting — and the owner console's own live poll repainted it from the same latch every fifteen seconds, which is the one case a status badge is looked at for. There are three states now (`Live` / `Reconnecting` / `Bot offline`) drawn from `_gateway_state()`, which is `bot.is_ready()` — the `/health` endpoint has carried that distinction and documented why since before this pass, and the badge was the one surface not reading it. The span moved into `templates/gateway_badge.html` and its painter into `static/gateway.js`, **one copy for both consoles**, because two copies is how they drifted in the first place: they were the same rule spelled twice and only one of them had gone stale. The script polls `/health` itself, refreshes on a tab returning to view rather than after the interval, and **falls back to the offline state on a value it does not recognise** — a badge stuck on Live is the defect. The fleet console sets `MEOW_GATEWAY_FED` and feeds the badge from the `/health` poll it already runs, so one page does not open two requests to the same address. **The lag was not the server, and saying so took a measurement rather than a change.** All seven console routes render in **0.7–1.0 ms** warm (24 ms for the first, which is Jinja parsing an 185 KB template), `/health` answers in 0.2 ms, and a module switch already returns at once — `_resync_commands` queues the publish for three seconds later and the page waits on a timestamp advancing, not on a sleep. The console *is* 141 KB of HTML per page, which compresses **4.3×** (1,026,178 bytes walking all seven once → 238,370), and I nearly added `gzip` in Flask for it — then checked production first and found **`content-encoding: br` already on both `meow-bot.pages.dev/login` and Render's own host**, so Cloudflare and Render are both compressing at the edge and the layer would have been work with no user-visible effect. What is left is the free tier's cold start (30–50 s after ~15 idle minutes) and Discord's own propagation plus the deliberate three-second settle — properties of the plan and of Discord, not of this code. **The policies say how it was built, and the claim in them is checked against the tree.** Both documents are **Version 4** with an AI section, and the second half of it is the half that could quietly become false: *no part of the running Bot is AI* is now grepped for — a provider named in an import, a URL or a client constructor fails the test and says to update the disclosure in the same commit. The first half is about the source, not about anybody's data, which is the only way the sentence is both true and worth printing. **`docs/discord-directory.md` is what to paste and what no code can supply**: the App Directory's requirements with how each is held, the listing copy derived from the registry, and the two gates that need the operator — a **verified** application (which needs the app in 100+ servers) and two-factor on the account. Server Discovery is documented as closed rather than configured, because it wants **1,000 members** and no amount of code supplies a member count. **Two of my own claims in that document were wrong within the hour**, which is why it has a guard now: it told a submitter to look for the invite links in `cogs/help.py`, where they have never been (they are `INVITE_PERMISSIONS` and `MINIMAL_INVITE_PERMISSIONS` in `utils/discord_auth.py`), and the character count it quoted was 195 rather than 178 — so the numbers are asserted against the registry, and the first version of that assertion failed on text that was present and merely folded across a line, which is what a document is. **One trap is worth recording because it cost the most time of anything here.** After mutating each source file to watch its guard fail, restoring it with `cp` left a `.pyc` whose recorded mtime and size still matched, so the *passed tests disagreed with the source on disk*: `_badge_state` was correct in the file and executing the mutated bytecode, and `inspect.getsource` showed the right code while `__code__.co_names` showed `('_bot_ready', '_BADGE_STATES')`. Deleting `__pycache__` fixed it. Source-level diffs and `diff -q` cannot see this — **a mutation run has to clear the bytecode cache, not just put the file back**. Verified through the real surfaces: the three badge states rendered by the actual Flask harness (`PREVIEW_GATEWAY=connected|reconnecting` now picks which one the preview draws, the way `PREVIEW_PING` already picked the latency — the middleware had no way to show the middle state before), and `static/gateway.js` driven in a real browser with a stand-in `/health`: the page opens painting `badge-online`/"Live" and is repainted to `badge-warn`/"Reconnecting" **by the script's own first read**, with `MeowGateway.paint` then asserting all three states, the pulse dot added for `connected` and removed for the other two, and an unrecognised value landing on offline; no console errors. **1,660 tests pass, 2 skipped**, with 16 new in `tests/test_guild_directory.py` and 5 in `tests/test_legal.py`; five guards watched to fail by mutation (the directory fallback removed, the empty-list refusal removed, the unknown count written as zero, the badge put back on the latch, the leave hook removed). **Two tests were asserting the wrong thing and were fixed rather than relaxed**: the site's page test hardcoded `September 19, 2026` while its own name said the date comes from the document (it reads the document now), and the systems page's promise was pinned to a sentence that a copy pass had already shortened (it asserts both halves of the promise). One thing worth knowing for the next addition: **both documents sit within ~20–35 characters of Discord's 6,000-character embed ceiling** — PRIVACY was at 5,975 of 6,000 before this pass — so anything added to either must be paid for in the same commit or a section drops off the `/privacy` card.

- **📄 The landing page is three pages now — a front door, `/systems` and `/start` — sharing one layout, and the front door is down from a 68 KB scroll that answered everything to a 22 KB page that answers one question**: *"its soo much text and info it can get confusing use multiple pages"*, and the diagnosis was right about the cause: `/` was one document that answered *what is this*, *what does it do*, *how do I get it* and *how do I sign in* whether or not the reader had asked any of them, so the four questions were one scroll and each one was buried in the other three. **What each page is now is a decision about which single question it answers.** `/` is the front door — the headline and the live status, the deployment's own four counts, the *shape* of what is in it (a count, a grid of names, the name of the system that has no toggle), three numbered lines, and the one button. `/systems` is what is in the bot — every system with the commands it declares, the panel a reader can operate to check the page's one unverifiable claim, and the two dashboard previews with `figures.js` re-reading them. `/start` is how to add it — the three steps with both grants side by side, the ways in, and the closing call to action. **The head, the navigation, the phone menu and the footer moved into `templates/site_base.html`**, with the header in its own partial, so a nav link cannot be added to two pages and not the third — the same rule the shared registry already enforces for counts, applied to chrome. The layout is a `{% extends %}` rather than three copies, and the hero band is the base's too (a page fills in its headline; the plate, the header and the reveal are the same), with a `.lp-hero-compact` modifier for the two pages whose opening is a heading and a sentence rather than a field to land in. **The old anchors stayed put**, and that is not nostalgia: a fragment is never sent to the server, so no redirect can rescue `/#features` — the only way a link somebody pasted months ago still lands somewhere is for that section to still be at that address, so `/` keeps `features`, `start`, `ways-in` and `add` on its teasers and the full versions live one click further on. Every cross-page link is absolute for the same reason (`#features` now means a different section on three different addresses), the shared menu is asserted to contain no bare fragment, and a test walks every `href` with a fragment to the page it names and insists the id is there — because **the way this change breaks is not a 404**: it is a working page whose link scrolls nowhere, with nothing in any console and nothing a crawl would notice. **Two defects came out of rendering it rather than reading it.** The first was mine and it was exactly the class this file keeps finding: the front door prints *12 systems, 11 of them switchable* and then showed **eleven cards**, which is a heading and a list that disagree to anybody who counts and no way to find out what the twelfth is. The switchable list and the total differ by the system that cannot be switched off, so that system is now **named from the registry** (`features.always_on_names()`, read by `/systems` too) instead of being left as an unexplained gap — and the test for it renders, because the name *is* the registry's, so a template containing it verbatim would be the defect the test is looking for. The second is that **the themes never reached the landing page at all**: `body.landing` declared its own `--lp-accent: #5b5ef0` beside `:root`'s `--accent`, a literal second copy of the accent that a theme could not reach, so picking Sakura recoloured every button and left all nineteen of that block's own accents indigo — two palettes on one page, which is the mistake `--accent-rgb` was introduced to end one level up. It is `var(--accent)` now, with the four hero dots that were hardcoded hex fixed with it, and a browser confirms Forest gives `--lp-accent: #2fa06b` and an `accent-text` nav underline. **Six test files pinned the one-page shape and each was re-pointed at the page that now owns what it guards** — the figure tiles and the figures `index.html` check to `/systems`, the third-party and double-slash sweeps to all three pages plus the partials, the stylesheet and reveal-script checks to the layout with the pages asserted to reach it (a loop over the old three would have passed by covering only the consoles, and `assertIn` on a string that moved into an include fails loudly, which is how that was caught). **The selling-surface guard follows the new indirection rather than being loosened by it**: the three routes spread in `_site_context()` instead of spelling `selling=`, so the walker accepts the provider *and* a new test asserts that provider really carries the flag — without it, an emptied helper would satisfy every page at once. Nine new guards, each watched to fail by mutation: a dropped anchor, a bare fragment in the shared menu, the full list grown back onto the front door, a page declaring one id twice, `_site_context()` losing the gate, and `/systems` dropped from the builder's page table. Verified by driving all three built pages in a browser: the demo walks **90 → 64 → 0** commands as systems come off with the readout and the per-system On/Off labels following, the empty state replaces the list at zero, reset restores it, `/start` offers both grants (`permissions=8` and the smaller bitfield) and says *Two ways in* on a deployment with no OAuth secret because that is what it has, the theme picker works on all three, and each page marks its own nav link with `aria-current`. 1,642 tests pass, 2 skipped. **Left alone deliberately**: the sitemap still names `systems.html` and `start.html` with their extensions, which Cloudflare 308-redirects to the clean URLs — the same wart `/pricing.html` has had since it was written, and fixing it means threading the computed page list through `_sitemap`, which is pure and heavily tested. Worth its own pass rather than a corner of this one.
- **🎨 The product has four themes now instead of one indigo, and the control that picks one costs no flash of the wrong colour on any of the eight pages**: *"smooth and good looking with multiple themes to choose from"*, and the interesting part was not the four palettes but the three files that each had to agree about them. **A theme is one idea written down three times** — the stylesheet decides what it *is*, the partial decides what a visitor can *pick*, and the script decides what is *remembered* — and every failure of it is a disagreement between those three, which is the same defect class this repository keeps finding in pairs of renderers. Add a theme to the menu and not the sheet and the menu offers a dead option; hand the script a name the sheet never declares and a stored choice reverts with nothing on screen to say so. So each theme is six tokens and **the same six**, and four tests hold the three copies together: the sheet defines every theme and nothing else, no block is missing a token another block has (a theme that forgets `--accent-rgb` keeps the default hue inside its glows and rings while its buttons change — which reads as a wrong *colour*, not as a missing line), the picker offers exactly the themes that exist, and the script's own list matches both. **The script has to run before the first paint, and that is a property of where it sits rather than of what it does**, so it is a plain `<script>` in `<head>` rather than a deferred one — deferred, a visitor who chose Sakura watches every navigation arrive indigo and turn rose — which is why it is its own small file instead of being folded into `static/motion.js`, and why the test asserts on the *absence of `defer`* rather than on the URL. Nothing about it is allowed to break a page: with `localStorage` refused (private mode, blocked cookies, a `file://` open) it applies the default and never throws, and a stored name that no longer exists in `THEMES` falls back rather than being set on `<html>` as an attribute no stylesheet matches — which would leave the page default while the picker, reading the same value, said otherwise. **`midnight` is `:root` and deliberately has no block of its own**: a visitor who has chosen nothing, and one whose choice has since been retired, both get it, and a test fails if the default ever grows a block. **Two defects came out of driving it rather than reading it.** The first was mine and it was a colour written down twice, which is the defect I had spent the pass before finding in other people's code: `theme.js` was overwriting each page's `<meta name="theme-color">` with one hardcoded `#0c0c0d`, and the eight templates declare **three different values on purpose** (`#08080a` on the landing page, `#0b0e13` on the three document pages, `#090c10` on the two consoles) — because a theme moves the *accent* and the page background is a per-page constant. So every page had begun advertising a fourth colour that matched none of their backgrounds. The meta is the page's now and the script touches it no more, with a test that says why. The second was a test that **passed for the wrong reason**: the first version asserted the string `theme-color` appeared nowhere in the script, which the explanatory comment I had just written about it made fail — it now asserts what the code *does* (no selector for the meta, no write of `content`), because a test that forbids a word forbids the paragraph explaining why the word is gone. Verified in a real browser rather than in the markup: opening the picker, choosing each of the four in turn, and reading the computed `--accent`, the trigger's label and swatch, `aria-pressed`, `localStorage`, and the page's `theme-color` at every step — the accent reaches the primary button's background **and its glow** (a 0.12s transition is mid-flight if you read it in the same tick, which is exactly how that one looked broken at first), `theme-color` stays `#08080a` through all four, picking `midnight` removes the attribute, and the menu opens and closes. No console or page errors. **Seven guards, each watched to fail by mutation**: an option offered with no palette, a deferred theme script, a theme block missing a token, a swatch colour reused, a retired theme accepted from storage, the script rewriting `theme-color`, and a hook the script queries that the partial never renders. 1,628 tests pass, 2 skipped, 19 of them new in `tests/test_theme.py`.
- **🔢 There is one answer now to *how many systems* and *how many commands* — twelve and ninety, on every surface — the footer stops linking a locked door, and the landing page stops publishing the operator's own laptop as the deployment**: three screenshots, three defects, and the first two were one defect wearing different clothes. **The front page said eleven systems and a hundred commands while the hub a click away said twelve and ninety.** Neither count was broken: *systems* was the dashboard's module list on one surface and the feature registry on the next, and *commands* was every declared string — `/market buy` counted as its own command — against the distinct names Discord's picker actually publishes. Each was defensible alone and together they were a page arguing with itself, which is the failure `utils/features.py` exists to prevent. The definitions now live in the registry and nowhere else (`system_count`, `command_count`, and the `ROOT_COMMANDS` set they are derived from), and both call sites read them: `keep_alive._gateway_counts`, `keep_alive._home_stats` — which had each worked the same two numbers out separately, the second time in a different unit — and the hub's own counts. A page may show two numbers about one word only when both are labelled, which the systems page already did and the front page now does: **twelve systems, eleven of them switchable**, because the always-on system is the difference and saying so is cheaper than explaining a missing card. The live command tree is deliberately not consulted for the marketing figure any more: a tree measured inside a running process is a fact about that process, which is how one visit could print a hundred and the next ninety. **The footer's Source code and GitHub links pointed at a private repository**, so every visitor got GitHub's *this is not the web page you are looking for* — on the footer of the page that asks them to trust this bot with their server, beside links that work. `SOURCE_REPOSITORY` is empty now and every template asks whether there is one, the privacy policy names Cloudflare (which does see a visitor's IP, and was never disclosed) in place of GitHub (which no longer serves anything to anyone), and `/legal` stops sending members to the repository for the rest of a truncated document — it sends them to the published page, which is the thing that exists. Two internal paths left the public text with it: the policy's `docs/legal/data-requests.md` and the legal footer's note that the procedure lives there, both of which told a reader to go and open a file they cannot reach. **And the third, found by reading the built page rather than the diff**: the landing page's activity tiles are drawn server-side from the tracker, and a build renders through the real app — so `467 messages this week`, this laptop's own database, was rendered into a public page as the deployment's figures. The hub's pages have applied that rule from the day they were written (`read_tracker=not static_copy`) and `_home_page` never did. Absent now, not zero. Verified the way each was found: the build's output carries twelve and ninety and a dash for every unread window, no `github.com` anywhere in the four pages, the legal embed stays inside Discord's ceiling with no section dropped, and the front page renders in the browser with its two counts and its four blank activity tiles. Five guards, each watched to fail by mutation: the tracker read put back, the module count put back, the entry body re-published, the cursor rules deleted, and a setting name planted in a headline to prove the hub's own guard can still see one. 1,609 tests pass (209 subtests), 2 skipped.
- **🩹 The site's first real publish failed and would have shipped nothing, because the landing page's new counts raised over a table that a build clone does not have — and the copy that answered 500 was mine**: the figures read was written against a deployment's own database and the *build* renders through the real app, so `_scalar` reached `SELECT … FROM activity_messages` in a checkout where **`bot.db` is git-ignored and therefore absent**. The Pages build answered exactly that — *"no such table: activity_messages"*, ``/ answered 500 while building index.html — the site would ship a broken page`` — and because the builder refuses to publish a page that answered 500, the deploy failed and the site stayed on the commit before it. **A marketing page must not be brought down by a table it only wanted a count from**, so the read now reports the module's own answer for it: a window with no schema to count in is **absent, not zero**, which is the rule `utils/figures.py` already states for a sleeping host at its extreme. **Both renderers were getting that wrong too, and only one of them was mine.** `_activity_tiles` and the page's own script each fell back to `"0"`, so an unread window was spelled as a *measurement* by whichever of the two painted last — **0 messages** under a card whose module spends a paragraph explaining why it never says that. Both fall back to the em dash the control figures already used. Verified the way the failure was found: the build command CI runs, run against a **clean clone made from `git ls-files`** — no `bot.db`, which is what Pages clones — now builds all four pages, and the workflow's own five post-build checks pass on its output (invite link, `_headers` with the CSP and `frame-ancestors`, the worker's two config files, the cat cursor, and no source tree in the 20 uploaded files). The front page renders with `—` for every figure it has not read and `11`/`100` for the two it can read from the feature registry, and the live `/api/figures` fills the same card in from the host that has a database. Three guards were watched to fail by mutation: the `OperationalError` catch narrowed to an exception that never fires, the server tile's fallback, and the script's. 1,551 tests pass (207 subtests), 2 skipped, 4 new. **Worth recording from the same deploy**: Render took `df1084c` on its own (`new_commit`, finished, previous deployment deactivated) and serves it — `/api/figures` answers 200 with 3 servers, 42 members and 467 messages this week, and all seven dashboard routes and all four console routes answer 302 rather than 404. **GitHub Actions is the thing that cannot publish right now**, and not for a code reason: both workflows' jobs print *"The job was not started because recent account payments have failed or your spending limit needs to be increased"*, so `Site` never reaches its upload step and `Tests` never runs at all. The Pages project's own Git integration is the working path in the meantime, which is what this fix unblocks.
- **📄 Neither console is one long page any more, the site stops advertising what it is giving away, and the operator's console finally answers what the bot is *doing* rather than only what it is standing on**: four complaints that were one habit — a page answering a question about *everything* because splitting it was never worth a commit. **The dashboard is seven pages and the console is four.** `/dashboard` was 3,285 lines and the owner's console was one 761-line scroll, so "the composer" and "the status panel" were the same address, the rail was a scroll spy guessing which of nine sections the reader was looking at, and the palette's actions had to name a section that might not be on the page. Both consoles now have real pages (`DASHBOARD_PAGES`, `OWNER_PAGES`), each route renders one section, and the rail is seven links whose current page is `aria-current` — which the server sets and no script can disagree with. The panels were written as guarded IIFEs for exactly this, so the split is two conditions and three guards per page rather than a rewrite: the composer's listeners only register where a composer exists, and the scheduler's poll only starts where a queue does. A palette action that lives on another page now travels as a hash (`/dashboard/systems#btn-enable-all`) and the destination focuses it on arrival, so "Enable every system" from the Status page lands on the button instead of at the top of a page containing it. **The front page stopped describing a shop.** The landing page carried a **PLANS** band headed *"Everything is free today"* with a price-shaped card under it — leftover premium furniture on a product with `PLAN_CHECKOUT` off, and the one thing a visitor scrolling to the bottom of the front door should not find. It is gone, and what replaced the two preview cards' furniture is the deployment's own counts: `utils/figures.py` answers with **counts and never names** (a landing page may say *1,669 people reached*; it may not say which twelve — the per-channel and per-member breakdowns stay behind the dashboard's sign-in), reports **absent as absent rather than as zero** (a sleeping free-tier host has no gateway to ask, and a confident *0 servers* reads as a bot nobody uses), reads the tracker's tables synchronously on the cached path the rest of the web side already uses, and repeats the tracker's window arithmetic rather than importing it — with `tests/test_figures.py` comparing the two, because "last 7 days" has to mean the same seven days `/activity` and the weekly digest mean. **The console's Infrastructure page gained the half a third party cannot answer.** UptimeRobot's monitors say whether a host answers; nothing there knows whether anybody uses what it hosts. *What the bot is doing* is drawn from the same payload the landing page draws and `/api/figures` serves — servers, members, and the tracker's messages, voice, people and channels for **Today / Last 7 days / Last 30 days / All time** — server-rendered, so it says something true before its own script has run, with the window buttons switching between numbers already in the payload rather than asking for them. **And the box beside it was lying, which is what "fix the box" was about.** *This deployment* read **"Read when this page was rendered"** over three rows of which exactly one was ever re-read: the page polls `/health` every fifteen seconds for the gateway figure, and the site-publishing verdict and the configuration count sat beside it as old as the page load. All three rows are painted from that one poll now, the stamp says *re-read just now* rather than naming a time it did not read at, the first read happens on load rather than fifteen seconds later, and the dashboard's own copy of the card — which has no such poll — keeps its honest *"Read at render time"* wording instead of borrowing the console's. **Three defects came out of the split, two of them in the guards rather than the code.** `paintDeployment` re-stamped the card on an answer that carried neither block, claiming a read of rows it had not read — the same defect one layer down, a time that is true of the request and false of the readings under it found by running the painter in a real engine against a stubbed DOM rather than by reading it. A test that pinned the *identity page's* profile form was still asking `/dashboard` for it, which is the split working: it follows the form to `/dashboard/identity` now. And the selling-surface test that guarded the palette's dangling comma matched the entries the array happened to contain, so it broke the moment the array was restructured — it asserts the gate's **shape** instead (a selling gate inside a script opens where a statement opened and hands nothing to a shared list, and the array literal carries no conditional at all), which is the rule that actually keeps `}, ];` out of the page's script, and it was watched to fail by moving the boundary to a comma. 1,547 tests pass (194 subtests), 2 skipped, 19 of them new in `tests/test_figures.py`. **Considered and not done:** `scripts/build_site.py` still publishes `/pricing` while selling is off. Nothing links to it, no template offers it and its content is a free product's page that says paid plans may come later — which is what the operator asked to keep — but it is still a reachable orphan, and publishing it conditionally means threading a computed page list through `rewrite_url`, which is pure and heavily tested. Worth its own pass rather than a corner of this one.
- **🧾 "Commands in this server" was a promise about Discord's picker answered by our own database, so the panel now records what Discord actually came back with — and it stopped making you wait four seconds for a number it already had**: the panel is named for Discord's command list and says in its own copy that a command whose system is off is *"not in this server's picker at all"*. Both of its figures came from the local module cache, which is what the server **asked for**: ninety whether the republish had landed, was still behind its three-second settle, or had been refused outright by a spent daily write budget. The one place a refusal was visible at all was a line in the process ring buffer, behind a button on a different page, and the page's only gesture at any of it was a flat four-second timer that re-read the same local numbers — the wait changed nothing, which is why "I turn it off and it comes back after some time" had no answer on screen. **The missing half is `utils/command_publish.py`**: the publisher writes the outcome of every pass down and the page reads it, at the one choke point every publish already goes through (`_sync_scope`, so boot, both drains, the bulk publish and every module switch are covered by construction, plus the two paths that raise and never return a status at all). Four facts, each exactly true or honestly absent — whether Discord's copy was *read*, how many commands are **still to write**, when the pass finished, and Discord's own words when something went wrong — reported in four tones the panel colours with the dots the Status panel already uses. **The wording has one author.** This box is painted twice, by the server and by the page's own refresh, and the Status panel is the object lesson: two renderers that each spell the sentence is how the same box says two different things depending on which one painted it. So the template prints `command_scope_publish.text` and nothing else, and a test fails if the template ever starts composing the sentence from the numbers. **A count that is ambiguous is not printed.** A pass refused part-way through its writes cannot know what Discord holds — some of the writes added a command and some replaced one — so `held` is `None` there, because a figure under a heading is read as a measurement and an approximate measurement is worse than no measurement. **Reading this wrote a real bug into the publisher and then caught it.** `_sync_scope` has been reporting `registered = total - pending`, and `total` counts a *stale* command twice — once in what Discord already holds and again in the names to write — so the boot log's per-server count has been over by the number of changed commands since it was written. It is now `len(registered) - removed + len(missing)`, the three buckets being disjoint by construction. **And the four seconds are gone, replaced by a fact.** `/api/toggle` answers with the timestamp of the publish it supersedes and the panel asks again until that timestamp is *newer* — two readings of the server's own clock, never the browser's against the server's, because a host in another region would otherwise make the wait forever or give up early. A refused publish is a recorded outcome with a reason, so there is nothing left to wait out; a long wait now says so instead of leaving a line that still reads "checked four minutes ago" as if the click did nothing. **Three smaller ones came out of building it.** The ledger's first test run filed the publisher's records under `"<Guild id=… name=…>"` while the panel looked for an integer, so every server would have read as *never published* — the key unwraps a guild now, and the test that caught it pins a guild and its id to one record. The shell's own guard — *every id the page's script looks up must exist in the rendered page* — caught a third renderer immediately: the test helper that renders the page had not been given the reading, which is the same drift `scripts/preview_dashboard.py` warns about, so the helper builds it from the module rather than restating it and the harness records a real pass so its preview shows the line a deployment shows. And one existing assertion had to be made precise rather than weaker: `test_a_different_rulebook_reaches_the_page` forbade the string `2000` anywhere after the stylesheet as a proxy for "the template states no limit of its own", so it tripped over a two-second poll interval the moment one existed; it now looks in the limits declaration and at the markup attributes, which is what it always meant. Verified by driving the real panel in a browser through all four states and the whole refresh path: the server render carries the line before any script runs (green dot, "Discord holds 90 commands, matching what this server's switches say"), flipping a switch leaves the counts instant and then walks the line from *matching* to **"Discord still needs 3 commands written — 2 refused (a, b), and the next pass is in 45 seconds"** with a gold dot, only after a re-read answers with a newer stamp than the click, and all four tones paint the right colour with the panel keeping its last reading rather than blanking when an answer carries none. No console or page errors. Mutations watched to fail: the old count arithmetic, an unread scope recorded as agreement, the raise path left unwritten, and the ledger keyed by the object rather than its id. **Reading it back with fresh eyes found the clause that could never be right, and a page borrowing another scope's reading.** The one line `describe()` gained its `expected` argument to print — a count that disagrees with the switches — was composed as an opening with the disagreement *appended*, so it read **"Discord holds 87 commands, matching what this server's switches say. Discord holds 87 — 3 fewer than this server's switches say it should"**: it claimed agreement and named a disagreement in the same breath, and that is reachable on the first page load after any switch flip, because the panel's own figures move immediately while the last publish's do not. It is now one of three mutually exclusive shapes — agrees, disagrees, or no count to compare — so no sentence can say both. **And a page with no server selected was reading Discord's *shared* lane.** The route answers "no server" with the id `0` and renders the default switches, and the line mapped that sentinel onto `None`, which is Discord's global scope — the lane that holds the two DM-safe commands — so an unscoped page reported "Discord holds 2 — 2 more than this server's switches say it should" under a heading whose figures were the defaults, about a server it was not showing. The id is handed over as it is now, and a scope nobody has published has no reading, which is the honest answer. Both were driven through the **real route** before and after, and both guards watched to fail by mutation. 1,509 tests pass (1,511 under `unittest` discovery), 2 skipped, 2 more than the pass above.
- **🔎 The monitors panel read "Not connected" beside "read 0 seconds ago", because `at` is stamped by every *attempt* rather than by every reading**: the first thing the UptimeRobot integration did on a real deployment was contradict itself in the two lines a reader trusts most. `check()` sets `at` on all four of its outcomes — including the one where no key exists and **nothing is asked at all** — and `snapshot()` aged it unconditionally, so a panel with no data said a reading had been taken seconds ago while the box underneath said *"Not connected: set UPTIMEROBOT_API_KEY"*. Both sentences were true of different things, and the one that looks like a measurement is the one people believe. The age line now describes **the reading on screen**: with rows to age it counts them; with nothing to age it says `not connected`, or `no reading` when the API refused or could not be reached, instead of counting up from a clock the API never touched. **And the rows now carry their own timestamp.** `record()` keeps the last good readings through a failed refresh — deliberately, so a bad minute at UptimeRobot does not read as "you have no monitors" — but it kept them while stamping them with the time of the *attempt*, so a twenty-minute-old reading was called "read just now" in one line and "the last reading" in the next; `at` travels with the rows now. The sentence in the box uses the same worder as the line above it, because `taken 1200 seconds ago` beside `read 20 minutes ago` is one fact spelled twice — the exact defect the age line itself was fixed for a pass earlier, still living one line below it. `/health` stops publishing `checked_seconds_ago` when there is nothing to age, since an unauthenticated endpoint reporting "checked 0 seconds ago" on a deployment that has never asked is the same lie in a different place. **One more, found by driving the page rather than reading it:** the panel has two renderers — the server's and the script's — and the script appended a chip naming the setting to paste on top of a `detail` sentence that already names it, so pressing **Refresh** printed the variable a second time as a stray token after a full stop. The name belongs to the module that composes the sentence, so the template must not spell it, and a test says so. Verified by driving the real panel in a browser through all four states in one sitting — answering (2 rows, `read 1 minute ago`), the unconfigured payload (`0 rows`, `not connected`, no false claim), a return to answering, and a **Refresh** at each step — with no console errors, and by mutating each fix to watch its test fail: the rows losing their timestamp, the age line trusting `at` unconditionally, the detail sentence going back to raw seconds, and the script re-adding the chip. 1,476 tests pass (1,478 under `unittest` discovery), 2 skipped, 4 of them new.
- **💸 Nothing is for sale, so nothing links to the shop — on any of the six surfaces — and the page that explains that no longer quotes a price at you**: the gate added last week hid the tiers and the Buy buttons, and left every *link* standing. The dashboard's topbar and its command palette, the owner console, the landing page's nav in three places and its "See what you get" button, and the policy pages' nav and footer all pointed at `/pricing`, so the shop was one click away from everywhere and its entire content was that there is no shop. Worse, two of those links were in a worse state than visible: the pricing page's own free card still read **`₹0` beside a `/mo`** — a price tag *and* a billing period, on the one element whose job is to say the product is free. There is no number on it now, and no tier chip either; the band reads `Free · Everything included` and nothing else. **Gating the templates alone would have broken the other direction, and that is the half worth reading.** Jinja reads a missing name as false, so a route that forgets to pass the flag looks *perfectly correct* while the product is free and silently loses the link the day it is not — and three routes never passed it: the dashboard, the owner console and the policy pages. The flag reaches all five now, `scripts/preview_dashboard.py` passes it too (it renders two of those templates itself, and a harness that agrees about the payload and disagrees about a field is where every defect in that file has come from), and `tests/test_selling_surface.py` pins the two halves separately because they fail differently — the **templates** by rendering each one both ways, and the **wiring** by walking every `render_template(...)` call in `keep_alive.py` with a paren-matching reader and insisting the templates that show a topbar are never rendered without it. Four surfaces are driven through the real routes instead, with `plans.selling_enabled()` patched, and the reason that list is four and not six is recorded in the test: `/dashboard` and `/owner` need a session. Its own trap is recorded too — **the landing page answers a `curl` user agent with one health line** rather than the document, because that is what a monitor polls, so a check that does not send a browser's user agent inspects a 34-byte string and concludes the links are gone. Verified by switching the real environment variable both ways and reading all six pages: with selling off, **zero** occurrences of `/pricing`, of `₹`, and of `/mo` anywhere; with `PLAN_CHECKOUT=1`, every link back where it belongs — 4 on the landing page (menu nav, main nav, CTA, footer), 1 on the dashboard *and* 1 in its palette, 1 on the console, 1 on the pricing page's own nav, 2 each on the policies — plus the three tier prices and their periods. Each guard was watched to fail by mutation: the owner route's flag removed, one link un-gated, and the free card's price restored. **And the channel picker from your screenshot was white rectangles.** *"Fix the UI/UX"* showed rows painted `#fff` with a black `✓` and no channel name, and the cause was that each row is a **`<button>`** — the right element for a thing you press — which arrives from the browser with its own light background and its own black text, and `.picker-row` had never turned either off. Nothing about `display: flex` does that, and it is the same defect in `.mention-result`, so the tag picker was blank too; the `✓` was the only thing painted in a colour the browser did not supply, which is what made it read as a broken stylesheet rather than as two overlapping backgrounds. Both now unset the native control by hand — `background`, `color`, `font`, `border` *and* **`appearance`**, since `background` alone is enough in Chromium and not on Safari. Measured in a browser under the real sheets rather than eyeballed: both rows compute `appearance: none`, the row background is transparent so the card's `rgb(23,23,26)` shows through, the selected row takes the accent tint, and the channel name is `rgb(244,242,239)` on that surface — a contrast of **8.76:1** where it had been near-white on white. 1,472 tests pass (1,474 under `unittest` discovery), 2 skipped, 8 of them new.
- **🔐 The dashboard had two doors into one room and the looser one decided, so a server's moderators could sign in to it — and every picture it showed was blocked by its own security policy**: two reports, one afternoon, and the second turned out to explain weeks of "the logo is still broken". **The permission bar was stated twice and enforced at two different heights.** A one-time link is only minted for somebody with **Administrator**, but `Sign in with Discord` accepted **Manage Server** as well (`ADMIN_BITS = MANAGE_GUILD | ADMINISTRATOR`), and Manage Server is the bit Discord hands to moderators — servers routinely grant it to a whole staff role. So anyone on that role could open a server's dashboard and reach every one of its eleven system switches, its command list, and the bot's name, picture and bio *in that server*, while the link beside it refused exactly those people. The bar is `ADMINISTRATOR` (or being the owner) now, so both ways in ask the same thing, and a test pins them together so whoever loosens one has to loosen the other in the same commit and will read the failure while doing it. **The images were a single defect seen from four places.** Our own `Content-Security-Policy` sets `img-src 'self' data:` — worth keeping, it is what stops a page whose template was tampered with from dialling out — and every template was handing the browser a `cdn.discordapp.com` URL: the server icons on `/servers`, the signed-in person's avatar in the header, and the bot's own avatar fallback, which is why the fix made one pass earlier found the avatar *correctly* and still rendered a broken image. Uploaded pictures were the only ones that worked, and only because `/api/identity/image` already served them from here. **The fix is origin-side rather than a wider policy**: a new `/api/image` answers for exactly three pictures — the bot's avatar, the signed-in person's, and a server icon the session may already see — and `discord_auth.fetch_image` re-checks the host even though every address is built here from a cached Discord object, so the worst a crafted parameter can reach is a Discord picture. Its 404 is a *normal* answer: the row draws the server's initial underneath and the picture over it, so a picture that never arrives uncovers the letter instead of the browser's broken-image glyph. `discord_auth.icon_url` is deleted with it — dead, and it built precisely the address the policy blocks. **A second console, for the seat that was never served.** `/dashboard` answers *what is this one server doing*, one server at a time, on purpose; the operator's question is **which** of them is the one that is wrong, and answering it there meant opening them one at a time and remembering. `/owner` is that answer: every server in one list beside the numbers that make a bad one obvious — members, systems on, and the names of the ones that are off — with the infrastructure underneath, reachable by the passkey and by nothing else. A scoped link and a Discord sign-in are redirected out of it rather than shown a reduced copy, and the tests assert on what was *served* (no server name, id or count comes back) rather than on a status code, because a 302 in front of a rendered fleet would pass the weaker check. **It is interactive, and so is the front page.** The console filters and orders its servers locally — `/` focuses the filter, as on every list page people already use — counts its figures up on first sight, re-reads the gateway every fifteen seconds **only while the tab is visible**, and keeps this process's own log behind a button. The landing page gained the one claim a reader cannot check by reading: *a system switched off leaves that server's picker and nowhere else* now ships as something to operate — press any of the eleven systems and watch the command list change, built from the same registry the cards above come from so it cannot drift from them or invent a system. It renders **with everything on** before any script runs, which is the truth about a server nobody has configured. **Three defects came out of rendering the pages rather than reading them.** The console's search box gave `.search-icon` to an inline `<svg>` — a class written for a text glyph (`🔍`), which carries no width, height or fill, so the icon drew as a **173 px black disc** across the page; sized and stroked for an SVG now, with the rule pinned so deleting it fails a test. And the landing page had been printing **`//rank`**, `//balance` and `//daily` **on the live site**: every name in the registry already carries its leading slash, and the template added its own, which the dashboard's module cards have never done — so the two surfaces disagreed and only one of them was wrong. The payload that feeds the new panel is serialised in Python and marked `|safe` on purpose: Jinja's autoescaping turned every quote into `&#34;`, which is valid HTML and invalid JSON, so the panel fell back to its static state on every page while looking entirely correct in the markup. 1,464 tests pass (1,466 under `unittest` discovery), 2 skipped, 54 of them new.
- **🛠️ The dashboard's message composer was dead, and nothing on the server, in the render or in the suite said so**: driving the rebuilt page found it in the first minute — tick a channel and the counter says **"1 of 5 selected"** while the reason under the box still reads **"Pick at least one channel."** and the Send button stays disabled, with a `TypeError` in the browser console. The cause was one id used twice: the composer's section heading was `<h2 id="composer-title">` and the embed-title *input* was `id="composer-title"` too, so `getElementById` handed the script the **heading** — an element with no `.value` — and every call to the composer's rulebook died on `.value.trim()`. The button can therefore never enable, which makes the dashboard's headline feature unusable, and the failure is invisible from every direction this project normally checks: the page renders, the markup is valid HTML, the endpoint is fine, and all 1,395 tests passed. The heading has its own id now (the input keeps the one its `<label for>` and the script both need), and **the class is guarded rather than the instance**: `tests/test_shell.py` fails if any template declares the same id twice, if an id the page's script looks up is missing from the rendered page, or if an id it reads as a form control lands on something that is not one — 65 looked-up ids, all four mutations watched to fail. **Three more defects came out of the same pass.** A double-clicked module switch sent **one** request and kept the second click's flip, leaving `checked=true` beside `aria-checked="false"` and a label reading "Disabled" — the page contradicting itself, and the next reload silently undoing the switch; the pending guard now puts the switch back to the state actually in flight and says which. The **schedule time was not part of the composer's rulebook**, so a time in the past left an enabled button directly under a field reading "that is in the past", and the field never re-asked the gate at all; both are fixed and the hint and the refusal now agree. And the palette's trigger **had no accessible name below 860px**, where the stylesheet hides both its wording and its key hint — a 39px magnifier an assistive reader cannot name. Two smaller truths came with them: the key hint said `Ctrl` on a machine whose handler accepts `⌘` (the operator's), and the status panel's age line read **"read 1 minutes ago"**. **The preview harness got three of its own**, which is where every defect in that file has come from — a hand-written stub that agrees with the real endpoint about the shape and disagrees about a field. Its `/api/uptime` never sent `age_text`, so the panel's "read N seconds ago" line sat on "not read yet in this process" through every Refresh while the harness's own comment claimed it moved; its invite links carried a placeholder application id while `render.yaml` commits the real **public** one, so "Add to Discord" went to an application Discord does not have; and `/pricing` and `/health` answered Flask's 404 although the dashboard's topbar links to Pricing. Verified the way the defects were found: the composer now sends (one POST for three rapid clicks, receipt "Sent", body cleared), a double-click leaves all four switch signals agreeing and the `10 / 11` metric with them, a past time disables the button, a scheduled message queues and cancels, a draft survives a reload with its channels, and the palette opens on `Ctrl`/`⌘`+`K`, filters, runs an action and hands focus back. 1,410 tests pass (1,412 under `unittest` discovery), 2 skipped.
- **🖥️ The control panel was rebuilt around a command palette and one stylesheet, the pointer became a cat that can never become the *only* cursor, and the deploy finally asks UptimeRobot what it has seen**: three things the page had outgrown at once. **The layout left the template.** `templates/dashboard.html` carried 1,660 lines of CSS inline, which made it the third-largest file in the repository and meant a change to one card was a change to a page; the shell — the rail, the topbar, every card, every control, the palette and the toasts — is now `static/dashboard.css`, and the template keeps the two rules that genuinely belong to the *document* rather than to the sheet (its own palette-open state and its own paper). **Reaching anything no longer means scrolling three thousand pixels of module cards**: the topbar's *Jump to anything…* opens a command palette on `Ctrl`/`Cmd`+`K` that indexes the seven sections, all eleven systems by their own names and their commands, and the actions that are otherwise a scroll and a click away — filtered as you type, arrow-navigable, `Enter` opens, `Esc` closes and returns focus to the button that opened it, with a **"Nothing matches that."** line because a dialog that goes blank on a typo reads as a broken dialog. Verified live rather than read: the palette opens with focus already in its input, `econ` narrows to two systems, a junk string shows the empty state, and `Escape` puts focus back on the trigger. **The custom cursor is a cat now, and the interesting half of that is the failure it is built to avoid.** A dot tracks the pointer exactly and a ring chases it with weight, a click prints a paw, and a dim accent light drifts behind the content — additive, pointer-driven, and the loop stops itself as soon as the ring has caught up, so an idle page costs nothing (the same budget the deleted paw-print canvas was removed for). The native cursor is hidden by **`html.has-meow-cursor`** — a class the script adds *after* the layer is in the document, on a fine pointer, with motion wanted — because `cursor: none` on a page whose script then fails is a page with **no pointer at all**, and the sheet's own comments say so. Both cat SVGs are the base layer underneath it, so a touch device and a reduced-motion visitor get the cat as their real cursor while the layer draws for nobody. `tests/test_shell.py` (15 new) pins the properties rather than the pixels, and its CSS reader was **rewritten after it passed for the wrong reason**: the first version split the sheet on `}`, which handed the first rule inside each `@media` a "selector" that was the whole file up to that point, so a *bare* `cursor: none` would have passed by picking up the word `has-meow-cursor` from a rule hundreds of lines earlier. It walks the braces now. Four mutations were watched to fail — a bare `cursor: none`, the palette's dialog role removed, a rail anchor pointing at a section that does not exist, and the layout moving back into the template. **And the deploy can now answer *has this been reliable lately*, not only *is it up now*.** `utils/uptime_status.py` reads UptimeRobot's read-only API — the same monitors already watching this host — through `getMonitors`, which returns the state and the 1/7/30-day ratios in one response. The rows are rendered in the dashboard's status panel and served by `/api/uptime` behind the sign-in, while **`/health` carries counts and one ratio and no names**, on the same rule `config` and `site_guard` already follow. Nothing is fetched while a page is being answered: a background loop on the bot's own event loop refreshes a snapshot and every surface reads that, so an open dashboard costs UptimeRobot nothing. Absent configuration is a *state* — with no key the panel names the variable instead of showing an empty list, because an empty list reads as "you have no monitors" and that is a very different claim from "the integration is off" — and a refresh that could not ask keeps the last rows, marked stale. **The change found two defects of its own, both by being driven live.** The panel rendered the raw `at` timestamp as an age, so it said **"read 1789848173 seconds ago"**; age is a fact about *now* and is worded where it is read (`age_text`). And `headline()` — which decides whether a change is worth a card in `#bot-error` — re-reported the same transition to every caller, so a manual refresh beside the loop's own pass would have sent the same alert twice; it now *spends* the transition and says it exactly once. The test that pinned the first of those was itself wrong and was fixed rather than the code: it asserted the epoch appeared nowhere on the page, and the page legitimately carries the current epoch as `SERVER_NOW`, the clock-skew seed "in 2 hours" depends on — the assertion now names the sentence (`read <at> seconds ago`) instead. **UptimeRobot is declared where it has to be to exist**: `UPTIMEROBOT_API_KEY` and `UPTIMEROBOT_STATUS_PAGE` are in `render.yaml` as prompts and in `.env.example` and `docs/deployment.md` with what their absence costs, because a setting the code reads and the deploy never offers is a feature that is simply off on the host with no error to show for it — the omission this manifest has been caught by three times. 1,403 tests pass (1,405 under `unittest` discovery), 2 skipped, 41 of them new.
- **📡 Something now watches the public site from inside the bot, because the failure it watches for has no symptom anywhere else — and its first live run proved the point by reporting a leak that was not there**: when the Pages project spent hours publishing this private repository, every page on the site looked correct, the bot stayed connected, and the deploy reported **success**; the one signal was a line in a build log nobody reads. Fixing the settings could not cover the part that mattered — **nothing would have noticed a second time**. `utils/site_guard.py` asks the site for nine paths that exist in this checkout (a root script, a package module, a feature file, a config, a dotfile, the README and a test) and says so when one of them answers. It runs **on a timer in the bot process, not in CI**, for the reason this project had just lived through: at the time it was written, the `Tests` and `Site` workflows had been failing for an hour and a half because the account's Actions jobs would not start at all — *"The job was not started because recent account payments have failed"* — so nothing in them ran, and a guard against a publishing mistake that can be switched off by a payment failure is not a guard. This process is always up and is already in the operator's server, so the alarm reaches a person with no new service, token or dashboard. **The first live run reported the exposure as still open, and it was wrong** — the most useful thing this change produced. The deployment was 14 files and clean, while seven paths answered `200` with 71 KB of real source, `cf-cache-status: HIT`, `age: 2073`: copies of the bad deploy still being handed out at the bare URL half an hour after the origin had begun refusing it. Pages keeps a previous deployment's assets served for a while so a rollout cannot break an app mid-visit, and **re-deploying does not clear it** — three deployments followed and the copies stayed, with `age` climbing past 2500s while the stored response carried `max-age=300, must-revalidate`, and a client `Cache-Control: no-cache` did not force revalidation either.
