So the guard asks with **a unique query string**, which is a different URL to a cache and therefore asks the deployment; a `GET`, never the cheaper `HEAD`, because a host answering `405` to `HEAD` would read as *not exposed*, which is the one answer this must never give; and it asks the bare URL **only after** the deployment has refused a path, so a stored copy is found without inverting what its answer means. Verified that cache-busting does not cost the check its teeth: `/`, `/static/meow.css`, `/pricing` and `/login` still answer `200` and `/dashboard` still answers `302` with a query string attached. **Four verdicts, because the remedies differ and only one of them is the build**: `exposed` (the deployment is publishing the tree — fix the build settings, reported every cycle), `cached` (a stored copy still answers while the deployment refuses it — the build settings are correct and a redeploy will not help; the card says *not this*, which is the whole reason the near-miss above is a near-miss), `unreachable` (nothing was established, because *no leak found* and *no answer* are different claims and only one of them is reassuring), and a fifth that came out of thinking about how this reads wrong: **a control probe** for a path that does not exist, since a host that answers everything makes a `200` on `/bot.py` say nothing about `/bot.py` — that verdict is `fallback` and the card says the site cannot be checked this way rather than calling it a leak. `/health` carries the state as `site_guard` with counts and **no names**, on the same reasoning as `config`: that endpoint is unauthenticated, and the alert that names paths goes to the operator's channel. It is deliberately not folded into the top-level `status`, because the bot being healthy and the site being wrongly configured are separate claims. `SITE_GUARD_INTERVAL_SECONDS` (six hours) and `SITE_GUARD_FIRST_CHECK_SECONDS` (ninety, so a deploy is re-checked shortly after boot) tune it. **The check was verified by breaking it**: four mutations of the module each watched to fail — removing the cache-buster, removing the control probe, removing the throttle, and treating a cached copy as an exposure — costing 1, 2, 1 and 7 tests respectively, so no line of it is decorative. Two more were aimed at `docs/deployment.md`, whose own hand-check would have read the cache, and at documenting a path the check does not watch; both were caught. Driven end to end against the live host, where it reports what is actually true today: `cached`, seven paths, the deployment not the problem. 1,362 tests pass (1,364 under `unittest` discovery), 37 of them new.
- **🚨 The site at `meow-bot.pages.dev` was publishing the private repository, and the same misconfiguration is why `/dashboard` and `/login` answered 404 there**: the Pages project had a Git integration pointed at this repository and **no build command and no output directory**, and a static host handed no instructions publishes the directory it is given — so it published the checkout. Its own log said so in plain words: *No build command specified. Skipping build step.*, then *Uploading... (185/185)*, and this repository has exactly **185 tracked files**. While that lasted, `/bot.py`, `/utils/cloud_sync.py`, `/cogs/economy.py`, `/render.yaml`, `/Procfile` and `/.env.example` all answered **200** at the project's public address — the whole tree, readable by anyone, with `README.md` as an index. **No secret value was exposed**, and that is a property of where secrets live rather than luck: every credential is read from the host's environment and none is committed, which is exactly the discipline that made this survivable. The same missing build command explains the second symptom, which was reported separately as several `404`s: `_worker.js` and `_routes.json` are written *by the build*, and with no build there was nothing to reverse-proxy `/login`, `/dashboard`, `/servers`, `/api`, `/market` and `/health` to Render — so the dashboard and the sign-in page 404'd on the Pages domain while working on Render, and the two hosts disagreed about what exists. **The fix is the two settings that were empty**: the build command now runs the same thing CI does (`pip install -r requirements.txt && python scripts/build_site.py --out _site --proxy`) with the output directory `_site`, and the retried deployment uploads **14 files instead of 185** — the four pages, their assets, and the worker's two config files. Verified by probing production rather than by reading the config: eleven source paths answer **404** (including the ones that answered 200 an hour earlier), all four pages and the stylesheet still answer **200**, and `/login` now serves the real sign-in page (`<title>Sign in — Meow</title>`) while `/dashboard` and `/servers` answer **302 → /login**, which is the correct answer for a signed-out visitor. `scripts/build_site.py` refuses to write into the repository root now — the guard is a `SystemExit`, not a warning, because by the time the upload runs it is too late to take it back — and it is pinned four ways (the root spelled four ways, plus a real build into an ordinary directory so the guard cannot become a trap), each watched to fail by removing the check. The whole configuration is written down in `docs/deployment.md`, including the two commands that tell a correct deployment from a broken one, because the setting that caused this is invisible from the repository and one dashboard edit away from coming back. 1,325 tests pass (1,327 under `unittest` discovery), 2 of them new.
- **🧹 Two things on the dashboard were drawn by something other than the dashboard, and both are gone now — the image pickers and a full-page canvas of drifting paw prints**: the identity panel's two image controls were the browser's own `<input type="file">`, so "Choose file" and "No file chosen" arrived in the operating system's typeface and its own colors, which CSS cannot style at all — which made the two controls an administrator uses to set the bot's picture and banner the one place on the page where the product stopped and macOS started, sitting immediately above a Save button this page draws for itself. Each is a styled `<label for>` beside the input now, with the chosen filename in the page's own mono face. The input itself is still in the DOM and still focusable — visually hidden rather than `display:none`, because a display-none input cannot be reached by keyboard and the whole control would then be mouse-only — so the save path reads exactly the `files` it read before, and the code that clears the inputs after a save fires the same `change` event, so a successful upload cannot leave a stale filename sitting on screen next to an input that no longer holds a file. **`static/particles.js` is deleted**, along with the `<script>` tag that loaded it on five pages: the dashboard, the market, the sign-in page, the policies and the server picker. It painted a fixed, full-viewport canvas of 45 drifting dots and paw prints from a `requestAnimationFrame` loop that redrew every shape every frame for the entire life of the page, on a control panel that people leave open in a tab. It carried no information, and the two pages designed after it never reached for it, which is the tell rather than the argument. `tests/test_shared_system.py` now pins the absence instead of the behaviour, because removing a decoration is not a thing a test can notice: the file and every reference to it are asserted gone, so putting the canvas back has to be deliberate. 1,323 tests pass (1,325 under `unittest` discovery), 2 of them new.
- **🩺 Nothing in this deployment knew what it was missing, so a feature that was configured-and-off looked exactly like one that was broken — there is one place that answers it now, and finding it turned up a bug that made every `.env` setting invisible to the half of the app that reads the environment at import**: forty-odd `os.getenv` calls were spread across the tree, each with its own fallback and its own private decision about whether to say anything when it came up empty — three printed a warning, the rest said nothing — and nothing anywhere held the whole set. The only way to answer "is this deployment configured?" was for a person to read two files and the host's dashboard and hold them in their head. That gap has now cost this project three real incidents: `DISCORD_CLIENT_ID` declared in `render.yaml` and absent from the running service, so the live site rendered one button fewer and nothing said why; a deploy that skipped publishing for four commits while every run reported **success**; and, found while writing this, **`load_dotenv()` called *after* `keep_alive` and `utils` were imported** — their module-level reads had already happened, so a `.env`, which is the documented way to configure this locally, reached `DISCORD_TOKEN` and nothing else: the dashboard ran on a throwaway session key with passkey sign-in switched off and backups disabled, on a machine where all three were filled in. `utils/config_audit.py` is now one declared set — every setting, what it turns on, what its absence *costs*, and whether it is a credential — with three consumers on surfaces people already use: the **boot log** opens with what is off and what that costs, before anything connects; **`/health`** gains a `config` block that counts and deliberately does not name, because it is unauthenticated and "this deployment has no sign-in secret" is not a sentence to hand a stranger; and **`/api/config`** carries the names and the costs behind the administrator's sign-in, where a one-time link counts as an administrator on purpose — *why the operator cannot get in the other way* is the most useful thing it has to say, and `ADMIN_PASSKEY` being the missing setting is exactly that case, so requiring the passkey would hide the answer behind the question. It reports presence and never a value, for every setting, secret or not, so the property does not depend on the route being guarded correctly. **The list is checked against the code rather than trusted**: two tests walk the tree for every way it reaches for the environment — including the two indirections a plain `getenv` search misses, a name handed to `_env_flag(…)` and the names held as values inside the `*_ENV` mappings — and fail in both directions. That caught a real omission in this registry's own first draft (`DISCORD_CLIENT_ID`, the variable the whole incident turned on), five settings whose absence turns something off but which `.env.example` documented nowhere (the update channel and role, the deploy hook, the owner id, and `DASHBOARD_HTTPS`), and a guard that passed on a *mention*: deleting `- key: FLASK_SECRET_KEY` from `render.yaml` still satisfied the first version, because the paragraph above that entry explains the omission and names the variable in prose. All three are fixed and each was watched to fail by mutation, which is how the third was found at all. **Two absences are deliberately not reported**, and the reasoning is the same in both cases — a report that cries wolf every boot is a report nobody reads by the third deploy, which is the failure this exists to prevent: `DASHBOARD_HTTPS` and `TRUST_PROXY_HEADERS` are *inferred* from `RENDER`/`RAILWAY_ENVIRONMENT`, so on Render they are absent and correct at the same time, and they are reported only where nothing supplies them; `MARKET_ROLE_ID` is a retired spelling of `MARKET_ROLE_IDS`, so one of the two is always unset and flagging it would flag every deployment that configured the gate correctly. Verified through the real surface rather than from the code: a genuine `python bot.py` boot prints the block and then the warnings that already existed, `/health` names no setting and still answers `status: ok` so no monitor changes behaviour, and `/api/config` answers **401** unauthenticated, **403** for a guild-scoped session, and **200** with the full table for the operator — with the request driven against a sentinel value planted in every variable to prove none of them is echoed. 1,321 tests pass (1,323 under `unittest` discovery), 18 of them new.
- **🧭 The deploy's success line announced a hostname that no longer exists, and the four places the Pages project is named are now compared to each other**: the project was renamed `meow-bot-site` → `meow-bot`, and the commit that renamed it updated the API calls and the `--project-name` with it — but not the last line of the run, which printed `Published <sha> to https://meow-bot-site.pages.dev`. That host now answers **530, project not found** (measured, not assumed: `wrangler pages deployment list --project-name meow-bot-site` refuses it *Project not found*, while the `meow-bot` project is serving `86eaa55`), so the one line a person reads to decide whether a deploy worked pointed at a URL that does not exist. It is the shape a rename leaves behind: every functional reference moves in the commit that renames the thing, and the *message about* the reference is the one nothing compiles or calls. `tests/test_site_workflow.py` no longer compares each mention to a literal but compares the four to each other — every `pages/projects/<name>` the preflight and the landed check ask, the project named to wrangler, and the URL printed on success have to be one name — and the assertion was watched to fail by putting the dead host back.
- **🎨 The dashboard, the market and the front page are one product now, and the reason they were not was not the one anybody would guess**: it was not motion, and it was not the layout. `templates/dashboard.html` re-declared **every shared token under the same name with a different value** — its `--bg` was the blue-black that `static/meow.css` documents as the thing it moved away from, its accent was Discord blurple beside the shared indigo, its radii disagreed by two pixels — so a page linking the shared sheet and this one painted two palettes at once, and neither was visible as a bug. Underneath that sat a *literal* layer duplicating the same roles a third time (`#4ade80`, `#ff6b6b`, `#facc15`, `rgba(88,101,242,…)`), and `templates/market_live.html` carried its own `:root` whose names — `--bg`, `--accent` — are the shared sheet's own, so it was repainting rules it only meant to read. The shared sheet had leftovers of the same kind: the aurora's grid was slate and the primary button's glow was blurple, a stale hue inside the file that owns the palette. There is one palette now, every `var()` on every page resolves, and the market's block is *aliases* (`--panel: var(--surface-card)`) rather than copies — an unresolved `var()` is an empty value, which is exactly how this stayed invisible. **Motion arrives on `motion.js` with the amount decided per page**: the dashboard gets the same primitives and **no curtain**, because a landing page's job is to arrive and a control panel's is to already be there — a progress bar in front of a switch somebody came to flip is a cost paid every visit for one impression. Reveals are 10px rather than 28px and stagger only across the first screen; everything below reveals on scroll. **The reveal primitive itself had a defect that only the dashboard could expose**: it left `opacity` and `transform` inline forever, and an inline style outranks every CSS rule for the life of the page, so a revealed `.module-card` could never dim when `.busy` was set and never responded to a hover — a reveal removing behaviours it had never touched, correct in each file and wrong in the pair. It now hands the element back to its stylesheet when it settles, on the reduced-motion path too, which is the path that exists precisely so the page can be still. Every remaining hand-written `?v=` string went to `asset_version()` with it — the favicon in seven templates and `particles.js` in five — the same defect class as the stylesheet the previous commit fixed, and the dashboard was the last place carrying them. `tests/test_shared_system.py` pins the rules rather than the hex, because every one of them failed silently: a template adding `:root { --bg: … }` looks like a reasonable local choice and nothing on screen says otherwise.
- **🆓 Nothing is for sale, so nothing is advertised — and the invite asks for `Administrator` again, beside a second link for owners who will not grant it**: two decisions this repository had argued the other way, each revisited on the operator's call. **The premium surface is gone while `PLAN_CHECKOUT` is off.** The page kept describing three tiers — prices in Indian rupees, feature lists, a "Most Popular" badge, and a comparison table with columns for plans nobody could buy — with only the *buttons* gated by the switch, so a visitor who opened it to ask "what does this cost me" was answered with a shop that is not open. With selling off it is now a free product's page: one plan, every system from the registry the bot actually loads (including the five this page used to sell), the real price from `utils/plans.py`, and a note that paid plans may come later — worded so it cannot promise what `PLAN_ENFORCEMENT` could take back, which is what `test_the_free_page_says_what_happens_later_without_promising_the_world` pins from both sides. The tiers, prices and Buy buttons all return together when `PLAN_CHECKOUT=1`: one switch, two whole pages, and no in-between state where a price is listed and cannot be paid. The FAQ moved with it — answers about payment methods and cancellation are about a purchase nobody can make, so they appear only where there is one, and the free state answers the questions a visitor actually has. `/bot plan` follows its own advice: with nothing on sale it no longer tells an administrator to activate a key they cannot buy. **The invite asks for `Administrator` again**, and the argument that removed it is still the honest one — a public invite is the page's real job, and Administrator is the first thing a careful owner reads before closing the tab — but the operator weighed it against the cost the other way round: the explicit list is *always* one permission behind the code, and every feature added after it was written reaches somebody's server as `Missing Permissions`. So the answer is two links, not one, offered together wherever a person decides to add the bot (the landing page's step card and plans band, the pricing page's free card, `/servers`), built by one helper so a page that offers the Administrator link can never fail to offer the other. Both are pinned: the default carries Administrator and **nothing else** — checked against `discord.Permissions(administrator=True).value`, so a second bit cannot ride along — and the limited one must never grow it, asserted against the sum of the bits as well as the names, because a bit quietly *added* to that list keeps every name-based assertion passing. The site tests decode the real `permissions=` parameter out of both links on two pages, which is where the one harness trap lives: Jinja escapes the `&` between query parameters, so the markup has to be read back as `&amp;` before the query is parsed or every key comes out as `amp;permissions`. README's setup steps no longer describe one grant, `docs/architecture.md` answers for both links and both pricing states, and 1,301 tests pass (1,299 under pytest, 2 skipped) — 7 new, and three that used to assert the old behaviour were rewritten rather than deleted: the pricing page's "the tiers stay, this is 'not on sale yet'" test now asserts the opposite, because that was the defect.
- **🚫 A token that can read a Pages project is not a token that can publish to it, and the preflight now asks the second question**: the first real run of the deploy workflow got past the configuration check — which is what the commit below made visible — and then died inside wrangler with `Authentication error [code: 10000]` on `POST /accounts/…/pages/projects/meow-bot-site/upload-token`. Measured through the API rather than guessed: the deployed token answers **200** to the project GET and **403** to that POST, and its own policy list holds 241 permission groups including `Pages Read` with neither `Pages Write` nor the dashboard name for it, *Cloudflare Pages: Edit*. Every read in the tree works, and publishing was the one thing it could not do — which "is this token valid" and "can it read the project" both answer yes to. So the check is the capability the deploy actually needs: a Pages **upload token**, which creates nothing, publishes nothing and mints only the short-lived credential the upload would use, so it exercises no authority the next step does not already have. A refusal now names the permission, the API's name for it, the dashboard's name for it, and the command that replaces the secret, instead of a stack trace from inside a third-party action. Two smaller things in the same step: a missing `CLOUDFLARE_ACCOUNT_ID` is refused *before* any call at all, where it used to pass whenever the token endpoint answered 200 and then fail in wrangler with the project unnamed; and acceptance no longer keys off the read status, which a read-only token passes — pinned as absent, because that is the exact mistake. All four paths were executed against the live API: the real token is caught with *Cloudflare Pages: Edit* named, a garbage token reports both statuses, a missing account id fails by name, and no token still warns and skips green. The success path cannot be run from here, because it needs a token that has the permission, so the next run after the secret is replaced is what demonstrates it.
- **🚦 A green workflow no longer means a published site, and the deploy now checks that it landed**: `CLOUDFLARE_ACCOUNT_ID` was set and `CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN` was not, and the site spent four commits in that state while every `Site` run reported **success** — `meow-bot-site.pages.dev` was serving `0af327e`, from before all four of them. Nothing was broken except the signal: every build guard was about the build, which was fine, and the one thing that reached a person was a `::notice`, which does not appear on the checks list — so `success` was true of the build and false of the deploy, with nowhere to notice the difference. The missing-token path now emits a **warning**, which is on the checks list, and writes a job summary headed *Published: no* naming the secret and the command that sets it. A token that is set but rejected fails **before** wrangler starts, with the status the API answered, because an expired token otherwise surfaces as an opaque 401 from inside a third-party action. And an accepted upload is a different claim from a published site: the deploy job downloads an artifact and never checks out the repository, so `wrangler pages deploy` attached no commit at all and the deployment was unattributable — it now passes `--commit-hash` explicitly, with a fixed message because a head commit subject would reach a shell in a job that holds a token, and a final step asks Cloudflare which commit production serves and fails if it is not this one. `tests/test_site_workflow.py` pins the rule with a dependency-free read of the YAML, and the central assertion was watched to fail by putting the notice back. One trap is recorded in the workflow itself: the reader is a **single line** on purpose, because `python3 -c` over a multi-line block scalar fails on the indent as an unexpected indent on its first statement — a YAML parse error in a workflow whose only symptom is that it never runs. **One thing only running it could find**: the token endpoint answers **401** for an *account-owned* token, and a Pages-scoped token usually is one — the token this deployment now uses returns `401 Invalid API Token` there while the Pages API answers 200 and deploys with it, so a preflight that insisted on the token endpoint would have red-failed a good token *before* wrangler ran, which is worse than the silence it replaced. It asks the account's Pages project as well now, and only both failing is a refusal; a missing account id gets a sentence of its own instead of a confusing 404.
- **🔒 The premium tiers stay, and nothing can be bought until an operator says so: selling is now a switch, not a consequence of having a checkout link**: two URLs pasted into a host during an evening of store setup would have put a Buy button on a public page and started charging — the change that goes live without anybody deciding it. `PLAN_CHECKOUT` (committed as `0`) is that decision, held separately from the two Lemon Squeezy links for the same reason `PLAN_ENFORCEMENT` is a switch rather than an inference: configuring a store and opening it for business are two different acts. With it off, **no host shows a Buy button**, on any page, however many links are filled in — and the tiers are *not* hidden: `/pricing` still lists Free, Pro and Enterprise with their prices, features and the systems each one unlocks, describing where the project is heading rather than what it charges, with every button on the invite. The gate sits in **one place**, `plans.checkout_url`, which is where every surface that offers a plan for money already asks, so a page added later cannot leak a price by forgetting to check a switch. The on/off reading is spelled out rather than left to `bool(os.environ.get(...))`, because the empty string and the word `"0"` are both false strings an operator may write and `bool("0")` is **true** — a switch that turns itself on when somebody writes `off` is the one failure a person cannot see, so `off`, `no`, `false`, `0` and blank all read as off and `on`, `yes`, `true` and `1` as on. The CI warning follows the switch instead of nagging: it fires only in the state that is a mistake — selling switched on with no link to sell through — and stays quiet when buying is deliberately off, because a warning that appears on every push is a warning nobody reads. Committed `0` in `render.yaml` and both documented in `.env.example`, which also lost the two junk `[TEMPLATE]` placeholder lines it had been starting with.
- **💳 A plan a server can actually be on, and a Lemon Squeezy licence that proves it — plus four claims the pricing page was making that nothing could keep**: the store sells two products (Pro ₹499/mo, Enterprise ₹1,499/mo) and the page listed three tiers, and until now nothing connected the two: both paid buttons pointed at the invite page, and buying would have delivered nothing at all. `utils/plans.py` is the missing half — the plan model, and the licence. **A licence is activated per server** (`/bot plan`, administrator, one command with optional options because `/bot` is one of Discord's hundred slots), which is what makes "up to 5 servers" the *store's* rule rather than a number this bot invents and then gets wrong: Lemon Squeezy counts activations against the licence, each server keeps its own row, and releasing one here hands that activation back for somewhere else. A key for a product this deployment does not sell is valid at the store, so it is **handed straight back** rather than left spent, and the refusal names the `LEMONSQUEEZY_VARIANT_*` variable that would fix it; a network failure never downgrades a paying server, and a refused release keeps the record it could not undo, because forgetting a licence the store still counts is how the same seat gets sold twice. Validation uses the store's **public** licence API — no API key is stored and no webhook endpoint has to exist. **Enforcement ships off** (`PLAN_ENFORCEMENT=0`), deliberately: switching it on takes Support Tickets, Economy & Casino, the Market, Starboard and the Activity Tracker away from every server that has not activated a licence — including servers using them today — and holds a free server to one server and no scheduled messages. That is the operator's decision, not a side effect of wiring checkout, so the plans are recorded and displayed and the pricing page says in words that limits are not switched on yet. When it is turned on the withholding is applied in **one place** — `utils/modules.get_states`, which the command gate, the per-server command list and the dashboard all read — so a switch, a picker and a refusal cannot disagree about the same server; the dashboard's single toggle refuses in its own words rather than springing back with "could not save", and "enable all" reaches only the systems the server may have. **The page stopped selling things that do not exist.** The monthly/yearly toggle switched every price to a yearly number with a "Save 20%" badge, and both products in the store are monthly subscriptions — a price nobody could ever be charged, which is the same defect as the dead buttons this page was already rebuilt to remove, one layer further in. Prices are now the store's own (₹0 / ₹499 / ₹1,499) and come from the plan model, so the site cannot advertise a tier the code lacks; both paid cards link at the merchant of choice when the deployment has a checkout URL, and say "No billing yet" when it does not. And **four FAQ answers described a product that does not exist**, each of which reads perfectly plausible: a *7-day free trial of Pro features* on every new server (no product offers one and no code implements one); *"we're setting up our payment processors"* (stale the moment checkout is configured); *"we use encrypted backups"* — the cloud backup is a **raw SQLite file** in a private bucket, and encryption at rest was deliberately removed with the old Gist backup, so the page was contradicting the privacy policy it links to; and a *30-day* post-downgrade retention window that appears in no code path and disagrees with the policy's own 90 days for message history and 365 for activity. Each is now pinned by name in `tests/test_pages_site.py`, because the way they fail is by reading as true. One thing the docs guard caught while this was being written, and it was right to: the checkout variables were being read as `f"LEMONSQUEEZY_CHECKOUT_{plan}"`, which is a name no grep finds — so `.env.example` looked like it documented two variables nothing read. They are spelled out in `CHECKOUT_ENV`/`VARIANT_ENV` now, and the key is only ever shown masked (`38b1…4d51`). **A refusal is not an outage, and the first version of this client could not tell them apart**: the licence API answers a key it has never issued with `404` and the documented falsey body (`{"activated": false, "error": "license_key not found."}`) — measured against the real endpoint, not read off the docs — and every non-200 status was being raised as a transport failure, so a buyer who mistyped one character would have been told *"Could not reach Lemon Squeezy"* and gone to check their connection instead of their receipt. A body carrying the API's own result shape is now returned as the store's answer, while a genuine outage (a `502` and an HTML gateway page, an unreadable body) still reports itself as one. 36 new tests (**1,259 passing**), and `.env.example` plus `render.yaml` declare the four new variables with `PLAN_ENFORCEMENT` committed as `0` so the deploy has a definite state instead of a prompt.
- **🧪 The test suite had been failing on GitHub, and the harness it blamed was broken only where nobody had a database**: every `Tests` run since 09:07 was still "in progress" — fourteen of them, the oldest for seventy-six minutes — each parked at `Run test suite` with no log published and no red check, so for an hour and a half the repository looked verified while nothing was. The suite is not slow: it is **1223 tests in 15 seconds** locally, on the same committed code. What was *wrong* is more interesting than the hang, because it was found without it: reproduced the CI shape exactly — a checkout with **no `bot.db` and no `.env`** — and the suite failed two tests, `PreviewHarness.test_the_harness_refuses_what_the_real_endpoint_refuses` and `test_toggling_a_module_in_the_harness_is_reversible`. The cause is that `scripts/preview_dashboard.py` copies the operator's `bot.db` into its temp directory *if it exists*, and a fresh clone, a CI runner and any new contributor have no such file, so the pages were handed an empty SQLite file with **no tables**: `/api/toggle-all` answered `500 no such table: guild_modules`, and the harness's own logs said so (`[MODULES] ❌ Failed to save leveling=False for guild 777001`). Both tests passed on this machine only because it happens to have a populated `bot.db` — the classic shape of a suite that cannot fail in the place it is supposed to run. The fix is to build the schema from the bot's own idempotent `setup()` against the copy when there is nothing to copy, in a thread if the import ever happens inside a running loop; verified by running the whole suite in a clean checkout with neither file present, where it is now **1223 tests, OK** where it was two failures. The workflow is hardened against the *invisibility* of the other problem rather than the guess about its cause: `faulthandler.dump_traceback_later(120, exit=True)` dumps every thread's stack and fails the job if the suite blocks, `timeout-minutes: 20` bounds it from outside, and `concurrency` cancels superseded runs instead of stacking fourteen of them behind a wedged one. The next run's log names the line it is stuck on, which is the diagnostic this could not produce before.
- **🐾 The bot's status is editable now, and the line that was reported is gone**: the presence was a twelve-line tuple hardcoded in `bot.py`, so the only way to change one word was to edit the file and redeploy — and `/bot status` did not exist, which made "remove this one" a code change. The list lives in the database now (`utils/presence.py`), read fresh on every cycle, and **"Knocking over your water glass 🐾" is not in the defaults**: I pinned that with a test that names the line, because a default is exactly where a removed thing quietly comes back on a fresh install. `/bot status` shows the rotation, `text:` adds one, `type:` picks playing/watching/listening/competing, `interval:` sets the minutes, `clear:` turns the activity off entirely ("just be online" is a legitimate choice, not an error), and `reset:` restores the shipped list — one subcommand with optional options rather than five, because Discord gives an application 100 chat-input commands in total and this bot has spent that budget before. **The honest limit, said out loud in the command's own reply: a bot's presence is application-wide.** Discord has no per-server status — an activity belongs to the gateway session, so every server sees the same line at the same moment and a server administrator who could set it would be setting it for every server, last speaker winning. That is the opposite of the *member* profile (nickname, avatar, banner, bio), which Discord does store per guild and which is why `utils/identity.py` can give each server its own name and picture while this cannot; those stay on `/bot avatar` and the dashboard's identity panel. Everything that could be wrong about a value read on a loop for the life of the process is made total rather than left to raise: a hand-edited or truncated setting falls back to the defaults, one bad entry is dropped instead of invalidating the list, an out-of-range interval is clamped (a 0-minute floor is what stops the loop hammering Discord's endpoint), and a failed settings read is reported and retried rather than killing the rotation and freezing the status until the next deploy. The rotation also no longer repeats the previous line, which a plain random choice does one time in eleven and is the only repeat anybody notices. Verified by driving the real command handler: show/add/clear/reset each did what they say, a 200-character line was refused with Discord's 128 named, an unknown type was refused with the valid ones listed, and an empty list cleared the activity rather than conjuring the jokes back. 16 new tests, **1,214 pass on pytest** (`1,216` under `unittest` discovery).
- **🔒 The invite link no longer asks for Administrator — it asks for the seventeen permissions the bot is actually built from**: a public invite is the page's real job, and `Administrator` is the first thing a careful server owner reads before deciding whether to add a bot to their community. It was also the wrong kind of grant: Administrator makes every other bit irrelevant, so a fault in one command is unbounded — it can do anything, anywhere, rather than being limited to what that command does. The list is now explicit and self-documenting (`utils/discord_auth.INVITE_PERMISSION_BITS`), **each entry naming the call site that needs it**: kick/ban/moderate for `/kick`, `/ban`, `/mute` and automod's timeout; Manage Messages for `/purge`; Manage Channels for `/lock`, `/unlock` and ticket channels; Manage Roles for level rewards and `/defaultrole`; Change Nickname for a server's own name and picture; View Audit Log for telling you who added the bot; the two thread bits for the ticket panel and the `/help` panel. **What is left out is as deliberate as what is in**, and was checked against the tree rather than assumed: no `Mention Everyone` (announcements ping a *role*, which needs no permission), no `Connect` (music is gone and nothing joins voice — the tracker reads state changes, which needs only View Channels), no Manage Server, Manage Webhooks, Create Invite, or the member move/mute/deafen bits. The old comment defended the blanket grant as "one number in the URL rather than a long hand-tuned list nobody can check", which is true and was still the wrong trade. Pinned by decoding the bitfield with discord.py rather than comparing it to a literal — and the existing test is the reason this survived so long: it asserted `permissions == str(ADMINISTRATOR)`, so the grant was pinned to *itself* and no test ever asked whether it was the right one. Restoring Administrator now fails by name. README's setup steps no longer tell anyone to grant it, and the table explains what a `Missing Permissions` error in your own server means instead. **1,198 tests pass** (`1,200` under `unittest` discovery), 5 new.
- **🌐 The public site is a static build published to Cloudflare Pages, and the one thing it would have silently lost was the cat cursor**: the front door, the pricing page and the two policies were reachable only through the single Render process that also runs the bot, and that host is a free tier that sleeps — so the pages worth reading *before* you trust a bot with a server were shut whenever nobody had knocked recently. `scripts/build_site.py` renders them into static files and `.github/workflows/site.yml` builds and publishes them. **GitHub Pages was the first choice and is impossible here**, which is worth writing down rather than rediscovering: this repository is private, the owner is on GitHub's free plan, and Pages does not cover that combination (`422 Your current plan does not support GitHub Pages for this repository`). Two runs proved the build was fine and failed on that one restriction — the second after `configure-pages` tried to create the site itself and was told `Resource not accessible by integration`, because creating a Pages site needs repository admin and the workflow token cannot have it. Cloudflare Pages also settles the *reason* for the build: it is served from a CDN and never sleeps, and the rendering stays in Actions because Cloudflare's build image has no Python — the host is handed a finished directory. The part that makes the output work anywhere is that a static host may serve from a subpath, as a GitHub **project** site does at `/<repo>/`: every root-relative URL a template emits (`/static/meow.css`, `/pricing`) points outside the site and 404s, so all of them are rewritten to relative paths, while the pages that genuinely need a process (`/dashboard`, `/login`, `/servers`, the market, `/health`) are rewritten to the live app instead of to a dead path. The build renders through the **real** Flask app's test client rather than a second renderer, so the page on Pages and the page the bot serves are the same template with the same context and cannot drift into disagreeing about the product. **Four defects came out of doing it, three of them invisible from the code.** The cursor was the bad one: a stylesheet's `url()` resolves against *the stylesheet*, and `meow.css` already sits in `static/`, so rewriting `/static/cursor-default.svg` the same way as a page's link produced `static/static/cursor-default.svg` — the server log says it plainly (`GET …/static/static/cursor-pointer.svg → 404`), and the only symptom in a browser is the system arrow, because a cursor image that fails to load is not reported anywhere. It is fixed by giving CSS its own rule (a bare file name, no `static/` prefix) and pinned by a test that resolves the path the way a browser does and insists the file is there. **The live site had no way to add the bot at all**: the templates deliberately *omit* the invite button when no client id is known, and `DISCORD_CLIENT_ID` was never set, so the whole marketing page had zero invite links and said nothing about it — the id is public (it is in every invite link anybody can read), it was recovered from the bot's own application, declared in `render.yaml` for the live host and defaulted in the builder for the static one. That required the deploy-manifest guard to become *precise* rather than weaker: it forbade any literal `value:` in `render.yaml` on the grounds that it would commit a credential, and it now allows one only for a key on an explicit public allow-list and refuses any key whose own name says `SECRET`, `TOKEN`, `KEY` or `PASSKEY` — proved by feeding it a committed `ADMIN_PASSKEY`, `FLASK_SECRET_KEY`, `SUPABASE_SERVICE_KEY` and `DISCORD_TOKEN`, all four still refused. **A file answered a question about a process.** The landing page's status pill is a claim about the running bot, so the static copy promised *"a sleeping free-tier host takes a moment to wake"* and "live figures appear as soon as it does" — forever, on a host where that is never going to happen. It now says what is true of a static copy and points at the host that can answer. And the **pricing page sold two tiers nobody can buy with two buttons that went nowhere**: "Upgrade to Pro" and "Contact Us" were both `href="#"`, and nothing on the page mentioned that every system it prices is switched on and free in every server today — so it now says so once, above the prices rather than under them, and both buttons do something real. 17 new tests, one of which is the cursor test that found the cursor bug, and the whole suite is green at **1,192 passing on the `pytest` runner**.
- **⏱️ A deploy now posts its own update note within seconds — the boot no longer queues behind the command publish**: every "the update isn't there" has one cause, and it was found by reading the live process rather than the code: `on_ready` **awaited** `sync_commands(bot)` before it started the 25-second cloud backup loop, bound the house channels or posted the release note, and Discord paces command writes hard enough that the live host spent **eighteen minutes** giving one server its 90 commands back (the module-state incident had left Development's picker empty). So for eighteen minutes after a successful deploy there was no update note in any server, no backup of anything earned in that window, and not one line saying so — an update that *had* been deployed read exactly like one that never shipped, which is how it was reported three times. The publish now starts on the bot's own loop as a task and **nothing waits for it**: same compare-first rule, same one-request upserts, same background drain when the daily write budget runs out, same per-server lists — only the waiting is gone. Its outcome is still printed when it finishes (`[SYNC] command publish finished: {...}`), and a publish that cannot even be started is reported instead of quietly skipped, with the coroutine closed so it cannot surface as an unrelated "never awaited" warning. Pinned from both ends: `on_ready` must not await `sync_commands` (and must still start it, on the loop, with its result reported), and the helper itself is driven with a publish that never finishes — it returns immediately, a publish that explodes is reported and never raised into the boot, and a loop that refuses the task answers `None` instead of crashing. **1,174 tests pass on both runners** (`1,176` on `unittest` discovery), 7 of them new.
- **🎨 There is a front door now, and the whole site wears one design system — with the four defects that rebuilding it exposed fixed**: `/` used to redirect, so the first thing a visitor met — a server owner following an invite, anyone reading the link in the README — was a password box for a panel they had never heard of. It now answers three questions in the order they are asked: what this is, what it is doing *right now*, and how to add it. **`static/meow.css` is the whole design system** and every page links it — the dashboard, the sign-in, the picker, the market, the landing page and the two policies — each page's own CSS coming *after* it so a page still wins where it has opinions (the control panel's density is not something the shared sheet should flatten). What it carries: the palette the dashboard already used (so the market stops being a second opinion on what dark looks like — it was `#1a1d23`, the rest of the site `#0b0e13`), the surfaces, the buttons, one focus ring, and an animated aurora of three slow radial washes over a faint grid behind every page. Every animation has a reason — the drift is *place*, the staggered entrance is orientation, the hover lift is an affordance, the status pulse is the gateway answering — and all of it stops under `prefers-reduced-motion`, with `.reveal` explicitly left visible so a suppressed animation can never hide the content. **The two policies are pages now** (`/privacy`, `/terms`): the same files the Discord cards read, rendered by `utils/webdocs.py` through the same `cogs/legal.read_document`, so the web page and the card cannot disagree about which file is the policy — escaping before any markup is applied, no `javascript:` href, and every section present, because Discord's 6000-character budget is a reason to tighten wording rather than a reason a policy should be unlinkable. **And the landing page's figures are only the ones that are true**: servers and members come from the gateway and are *absent* when it has not answered, because on a sleeping free-tier host a confident **0 servers** reads as a bot nobody uses rather than as a process that has not woken up, and the feature cards are the registry's own list so the page cannot advertise a system the bot does not have. `/`, `/privacy` and `/terms` are also the first pages the preview harness serves by **delegating to the real views** rather than restating them, because a harness that describes a route answers differently from the route — which is where every defect found in that file has come from. Four defects came out of the rebuild, all fixed here: the **preview rendered every page unstyled**, because the script runs from `scripts/` and Flask's default static folder was therefore `scripts/static` — the one place the design system could not be looked at, which is the only thing that file is for; the nav's **"Add to Discord" rendered its label in the nav's grey on the accent fill** (about 2.2:1, on the primary call to action) because `.site-nav a` — a class *and* a type — outranks `.btn-primary`, which only looking at the page could have found; the **markdown renderer tore wrapped bullets apart** and printed PRIVACY.md's one emphasis span that crosses a line break as literal asterisks, because it rendered each source line in isolation instead of joining a block first (so a bullet's second half appeared as body text); and the templates could not use `url_for` for the stylesheet at all, because the dashboard and market templates are rendered by the test-suite's own Jinja environment with no Flask context — the literal path is what production serves, and 47 tests said so. Verified by looking at every page in a browser: console clean, every request 200 or 304, no element wider than the viewport, and the label colours measured rather than assumed. 25 new tests, **1,168 pass on both runners** (`1,170` on `unittest` discovery).

- **🔓 Signing in with Discord could hand you to somebody else's site, and the "picture for this server" panel could not accept a picture**: both were found by driving the real surfaces and asserting on what they actually emitted, and both are in the sign-in and upload paths that were added last pass. **The open redirect was a tab.** ``_safe_next`` allowed any string starting with ``/`` that did not start with ``//`` — and the *tab* is stripped when Werkzeug builds the redirect's ``Location`` header, so ``/\t/evil.example`` arrived at the browser as ``//evil.example``: the person completed Discord's sign-in **on this domain** and was then handed to a protocol-relative URL, which is the whole shape of a phishing link. A newline was worse again — the header setter refuses to write the value at all, so the callback **raised and answered 500** *after* the session had already been replaced, i.e. you were signed in and shown an error page. A backslash was the same trick one layer down, because browsers normalise it to ``/`` for http(s). Only a printable, same-site path is a path now, and both outcomes are pinned end to end: the hostile shapes are driven through the real callback (which lands on ``/servers``, not off-site) and every shape of ``next`` is checked, including the ones that must still come through (``/dashboard?guild_id=5``). **The upload was a number, not a bug in the upload code.** ``MAX_CONTENT_LENGTH`` was **16 KB for the whole app**, and Flask checks it *before* any view runs — while ``identity`` accepts images of up to **4 MB**. So a 20 KB PNG was refused by the 413 handler with *"That message is larger than the dashboard accepts (16 KB). Shorten it or split the send."* and a 48-byte one was applied; the feature that lets a server give the bot its own picture could only ever accept a file smaller than a favicon, and the refusal it got was about messages. Only the uploaded file in the suite — 46 bytes — was small enough to pass, which is why nothing caught it. The app-level cap is now the largest legitimate request and each route's real limit is applied **per request**, so the tight 16 KB still holds everywhere else (a 30 KB composer body is refused in the message's own words) while ``/api/identity`` gets a bound that two pictures fit in, with a refusal that names the upload and its size. Everything above was mutation-checked — putting the old ``_safe_next`` back fails five tests by name, and putting the single 16 KB cap back fails the two upload tests — and **1,143 tests pass on both runners** (`1,145` on `unittest` discovery), with the live dashboard re-driven in the browser afterwards: console clean, every request 200, and an 11 MB body refused as an upload rather than as a message.
- **🪞 The preview was serving a page from before the edit, and the composer's counter claimed tags it did not have**: two defects of the same family — an instrument that reports something other than what is there. Flask defaults `TEMPLATES_AUTO_RELOAD` to `app.debug`, which the preview harness leaves off, so a long-lived preview held the **bytecode-cached** template: change the page, reload, and the browser shows the old one — the failure mode where a fix is verified against a page that never received it, and which this pass hit for real (an edit was read back as absent from a server that had started before it). The harness now rereads the template per request, which was watched rather than assumed: a sentinel written into `templates/dashboard.html` appears on the next fetch with no restart, and disappears again when it is reverted. The second was the composer's own counter, which said **`0 / 2000 incl. tags` on an empty box** — a claim about a count that has nothing in it, inviting the reader to hunt for tags that are not there. It now names the tags only while tags are actually in the total (`0 / 2000` empty, `5 / 2000` with text, `27 / 2000 incl. tags` with one chip), and still says nothing in embed mode, where the tags ride in the content and the server counts them separately — the same rule `_build_message` applies. Both are pinned: the guard that forbids the template from stating a limit of its own caught the first draft of this fix, because the reasoning comment quoted the limit it was reasoning about, which is exactly the class of drift it exists to prevent; **1,133 tests pass on both runners**, `bot.db` untouched (mtime 12:20 against a 14:12 clock), and the live page re-driven afterwards — empty, text-only, tagged, embed, and the 26th tag refused with the bot's own *"At most 25 tags per message."* — console clean, every request 200.
- **🎭 The preview answered "SENT" to messages the bot refuses, and let its queue pass the cap it was reporting to the page**: driving the dashboard as its first user would — a 2,100-character body pasted in, then a tag added — produced a receipt reading *"SENT 13:24"* where the bot answers *"That message is 2100 characters; the limit is 2000."* and posts nothing. The page had even marked its own counter `counter over`; nothing acted on it. This is worse than an unfaithful preview: the harness is the instrument a defect is *found* with, so one that reports success invents a working feature and hides the real one behind it. Both composer endpoints now call the **real** validator (`keep_alive._build_message`) instead of restating it — the same reason the scheduling endpoints already call `_schedule_timing` — so the harness cannot disagree with the bot about channels, tag counts, a title with no embed, or length; the refusals are compared word for word in a test rather than trusted to a shared import, and one of them is reachable *only* through a tag (a body of 1,990 characters plus one resolved mention is 2,013), which is exactly the case a `maxlength` attribute does not prevent. Two more doors of the same lie were closed with it: the scheduling route could **queue** a message the bot refuses to queue (it hand-rolled its own checks, and had no length rule at all), and the queue accepted past the `max_pending` it was handing the page — filling it to *"32 of 25"* with 429s never returned. A generated message is the one kind with no text to validate, so the builder is deliberately **not** run for it, exactly as the real route does not: the first version of this fix refused the only scheduled message that writes itself, which is the same defect from the other side — refusing what production accepts. Reported, not changed: with the queue full the page prints "25 of 25" and its Schedule button stays enabled, so the limit is learned from the toast rather than before clicking; the toast does name the cure. Verified through the browser after the fix: the over-limit send now shows *"Could not send: That message is 2100 characters; the limit is 2000."* with the body still in the box, a generator scheduled with nothing written still succeeds ("Weekly server digest — built when it posts"), and the 26th schedule is refused with *"cancel one before adding another"*. 1,131 tests pass on both runners.
- **🫥 The command panel said "Every command the bot has is offered on this server" directly above the thirty-one names that were not, and the preview let the same system be on in one panel and off in the next**: two defects the same shape — a page describing a state it was not in. The note is a `.field-head`, so `display: flex`, and `display: flex` outranks the browser's own `[hidden]` rule; the rule guarding this family already existed for four other elements and this one — the newest — had been missed, so the attribute was set by the server's render *and* by the live refresh and the browser ignored both. It is now guarded by class rather than by that one id, so the next `.field-head` is covered too, and the guard test reads the **template source** as well as one render: an element hidden by `{% if … %} hidden{% endif %}` only carries the attribute in the renders where its condition is true, which is how a fixture whose state has nothing hidden could not see it. Restoring the missing rule fails that test by name (`.field-head`). The harness (`scripts/preview_dashboard.py`) had the second version of the same lie: its switches flipped their own in-memory stub and wrote nothing down, so the module cards said *Economy on* while the command list and the All-servers page — both reading the real store — said *off*, and only the stub was believed. It now writes through the real `utils.modules` (safe by design: the process works on a copy of the database made at import), which makes every panel agree and makes the preview show what production would instead of what it was seeded with — driving Economy off through the page now moves **all** of them: the switch, the panel's 90/0 to 59/31, and the All-servers line to "10 of 11 systems on · off: Economy & Casino". Its two bulk endpoints do the same, and "disable all" reaches every toggleable module rather than the three cards on screen, which is the state an operator is actually in when the bot looks broken. One test had to stop reading the harness's seed values as truth: it now renders first, so its baseline is what the page shows, and names a server no other test switches — a baseline of seed values is right only by accident once another test has legitimately changed the store. Driving the All-servers page found a third: its **Manage** button posted to `/servers` and was answered with the same page — "this harness holds no session, so there is nothing to switch" — so the one control for opening another server visibly did nothing. The harness now keeps which server it is in, redirects the way production does, and the picker says **Open** where you already are and **Manage** on the rest; an id it is not in is refused in production's own words at its own status. 1,129 tests pass on both runners, and the checkout's `bot.db` is untouched (mtime 12:20 against a 13:17 clock).
- **🗂️ Every server now has its own command list, and the bot can be renamed and re-pictured in one server without touching another**: Discord reaches a command to every server an application is in, and offers **no** way to narrow that per server — no permission bit, context or option hides a global command in one guild while another keeps it. So a server that turned the market off kept `/market` in its picker and kept being told the market was off when a member clicked it, which is what "make the commands separate per server" was actually about. The tree is now registered **per server**, from that server's own module state: turn a system off and its commands leave that server's picker; turn it back on and they come back. Three pieces make that real rather than theoretical — **the dashboard republishes a server's list when a switch flips** (a module toggle *is* a command-set change; the writes happen on the bot's own loop, and a burst of switches costs one publish, not eleven), **a server added while the bot is running publishes on join** (the shared scope no longer carries the tree, so without this a new server would sit with an almost-empty picker until a deploy), and `force_sync.py` — the operator's own tool — now publishes each server's filtered list instead of the whole tree. The rule itself is one pure function (`utils/command_scope.py`), read by the publisher, the tool and the panel so they cannot disagree. **Discord's global scope keeps only what has to work with no server to resolve it** — the help panel and the policies, the two commands a member can run in a DM — and the copies of everything else that were still registered there are removed by a migration with one guard that matters: only once **every** server has published its own list with nothing pending. A failed publish followed by a successful delete would leave that server with no commands at all. Nothing is ever removed by name-absence: a command may only be taken out of a scope that the feature registry declares and that the scope's own modules turn off, so a typo upstream can leave a command visible but cannot delete one somebody is using. `sync_commands`'s old "guild lane" (and its nine skipped tests) come back as the only lane, this time because a per-server list is the feature rather than a duplicate of what the global scope already carried. **For safety, two defects the invariant behind this change found**: `cogs/tracker.py`'s `/activity` commands checked **nothing** — the listeners that *collect* activity honoured the toggle, the commands did not, so a server with the tracker off kept all three while reading rows nothing was writing any more; and `/dbstatus` was refused whenever the **moderation** module was off, because it was missing from the four names `cogs/server_control.py` exempts from that cog's gate — asking whether the database is healthy has nothing to do with moderation. Both are fixed, and the invariant is now a test: *every command a server loses is one the bot would have refused there* — the real cogs are loaded, every module switched off, and each command's own `interaction_check` called, so the hidden set and the refused set must be the same set in both directions. That test is what found both defects. **A server can also put its own name, picture, banner and bio on the bot**: Discord keeps those four on the bot's **member** in each guild (`PATCH /guilds/{id}/members/@me`), so they are per server by construction and nothing in the new panel can reach the application profile — `/bot avatar` and `/bot banner` stay the operator's and stay global, which is the containment the global profile is missing today. Who may: a one-time dashboard link is minted by the `/help` panel for an **administrator of one server** and is scoped to it, so that session changes its own server and no other (pinned: a request naming another server lands on its own); the passkey is the operator's and may do the same anywhere. Images are sniffed and measured before Discord is asked (a sentence naming PNG/JPEG/GIF/WEBP instead of a bare 400), the length limits are Discord's own, a save is applied to Discord **before** it is recorded so a refusal cannot leave the panel showing a name the server does not have, only the fields actually submitted are sent (a nickname change cannot clear a picture), and the stored picture is served from our own record rather than hot-linked, which the page's own CSP would block. The dashboard carries both panels and reads the command-scope numbers from the live endpoint rather than guessing, so the panel and the picker cannot drift. Verified in the browser against the real template: the save posts multipart to the real route and surfaces the harness's honest refusal, and switching Economy off moved the panel from 90 offered / 0 hidden to **59 / 31** — the 20 economy commands plus the 11 that need economy *and* another module at once. **Two more harness fixes** (`scripts/preview_dashboard.py`), both the shape this file keeps producing: its command list was built from the features' own `gate_commands`, which omits the commands several modules gate together, so it claimed the bot had 79 commands where it has 90; and the identity panel was rendered disabled, which hid the form's own error path from the one tool that exists to look at it. A live `force_sync.py --dry-run` confirmed the per-server filter against the real application — the home server would receive all 90, and the Development server 7 — and is also the reason the migration itself was **not** run from this checkout: the local `bot.db` still holds the all-off module rows from the incident two passes earlier, so publishing from here would have stripped 83 commands from that server. The deploy restores the current backup, and the bot publishes from that state on its next boot. 1,059 tests pass (was 992; 34 new for the identity store and endpoint, 24 for the scope rule and the publisher, and the nine guild-lane tests are un-skipped rather than replaced).
- **🔐 A server's own administrators can now open the dashboard and see only the servers they run, and the bot can be added to another one from the page**: both existing ways in belonged to the operator — a one-time link is scoped to the one server it was minted in, and the passkey is the whole bot — so neither could answer the question every other bot's dashboard answers: *which of my servers is this bot in, and which of those may I manage?* That answer depends on the person, so the dashboard grew a third way in. **Sign in with Discord** asks for `identify` and `guilds` and nothing else (no email, no connections, no messages), and the rule lives in one pure function (`utils/discord_auth.py`): you see a server when you hold **Manage Server** or **Administrator** or own it *and* the bot is already there — the intersection, because a server the bot has left has nothing to configure and a server you cannot configure is Discord's refusal arriving later and less clearly. `permissions` arrives from Discord as a **string**, so the filter parses it; comparing it to an int would keep every owner and drop every administrator, which reads as "the list is just short" rather than as a bug. **The session is scoped, not merely filtered**: `_resolve_guild_id` gained a `user` branch that checks every request against that person's list, recomputed from the bot's live guilds each time — so a request naming somebody else's server is served their own, a server the bot has left or the person was demoted from stops being reachable immediately rather than when the cookie expires, and `/servers` refuses a hand-posted `guild_id` by name ("You do not manage that server.", 403) instead of switching. `state` is single-use and compared in constant time, because the callback is a URL anybody can make a browser follow; `next` is accepted only as a same-site path, so `//evil.example` cannot turn sign-in into an open redirect; and the access token is used for two lookups in the same request and never stored — the session keeps a Discord id, display name, avatar URL and the server list, and nothing about the person reaches the database. **The bot can also be added to another server from the dashboard**: `invite_url()` builds Discord's own authorize URL with `bot`+`applications.commands` (without the second scope the invite produces a bot with no slash commands at all, which the README already documents as a support case), offered in the header and on `/servers`, with `guild_id`+`disable_guild_select` when a specific server is meant. `/servers` is also where somebody who manages nothing lands: an empty state that says so and offers the invite, rather than a dashboard showing a server they cannot open. **With no client id and secret the method is simply off** — the login page says which two variables are missing and prints the exact callback URL to register for the host it is running on, because an unregistered redirect URI is the most common way this feature "just says no" and Discord's own message reads like a bug in the bot. The passkey and one-time link are untouched, and a passkey session is not offered the picker (it already reaches every server). Docs move with it: the Privacy Policy's flat "no profile information is requested" is now false and says what signing in actually shares, `TERMS.md` says your Discord permissions are the credential (so removing somebody's roles removes their access here), `.env.example` documents both variables and the redirect URL to register, and `docs/architecture.md` answers both new questions. The policy had to be **tightened wording for wording** rather than appended to: Discord renders the whole document on one card inside a 6000-character budget, `test_legal.py` fails if any section falls off it, and the new facts put it 1,070 characters over — so the same facts are stated in fewer words everywhere, both documents carry the same new effective date (another thing that test checks), and the file now fits with room to spare. 47 new tests, and the ones that matter were watched to fail: the string-permissions case, the intersection, the other server's page, the forged form, and the replayed state.
- **🧹 This checkout's database now agrees with the deploy, which it had not for two days**: the local `bot.db` still held all eleven modules switched **off** for the Development server — the rows `scripts/preview_dashboard.py` wrote during the incident — while the backup the deploy actually restores holds none off. Read side by side rather than assumed, the local copy was the odd one out, so it was refreshed from the newest Supabase snapshot (`restore_from_cloud`) and both servers now read **90 of 90** commands from here. The safety copy of the old file sits beside it as `bot.db.before-refresh-2026-09-19` (`.gitignore` now covers `bot.db.*`). Correcting the record while doing it: `force_sync.py` was **never** the destructive path this changelog warned about — it upserts and retires named commands and never applies the per-server removal rule, so a run from a divergent checkout would have published a short list into one server's scope and left every command visible, wasting writes rather than taking commands away. The boot is the only path that removes the stale global copies, and it runs on the deploy, from the restored database.
- **⏱️ Eleven module switches now really do cost one publish, and the coalescing that protects the write budget is tested at last**: the dashboard's **Disable all** flips every module in a row and each flip asks the bot to republish that server's commands, so a burst is coalesced behind a short settle window — but the pass cleared its own "this server changed" flag *before* waiting, which booked every click that landed during the wait as a fresh change and bought a second full pass over every scope for a state that had not moved. The flag is now cleared after the wait, so the wait absorbs the burst while a switch that flips once the publishing has started still gets its own pass. Measured rather than argued: eleven clicks 10 ms apart produce **one** publish that carries the state the clicking left behind, a click landing mid-publish produces exactly **two** (the second carrying the new state), and a second click while one is already queued starts no second task. None of that had a test — only the dashboard's *call* to a stubbed hook was pinned — so the burst, the mid-publish click and the refusals (a server the bot is not in, a bot whose loop is not running, no server named) are pinned now, and clearing the flag in the old order was watched to fail the burst test with `2 != 1`. Everything else was driven against a fake application that models Discord's two scopes as the API does — per-scope commands with the id a delete needs, reads shaped as `AppCommand` objects carrying the server-only keys and **omitting the ones still at default** (the shape that once made every unchanged command look changed), and a server's commands shadowing a global one of the same name — and asserted on **what a member would see in the picker** rather than on what we stored. Starting from the live application's real state (all 90 commands global, nothing per server, which is exactly what the real application holds today), the migration registers each server's own list, leaves every server seeing all 90, trims the shared scope to `help` and `legal`, and then: a module switched off in one server removes its commands from **that** server only; switching it back on returns them; a second boot with nothing changed costs **zero writes and zero deletes** (the 200-a-day budget is spent on real changes only); a server added while the bot runs publishes its own list on join; a server whose publish fails leaves the shared scope **untrimmed**, so no server can lose commands to a half-finished migration; the commands queued behind a refused one still register; and with every module off a server keeps exactly `help` and `legal`, which are structurally undeletable because they are the two names the feature registry does not declare. 33 checks across two harnesses, all of them passing after the fix. Verified through the dashboard's own surface too: the live panel read **90 offered / 0 hidden**, matching the rule computed independently in Python, then **59 / 31** the moment Economy was switched off through the page — the same numbers, with the same 31 hidden names, that `command_scope` computes for that state.
- **🐞 `/bot bug` failed with `50035 — Invalid Form Body … placeholder: Must be 100 or fewer in length`, so the report form never opened for anyone**: the command's whole job is to open one modal, and Discord refuses a text input whose placeholder runs past 100 characters — at the moment the modal would appear, not when it is built — so `/bot bug` and the `/help` panel's 🐞 button both answered *"An unexpected error occurred"* and the reporter had no way to file anything. The string was `BUG_REPORT_HINT`, **122 characters** long, passed to `discord.ui.TextInput` by name so nothing about the call site looked wrong; it now reads *"What happened, and what you expected instead? The command you ran, the steps, and any error text."* at 97, and the shape of the failure is checked rather than the one string: `tests/test_static_health.py` measures every `placeholder`, `label` and `description` handed to a `TextInput`, `Select`, `SelectOption` or `Button` in the live tree against Discord's own cap for that component, resolving a module-level constant as well as a literal, because the value that broke was a constant and a comparison against the literal alone would have missed it. Both facts were confirmed against the object the bot actually sends: the real modal, built through the real code path, serialised to the payload Discord receives — `components.0.components.0.placeholder`, character for character the path the 50035 error named — now measures 97. The guard is mutation-checked by putting the 122-character hint back, which fails it by name. Reported, not changed: the same sweep looked for a second instance of the class and found none — the only other computed component strings are a trivia answer option (longest 12 characters, against 80) and the ticket category names (longest 32, against 100).
- **📈 Nobody could reach the market pages from the dashboard, and the preview 404'd on the first click of the link that now goes there**: the charts are served at `/market/<symbol>`, and **nothing** in any template linked to them — the only way in was a member's `/market` command in Discord, so an operator reading the control center had to already know the URL. The header now carries **📈 Market** beside the server switcher, the same tab and the same page family. Driving it in the browser turned up three more dead ends, all in `scripts/preview_dashboard.py` and all the same shape as the `/` 404 fixed one pass earlier: the new button lands on `/market`, which production **redirects** to the first instrument and the harness answered with Flask's 404; an unknown symbol rendered a bare `<h1>404 nope</h1>` where production writes "404 — nope is not on the market" and offers BTC, so a misspelt symbol (a thing people do) taught nothing; and the header's own **Sign out** button 404'd, because the harness serves no session — both `/login` and `/logout` now answer a page saying exactly that, with a way back, rather than a wall. What held up under careless hands, re-checked after the fix: sending with nothing ticked and nothing written (“Pick at least one channel first.”, no request at all), three clicks on **Send** as fast as they can be issued (**one** POST, one receipt, the button back to "Send message"), a junk channel filter (“No channel matches “zzzz”.”), a schedule time in the past (refused by name, no request), a time 49 hours out ("in 2 days", which is the floor-to-nearest fix from the previous pass still holding), and the market page under the real CSP with the chart drawn and “live · prices from preview · updated 12s ago” — every request 200, the only console entry the intentional 404 of the bogus symbol. The decisive check of the database fix was made through the browser rather than read: clicking the preview's **Disable all** turns its own three switches off and leaves **production's eleven modules enabled** — the exact click that caused the outage last pass.
- **🩺 Every gated command in the main server answered *"the X system is currently turned off"* — switched off by the dashboard preview, which was writing the live database**: all eleven modules in the Development server were off, so `/balance`, `/rank` and every other gated command replied that the system was turned off *on this server* while the same commands worked everywhere else — a bot that looks broken in exactly the server its owner was looking at. Nothing in Discord had switched them off: the eleven rows in `guild_modules` were written seconds apart on 17 September, by `scripts/preview_dashboard.py`. That harness is the documented way to eyeball the dashboard, it imports the real `utils.db`, and the pages it serves write through it — module toggles, `/api/toggle-all`, scheduled messages, channel bindings — so one click on its bulk toggle in a playthrough was enough to turn all eleven off, and the bot running on the same checkout uploaded that database as the **production backup**, which is what the Render deploy restored. Two fixes, one of them outside the code: the state is repaired (all eleven modules back on for Development, through the real `/api/toggle-all` so the change persists *and* schedules its own backup, then read back off the live page the operator sees), and the harness now copies `bot.db` into a temporary directory before `keep_alive` is imported, so what a person clicks in the preview is discarded with the process and the live file is never opened. The copy is made before the import because the import is what reads the path — the same ordering trap that made a test's database patch useless once. Reported, not changed: the 3,680 fixture rows already in `scheduled_messages` are inert and prune themselves under the existing 90-day history window.
- **🧪 The suite was writing into the operator's database, and both runners now run somewhere else**: the same `bot.db` held **3,680** rows in `scheduled_messages` for guild `777001` and channels `555001` — fixtures from `test_scheduler.py`, the one database-touching test file that does not use `tests.support.TempDatabaseMixin`, because `db.setup()` and every insert went through the *default* path. It was not contained: the bot running on this checkout uploaded it on its 25-second cloud loop, so developer fixtures are in the production backup and in the live instance. `tests/support.py` already stated the rule — nothing in this suite may touch a file inside the project — but a guard each test has to remember to use is not a guard, and the fix had to be a file **both** runners load: the check in `.github/workflows/tests.yml` discovers with `unittest`, which never looks at a `conftest.py`, so the redirect lives in `tests/__init__.py`, which is imported before any module inside it by pytest and by discovery alike.
