The suite now builds its schema in a temporary directory, and after a full run of each runner the operator's `bot.db` is **byte-for-byte unchanged** (`sha256` compared before and after) — which is the claim, rather than a reading of the code. `tests/test_db_isolation.py` pins all of it: the suite's path is outside the project, `cloud_sync` resolves the *same* file it does (a backup of a different file is how this data got uploaded in the first place), the `unittest` runner gets the guard too, and the preview harness — checked in its own process, because importing it would move this process's database — reports a path outside the project while leaving the live file's size and mtime untouched. 4 new tests (`984 → 988`).
- **🔢 The command picker was missing the two newest commands and still offering twelve dead ones, and every boot said nothing about it**: `/bot` and `/legal` were in the tree and could not be created, while `/dashboard`, `/updatedb`, `/sync`, `/setavatar`, `/setbanner`, `/addlevelrole`, `/removelevelrole`, `/privacy`, `/tos`, `/meowfee`, `/activity-top` and `/activity-channel` were still registered even though the code had folded them away — so typing `/botdb` matched nothing, which is the screenshot this started from, while `/updatedb` offered a door that no longer exists. Discord read back **exactly 100 chat-input commands**, its ceiling, and twelve of them were dead: `RETIRED_COMMANDS` — the *only* list that may delete a registered command, because the publisher can never infer a removal from a missing local name without risking the destructive bulk overwrite this project already lived through — still named just the three `upload` commands, so the folds left their commands registered forever. With no slot free, creating a new command is refused with ``30032 Maximum number of application commands reached``. Two more faults hid it: `_publish_scope` returned on the **first** refusal, so the three legitimate updates queued behind the create (`activity`, `eco-admin`, `levelroles`) never went out either, and the boot line printed only `published 0, 32 still pending` — the reason was sitting unprinted in the sync's own `detail` field, and the only automatic retry was scheduled for a *spent write budget*, so the identical silent no-op repeated on every boot. All three are fixed: the twelve folded names are on the retirement list (with `/activity` deliberately left off — the group *is* that name now, so deleting it would take the group with it), a refused command no longer stops the ones after it while a spent daily budget still stops everything (that window is closed for every command), and the boot prints the reason and, for `30032`, names the cure. `force_sync.py` — the documented path for a terminal — now retires too, which it did not, which made it unable to free a full scope at all; it also waits out a short `429` rather than leaving a command undeleted. Verified live against the real application: the tool retired all twelve and published `/bot` and `/legal`, and Discord now holds **exactly the 90 commands the tree has** — `/bot` with its six subcommands, `/legal` with `privacy`/`tos`, `/activity` as the group, no folded name anywhere — leaving ten slots free. The ceiling was the diagnosis and not a theory: the creates that had failed on every boot succeeded the moment twelve slots were freed. 7 new tests (`973 → 980`), and the old pinned assertion ("exactly the upload commands") is the one that had to change.
- **🔧 Every command in the bot failed at random, and neither host log named the cause: two copies of the bot were online at once, and a command that breaks left no trace anywhere the operator reads**: `/balance` answered *The application did not respond*, the same command had worked an hour earlier, and `#real-time-cmd-data` — the channel built to show exactly this — stayed empty. The host logs said `40060 Interaction has already been acknowledged` and `10062 Unknown interaction`, which is the signature of a *duplicate* bot rather than a broken one: Discord delivers an interaction to **every** connected session of one application, so with a copy running on the Mac and the deploy running on Render each command was handled twice — whichever copy answered first won, the other raised *already been acknowledged*, and an interaction that reached a session that had gone quiet was answered too late to be accepted at all, which is what a member sees as "the application did not respond". The second copy was not a guess: the backup lease in Supabase named it (`srv-daiqibp594qs739qebc0-hibernate-…`, refreshed every two minutes). Nothing in either log pointed at it, so three things are fixed. **The boot now says it out loud**: the check that already stops two copies fighting over the *backup* (`cloud_sync.other_live_copy`, off the event loop) is asked the same question about the *gateway*, and a boot that finds another live copy posts one card in the operator's own `#bot-error` naming the other copy, the symptom in the member's words, and which copy to stop — this one, since the copy holding the database is the one keeping everyone's progress. A lease that cannot be read, an empty bucket and a stale lease all answer "nobody", because a false alarm here is worse than silence and "I cannot tell" must never stop a legitimate boot — and the first deploy after this change proved the point: the container that came up **reported the pod it had just replaced as a second copy**, telling the operator to stop the deployment they had just made. Render gives every container its own hostname and a rolling deploy overlaps the two, so the lease names "another instance" that is the same deployment's previous pod; a sibling of this container's own service is now logged and not announced, while a laptop beside a deploy — the case that actually breaks commands — still is, and the backup's own rule is untouched, because refusing to write while the previous container may still be writing is what the lease is for. **A failed command is written down now**: `on_app_command_completion` is dispatched only in the `else` branch of discord.py's own call, so a command that raised never reached the log at all — the empty channel read as "nobody uses this bot" while the bot was in fact broken, which is the exact inversion the log exists to prevent. `command_failed` posts the missing entry — the command, the runner, the server, the channel and the **reason**, unwrapped from `CommandInvokeError` — and it never touches the interaction, because the failures worth recording are precisely the ones whose answer already failed; only a *Discord* error carries its message, while a Python-level failure keeps its type alone, since a message raised while handling what somebody typed can quote it back and this log promises never to copy that. **And a failed command is answered now**: `cogs/tracker.py`'s three `@…​.error` handlers ended with `raise error` for everything that was not a permission failure, and discord.py calls a command's own handler *before* `tree.on_error` without guarding it — so the tree's handler never ran, the interaction was never answered, and the member got silence for every failure that was not a missing role. They answer the permission case (through `style.send_card`, so a response that is already done cannot raise a second time) and leave the rest to the tree's handler, and a static rule over the live tree fails the suite if any command error handler re-raises again. Verified against the real thing rather than argued: both new cards were posted to the real `#real-time-cmd-data` and `#bot-error` through the REST API on a client that **never opens a gateway session** (so the copy that is still running could not lose an interaction to the check), read back component by component — `-# /balance` / `## ❌ Command failed` / `Reason: NotFound — 404 Not Found (error code: 10062): Unknown interaction`, and the duplicate card naming the Render copy and the fix — and deleted again, leaving both channels empty. 22 new tests (`951 → 973`).
- **🗓️ A message scheduled two days out said "in 1 day", and the preview answered its own documented address with a 404**: the composer was driven in a browser through the flows its first real user would attempt, careless versions included, and one sentence in it was wrong. `scheduleWhen` truncated to the whole unit — `Math.floor` on days, hours and minutes — while the time picker stores **minutes**, so "the same time in two days" is really 1 d 23 h 59 m away and floored to **"in 1 day"**: a full day early, on the one number that decides whether an announcement gets double-booked, printed both in the confirmation toast and on the queue row. It now rounds to the *nearest* unit, which is wrong by at most half a unit where truncation was wrong by a whole one, and a boundary lands exactly on the next unit up (23 h 59 m is "1 day", not "24 hours"). Four boundary cases pin it, at two days, one day, an hour and half an hour, and reverting to `Math.floor` was watched to fail it. The rest of the pass held up under hands: sending with nothing ticked, sending nothing at all, three clicks on **Send** as fast as they can be issued (one POST — the button reads **Sending…** and refuses re-entry), five on a module switch (one request, right end state), the bulk buttons' confirmation, a junk channel filter, a schedule time in the past, cancelling and firing from the queue, a reload **during** a send (the history row is written before the answer, so the page comes back with the send recorded and nothing double-posted), a message of 2500 characters, a typed `@everyone` (inert by construction: the endpoint builds `allowed_mentions` from the tag picker, never from the text), and the tag path end to end — a chip added, sent, and the stored payload read back as `mentions: ["@everyone"]`. Two suspicions were chased and dismissed by reading the surrounding code rather than the diff: an over-limit send *is* refused, on the second check that measures the assembled content including its tags, and a counter that read 31 for one `@everyone` chip was counting a 21-character draft the browser had restored into the textarea. What was actually broken was `scripts/preview_dashboard.py` again, and in four places: **opening the port it prints answered Flask's 404**, because the harness served `/dashboard` and never `/`, which is the first step of its own usage line; the **market page drew a permanent "offline — could not reach the bot"** over a page whose every request had answered 200, since the stubs returned `{"quotes": {}}` and `{"candles": []}` and the page's `renderQuotes` threw on the object while its `catch` blamed the network; the **server switcher navigated to `?guild_id=` and the harness ignored the query**, so picking the second server came back showing the first with the select snapped back — a control that visibly does nothing, in the one tool whose job is showing what the page does; and the **generator picker offered a single option**, because `schedule_content_choices` was never passed, so the one kind of scheduled message that writes its own text could not be reached from the preview at all. All four are fixed and pinned: `/` answers the liveness line for a monitor and redirects a browser (as production does), the market stubs are now built by **`quotes_payload` and `series_payload` themselves** so the preview cannot drift from the keys production sends, `/dashboard?guild_id=` renders the server that was asked for and falls back to the first on an unknown id exactly as `_resolve_guild_id` does, and the generators come from the registry the endpoint validates against. Each was mutation-checked (the floor put back, the old stub shapes put back, the route renamed away, the guild id hardcoded) and each reported the failure. The 60-point series is labelled **observed**, not exchange OHLC, because the page prints that label and inventing traded prices is the claim this project keeps removing. 5 new tests. Reported, not changed: the harness serves no `/login` or `/logout`, so its visible **Sign out** button is a dead end (it is a render harness with no session, and pretending otherwise would teach nothing); its send stub reports success for a channel the real endpoint refuses; and it re-implements 24 endpoints by hand, which is where all four of these defects came from.
- **🔍 Driving the dashboard in a real browser found the harness lying, not the page**: the composer was put through the careless versions of its own flows — sending with nothing ticked, sending nothing at all, three clicks on **Send** as fast as they can be issued, five on a module switch, a junk channel filter, a schedule time in the past, cancelling everything, and a message full of `@everyone` — and it held up: each dead end answers with a sentence naming the fix ("Pick at least one channel first.", "Write something to send.", "That time has already passed — pick a time in the future."), the send button reads **Sending…** and refuses re-entry so three clicks make **one** post, every switch has a pending guard, a typed `@everyone` cannot ping (the endpoint sends `allowed_mentions` built from the tag picker, never from the text), the over-limit counter goes `over` at 2000, and an emptied queue says "Nothing queued. Write a message, choose Schedule, pick a time — it is posted even if this page is closed" rather than showing a blank box. What the same pass *did* find broken was `scripts/preview_dashboard.py`, the documented way to eyeball this page: it had **no `/api/toggle` or `/api/toggle-all` route**, so every module switch and both bulk buttons were answered with Flask's 404 page — the page showed its red "Request failed (404)" toast and the switch sprang back, which is the dashboard's headline control looking broken in the one tool that exists to check it. Both routes (plus `/__last-toggle`, matching the harness's existing `/__last-send`) are now served, a missing module is refused the way the real endpoint refuses it, and a test reads the page and the harness and fails if the page ever calls an endpoint the harness does not serve — mutation-checked by renaming the two routes away and watching it report them. The same pass corrected the harness's seed data, which omitted the channel `name` from a *successful* receipt: real rows carry one, and seeding one without made the page's id fallback look like a defect in a browser when it is in fact the honest answer for the only two records that can lack a name ("I am not in that server", "That channel no longer exists"). Reviewing that work adversarially turned up three more: the two new harness routes answered an unknown module **404** where the real endpoint answers **400 "Unknown module"** (a stub that lies about the refusal teaches the page's error path the wrong thing, so both routes now match ``keep_alive``'s status and words, including "No guild available"); the operator-log setup card was called **inside the boot loop's single `try`**, so a failure on a cosmetic card would have skipped binding every server after it — the exact inverse of that loop's purpose, and it now has its own guard; and fuzzing every entry point of `utils/operator_log.py` with a client whose lookups raise, a guild whose name is ``None``, a member whose display name is ``None`` and a command whose qualified name is ``None`` found ``announce_setup`` **raising** where its siblings returned ``False`` — its guard had covered the send but not the lookups that find the channel to send to. All four are pinned, the fuzz is now a test (no entry point may raise, whatever it is handed), and the setup card was re-posted to its real channel afterwards and read back to confirm the restructure did not change what it says.
- **📊 The operator can now see who has the bot and what people actually run with it**: two channels in the bot's own server were named for this and fed by nothing. `#active-servers` now receives a card every time a server **adds** the bot — its name and id, its size, its owner, and who invited it (read from the server's audit log when View Audit Log allows, and honestly marked *unknown — needs View Audit Log* when it does not, which a fresh invite has not granted yet) — and a card every time one **removes** it, which is the same event whether the bot was kicked, the server deleted, or the bot banned, plus how long it had been there. `#real-time-cmd-data` receives every command anyone runs anywhere: the command, the person, their id, the server, the channel and the time. It hooks **completion**, not `on_interaction`, because a command that failed never completes and a log that counted attempts as uses would read as healthy while the bot was broken. A server added *while the bot is running* is set up on the spot rather than waiting for a restart: the join handler binds that server's own house channels by name and sends it the release note too, and those three steps are isolated from each other so a channel list it cannot read does not cost it the note or the log entry. Both channels belong to the operator by construction — `roles_for` refuses `guilds` and `cmdlog` on any other server, so a member's server cannot bind them even by naming a channel to match, and nothing this module writes ever lands in a member's own server. Three rules, each from a failure this project already had, and each pinned by a test: an entry may never break the thing it logs (a missing channel, a refusing channel and an unreadable audit log are all reported and swallowed — a command that just worked must not fail on its way into the log); an entry may never ping, which is not theoretical since a **server's name is a stranger's text** and `@everyone` is a legal server name — the real payload was read back to confirm it carries `AllowedMentions(everyone=False, …)`; and **what a person typed is never copied into the log** (a `/bug` report, a `/pay` note, a `/purge` reason are the person's, and the command, its runner and its outcome are what the log is for). Verified live against the two real channels: a join, a leave and a command each posted once with the right fields — server and member as plain name-plus-id, never a raw mention — and all three were deleted again, leaving both channels empty. Both halves of the first run are stated rather than assumed, because a feature that is installed and writes nowhere looks exactly like one that works: the boot that binds these channels posts a **one-time card in the channel itself** saying what arrives in each (and, if one of the two is missing, naming the channel to create), and the first entry that finds nothing bound reports **where the operator already looks** — the update channel, then the error channel, then the log channel — naming the channel to create, that `/bot db` binds it without a restart, and that the notice is sent *once per channel, not once per entry*, so it cannot bury the channel it is trying to save. With nothing bound anywhere it says so on the host once and stays quiet rather than looping. The removal card also stopped implying a reason it does not have: Discord reports a kick, a deletion and a ban as one event, and the card now says exactly that instead of letting the reader guess. Both cards were read back out of the live channels with Discord's own renderer before being deleted, which is how two faults in this pass were caught: the setup card came out **silent on a real first run** because it consulted the store for bindings it had just been handed (a write that had not landed would have cost the one card whose whole job is to explain the channel), and the notice interpolated a whole sentence into a slot meant for an entry name, printing "I had a one card each time a server adds the bot to write". 28 new tests, and removing the two calls from `bot.py` was watched to fail four of them.
- **📜 The policies now describe the bot that is actually running, and the operator's half is written down**: both documents were dated 16 September and had fallen behind — they predated the dashboard composer and its scheduler, the `/bot bug` path, and the move from an encrypted Gist to a private Supabase bucket. Version 2 of [`PRIVACY.md`](PRIVACY.md) and [`TERMS.md`](TERMS.md) fixes the claims that were simply wrong and adds the ones a member cannot discover for themselves: a bug report **leaving its server** for the bot's own when the server has no bug channel, and carrying the reporter's mention and server name with it; announcements composed in the dashboard — including the ones **scheduled** to post later — stored with their text, target channels, repeat rule and who queued them; the dashboard's own footprint (the `meow_session` cookie, HttpOnly / SameSite / Secure on HTTPS, up to 8 hours, the single-use 15-minute sign-in link, and a login rate limiter that counts failures **per address in memory only**, never in the database); the two retention windows (365 days of activity, 90 days of queued-and-sent message history) rather than one; and the backup's real protection — a private bucket holding **raw** `bot.db`, so the service-role key is the database's password and there is no second layer to promise. The third-party list was checked against the code rather than copied forward: the market's seven providers and the chart page's **unpkg.com / cdn.jsdelivr.net** are named, and the font CDN that no page has loaded since the dashboard dropped webfonts is gone. Three operator documents were missing entirely and now exist: [`docs/legal/README.md`](docs/legal/README.md) (what is served where, and the rule that these documents are contracts with the code), [`docs/legal/data-requests.md`](docs/legal/data-requests.md) (the store-by-store procedure for an access or deletion request, including the two traps that make the obvious version wrong — a boot restores a backup, so a local deletion can be undone, and a snapshot written before the request still holds the data), and [`docs/legal/sub-processors.md`](docs/legal/sub-processors.md) (every outbound service, what it receives, and how to drop it). One piece of stale configuration came out with them: `.env.example` still offered `GITHUB_BACKUP_TOKEN`, `GIST_BACKUP_ID`, `BACKUP_ENCRYPTION_KEY` and two cadence variables for a Gist path that had been deleted — telling an operator their database was encrypted at rest while the Privacy Policy said otherwise. A test now fails if the template documents a variable no code reads. All of it is pinned to the code it describes: the retention numbers come from `tracker.RETENTION_DAYS` and `scheduled.HISTORY_DAYS`, the cookie and link lifetimes from `keep_alive`'s own config, the CDN from the page that loads it, and the documents must still fit Discord's render budget with their last line arriving on the card — because a policy a member cannot read is not a policy. Each new guard was mutation-checked (the dead backup variables put back, the retention window mis-stated) and reported the failure; 18 tests where there were 8. Reported, not changed: the dashboard's CSP still allows a font CDN that nothing loads.
- **🐞 A bug report is a message again, and there is now a command for filing one that an administrator can reach**: reporting a fault in the bot was spelled two ways and both were wrong. The surface people actually reached for — the ticket panel's own *Bug Report & Glitch* category — opened a **private ticket channel**, which is a support conversation with one member, not a report the operators can see; and the `/help` panel's 🐞 button only worked in a server that had happened to create a channel named `#bug`, refusing everywhere else with "this server has no bug-report channel", which reads to whoever filed it as the bot losing their report. So `#bug` stayed empty. Reporting is now one path: the report is a **card in a channel** with a thread on it, never a ticket — the ticket panel is left as it was for members' own problems — and it is asked for in this order: the server it was filed in, then the **bot's own server** (`BOT_SERVER_GUILD_ID`, defaulting to the server whose `#update`, `#bot-error` and `#bug` exist for exactly this). The first target that accepts the post ends the walk, so one report is never posted twice, a channel that refuses falls through to the next, and a channel deleted since the panel was posted no longer strands the report. The bot's own server is looked at *by name* when no binding exists there, because a report must not be refused merely because a boot has not happened yet — the only rule reused is the one the binder itself uses, nothing is created, and a server that renamed its own channel still gets what it bound. `/bot bug` is the new administrator command (`/help`'s button stays open to everyone, since finding a bug is not an operator privilege), it reports as itself in the card's `Filed via` field, and the card names the server it came from — so a report that arrives in the bot's own server still says which server it was filed from. Verified against the live servers with a real post: a report filed in Hmm resolves to Development's `#bug` (bound or not), posts exactly once with `Server: Hmm` and `Filed via: /bot bug`, creates no ticket channel, and was cleaned up afterwards. 19 new tests, driving the real modal and the real resolver, including the case that produced the complaint: a server with no bug channel of its own.
- **✂️ Three command slots came back and no feature did**: `/dashboard`, `/addlevelrole` and `/removelevelrole` were spending three of Discord's hundred on doors the bot already offered somewhere better. The dashboard's single-use sign-in link is minted by the **⚡ Open Dashboard** button on the `/help` panel an administrator already opens — the same 15-minute token, scoped to the same member and server — and the level-role verbs are one group: `/levelroles view` for anyone, `/levelroles add` and `/levelroles remove` behind Manage Roles, each audience still declared separately in the one permission table. The tree is now **90 of 100** top-level commands, ten free, where it was at the ceiling and one command away from Discord refusing the next one outright. Folding them turned up the defect the folds hide best: the panel was still *advertising six commands that no longer existed* (`/meowfee`, `/activity`, `/activity-top`, `/activity-channel`, `/privacy` and `/tos`, each folded away earlier), which a member reads as "unknown command" with nothing in any log — the text now matches the tree, every "run `/dashboard` in Discord" instruction (the login page's own error states, the boot log, `README.md`, `SECURITY.md`, `docs/`) names the button instead, and a test walks the panel and fails if it ever names a command Discord does not have — mutation-checked by putting the three stale names back and watching it report all three. Merging `/lockdown-server` into `/lockdown` was the obvious fourth saving and is deliberately **not** taken: those two carry different audiences (Manage Channels versus Administrator), the permission table declares one audience per command, and merging them would either hand a channel moderator a server-wide lockdown or make the table stop describing what is actually enforced. One trade is taken knowingly: `/levelroles add` and `/levelroles remove` were hidden from members without Manage Roles as top-level commands, and as subcommands they are now *offered* and refused by the check instead — which is what all 39 subcommand audiences declared in that table already do, because discord.py writes `default_member_permissions` into the payload only for top-level commands (`Command.to_dict` skips it when a parent exists). Enforcement is unchanged and driven by the suite, which calls every gated command's own checks with an empty permission set and asserts it refuses; the gap is in how the tree is published, it predates this change, and it is worth its own fix.
- **🚀 `/bot push` — one command that puts an update live, and says which of the two things it did**: nothing inside Discord could ship a change. A bot process cannot commit — what is running on the host was built from the repository — so the command has exactly two honest shapes, and it never confuses them. With a Render Deploy Hook URL in `RENDER_DEPLOY_HOOK_URL` it POSTs the hook and hands the build over, and the boot that follows announces the release to every server by itself, which is why the command deliberately does *not* also post the notes (that would double them). With no hook it says plainly that no code moved and posts this version's notes there and then — read from the changelog that shipped *with* the running code, so they describe what is actually live. Either way a card goes out **before** anything is attempted and is edited with the outcome, because a deploy restarts the process and a record that was never written is the one failure this command must not have; it reports in the server's `#test-ground` when one is bound — the channel an operator is watching while they test — falling back to the update channel, and its reply maps each outcome (`posted`, `up_to_date`, `unbound`, `unreachable`, `failed`) to a sentence instead of a shrug. The hook URL is a credential, so it is never printed anywhere: a failure reports the status and the exception's *name*, not the URL that exception was quoting back.
- **🧩 The bot's own commands now cost one command slot instead of four**: `/setavatar`, `/setbanner`, `/sync` and `/updatedb` are `/bot avatar`, `/bot banner`, `/bot sync` and `/bot db`. Discord allows a hundred top-level commands per application and this bot was using exactly a hundred, so the next command anyone wanted would have been refused outright — the same wall that once stopped this cog from loading at all. A subcommand nests inside its parent and costs none of that budget, so the group leaves three slots free (97 of 100) and a test now fails if the tree ever closes that gap again. `#test-ground` also joins the house channels as `test` — decorated names still bind — and `/bot db`'s "still unbound" hint is built from the binder's own name table now, instead of a hand-written list that could fall out of step with it.
- **🔁 Updating a card now reaches Discord, so a failure that repeats is counted onto it instead of posted again**: `utils.style.edit_card` built its Components V2 payload with `embed=None` beside `embeds=[]`, and discord.py rejects a payload carrying both — by *key*, not by value — so every V2 edit raised `TypeError` and dropped into the legacy retry, which Discord refuses outright on a message that is already a card. The first caller this reached was the new repeat-counting on the error channel: the second occurrence of a failure could not be counted onto the card in the channel, so a second card appeared instead. The payload now carries exactly one embed keyword, a card that genuinely cannot be edited reports the real error instead of the rejection it provoked, and the starboard's star-count edits — failing silently the same way on every V2 card — are fixed by the same change. Caught by driving the live API: a real error card now reads *Seen **2** times since …*.
- **📍 Decorated channel names bind anyway, and a server added later gets its ping**: binding compared channel names exactly, so the home server's own `💬・bot-cmd` was invisible to it and a server that never ran `/updatedb` had nowhere to put level-up cards; names are now matched on their letters and digits too, with an exact name still winning over a decorated one. The release note's ping was one role id belonging to the home server, so a second server received the note with no mention in it; that id still wins where the server has it, and any other server is matched by role name (`Dev`, `Admin`, …) instead. Verified against both real servers: `#💬・bot-cmd` binds, and the new server's note pings its own `Dev`.
- **🛡️ A mis-set environment variable can no longer take the bot offline, and the health endpoint stopped claiming a disconnected bot is ready**: `ENABLE_MESSAGE_CONTENT_INTENT=1` beside a Developer Portal that has not granted Message Content makes Discord reject the *entire* identify payload, so the bot never connects at all — the app stays up answering HTTP while every command is silent, and the only clue is one line in a host log nobody reads. The bot now asks Discord what it has actually been granted (the `/applications/@me` flags) *before* asking for anything, and starts without an intent the portal has not enabled, naming what it dropped and which toggle brings it back; it never adds an intent nobody asked for. Separately, `/health`'s `bot_ready` came from a flag set once inside `on_ready` and never cleared, so a bot that had since dropped off the gateway still read as ready — the opposite of useful when the question is whether it is online. The endpoint now also answers a live `gateway` field (`connected`, `reconnecting`, `never_connected`) from `is_ready()`, keeping `bot_ready` for the monitors that already read it. The error channel no longer posts a card per failure either: repeats of the same failure are counted onto the card already in the channel ("Seen **7** times since 19:52 UTC") for ten minutes, so one broken command cannot bury the channel that exists to report it, and a card that can no longer be edited is forgotten so the next failure posts fresh instead of counting into a void. `/updatedb` now re-binds the house channels it can find by name and says which roles are still going nowhere — the one way to do that without a restart. 28 new tests, two of which caught bugs written in the same sitting: the intent flag check shifted its bits twice and read as "nothing was ever granted" (which would have silently dropped the members intent as well), and the failure card had lost the line naming who ran the command.
- **📍 A second server is now a first-class server: every guild says where the bot talks, and it listens**: four of the bot's house channels each pointed at one guild or at nothing at all. The update channel was a literal in `bot.py` naming the home server's, so a server added later never received a release note; the failure path knew only a process-wide `ERROR_WEBHOOK_URL`, which cannot say *which* server a command broke in; `bot_cmd_channel_id` was written by `/updatedb` and read by leveling, but nothing ever bound it, so every server but the home one posted **no level-up cards at all**; and `#bug` had nothing behind it. `utils/bot_channels.py` is now the one place that knows: `update`, `error`, `bug`, `commands` and `log` are per-guild settings, filled from channel *names* the first time a server is seen (`#update`, `#bot-error`, `#bug-report`, `#bot-cmd`, `#logs`, most specific name first) and authoritative from then on — so renaming `#bot-error` tomorrow does not quietly move where the bot reports. Binding runs for every guild in `on_ready` and is idempotent: the first boot after this deploy sets a new server up with no command and no dashboard visit, and every boot after it changes nothing. The deploy digest now fans out **per server**, in that server's own channel, pinging the dev role only where that role exists, and each server's "already told" record is its **own** row — so a server whose channel is unreachable retries by itself instead of dragging the others into a re-announcement it already showed them, and a server that has never been told still gets its first note instead of inheriting one it never saw. The pre-existing process-wide record is handed to the server it describes, once, so the server that has been reading these notes all along does not suddenly see its whole changelog again. An unexpected command failure also lands in that server's error channel, naming the command, who ran it and the error, with the webhook reporting unchanged. `#bug` gets a producer without costing a command slot the bot does not have: the existing `/help` panel carries a **🐞 Report a bug** button that opens a modal, posts the report to the guild's bug channel and opens a thread on it, so the discussion stays next to the report. Verified by driving the real announcement with two stand-in servers: each is told exactly once in its own channel, the role is pinged only where it exists, a second boot announces nothing, and a server whose send Discord refuses keeps the note it is still owed while the readable server is told; the same run under the old single shared record leaves the second server with **nothing**, which is the bug this fixes. 38 new tests, including one that fails if any single channel name could bind two roles.
- **🔔 A scheduled message that does not go out now tells someone, in the place the server already watches**: every other part of a scheduled message was engineered to be trustworthy — what gets posted is what was stored, the claim is exactly-once, a repeat keeps its own slot — and the *failure* of it was the one part with no audience. It was written down on the row and shown in a panel somebody has to open, which means a daily announcement that quietly stopped was invisible until someone wondered why it was quiet; the person who queued it was recorded and never told anything. A delivery that did not arrive now posts a card in the guild's **log channel** — the same `log_channel_id` the ticket ratings and moderation cases already use — tagging the person who queued it and nobody else: **⚠️ did not go out** with the reason, the channels it was meant for, the message itself, and what happens next (**"it tries again 09:00 — failure 1 of 3, then it stops"** for a repeat that still has attempts, **⛔ "has been stopped"** once it has given up, **⚠️ "part was refused"** when most of the announcement arrived and one channel did not, **⏭️ "was skipped"** for a run too late to be worth sending, and **"interrupted by a restart"** for one left mid-flight, which is the case where a half-posted message may already be sitting in the channel). A message that *did* arrive is not news — it is visible where it landed — and a generator with a quiet week to report is not news either, so neither posts anything. With no usable log channel the person who queued it is **DM'd** instead, and only when neither is possible does it become a line in the bot's own log; a card that cannot be delivered is caught, so one unreadable alert can never cost you a delivery. Verified against the gateway stand-ins by driving the real delivery loop: a failure posts exactly one card naming the channel and pinging the queuer, a partial send keeps the channel that refused it, a repeat that failed once says when it tries again, the third failure says it stopped and stops counting 0-of-3 for a partial, a quiet week and a successful send post nothing, and re-running the loop does not post a second card. A card built from a 5,000-character error is checked against Discord's own embed limits field by field, because an alert the API refuses is a failure nobody is told about — the exact thing this exists to prevent. 12 new tests, and the alert call, the success-is-not-news rule and the DM fallback were each removed in turn to watch their test fail. Your server's log channel was read back out of the live backup to confirm the card has somewhere real to go. Reviewing it turned up four things, all fixed: the restart sweep now takes **ownership** of each interrupted row with a conditional write (the same "the affected row count is the answer" idiom the delivery path uses), so two instances sharing a database cannot each post a card for one interruption; the sweep reports what the store **recorded** rather than assuming, because an interrupted *repeat* is not finished — it keeps its next slot, and the card says so instead of telling you to queue it again; the DM fallback asks Discord for that one id rather than trusting a member cache that holds only people the bot has met (the Members intent is off), which is what makes "the person who queued it is told" true rather than likely; and a skipped or interrupted run no longer reads as *"failure 2 of 3"*, which implied the run had counted against a streak it does not touch.
- **🧾 Everything the dashboard posts is written down, not only what it schedules**: **Send now** was the one path whose result had nowhere to live — the per-channel report was handed to the page as JSON and died with the tab, so a message the bot sent as itself left no trace beyond a `print` into an in-memory ring that goes with the process. The panel's **Recently sent** list therefore held the scheduled half of what the dashboard had posted and silently omitted the rest, which meant the sends that matter most had no answer an hour later: *which* channels got that announcement, did any of them refuse it, and who sent it. Every immediate send is now filed as a finished row in the same table — a new `source` column, added additively so a live database (and every cloud-restored copy of it) upgrades on boot — carrying the channels, the per-channel ✓/✕ with the reason, who sent it, and the moment. It is written as `sent`/`failed` and **never `pending`**, so the delivery loop cannot find it and post it a second time; it never counts against the 25-message queue cap; and the 90-day prune retires it like any other history row. The list marks the difference — a **`sent now`** pill for an immediate send, the repeat for a queued one — and refreshes as soon as a send lands, so the record is there without a reload. One fault fell out of the same surface: **the receipt's retry footer was on screen with nothing to retry** (`Retry the 0 failed channels`, under a clean send), because `display: flex` outranks the browser's own `[hidden]` rule and this was the one hidden element in the page with no guard — the page already carried three for exactly this reason. A test now walks every element the page hides and fails if its own CSS declares a `display` without a `[hidden]` rule, which is the whole family rather than the case that was noticed. Verified in a browser against the stand-in gateway: an immediate send appeared in the history as `sent now` with its ✓ and a link, a partial row kept the refusal beside it, and the retry footer is gone when there is nothing to retry. Eight new tests, with the recording call, the terminal status and the queue-cap separation each removed in turn to watch its test fail — and `db.setup()` run against a database in the old shape to prove the column is added, old rows read as `schedule`, and nothing else moves.
- **⏰ The dashboard can schedule a message, and the promise outlives the page that made it**: the composer gained a **Deliver — Send now | Schedule** control: pick a date and time, choose once / daily / weekly, and the bot posts it later as itself, into the same channels, tagging the same people, whether or not anyone still has the dashboard open. The queue is a **row in the database**, not a timer in the process — the message that gets posted is the one that was validated and stored when it was queued (tags already resolved against that server), so a restart, a redeploy or a cloud restore cannot lose it or change it. Delivery is guarded by one conditional `UPDATE … WHERE status = 'pending'`: the affected row count *is* the exactly-once guarantee, so the delivery loop, a second bot process, and an administrator's **Send now** cannot post the same message twice. A repeat keeps its own slot rather than drifting: a daily 09:00 post the bot slept through does not fire six times when it wakes — it fires once and moves to the next 09:00, computed rather than stepped — and three failures in a row stops it instead of nagging daily forever. A message more than 24h late is **skipped and says why** instead of arriving at the wrong time, one interrupted mid-send by a restart is written down as failed (with the warning to check the channel before resending) rather than retried, cancelling is recorded as *cancelled* in the history, and a **cancelled or sent message never disappears from the panel**. The rate limit is checked *before* a message is claimed, so a throttled "Send now" leaves it queued instead of stranded mid-flight. The panel shows **Queued** and **Recently sent** with a per-channel outcome (✓ with a link to the post, ✕ with the reason), the repeat, a ⚠️ `@everyone` marker, who queued it, and a countdown in your own time zone — while the *delivery* time is the server's, so a wrong clock on the machine scheduling it cannot move the post. Every limit the page offers (5 channels, 25 queued, the 24h catch-up, the three repeats) comes from the store the endpoint enforces. Verified in a browser against the stand-in gateway: queued a daily message (the posted body carried the exact snowflake and `run_at_ms`), fired it (receipt `Sent`, the row stayed queued for its next slot **and** appeared under Recently sent), cancelled one (gone from the queue, shown as cancelled), had a past time refused with the server's own clock, declined the `@everyone` confirmation (nothing was posted) and measured 0px of overflow at a 440px viewport. 265 tests, with each of the four guards — the conditional claim, the tick's per-message isolation, the rate-check ordering, and the queue-time channel check — removed in turn to watch its test fail.
- **🧾 The result of a send is a receipt now, not a three-second toast**: the composer peaks at one irreversible moment — the bot speaks as the bot, to up to five channels, sometimes pinging a whole server — and the only record of it was a stack of toasts that faded after three seconds plus a line reading "Sent to 2 channels". The server had already worked out a per-channel result and the page threw it away. There is now a **receipt that stays on the page**: one row per channel, ✓ with a link to the message or ✕ with the reason it was refused, a timestamp, the tags that could not be resolved (a dropped tag is part of what happened, not a toast that disappears), and — when something failed — **Retry the failed channels**, which repeats exactly the message that failed rather than whatever the form says by then, and keeps what already arrived on the receipt while it does. Two faults came out with it. A send that reached **nobody** was reported as `Could not send: Request failed (200)` and never reached the page at all, because `apiPost` treats `success: false` as an error while for this one endpoint that response *is* the answer. And a **mass ping** — @everyone or @here across several channels at once — was a single unconfirmed click: the preview now warns before the button is pressed and the send asks once, because that is the only part of a message that cannot be taken back by editing it afterwards. The receipt's summary is a pure function, and a test extracts it from the shipped page and runs it in a real JavaScript engine against every shape of reply (all sent, partly sent, nothing sent, a dropped tag, a retry, a channel named twice).
- **🔴 A deploy could change nothing you could see**: the dashboard and login pages went out with **no `Cache-Control` header at all**, so a browser was free to answer the next visit out of its own copy — and a rewritten dashboard looks exactly like one that was never deployed, deploy after deploy, with nothing in the logs and no error on the page to explain it. Pages and their APIs now say `no-store`, so the server's answer is the one you get; the two public market endpoints, which deliberately ask for 5- and 30-second caches, keep what they asked for. (Adding `?v=2` to the URL, or clearing site data once, gets a stale copy out of the way on any browser that is already holding one — including the embedded browser Discord opens links in, where a reload is easy to miss.)
- **🔊 Voice channels are in the dashboard composer's picker**: a voice channel has a text chat of its own, `discord.VoiceChannel` is `Messageable`, and `.send()` posts into that chat — which is where the people sitting in the call are actually looking. The picker only ever read `guild.text_channels`, so the one channel people sit in was the one channel a message could not reach; its own comment claimed a voice channel "is reached by joining it", which is not true of sending to it. Voice channels now appear under their own category with a 🔊 marker and follow the same permission rules as text channels (one the bot cannot post in is greyed as `no permission`, one it cannot see is not offered), and a send resolves them exactly like any other channel — driven in a browser against a stand-in server, the id handed back matched the channel and the post succeeded. **One thing cannot be known in advance**: Discord also requires the voice channel's text chat to be switched on, and no field in the API reports whether it is, so the refusal is the first thing that learns it — and it now says exactly that, because "missing permission" sent an administrator hunting for a role overwrite that was not the problem. (Forums are still left out, for the reason that is real: a forum takes threads rather than messages, so `ForumChannel` is not `Messageable`.)
- **🔴 Every control on the dashboard was dead, and the page looked fine**: one string in the composer's member-count note was built as `` `…of them (the Members intent is off), so tag anyone else by name` `` with a stray `"` and a `+` in the middle, which closed the template literal early and left bare words where an expression belonged — a **syntax error in the page's only `<script>` block**, so the browser discarded the whole thing. The channel picker, the tag search, Send, Clear and every module toggle did nothing at all, while the markup and the CSS rendered perfectly and no test noticed: the template renders, the ids are present, the strings are there to be asserted on. The note is one template literal again, and the gap that let it through is closed with a test that **renders the shipped template and runs the result through a real JavaScript engine** (`node --check`), plus a second that collects every `getElementById("…")` in the script and fails if the page never renders that id — both verified to fail against the exact bugs they were written for.
- **🎛️ The channel picker is a list you can click, not a `<select multiple>`**: it needed Ctrl/Cmd-click to pick a second channel, could not be searched, hid which channels were already chosen, and did nothing at all on a touch screen — making the one control that has to be easy the hardest part of sending a message. It is now a grouped list: a filter box (channel name or category), sticky category headers with their channel counts, a tick that fills in on the channels you picked, and a `2 of 5 selected` badge that turns amber at the cap. A channel the bot cannot post in is **shown greyed with `no permission`** rather than hidden, and announcement channels are marked `news`; the cap comes from the server's own `MESSAGE_MAX_CHANNELS`, so the page cannot offer a sixth channel the endpoint would then refuse.
- **⌨️ The tag search now behaves like one**: results are grouped under **Special / Roles / Members** headers instead of a flat run of rows, ↑ and ↓ walk them, Enter takes the highlighted row (or the best match), Esc closes, and the highlighted row is scrolled into view and announced through `aria-activedescendant`. The "we can only see N of M members" line and the empty state are notes rather than rows that look clickable. Two faults went with it: typing fast could let a **slower earlier answer overwrite a later one** (each request is now numbered and a stale reply is dropped), and a picked tag collapsed its group header onto the next one instead of vanishing with it.
- **👥 The dashboard tag picker lists the whole server now — and admits it when it cannot**: an empty box offered roles and nothing else, so tagging people meant typing a name for each one. Focusing it lists every member alphabetically, and the counts it is working from are printed under the list ("showing 12 of 36 members — the bot can only see 12 of them") whenever those two numbers disagree. The list is capped at 500 and **the cap is reported rather than applied quietly**, because a truncated list that says nothing is how a person you meant to tag stays invisible; typing a name still searches the live member list, so somebody past the cap is never unreachable.
- **🔴 Tagging somebody the bot had never cached answered "not in this server"**: the **Members privileged intent is off for this app** — `ENABLE_MEMBERS_INTENT` is unset and the app has it disabled in the Developer Portal, which is why its member *list* endpoint answers `403 Missing Access`, while a one-id lookup is allowed. So `guild.members` holds only the people the bot has happened to run into, and a tag for anybody else was dropped with "Could not tag X — not in this server": a flat lie about a member who is in the server. A tag is an id, so the path now asks Discord for that one id before giving up. Verified end to end against the live guild with an empty cache (tagging the owner posted `<@1549177590328205393> hello` and pinged exactly that person), and the boot log now names the intent and prints `N of M members are cached` per server, because an incomplete cache was previously invisible from inside the bot. **Enabling it is a two-step change: turn it on in the Developer Portal first, then set `ENABLE_MEMBERS_INTENT=1`** — the other order stops the bot from starting at all.
- **🔴 The person who *handled* a ticket was the one being invited to rate it**: the star prompt is posted in the ticket channel, which is exactly where the member who closed the ticket is standing — and whoever claimed a ticket is usually the one who closes it — so staff were being asked to mark their own work, and could: any tap opened the rating. The rating belongs to the opener now. The buttons resolve the ticket and turn anyone else away with the reason, and the same rule is applied again where the rating is actually written, so nothing can be recorded against the opener's name by somebody else. (The DM copy already went to the opener, which is the one place a rating could arrive with nobody around to give it.)
- **🔴 `@everyone` and `@here` vanished from the dashboard tag search while you typed toward them**: the endpoint offered them only when the query already *contained* the whole keyword (`"every" in query`), so typing `ev` offered nothing and the option looked missing — the opposite of how a role or a member is matched, where what you typed is looked for *in* the name. They are matched the same way as everything else now, so `e`, `ev` and `every` all keep them on offer. On the page they also rendered as **@@everyone**, because the client prefixed an `@` onto a label that already carried one.
- **🛠️ Two smaller faults in the same composer**: picking **@here** posted **@everyone** — the two keywords raised one shared flag, so asking for "everyone online right now" pinged the entire server; the keyword that was picked is now the keyword that is posted, while `allowed_mentions.everyone` still travels with the payload because that is what lets either of them notify at all. And a message that was **only a title, with "Send as an embed" unticked**, was accepted and then refused by Discord, since outside an embed the title is not sent and the message arrives empty; that request is now turned down with the reason, and a title with the embed ticked still posts on its own.
- **🔴 Every send from the dashboard composer answered "That channel no longer exists"**: the picker filled in correctly, but the ids it offered crossed to the browser as JSON **numbers**, and a Discord snowflake is larger than a JavaScript number holds exactly — `1548437081120833657` comes back from `JSON.parse` as `1548437081120833500`. The page therefore posted an id that matched no channel, and `guild.get_channel` returned `None` for every one of them, so the message could never be delivered. Channel, member and role ids are now **strings** and come home through `_int_or_none` untouched; the page's own guild id is a string for the same reason, since it is posted back with every request. Pinned by a test that every id the API hands the browser is a string, and by another that a string channel id still resolves; verified in a JavaScript engine against the JSON the running server emits — the posted body carries the exact id, where the identical pipeline with a number produced `...833500`. (The picker's stub ids were `1`, `2`, `3`, which survive any arithmetic, so the harness was rebuilt to serve real-sized snowflakes and to resolve a send exactly as the bot does — a browser checking it now sees the same failure the live page showed.)
- **🔴 The dashboard's channel picker was empty, and reported it as "Could not load channels: Request failed (500)"**: `/api/channels` walked `guild.text_channels` **plus `guild.news_channels`** — and discord.py has no `Guild.news_channels`, because an announcement channel *is* a `TextChannel` whose type is `news` (`channel.is_news()`) rather than a class of its own. So every authenticated request raised `AttributeError` before it listed a single channel, and the composer — the one feature whose entire job is choosing a channel — could never be aimed at one. The loop now reads `guild.text_channels` alone, which already contains the announcement channels. The reason the suite was green is the more useful half of the fix: the composer's stand-in guild *defined* `news_channels`, because it was written to satisfy the production code, and a fake that agrees with a typo hides it forever. A test now asserts that each stand-in exposes only names its real discord.py counterpart exposes — it fails against the old stub with `_Guild invents ['news_channels'], which Guild does not have` — and every attribute read on a Discord object across `keep_alive.py` and `cogs/tickets.py` was swept against the installed library for the same fault, which found none.
- **🔴 A server-scoped dashboard sign-in could drive every *other* server the bot is in**: the one-time link from `/dashboard` is scoped to the server it was created in, but `dashboard()` wrote the `0` fallback of a failed lookup back into the session — so a single page load turned a scoped session into an unscoped one, and from then on it could toggle modules on any server the bot sits in and (with the new composer) post in any of their channels. The scope test now reads the scope alone, a failed lookup is never persisted, and a scoped session gets its own guild or a `400`. Verified against a stand-in gateway: a scoped link for a server the bot has left can neither read states, toggle a module, nor deliver a message `(`, and the session is left untouched.
- **🔴 `/dashboard` answered 500 whenever the gateway had no real latency**: `discord.Client.latency` is `nan` until the first heartbeat is acknowledged and `inf` while the socket is rebuilding — and `nan` is *truthy*, so the `if latency:` guard let `round(nan * 1000)` through with a `ValueError`. The page an administrator opens *because* something looks wrong was the page that crashed. One helper now computes it and refuses anything non-finite, and the card reads `—` rather than pretending a missing heartbeat is a `0 ms` ping. `/api/voice-debug` had the same fault in JSON form: a bare `NaN` is not valid JSON and every browser's `JSON.parse` rejects it, so unknown latencies are `null`.
- **🔴 The market chart could never draw on any deployment, and the page blamed the CDN**: the chart's only library is loaded from unpkg with a jsdelivr fallback, and the dashboard's own `Content-Security-Policy` named neither host — `script-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline'` refused both copies, so the chart card honestly reported "the chart library could not be loaded" while the library was fine and it was our own header refusing it. Both pinned hosts are named now, and a test scans every template for external `.js` and fails if the policy forbids its origin. Verified both ways in a browser against the real header: with the old policy the console shows *Refused to load the script … script-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline'* for both CDNs, and with the fix the library loads and the chart draws.
- **🔐 On Render the dashboard had no signing key and no passkey**: `render.yaml` declared the Discord token and the backup bucket and nothing else, so `FLASK_SECRET_KEY` was regenerated on every boot — each deploy, and each wake-up from the free tier's sleep, signed the administrator out and silently killed any one-time link they were holding — and `ADMIN_PASSKEY` was unset, which disables passkey sign-in outright and left `/dashboard` in Discord (which needs the bot awake to answer) as the only way in. Both are declared now, as prompts rather than committed values. The sign-in link is also built from the URL of the process that minted the token: run from the development Mac it handed out links to the Render URL, whose process had never seen the token, which is what "that sign-in link has expired or already been used" actually was.
- **A dashboard that cannot listen says so**: if the port was already held (an older instance of the bot, typically) the server thread died inside itself and the only symptom was that the dashboard never loaded. It now names the port and the likely cause. The server shown when a session has nothing selected is also picked deterministically instead of out of a set's iteration order.
- **📣 The dashboard can post as the bot — in any channel, tagging anyone**: `/dashboard` gained a **Message composer**: pick one or more channels (up to five at once), write the message, search members and roles by name to tag them, choose a plain message or an embed with a title, and see a live preview of exactly what the bot will post before pressing Send. Everything is re-resolved against the live server rather than trusted from the browser, because a browser can send anything: a tag that is not a member or a role of *the server being written to* is dropped and named in the reply instead of pinged, a channel the bot cannot see is not offered and one it cannot post in is refused with the reason, the guild comes from the signed-in session so a server-pinned link cannot be talked into writing elsewhere, and the tags are placed in the message **content** rather than inside the embed — a mention inside an embed does not notify, which is the one thing a tag-someone feature must not get wrong. The 2000-character limit is measured with the tags included, an over-long message is refused with both numbers and the suggestion to send it as an embed, one client is capped at 30 sends a minute, and each send is reported per channel so a partial failure is never shown as a success.
- **⭐ A closed ticket now asks for a rating, and the answer goes to the log channel**: closing a ticket posts a one-to-five star prompt (with an optional comment) in the ticket channel **and DMs it to whoever opened the ticket**, because the member who closes a ticket is rarely the member who raised it. The rating is posted to the server's log channel as a card that tags **both** the opener and the staff member who claimed it, and a **thread is created on that post** so the reply can be followed up on. Two details make it hold together: the ticket row is written when it closes rather than when a star is tapped, which is what lets a rating answered from the DM still know who to tag once the ticket channel has been deleted a minute later; and the rating is a conditional `UPDATE`, so the same ticket answered twice — once in the channel, once in the DM — logs once and says it already has an answer. Who claimed a ticket is now recorded in the channel topic as well as the button label, since the topic is the only claim state that survives a restart. `/ticket setup`, `/ticket add` and `/ticket remove` are unchanged.
- **📊 Activity Tracker: per-day, per-channel message & voice time tracking**: new `/activity`, `/activity-top`, `/activity-channel` slash commands track every message sent and every voice-channel second spent, broken down by day and channel, with a 365-day rolling window. Voice time is measured in seconds via join/leave timestamps for sub-minute accuracy. Leaderboards rank members by messages, voice time, or active days over any period. Background cleanup purges old data every 6 hours. A race condition in the periodic voice flush that could create phantom entries and double-count time has been fixed.
- **🔴 The live market page went blank and blamed nobody**: `/market/<SYMBOL>` loads its chart library from a CDN, and that one blocking `<script>` was the whole page's single point of failure — if it was blocked, slow, or still in flight when a restored tab was captured, the reference error inside `initChart()` killed the rest of the same inline script, so the price, the symbol picker, the watchlist, the period move and the what-if calculator all sat on their `—` defaults behind a permanent "Loading live prices…". The API was healthy the whole time; the page just could not say so. The chart is now optional: quotes are fetched with a timeout, the chart script itself gets a deadline (a CDN that accepts the connection and then says nothing is a failure, not loading), the numbers are computed from the data rather than the canvas, a second CDN is tried, and a library that still will not load is **named in the chart card** instead of being drawn as an empty rectangle. Reproduced by pointing the page at a dead host — same stuck status, empty picker, empty watchlist — and re-checked both ways after the fix.
- **📎 The big-file upload feature is retired**: `/upload`, `/upload-big` and `/pin-upload-info` are gone, along with the instructions post the bot dropped into the upload channel on every boot, the `#uploads` channel binding in `/updatedb`, the dashboard card and `UPLOAD_CHANNEL_ID`. It earned its removal: `/upload` handed back an empty file.kiwi folder the member had to fill in by hand, and the host's preview of what did arrive never rendered, so "the upload finished but nothing shows" was the normal outcome rather than a bug. Members are back to Discord's own attachment limit. `cogs/uploads.py` and the four host clients (`catbox`, `file_kiwi`, `filehost`, `streamable`) are archived intact in `deleted_features/`, with restore steps in that folder's README; the `upload_channel_id` guild setting is deliberately left in the database, where nothing reads it. **The three commands are also taken out of Discord's picker**: the sync only ever upserts — that is what stops a spent write budget from clearing the guild — so a command with no local counterpart is neither missing nor stale and would otherwise sit there forever, failing for whoever clicked it. A short explicit list (`commands_sync.RETIRED_COMMANDS`) is now the only thing the sync may delete, one named command at a time, and a test pins that nothing else can vanish.
- **🔴 `/upload` stopped posting anything and told you to configure a channel**: the upload channel became `guild_settings.upload_channel_id`, then `UPLOAD_CHANNEL_ID`, then *nothing* — and with the environment variable unset every server got "❌ No upload channel is configured for this server" instead of a working command, while the sticky post in the same channel kept advertising it. The fallback is a real channel again, a setting that points at a deleted channel or another server now falls through instead of taking uploads down with it, a mistyped `UPLOAD_CHANNEL_ID` is a fallback rather than a crash at import, and the sticky post reads the same constant as `/upload`, so the two cannot disagree. There were three copies of that channel id in the tree; there is now one.
- **The sticky upload post could duplicate itself on every restart**: it looked for its own message in the last 50 messages, so as soon as the channel got busier than that it could not find the pin it had just made and posted another one. It asks for the channel's pins directly now, and only falls back to a history scan if that fails.
- **A burst of uploads could starve the database backup**: every upload holds a worker thread for up to five minutes on the same executor the backup loop uses, so fourteen concurrent `/upload` calls queued every snapshot behind them — the "my progress did not save" symptom arriving through a different door. Uploads are capped at four at once, which leaves the backup room to run.
- **🔴 The title clamp sat exactly on the boundary it was supposed to stay under**: Discord measures an embed title in UTF-16 units, so the `📎` prefix alone costs two and a "256-character" title was really 257 units — one emoji past the limit, refused with the very HTTP 400 the clamp exists to prevent. The limit is now measured the way the API measures it, which also stops a trim from slicing a surrogate pair in half and leaving a replacement character in the title. The same unit-aware rule bounds thread names, message content, and the failure card, and the sticky instructions embed is now pinned against Discord's embed limits too, since a rejected one fails silently — the only symptom is that nothing appears.
- **🔴 A long title could throw away the upload it had just paid for**: Discord rejects an embed whose title is longer than 256 characters, and `/upload` built its title straight from the member's `title` (or the attachment filename) with nothing bounding it — so `channel.send` raised *after* the bytes were already on catbox, the deferred command died with no reply, and the member got neither the link nor an error. The title is now clamped, and a rejected post is caught and answered with the link anyway, because the file is already uploaded at that point. The failure card had the same shape: a service error carrying thousands of characters pushed it past Discord's 2,000-character message limit, so the explanation was itself rejected — each reason is now one bounded line and the card is cut to fit.
- **🔴 Backups were uploading a database that was missing the newest rows**: the database runs in WAL mode, so every row committed since the last checkpoint lives in `bot.db-wal` — and the upload read the main file with a plain `open(..., 'rb')` after a **best-effort** checkpoint that quietly gives up when any other reader or writer is busy, which on a live bot is most of the time. The uploaded copy was therefore *older than the database it came from*, and the next restart restored that older state: this is what "my level and coins went backwards after a restart" is, and it is not a lost restore at all. Snapshots now go through SQLite's own backup API, which copies a transactionally consistent database including the write-ahead log, and a test commits a row, deliberately never checkpoints it, uploads, and then opens the uploaded bytes to prove the row is in them.
- **🔴 A failed restore looked exactly like an empty bucket, and then the empty database replaced the backup**: `_storage_list()` returned `[]` both when the bucket was genuinely empty and when the Supabase call itself failed — so a network blip at boot told the bot it was a brand-new server, and 25 seconds later its empty database was uploaded over everybody's progress. That is the reset. The failure is now a distinct value (`None`), the restore **retries** before giving up, and when it still cannot read the backup the instance is left **blocked from uploading** — permanently, until a restart succeeds — because an instance that could not read the only copy must never write over it. While it is still empty it keeps retrying in the background, so a container that booted during an outage heals itself instead of needing to be redeployed.
- **Only a descendant of the backup may overwrite it, and the bucket is consulted before the first write either way**: a restored instance is a legitimate heir; an instance that never restored is a stranger whose database is a different truth, and it is refused with the reason printed and shown in `/dbstatus`. That check used to depend on the startup restore having run, which is a guard with a way around it — the dashboard's own thread can ask for a backup — so the backup path now lists the bucket itself when nobody has, and being **unable to list it is not permission to write over it**. A file that will not open as SQLite is refused outright rather than treated as an empty server, which is the distinction between damage and a fresh start. A **collapse** in row counts is held back for four checks — long enough for a half-finished write or a restarting neighbour to sort itself out — and then accepted, because a server that really was reset has to be able to save again. A deliberate wipe can skip the wait with `CLOUD_SYNC_ALLOW_SHRINK=1`.
- **Two instances can no longer take turns reverting each other's progress**: the bot is run from Render *and* from a launchd agent on the development Mac, both pointing at one bucket, so whichever booted last uploaded its own idea of the database — the smaller, older one included. A **lease** object in the bucket names the host that owns the writes and is refreshed while it is alive (every two minutes idle, on every upload otherwise); another host that starts up restores the backup read-only and is told to keep its hands off until the owner has been silent for five minutes. It is deliberately advisory — an unreadable lease does not stop backups, since the descendant rule above is the one that must never fail.
- **A redeploy saves before it dies**: Render stops a container with `SIGTERM` and kills it seconds later, and a signalled Python process never reaches its `finally` block — so every coin and XP earned since the last 25-second tick was thrown away on each deploy. `main()` installs a `SIGTERM` handler that writes a final snapshot first.
- **🔴 The snapshot retention rule had never once run, and the failure was silent**: `_storage_delete` reused the shared JSON headers for a bodiless `DELETE`, and Supabase Storage answers exactly that with `400 Body cannot be empty when content-type is set to 'application/json'`. The error was read by a bare `except` and turned into `False`, which the pruner ignored — so the bucket held **15** snapshots under a documented "keep the newest 5", and every deploy added more. The request now carries no content type, a refusal is printed instead of swallowed, the prune reports how many it removed, and a test asserts the request shape itself — because the in-memory fake bucket in the suite accepted anything, which is exactly why this passed its own tests for weeks while production pruned nothing.
- **`/dbstatus` no longer stalls the whole bot while it asks**: the refusal check talks to Supabase and reads SQLite, and it ran inline in the command's coroutine — measured at **507 ms** of frozen event loop with a stand-in for the network, and the real Supabase has answered in seconds, during which no other command, no voice payout and not even the gateway heartbeat could run. It goes through `asyncio.to_thread` like the rest of that card, and a test asserts the decision is computed on a worker thread so it cannot quietly move back.
- **A server whose every backup was empty could never save again**: the restore prefers a full snapshot over an empty one, which is right, but when *all* candidates were empty it fell through to "no usable backup found" and blocked uploads **permanently** — so a brand-new deployment could bring the bot up, hand out coins, and never persist a byte. An empty backup is a worse answer than a full one, but it is still an answer: the newest empty candidate is now installed and saving resumes, with the reason logged.
- **`/dbstatus` says why an upload is not happening**: the refusal is computed by one function (`upload_refusal_reason`) that both the backup path and the card call, so the card cannot drift from the rule that is actually applied, and it names the backup it restored with the row counts it restored — the number a human needs to tell "the restore worked" from "the restore silently gave me nothing".
- **📎 `/upload` uploads the file now — it used to post a link to nothing**: the command downloaded the attachment and then only called file.kiwi's *empty folder* endpoint, which creates a folder for a human to drag a file into. The link that landed in the channel contained **no file at all**, and the failure looked like success. `/upload` now streams the bytes straight off disk to **catbox.moe** — 200 MB, no API key, permanent, and it answers range requests so Discord plays videos inline — and the message links to the file itself. Verified end to end by fetching the uploaded URL back and comparing it byte for byte with the source, including chunked (5 MB) uploads, an oversize file, an empty file, and a filename full of quotes and newlines; the body is built as an exact-`Content-Length` stream, because `http.client` would otherwise fall back to chunked transfer encoding, which catbox refuses.
- **file.io was dead weight and is out of the chain**: every upload paid a full round trip to a host that answers with an HTML page instead of a link, and then printed its failure in the member's error card. The chain is now catbox → Streamable (video, when credentials exist) → **file.kiwi** as the honest last resort, and the last-resort link says plainly that the member still has to drag the file in. A `catbox` 5xx that carries a URL in its body is treated as the success it is rather than thrown away after the bytes were already uploaded.
- **🔴 The update note could be marked as delivered without ever being sent**: the changelog hash was written to the database *after* the post attempt but unconditionally — so a missing channel, a rejected embed, or a deploy that restarted mid-announcement permanently consumed the note and no later boot ever tried again. That hash is now stored only once Discord has accepted the message, and the digest that goes into it is built for the limit rather than for the file: the newest section's entries, each cut to one readable line, capped at six entries and 3,300 characters, because an embed description over 4,096 gets a 400 — which is how "the update never showed up" happens. `tests/test_update_announcement.py` renders the **shipped** CHANGELOG through the real summarizer and fails if it stops fitting.
- **The pinned upload instructions are one embed, not two copies**: the startup sticky post and `/pin-upload-info` each carried their own hand-written text — both still advertising the dead file.io — so the channel's instructions drifted from what the bot does the moment either changed. Both now render `cogs.uploads.build_info_embed()`.
- **Supabase replaces the Gist for database persistence**: the old Gist-based backup was encrypted (NaCl), base64-encoded, and stored in a public-by-default gist — three moving parts that each had a failure mode. Supabase Storage is private by default (the service-role key is required for every read and write), so the application-layer encryption, base64 encoding, and public-gist warnings are all gone. The upload and download paths are raw binary, and the restore/backup cycle is the same: download on startup, upload every 25 seconds, hourly snapshots pruned to five. The `SUPABASE_URL` and `SUPABASE_SERVICE_KEY` environment variables replace `GITHUB_BACKUP_TOKEN` and `GIST_BACKUP_ID`. Existing Gist backups are still restorable — the first deploy downloads the legacy plaintext file from the Gist and writes it to Supabase, after which the Gist is never used again.
- **🐾 The Meow Fee: a small cut on coins moving between members, banked instead of burned**: sending coins (`/pay`) and the takings from a successful robbery (`/rob`) now carry **2%**, at least 1 🪙 and never more than 500 🪙, paid into **Meow Bank** — a per-server house account. The market's treat fee *leaves* the economy; this one stays in it, which is exactly why it is small: there is no inflation argument for making it bite, only the mild cost of passing coins to somebody else.
